There is scarcely a day that passes beneath the sun wherein men and women of obscurity do not perform great deeds, utter profound words, and endure noble sorrows. Among these forgotten heroes, philosophers, and martyrs, the majority will remain unknown until that distant hour when the mighty shall shrink to insignificance, and the humble shall rise to greatness. Yet, regarding others, the world's memory remains asleep; their lives and characters lie buried in the archives of nations, hidden from view. To the general reader, these figures offer no warmth; they are presented so briefly and coldly that they feel less like breathing stories appealing to the heart, and more like tiny hailstones striking the chest and glancing harmlessly off. Nor can one truly understand them, for an epitome is not a narrative, just as a skeleton is not a living figure.

Consequently, records of fundamental truths often remain a dead letter to the common folk. Writers have left too much to the imagination—a gift that is exceedingly rare. Here, therefore, the author of fiction may serve the public as an interpreter.

Consider a musty chronicle, penned in intolerable Latin, containing a chapter where every sentence bears a heavy fact. Within it is recorded, with harsh brevity, the strange history of a pair who lived unheard and died unsung four hundred years ago. They now lie, unpitied, in that stern page like fossils trapped in rock. Whether living or dead, Fate remains unjust to them. For if I can reveal what lies beneath those dry chronicler's words, I trust you will correct the indifference of centuries and grant those two sore-tried souls a place in your heart—for at least a day.

It was past the midpoint of the fifteenth century. Louis XI ruled France; Edward IV sat, wrongly, upon the throne of England; and Philip "the Good," having seized power through force and cunning against his cousin Jacqueline and shattered her spirit, reigned undisturbed over Holland, where our tale commences.

In the little town of Tergou lived Elias and his wife, Catherine. Elias engaged in both wholesale and retail trade, dealing in cloth, silk, brown holland, and, most notably, curried leather. This material was highly prized by the middle classes for its durability, able to withstand twenty years of wear. In a century where men were so liberal with their steel—even carving neighbors at dinner over minor disagreements—a jerkin that could resist a blade was a significant virtue.

The couple were comfortably situated and would have been free from worldly cares but for their nine children. When these offspring arrived, one per year, they were greeted with rejoicing, and saints were thanked rather than questioned. While parents and children were young, the latter were viewed as lovely playthings invented by Heaven solely for the amusement, joy, and evening solace of busy adults.

However, as the olive branches sprouted upward and the parents aged, witnessing firsthand the fate of large families, misgivings and anxiety mingled with their love. They belonged to a remarkably wise and provident people; in Holland, reckless parents were as rare as disobedient children. Thus, when a massive loaf arrived on a gigantic trencher, resembling a fortress surrounded by a moat, and appeared to vanish after the tour of the table, Elias and Catherine would exchange a worried glance. "Who will find bread for them all when we are gone?" they would ask.

At this observation, the younger children required all their filial respect to maintain their little Dutch composure. In their minds, dinner and supper came by nature like sunrise and sunset. As long as the sun traveled around the earth and the brown loaf circled their family circle—setting in their stomachs only to rise again in the family oven—they believed they were secure. But this remark awakened the national thoughtfulness of the elder boys. Being often repeated, it caused several members of the family to ponder, generating both good and ill thoughts depending on the thinker's nature.

"Kate, the children are growing so large; this table will soon be too small."

"We cannot afford it, Eli," replied Catherine, answering not his words but his thought, in the manner of women.

Their anxiety for the future occasionally took a less dismal but more mortifying turn. Free burghers possessed pride equal to that of nobles, and these two could not bear the thought of any of their blood descending into servitude after their deaths. Through prudence and self-denial, they managed to clothe all the little bodies, feed all the great mouths, and yet save a small hoard for the future. As this fund grew, they experienced a pleasure the miser hoarding for himself knows not.

One day, the second-eldest son, aged nineteen, approached his mother. With that outward composure that has so often misled observers regarding the true nature of this people, he begged her to intercede with his father to send him to Amsterdam to apprentice with a merchant. "It is the path suited to my nature," he pleaded. "Merchants are wealthy; I am skilled with numbers. Pray, good mother, support me in this, and I shall forever remain, as I am now, your debtor."

Catherine threw up her hands in dismay and incredulity. "What! Leave Tergou!"

"What is one street to me more than another? If I can leave the people of Tergou, I can surely leave the stones."

"What! Quit your poor father while he is no longer young?"

"Mother, if I can leave you, I can leave..."

"What! Leave your poor brothers and sisters who love you so dearly?"

"There are enough in the house without me."

"What mean you, Richart? Who is more thought of than you? Stay, have I spoken sharply to you? Have I been unkind?"

"Never that I know of; and if you had, you should never hear of it from me. Mother," said Richart gravely, though a tear glistened in his eye, "it all comes down to a single word, and nothing can change my mind. There will be one mouth less for you to feed."

"There now, see what my tongue has done," said Catherine, and the next moment she began to weep. She saw her first young bird standing on the edge of the nest, testing his wings to fly into the world. Richart possessed a calm, strong will, and she knew he never wasted a word.

The conversation ended as nature decrees all such discourses shall: young Richart departed for Amsterdam with a face so long and sad as it had never been seen before, and a heart like granite.

That afternoon at supper, there was one mouth less. Catherine looked at Richart's empty chair and wept bitterly. Overhearing this, Elias shouted roughly and angrily at the remaining children, "Sit wider, can't ye? Sit wider!" He then turned his head away over the back of his seat for a while, falling into silence.

Richart was launched and never cost them another penny; however, fitting him out and placing him in the house of Vander Stegen, the merchant, consumed their entire little hoard minus one gold crown. They began anew. Two years later, Richart found a niche in commerce for his brother Jacob, who left Tergou directly after dinner—which was served at eleven in the forenoon. At that supper, remembering the previous departure, Elias whispered, "Sit wider, dears!" Until that moment, Catherine had refused to acknowledge the gap at the table, for her daughter Catherine had beseeched her not to grieve that night. "No, sweetheart, I promise I will not, since it vexes my children," she had replied. But when Elias whispered "Sit wider!", she answered, "Ay! The table will soon be too big for the children, and you thought it would be too small." Delivering this with forced calmness, she raised her apron the next moment and wept sorely.

"'Tis the best that leave us," she sobbed. "That is the cruel part."

"Nay! Nay!" said Elias. "Our children are good children, and all are dear to us alike. Heed her not! What God takes from us still seems better than what He spares to us; that is to say, men are by nature unthankful—and women silly."

"And I say Richart and Jacob were the flower of the flock," sobbed Catherine.

The little coffer was empty again, and to refill it, they gathered like ants. In those days, speculation was largely confined to the card-and-dice business. Elias knew no way to wealth but the slow and sure method. "A penny saved is a penny gained" was his humble creed. Everything not required for business and the necessities of life went into the little coffer, secured with steel bands and a florid key. They denied themselves, in turn, the humblest luxuries, and then, catching one another's looks, smiled—with perhaps greater joy than self-indulgence could bestow. Thus, in three more years, they had gleaned enough to establish their fourth son as a master tailor and their eldest daughter as a robe-maker in Tergou. Their own trade enabled them to throw work into the hands of this pair. But the coffer was drained to the dregs, and this time the shop itself bled slightly in goods, if not in coin.

Alas! Two remained unable to earn their bread, and two were unwilling. The unable ones were: first, Giles, a dwarf of the wrong sort, possessing half stupidity and half malice, all head, claws, and voice. Run from by dogs and unbiased females, he was sided with through thick and thin by his mother. Second was Little Catherine, a poor little girl who could only move

"The soup is piping hot," announced Gerard.

"But how on earth are we to get it to our mouths?" queried the elder, speaking with evident despair.

"Father, the young man has provided us with straws!" Margaret replied, a mischievous smile playing upon her lips.

"Ay, ay!" acknowledged the old man. "Yet my poor bones are stiff, and truly, the fire burns too fiercely for a body to kneel over while using such slender tubes. By Saint John the Baptist, but the lad is remarkably dexterous!"

As he voiced his predicament, Gerard swiftly resolved it. In an instant, he loosened the knot at his breast, removed his cap, placed a stone into each corner of it, wrapped his handkerchief around his fist, snatched the flask from the heat, wedged it securely between the stones, and pressed the cap firmly against the old man's nose with a cheerful grin. Trembling slightly, the elder inserted the end of the rye-straw and drew deeply. Immediately, his pale, drawn visage seemed to ignite, growing brighter until it glowed warmly. Upon drawing a long, sustaining breath, he exclaimed:

"Hippocrates and Galen!" he cried. "'Tis *soupe au vin*—the restorer of all restoratives. Blessed be the nation that devised it, the woman who crafted it, and the youth who delivers it to the fainting. Give a sip, my daughter, while I recount to our young host the history and virtues of this sovereign remedy. This elixir was unknown to the ancients; we find it nowhere in their medical treatises nor in those popular chronicles that detail their cures for surgery and disease alike. Hector, in the *Iliad*, if my memory serves me correctly—

—was invited by one of the lady poets to partake of a draught of wine, yet he declined, citing that he was preparing for battle and must not consume anything to weaken his strength. Now, had the *soupe au vin* been known in Troy, it is certain that in refusing plain wine on those grounds, he would have added in his verse: 'But a *soupe au vin*, madam, I shall taste, and with gratitude.' Not only would this have been mere courtesy—a virtue lacking in no perfect commander—but failing to do so would have marked him as shallow and imprudent, unfit to lead armies. For men entering battle require sustenance and every possible advantage, as proven by the fact that foolish generals, sending hungry soldiers against well-fed foes, have been defeated throughout the ages by inferior forces. The Romans suffered a great defeat in northern Italy against Hannibal the Carthaginian solely due to this oversight. Now, this divine nectar grants strength to the limbs and ardor to the spirit in a single moment; taken by Hector at the critical juncture, aided by Apollo, Venus, and the blessed saints, it would most likely have secured victory for the Greeks. Observe how faint, weary, and heartbroken I was but a moment ago; well, I inhale this celestial cordial, and now behold me brave as Achilles and strong as an eagle."

"Oh, Father, now? An eagle, alas!"

"Girl, I defy thee and the entire world. Ready, I declare, like a foaming charger to devour the distance between here and Rotterdam, and strong enough to combat life's ills—even poverty and old age, which latter-day philosophers call the greatest evil. Denied; unless a man's life has been squandered—which, by the way, it generally has. Now, to the moderns!"

"Father! Dear father!"

"Fear me not, girl; I shall be brief, unreasonably and unseasonably brief. The *soupe au vin* appears not in modern science; but this is merely further proof, if proof were needed, that for the last few centuries physicians have been fools, relying on chicken broth and decoctions of gold, thereby attributing supreme qualities to meat possessing the least juice and to a metal possessing fewer chemical properties than any other. Mountebanks! Dunces! Murderers! Since, therefore, no wisdom can be gleaned from these sources, let us turn to the chroniclers. First, we find Duguesclin, a French knight, preparing to engage the English—who, at that time, ruled half of France and were fierce fighters by sea and land—he drank not one, but three cups of *soupe au vin* in honor of the Holy Trinity. Having done so, he charged the islanders; and, as might have been predicted, slew a multitude and drove the remainder into the sea. Yet he was but the first of a long lineage of holy and formidable warriors who, sustained, fortified, strengthened, and comforted by this divine restorative, achieved greatness."

"Dear father, pray join that venerable company before the soup grows cold." Holding the hat imploringly in both hands, she urged him to insert the straw once more.

This spared them the discussion of modern examples and afforded Gerard the chance to tell Margaret how proud his mother would be that her soup had benefited a man of learning.

"Yes! But," said Margaret, "it would suit her poorly to see her son give all and receive none himself. Why did you bring but two straws?"

"Fair mistress, I hoped you would permit me to kiss your straw, seeing there were only two."

Margaret smiled and blushed. "Never beg that you may command," she said. "The straw is not mine; it is yours: you cut it in that field."

"I cut it, and thus it became mine; but afterward, your lips touched it, and thus it became yours."

"Did it? Then I will lend it to you. There—it is yours again; your lips have touched it."

"No, it belongs to us both now. Let us share it."

"By all means; you possess a knife."

"No, I will not cut it—that would be unluckily. I shall bite it. Thus I shall keep my half; you will surely burn yours once you return home, I suspect."

"You know me not. I waste nothing. It matters little whether I fashion a hairpin from it or something else."

This response disheartened the novice Gerard rather than provoked him, leaving him silent. Once the bread and soup were consumed, the elderly scholar prepared to resume his journey. Then arose a small difficulty: Gerard, the adept, could not retie his ribbon as Catherine had tied it. Margaret, after watching his futile efforts with a knowing glance, offered assistance. For at her age, girls delight in alternating between coyness and tenderness, sauciness and gentleness, and she perceived she had momentarily embarrassed him. Then a fair head, crowned with a stately mass of auburn hair, glossy and radiant amidst the silver threads, bowed sweetly toward him. As it captivated his gaze, two white, supple hands moved delicately upon the stubborn ribbon, molding it with soft, airy touches. A heavenly thrill coursed through the innocent youth, and vague glimpses of a new realm of feeling and sentiment opened before him. These novel and exquisite sensations Margaret unknowingly prolonged; it is not natural for her sex to hasten anything pertaining to the sacred toilet. Indeed, when the tapering fingers finally subdued the ends of the knot, her mind remained uneasy until, employing a maneuver peculiar to the female hand, she made her palm convex and applied it with gentle pressure to the center of the knot—a sweet, little coaxing hand-kiss, meaning no more than, "Now be a good knot, and remain so." The palm-kiss was bestowed upon the ribbon, but the wearer's heart leaped to meet it.

"There, that is how it was," said Margaret, stepping back to inspect her work with one last keen scrutiny. Looking upward for simple approval of her skill, she received instead a gaze of such ardent longing and adoration that she quickly lowered her eyes, flushing all over. An indescribable tremor seized her, and she retreated with downcast lashes and betraying cheeks, taking her father's arm on the opposite side. Gerard, blushing at having startled her with his stare, took the other arm; and so the two youths proceeded, heads bowed and hearts conscious, supporting the aging scholar in silence.

They entered Rotterdam through the Schiedamse Poort. Unacquainted with the town, Peter directed Gerard toward the Hooch Straet, where the Town Hall stood. He himself accompanied Margaret to his cousin's residence in the Ooster-Waagen Straet; thus, almost upon crossing the threshold, their paths diverged. They exchanged a friendly farewell, and Gerard plunged into the great city. A profound sense of solitude descended upon him, yet the streets teemed with people. Then he lamented, too late, that out of delicacy, he had not inquired of his former companions who they were and where they dwelt.

"Beshrew my shamefacedness!" he muttered. "But their speech and bearing surpassed their station, and something whispered that they wished to remain anonymous. I shall never see her again. Oh, weary world, I hate you and your ways. To think I must encounter beauty, goodness, and learning—three priceless pearls—and never behold them once more!"

Lost in this melancholy reverie, allowing his body to drift wherever it pleased, he became disoriented. However, encountering a throng of people moving in a single direction, he joined them, reasoning they must be heading for the Town Hall. Soon, the noisy procession containing the brooding Gerard emerged, not onto the Town Hall square, but onto

A grave, white-haired seneschal approached their table, bowing with courteous deference to inquire if Gerard Eliassoen was among their party. Upon receiving confirmation, the official announced, "The Princess Marie wishes to confer with you, young sir; I am to conduct you to her presence."

Instantly, every face within hearing snapped toward the speaker, etched with a mixture of sharp curiosity and covetous envy directed at the man destined to enter a princess's chamber. Gerard rose immediately to obey.

"I wager we shall not see you again," Margaret said calmly, though a faint flush colored her cheeks.

"That you will," he replied, leaning close to whisper, "This is my good princess; but you are my queen." Turning back to the assembly, he added aloud, "Wait for me, I pray you; I shall return presently."

"Ay, ay!" Peter called out simultaneously, waking from his reverie.

With Gerard gone, the pair—dressed in simple, homely garb yet standing beside the man summoned by royalty—became the focal point of neighboring gazes. Observing this shift in attention, William Johnson stepped forward, feigning surprise while claiming kinship.

"And to think that I was at your backs, and you saw me not!"

"Nay, cousin Johnson, I saw you long since," Margaret answered coolly.

"You saw me, and spoke not?"

"Cousin, it was for you to welcome us to Rotterdam, as it is for us to welcome you at Sevenbergen. Your servant denied us a seat in your house."

"The idiot!" someone muttered.

"And I had a mind to test the proverb, 'Like maid, like master,' for there is often truth in bywords." William Johnson flushed a deep purple. Realizing Margaret's keen insight and suspicion, he wisely chose action over argument. He insisted they accompany him home immediately, vowing to demonstrate whether they were truly welcome in Rotterdam.

"Who doubts it, cousin? Who doubts it?" the scholar interjected.

Margaret thanked him graciously but hesitated to depart at once, citing a desire to hear the minstrels perform again. About a quarter-hour later, Johnson renewed his offer, noting that many guests had already departed. Only then did her true motive surface.

"It would be poor manners to our friend; moreover, he risks losing us. He knows not where we lodge in Rotterdam, and the city is vast; we have already parted company once."

"Oh!" exclaimed Johnson. "We shall remedy that. My young man—ahem! I mean my secretary—shall remain seated here to await him and escort him to my residence. He shall lodge with me and no other."

"Cousin, we shall be too burdensome."

"Nay, nay! You shall see whether you are welcome or not—you, your friends, and your friends' friends, if necessary—and I shall hear what the Princess requires of him."

Margaret felt a sudden thrill of joy at the prospect of Gerard lodging under the same roof as herself, followed swiftly by a slight misgiving. "But if your young man proves thoughtless and wanders off, and Gerard misses him?"

"He wander? Leave the spot where I placed him and ignore my command? Ho! Stand forth, Hans Cloterman!"

A figure clad in black serge and dark violet hose rose abruptly, stepping forward without a tremor of muscle. He was a solemn, precise youth, the very embodiment of gravity and starched propriety. At his appearance, Margaret, overwhelmed with happiness, struggled to maintain her composure. Whispering to Johnson, she declared, "I would stake my hand against the fire for him. We are entirely at your command, cousin, once you have issued your orders."

Hans was instructed to take a seat at the table, wait for Gerard, and guide him to Ooster-Waagen Straet. He complied silently, settling into the designated chair with calm efficiency. Margaret, Peter, and William Johnson then withdrew together.

"And indeed, it is time you retired to bed, father, after all your travels," Margaret remarked.

Hans Cloterman remained seated, waiting for Gerard with a demeanor of perfect business-like patience. Minutes ticked by, failing to stir any impatience in the flawless young man. Johnson dismissed the notion of his secretary abandoning his post for sport or youthful frivolity with a laugh of scorn.

As Gerard failed to appear, the patient Hans, no longer under his employer's watchful eye, amused himself by drinking goblets of strong Corsican wine. He consumed them solemnly, silently, and at precisely measured intervals. The wine proved potent, and Cloterman's resolve wavered. An hour after Gerard's departure, the model secretary finally succumbed to the notion that creation demanded he toast all good fellows, specifically naming the Duke of Burgundy present.

Rising gingerly but with exaggerated solemnity, he filled his ninth cup. Standing to his full height, he instantly toppled onto the grass, spilling the cold liquid over several ankles—whose owners scrambled away—but remaining utterly unmoved in his own long face. Even in the total eclipse of reason, his expression retained its gravity, primness, and infallibility.

Meanwhile, the seneschal led Gerard through winding passages to the door of the pavilion. There, young nobles, embroidered and feathered, sat as sentinels guarding the heir-apparent, shuffling cards by the flickering red light of torches held by their servants. A whisper from the seneschal prompted one of them to rise reluctantly, stare at Gerard with haughty surprise, and enter the pavilion. Moments later, he returned, beckoning the pair through further corridors until they landed in an antechamber. Here, three more youths, dressed like living tapestries of fur and embroidery, were deeply engrossed in the educational art of dice.

"You cannot see the Princess; it is too late," one declared.

Another agreed, "She passed this way but moments ago with her nurse. She is gone to bed, doll and all. Deuce—ace again!"

Gerard prepared to withdraw. However, the seneschal, wearing an incredulous smile, replied, "The young man is here by the Countess's orders; please conduct him to her ladies."

On this cue, a superb Adonis rose with an air of injury and led Gerard into a room where eleven ladies sat or lounged, chatting like magpies. Two, more industrious than the rest, played cat's-cradle with fingers as nimble as their tongues. At the sight of a stranger, all chatter ceased as if halted by a single complex mechanism, and every eye turned upon Gerard, as though the same invisible thread that silenced the tongues had guided the gaze.

Gerard, already uneasy, felt doubly disconcerted by this battery of stares and lowered his eyes to the floor. Then, spotting a vulnerability, the cowards giggled, enjoying their newfound prowess, much like hares fleeing ponds or frogs scuttling into water. A duenna intervened severely, "Mesdames!" and the women were instantly abashed, as if a string of modesty had been pulled.

The duenna took charge, marching Gerard before her in solemn silence. His heart sank, and he nearly turned to flee. "What must princes be," he thought, "when their courtiers are so freezing? Doubtless they derive their breeding from him they serve."

These reflections were interrupted when the duenna ushered him into a smaller room where three ladies worked and a pretty little girl tuned a lute. Dressed richly yet without ostentation, the duenza approached the lady hemming a kerchief and spoke softly. That lady turned toward Gerard with a smile, beckoning him closer. She did not rise, but set aside her work; her slight movement was suffused with grace, ease, and courtesy.

Conversation began immediately. "Margaret Van Eyck is an old friend of mine, sir, and I am right glad to receive a letter from her hand. Thank you, sir, for delivering it safely. Marie, my love, this is the gentleman who brought you that charming miniature."

"Sir, I thank you a thousand times," the young lady replied.

"I am glad you feel indebted to her, sweetheart, for our friend requests a small service in return."

"I will do anything on earth for him," the young lady responded with ardor.

"Anything on earth is nothing in the world," the Countess of Charolois stated quietly.

"Well, then, I will... What would you have me do, sir?" Gerard realized he had stumbled into high society.

"My sovereign demoiselle," he said gently, trembling slightly, "where there have been no pains, there needs no reward."

"But we must obey mamma. All the world must obey."

"That is true. Then, our demoiselle, reward me, if you will, by allowing me to hear the stave you were singing before I interrupted you."

"What! You love music, sir?"

"I adore it."

The little princess looked inquiringly at her mother, who smiled in assent. Taking her lute, she sang a *romaunt* of the day. Though only twelve, she was a well-taught and diligent musician. Her small fingers swept the chords with courage and precision, striking out arpeggios clear, distinct, and bright like twinkling stars. Yet, the true charm lay in her voice. It was not powerful, but round, clear, full, and ringing like a bell. She sang with a modest eloquence, lacking the tricks of feeling. Too young to be theatrical or sentimental, nothing was forced; everything flowed naturally.

It was nearly four in the afternoon. Eli was busy within the shop, while his eldest and youngest sons were away. Catherine and her little crippled daughter had grown increasingly anxious regarding Gerard; having waited in vain, they had ventured a short distance down the road, hoping against hope to catch a glimpse of him in the distance. Thus, Giles remained alone in the sitting-room—a space worthy of description, complete with its eccentric occupant.

The Hollanders have always been a peculiar and pioneering folk. They boast of inventing printing (with wooden type), oil-painting, liberty, banking, and even gardening. Most notably, centuries before the events of this tale, they invented cleanliness. While the English gentry of the time, clad in velvet jerkins and chicken-toed shoes, trampled upon floors covered in stale rushes—filthy receptacles teeming with bones, rotting food scraps, spittle, stray dogs, eggs, and every conceivable abomination—the hosier's sitting-room at Tergou presented a stark contrast. Its floor was paved with Dutch tiles, so brilliantly glazed and meticulously scrubbed that one might safely dine directly upon them.

A single large window dominated the wall. In its center stood a massive cross of stonework, raised in relief so distinctively that it appeared to the inhabitants as a literal cross, becoming a focal point of their daily prayers. The window panes themselves were tiny and diamond-shaped, fused together by strips of lead—a style still familiar today in our humble rural cottages. The room's seating consisted of rough, primitive chairs, save for one armchair. Its backrest rose perpendicularly from the seat, towering so high that a seated person's head would stop two feet below the apex. Crafted of oak and carved at the summit, this chair served as the throne for the household's unique resident.

Beside it stood a copper pail, designed to fit over the waist, filled with holy water, accompanied by a small besom for sprinkling blessings across the room. Dominating the scene, however, was a long, narrow, yet massive oak table. Clinging to its rim was a dwarf, his teeth locked onto the wood, his eyes blazing with intensity, his claws raised in the air like those of a pouncing vampire.

Nature, it seemed, had not created Giles out of malice. She had fashioned his head and torso with her usual diligence, but apparently, her attention wandered elsewhere, leaving the remainder to chance. The result was a human wedge, an inverted cone. One might justly quote Horace here: *"An amphora began to be shaped; why does the jar roll?"* His center of mass was entirely misplaced. If bisected, the upper half of Giles would outweigh the lower three times over. Yet, this very disproportion granted him abilities that would have baffled the ancient athlete Milo. His powerful arms carried no excess weight, allowing him to scale vertical poles with the agility of a squirrel or hang suspended from a tree branch by a single hand, dangling like a cherry on its stem. Had he possessed the ability to create a vacuum with his palms, as lizards do with their feet, he could have traversed ceilings with ease.

This pocket-sized acrobat was utterly obsessed with gripping the dining table with both hands to swing, and then—reaching the climax of his delight—he would seize the edge with his teeth, releasing his hands to cling to his massive ivory fangs like grim death itself. But even our highest joys are subject to interruption. Little Kate spotted Sampsonet in this precarious posture and froze in horror. True to her mother's nature, her heart belonged to the furniture, not to the twelve-mo gymnast.

"Oh, Giles! How can you?" she exclaimed. "Mother is coming. You're denting the table!"

"Go and tell her, little tale-bearer," snarled Giles. "You are the one destined for mischief."

"Am I?" Kate asked calmly. "That is quite new information."

"The biggest troublemaker in all of Tergou," growled Giles, tightening his grip.

"Oh, indeed!" replied Kate dryly.

Having launched this unexpected barb without suffering any visible backlash, she sat down quietly and began to cry. Just then, her mother entered the room. Giles immediately hurled himself beneath the table, peering out with a glare.

"What is happening now?" the woman demanded sharply. Turning her experienced gaze from Kate to Giles, noting his contorted position and sheepish expression, she hinted ominously at the prospect of ear-cuffing.

"Nay, mother," the girl said softly. "It was merely a foolish word Giles spoke. I wouldn't have noticed it at another time, but I am weary and worried about Gerard, you know."

"Let no one worry about me," a faint voice called from the doorway.

Gerard tottered in, pale, dusty, and utterly exhausted. Amidst a chorus of uplifted hands, cries of delight, and a mixture of curiosity and anxiety, he collapsed into the nearest chair. The long journey through Rotterdam, searching like a shadow for Margaret, and the arduous trek thereafter, had completely drained him. However, the elasticity of youth quickly restored him, and suddenly he was the center of an eager circle.

First, they demanded to hear about the prizes. Gerard recounted that he had been permitted to view the competitors' works, displayed in an immense hall awaiting the judges' verdict.

"Oh, mother! Oh, Kate!" he cried. "When I saw the goldsmiths' creations, I felt as though I ought to have fallen to the floor. I scarcely believed that all the goldsmiths on earth possessed so much gold, silver, jewels, and such exquisite craftsmanship. Truly, all arts are divine."

To satisfy the women, he proceeded to describe the reliquaries, feretories, chalices, crosiers, crosses, pyxes, monstrances, and other ecclesiastical marvels, alongside goblets, cups, watches, clocks, chains, and brooches, causing their mouths to water with desire.

"But, Kate," he continued, "when I turned to the illuminated manuscripts from Ghent and Bruges, my heart sank. My own work seemed like dirt by comparison. For the first minute, I nearly wept; but I prayed for a purer spirit, and soon I was able to admire them, thanking God for those lovely works and for the skilled, patient craftsmen whom I acknowledge as my masters. Indeed, the colored pages were so beautiful I forgot everything else. Yet, the following day, when the other prizes were distributed, they announced the winner for writing. Do you guess whose name was called first?"

"Yours," Kate declared confidently.

The others scoffed at her.

"You may well laugh," Gerard said gently, "but the herald shouted 'Gerard Eliassoen of Tergou.' I stood frozen; they pushed me forward. Everything swam before my eyes. I found myself kneeling on a cushion at the feet of the Duke. He spoke to me, but I was so flustered I could not reply. Then, he reached to his side—not to draw his sword and sever my dull head—but instead bestowed upon me a gold medal, and here it is."

A roar erupted, followed by a frantic scramble.

"And then he gave me fifteen magnificent golden angels!" Gerard exclaimed. "I had seen one before, but never handled one. Behold!"

"Oh, Gerard! Oh, Gerard!"

"There is one for you, our eldest; one for you, Sybrandt; one for you, Little Mischief; and two for thee, Little Lily, because God has afflicted thee; one for myself, to purchase colors and vellum; and nine for her who nursed us all and risked the two crowns upon poor Gerard's hand."

The gold revealed their true natures. Cornelis and Sybrandt clutched their coins with simultaneous glares of greed and envy directed at Kate, who had received two pieces. Giles grabbed his share, rolled it across the floor, and gamboled after it. Kate set aside her crutches, sat down, and extended her small arms toward Gerard in a gesture of heavenly love and tenderness. The mother, initially stunned by the sudden shower of gold falling upon her apron, finally cried out, "Stop kissing him, Kate; he is my son, not yours. Ah, Gerard! My boy! I have not loved you as you deserve."

Then Gerard threw himself on his knees beside her, and she flung her arms around him, weeping with joy and pride against his neck.

"Good lad! Good lad!" cried the hosier, moved deeply. "I must go and tell the neighbors. Lend me the medal, Gerard; I shall show it to my good friend Peter Buyskens. He is always boasting about how his son Jorian won a tin mug for shooting at the butts."

"Ay, do, my man," Eli encouraged. "Show Peter Buyskens one of the angels. Tell him there are fourteen more where that came from. Mind you bring it back to me!"

"Wait a moment, father; there is better news to come," Gerard said, flushing with joy at the happiness he had inspired.

"Better than this?" Eli asked eagerly.

Gerard then recounted his audience with the Countess, and the house rang with jubilation.

"Now, God bless the good lady, and bless Dame Van Eyck! A benefice? Our son! My cares are at an end. Eli, my good friend and master, now we two can die happy whenever our time comes. This dear boy will take our place, and none of these beloved ones will lack a home or a friend."

From

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was a man of considerable cunning. When addressing the novice, he began with a preamble entirely disconnected from his true objective. "The town records," he remarked, "are penned in a cramped hand, and the ink has long since turned rust-brown with age." With this opening, he extended to Gerard the honor of transcribing them into clear copy.

Gerard immediately asked what compensation he might expect. Ghysbrecht named a sum that would barely cover the cost of new pens, fresh ink, and blank parchment.

"But, Burgomaster," Gerard countered, "what of my labor? Consider this a year's work."

"Your labor?" Ghysbrecht scoffed lightly. "Do you call the marking of parchment 'labor'? Very little sweat is required for such a task, I assure you."

"It is labor, and highly skilled labor at that," Gerard insisted. "In every craft, skilled labor commands higher pay than brute toil, regardless of the sweat involved. Furthermore, there is the matter of my time."

"And your time?" Ghysbrecht pressed, his tone sharpening. "Why, what is time worth to a boy of twenty-two?"

Fixing his gaze keenly upon Gerard to gauge the impact of his words, the Burgomaster continued, "Let us say, rather, that you have fallen into idleness. You are in love. Your body remains among these chanting monks, but your heart belongs to Peter Brandt and his red-haired girl."

"I know no Peter Brandt," Gerard replied flatly.

This denial only hardened Ghysbrecht's suspicion that the exorcist was engaged in some deeper scheme. "You lie!" the older man shouted. "Did I not find you standing at her elbow on the road to Rotterdam?"

"Aha!"

"Aha! And you were seen at Sevenbergen just the other day."

"At Sevenbergen?"

"Yes, at Sevenbergen."

Now, this maneuver amounted to what modern strategists might call a bluff—a bold assertion presented as fact designed to provoke a reaction revealing the truth. Whether the youth had recently visited the place or not, Ghysbrecht sought to learn through Gerard's response. The outcome of this ruse, however, startled the crafty elder.

Gerard sprang to his feet, seized by a strange, feverish agitation.

"Burgomaster," he stammered, his voice trembling, "I have not set foot in Sevenbergen for three years. I know neither the names of those who may have mistaken me, nor where they dwell. Since my time is precious—though you seem to value it so little—I bid you good day."

With that, he darted from the room, his eyes flashing with intensity.

Ghysbrecht rose in a fit of furious rage, only to sink back into his chair, defeated yet unsettled. "He fears me not," he muttered grimly. "He knows something, if not everything."

Summoning his most trusted servant with haste, the Burgomaster nearly dragged the man to the window. "Do you see that fellow?" he commanded. "Move quickly! Follow him! But ensure he never sees you. He is young, yet ancient in his guile. Keep him under observation all day. Report to me exactly where he goes and what he does."

It was late at night when the servant returned.

"Well? Well?" Ghysbrecht cried, leaning forward eagerly.

"Master," the servant reported breathlessly, "the young man left your presence and headed straight for Sevenbergen."

Ghysbrecht let out a low, despairing groan. "To the house of Peter the Magician."

"Examine your own soul and record what you find!" cried Herr Cant, and the cuckoos of the earth seemed to echo his command. Peer into the deepest currents of the Rhine or the thickest reaches of the Thames, and paint the riverbed. Lower a bucket into a well dug of self-deception; whatever rises must be eternal truth, surely? Yet, first among things, no son of Adam ever truly reads his own heart unless he has first studied the hearts of others through years of observation. Even then, armed with borrowed wisdom and reflected light, he can merely decipher his own spirit, never reading it with fluent ease.

Halfway to Sevenbergen, Gerard cast a gaze inward and demanded of his heart why he journeyed there. Without hesitation, his heart replied, "We go driven by curiosity—to learn why she spurned us, to prove to her that our spirits remain unbroken, and that we are fully content with our honors and the benefice awaiting us. We desire neither her nor any ally of her fickle sex."

Soon, Gerard located Peter Brandt's cottage. A girl sat in the doorway, busy with her needlework, while a sturdy figure leaned against a longbow, conversing with her. A strange pang struck Gerard at the sight of the man. Upon closer inspection, however, he realized the stranger was past fifty, an old soldier whom Gerard recalled seeing display admirable marksmanship at the shooting butts. Moments later, the youth arrived. Margaret looked up, dropped her work, and emitted a faint cry, her complexion shifting rapidly between pale and flushed. Yet, these signs of emotion vanished swiftly; she turned colder and more indifferent than she likely would have had she not betrayed such agitation.

"What! Is it you, Master Gerard?" she asked. "What on earth brings you here?"

"I was passing and saw you," Gerard replied. "So I thought to bid you good day and inquire after your father."

"My father is well. He will arrive shortly."

"Then I suppose I may stay until he comes."

"As you wish. Good Martin, go into the village and tell my father that a friend awaits him."

"And not a friend of yours?"

"A friend of my father is a friend of mine."

"That is doubtful. It was unlike a friend to promise to wait, only to vanish the moment my back was turned. Cruel Margaret, you little know how I searched the town for you; how, lacking you, nothing brought me joy."

"These are idle words," she retorted sharply. "If you had truly desired my father's or my company, you would have returned. I had prepared a bed for you at my cousin's house; he would have honored you greatly, and perhaps, who knows, I might have done likewise. I was in a generous mood that day. Do not expect me to be so again, nor should you expect any young man to be."

"Margaret, I returned the instant the Countess released me, but you were nowhere to be found."

"Nay, you did not return, or you would have seen Hans Cloterman at our table; we sent him specifically to fetch you."

"I saw no one save a drunken man who had just fallen."

"At our table? Describe his attire."

"I paid little attention; he wore dark, somber clothing."

At this, Margaret's face gradually warmed. Soon, however, adopting an air of incredulous severity, she began a barrage of sharp questions, to which Gerard responded with utmost loyalty. Finally, the clouds parted, and they deduced the origin of the misunderstanding. This was followed by a surge of tender remorse, intensified because they had both wronged one another. More perilous still came mutual confessions. Neither had known happiness since; neither ever would have, but for this fortunate reunion.

Gerard spotted a *MS. Vulgate* lying open on the table and lunged toward it like a hawk. Manuscripts were his greatest delight, yet before he could reach it, two pale hands slammed flat onto the page, covering it, while a flushed face appeared above them.

"Nay, remove your hands, Margaret, that I may see where you read. I shall read alongside you at home; thus, our souls may meet within the sacred page."

"You will not?"

"Nay, then I must kiss them away."

He kissed them repeatedly until, overcome with shame, they withdrew. Lo! the holy book lay open to, "An apple of gold set in a setting of silver."

"There, now," she exclaimed. "I had been searching for it for ages, finding it only moments ago—and to be caught!" With a touch of inconsistency, she pointed it out to Gerard with her slender white finger.

"Yes," he said, "but today it is all concealed beneath that large cap."

"It is a becoming cap, I am told by some."

"Perhaps; but what it conceals is beautiful."

"It is not; it is hideous."

"Well, it was beautiful in Rotterdam."

"Yes, everything was beautiful that day," she sighed softly.

Just then, Peter entered, welcoming Gerard warmly and inviting him to supper. Margaret vanished, and Gerard engaged in a pleasant scholarly conversation with Peter. Soon, Margaret reappeared, her hair arranged in a silver net, casting a glance that was half-arch, half-coy. She glided around them, spreading the meal, radiating gaiety and happiness. In the cool evening, Gerard coaxed her outside. She initially objected but yielded; he urged her further along the road to Tergou, and though she declined, she eventually complied. They strolled hand in hand. When he finally had to depart, they vowed never to quarrel or misunderstand one another again, sealing their promise with a long, loving kiss. Gerard returned home on wings of joy.

From that day forward, Gerard spent most of his evenings with Margaret. Their attachment deepened on both sides until the hours they shared became the essence of their lives; the rest of existence they merely endured. At the start of this profound bond, all proceeded smoothly. Obstacles existed, yet they seemed distant and trivial to the hopeful eyes of youth and love. The feelings and passions of countless others that this union might thwart offered no warning smoke to reveal their volcanic nature and power. The path of true love ran smooth and placid until it had drawn these two young hearts forever into its current. And then—

On a morning of unusual brilliance, the streets of Tergou shimmered under a canopy of velvet clouds. Uncommon splendor filled the air; feathers danced in the breeze, and the gleaming hooves of horses clattered rhythmically along the cobblestones. Windows and balconies were lined with faces agape in wonder. The French Ambassador rode through the city en route to hunt in the nearby forest. Accompanying him was a retinue including several servants lent by the Duke of Burgundy to render him honor and attend to his pleasures. Leading the procession was the Duke's jester, a figure of grave and sedate majesty whose dignity made his more noble companions appear light and frivolous by comparison. Yet, whenever the atmosphere grew too thick with reverence, the jester would dismount in a manner so ignoble and comical that even the dignified Ambassador could not suppress a laugh. He would climb back onto his steed by grabbing the tail, a spectacle designed purely to provoke mirth, playing his role with theatrical flair.

Trailing behind the main group was a spectacle that drew even greater attention: the Duke's leopard. Mounted on a Flemish horse of colossal size and power, a huntsman carried a long box strapped securely to his waist. Perched atop this box, chained firmly to the rider, sat a brilliant leopard, crouching low. The crowd marveled at her glossy coat and distinctive spots, pressing close until one or two reached out to touch her or pull her tail. Instantly, the huntsman's voice boomed in terror, "Beware! In Antwerp, a man dared throw a handful of dust at her, and the Duke reduced him to mere dust!"

"Thank heavens!" someone cried.

"I speak the truth," the huntsman insisted. "The good Duke imprisoned him in a subterranean cell, where rats stripped the flesh from his bones within a single night. He served him right for molesting the beast."

A murmur of fear rippled through the Tergovians, who recoiled from the idea of teasing the sovereign's leopard. However, their spirits were soon restored by the arrival of the Duke's giant bringing up the rear. Standing seven feet four inches tall, this Hungarian giant possessed a treble, flute-like voice of negligible volume—a flaw he was entirely unaware of due to his vanity. As he approached, he spotted Giles perched atop a balcony. Stopping abruptly, the giant began to mock him.

"Hallo! Brother!" squeaked the giant in a high-pitched whine. "I nearly passed without seeing thee!"

"You are plain enough to see," bellowed Giles in a deep bass roar.

"Come on my shoulder, brother!" squealed the giant, extending a massive, mutton-fist-sized arm to assist him down.

"If I do, I'll cuff your ears," roared the dwarf.

The giant, sensing the homunculus's irritability and emboldened by the crowd's laughter, continued his jest. He failed to realize that the spectators laughed not at his wit, but at the absurd incongruity of the two voices—the gigantic body emitting a feeble whistle, and the tiny form unleashing a thunderous boom. It was a mountain delivering a squeak and a molehill belching thunder. The bizarre duet ended as unexpectedly as it began. Overcome with loss of patience and self-command, fueled by a rage born of his fearless nature, Giles leaped onto the giant's neck. Seizing the giant's hair with one hand and punching his head with the other, he attacked with ferocity.

The giant's initial impulse was to laugh, but the sudden weight and speed of the blows quickly extinguished that amusement.

"He! he! Ah! ha! Hallo! Oh! Oh! Holy saints! Here! Help! Or I must throttle this imp! I can't! I'll split your skull against the—"

He made a wild dash backward toward the balcony. Giles, realizing his peril, grabbed the railing with both hands and swung over just as the giant's head collided with the stone with a stunning crack. The crowd erupted in roaring laughter and exultation at the little champion's agility. Indignant, the giant seized two of the laughers, smashed them together like dumbbells, shook them, and laid them flat. Catherine shrieked, throwing her apron over Giles, while the giant strode wrathfully away to join the rest of the party.

Though no one present foresaw the future consequences, the immediate result was favorable. The citizens of Tergou became proud of Giles, listening with newfound kindness to his pleas for parchment. He conducted a steady trade with his brother Gerard in this commodity, begging for sheets gratis while Gerard supplied the copper coins.

Later that afternoon, Catherine and her daughter Kate chatted animatedly about their favorite topic: Gerard, his goodness, his impending benefice, and the brightened prospects of the entire family. Fortune had smiled upon them exactly as they desired. Beyond the advantage of a benefice that the Countess of Charolais would not refuse, there lay the feminine joy of having a priest, a holy man, within their own household.

"He will marry Cornelis and Sybrandt," Catherine declared. "For they can wed—good housewives—as they will. Gerard will care for you and Giles when we are gone."

"Yes, mother, and we can confess to him instead of a stranger," Kate added.

"Aye, girl! And he can administer the sacred oil to your father and me, and close our eyes when our time comes."

"Oh, mother! Not for many, many years, I pray Heaven. Please, do not speak of that; it always saddens me. I hope to go before you, dear mother. No; let us be cheerful today. I am free of pain, quite free of all pain; it seems so strange. I feel so bright and happy that..."

Kate paused, her eyes lighting up. "Mother, can you keep a secret?"

"Nobody better, child. You know I can."

"Then I will show you something so beautiful. You've never seen its like, I trow. Only Gerard must never know; surely he intends to surprise us with it. He hides it so well, and sometimes he carries it away entirely."

Taking her crutches, Kate moved slowly away, leaving her mother in a state of heightened curiosity. Soon she returned, holding something wrapped in cloth. She uncovered it to reveal a lovely painting of the Virgin Mary, adorned with all her insignias, wearing her tiara over a cascade of golden hair flowing loosely over her shoulders. Catherine was immediately struck with awe.

"It is herself," she cried. "It is the Queen of Heaven. I have never seen one like her before."

"And her eyes, mother! Lifted to the sky, as if they belong there, not to a mortal creature. And her beautiful hair of burning gold."

"To think I have a son who can make the saints live again upon a piece of wood!"

"The reason is, he is a young saint himself, mother. He is too good for this world; he is here to portray the blessed, and then depart to be with them forever."

Before they could finish admiring the masterpiece, a strange voice echoed from the doorway. Driven by instinct, the women hastily concealed the picture, though there was no real cause for alarm. Moments later, a man entered, casting furtive glances around. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the burgomaster, who had not visited the house in ten years.

Caught completely off guard, the two women simply stared at him and at each other, murmuring, "The burgomaster!" in a tone so laden with meaning that Ghysbrecht felt compelled to respond.

"Yes! I admit the last time I came here was not on a friendly errand. Men love their own interests—Elias's and mine were contrary. Well, let this visit atone for the last. Today I come on your business, and none of mine."

Catherine and Kate exchanged a swift glance of contemptuous disbelief. They knew the man far better than he imagined.

"It is about your son, Gerard," Ghysbrecht stated.

"Ay! Ay! You want him to work for the town for nothing. He told us."

"I come on no such errand. It is to inform you that he has fallen into bad hands."

"Now, Heaven and the saints forbid! Man, do not torture a mother! Speak out, and quickly: speak before you have time to coin falsehoods; we know you."

Ghysbrecht paled at this affront, spite mixing with his other motives.

"Thus it is, then," he ground out, speaking rapidly. "Your son Gerard is more likely to become a father of a family than a priest: he is forever with Margaret, Peter Brandt's red-haired girl, and loves her like a cow her calf."

Both mother and daughter burst into laughter. Ghysbrecht stared at them, bewildered.

"What! You knew it?"

"Carry this tale to those who do not know my son, Gerard. Women are naught to him."

"Other women, perhaps. But this one is the apple of his eye, or will be, unless you part them soon. Come, dame, do not waste my time and friendly counsel: my servant has seen them together a dozen times, gazing into each other's eyes like... you know, dame—you have been young, too."

"Girl, I am uneasy. Yes, I have been young and know how blind and foolish the young can be. My heart! He has turned me sick in a moment. Kate, if it should

While the burgomaster exposed Gerard to ridicule in Tergou, Margaret faced a quiet, yet profound distress at Sevenbergen. It was the sorrow of a housewife, deeper perhaps than words could easily capture. She approached Martin Wittenhaagen, the elderly soldier, her eyes wet with tears.

"Martin, there is nothing left in the house," she whispered. "Gerard is coming, and he is so careless. He forgets to eat at home. Once his labor ceases, he rushes straight to me, poor soul, often arriving faint from hunger. And to think I have nothing to offer my beloved servant!"

Martin scratched his head, perplexed. "What can I do?"

"It is Thursday," Margaret replied, her voice trembling slightly. "It is your day to hunt. Truly, I counted on you today."

"Nay," the soldier answered, shaking his head. "I may not shoot when the Duke or his companions are out on the chase. Read this, if you wish to understand why. I am no scholar."

He reached into his pouch and produced a parchment bearing a grand seal. It was a stipend and license granted by Philip, Duke of Burgundy, to Martin Wittenhaagen, one of his archers, in recognition of his wartime service and a wound sustained at the Duke's side. The document awarded him an annual stipend of four merks, payable by the Duke's almoner, alongside a license to shoot three arrows once a week—specifically on Thursdays—in any of the Duke's forests in Holland. However, strict conditions applied: he could only hunt for a seven-year-old buck or a doe carrying a fawn, and only if the Duke himself was not hunting that day, nor were any of his friends. To violate these terms meant forfeiting his salary, risking his life, and paying a fine of a penny.

Margaret sighed, falling silent.

"Come, cheer up, mistress," Martin said gently. "For your sake, I will risk my carcass; I have done so for many others who were not worth your little finger. Besides, it is no great danger. I shall merely step into the edge of the forest. There is a good chance they will drive a hare or a fawn within range of my arrow."

"Well," Margaret hesitated, "if I allow you to go, you must promise me not to stray far and not to be seen. It is far better for Gerard to go supperless than for harm to come to you, faithful Martin."

With the promise secured, Martin took his bow and three arrows, slipping cautiously into the woods, barely a furlong away. Faint horns echoed in the distance, signaling that the game was already stirring.

"Come," Martin thought, "I shall soon fill the pot, and no one will be the wiser."

He positioned himself behind a thick oak overlooking an open glade, drawing his bow—a formidable instrument crafted from English yew, standing six feet two inches tall and thick in proportion. Martin, broad-chested with arms like iron and cord, had handled this bow since infancy. He could draw a three-foot arrow to its full length; when released, the arrow flew so fast the eye could hardly track it, and the bowstring sang with a music akin to a harp. This weapon had claimed the lives of many sturdy soldiers during the wars of the Hoecks and Cabbel-jaws. In those days, battlefields were not shrouded in clouds of smoke; combatants were few, yet the casualties were numerous, for they fought with clear sight and purpose. Fewer bloodless arrows flew then than the bloodless bullets of modern times.

A hare cantered into view, then sat alertly, its ears forming a perfect 'V'. Martin leveled his massive weapon. The arrow flew, the string twanged, but Martin, eager to secure the kill, had rushed his shot. He missed by an inch. The arrow appeared to strike the hare but actually grazed the ground beside it, passing beneath her belly like a flash before vanishing into the short grass. The hare leaped three feet vertically and bolted at full speed.

"Bungler!" Martin muttered.

This was proof enough that he was not an habitual bungler; otherwise, he would have blamed the hare. He had scarcely fitted another arrow when a wood-pigeon settled directly on the branch above him.

"Aha!" he thought. "You are small, but dainty."

This time, he proceeded with care. He drew the arrow deliberately and loosed it smoothly. To all appearances, the shaft went cleanly through the bird, sending feathers flying skyward like dust. Yet, instead of dropping at his feet, the pigeon, its breast torn rather than pierced, fluttered weakly away. With a final, great effort, it rose above the canopy, flew fifty yards, and fell dead—but where, hidden by the dense foliage, Martin could not tell.

"Luck is against me," he said despondently.

He fitted another arrow and scanned the glade intently. Soon, he heard a commotion behind him and turned just in time to see a noble buck crossing the open space, but it was too late to aim. He slammed his bow to the ground in frustration.

At that exact moment, a long-spotted animal glided swiftly across the path after the deer; its belly nearly touched the ground as it moved. Martin snatched up his bow instantly. He recognized the Duke's leopard.

"The hunters will not be far behind her," he realized. "I must not be seen. Gerard must go supperless tonight."

He plunged into the woods, following the tracks of the buck and the leopard, which led toward his home. Not far in, he heard an unusual noise ahead—the violent rustling of leaves and the heavy trampling of the ground. Hurrying forward, he discovered the leopard atop the buck, tearing at its flesh with teeth and claws. The deer ran in frantic circles, bounding convulsively as blood poured down its hide.

Then, Martin made a desperate resolve to provide venison for Margaret. Drawing his arrow to the head, he drove it deep into the deer. Despite the predator on its back, the buck leaped high into the air and collapsed dead. The leopard continued to tear at the carcass as if nothing had changed. Martin hoped the beast would gorge itself until it slept, allowing him to claim the meat.

After waiting several minutes, he walked resolutely forward and placed his hand on the buck's leg. The leopard emitted a frightful growl and ceased sucking the blood. She regarded Martin with suspicion, sulky and guarded. What was to be done? Martin recalled that wild creatures could not withstand the steady gaze of a human. Standing erect, he fixed his eyes upon the beast. The leopard returned a savage stare, refusing to break contact.

As Martin continued to hold her gaze, the leopard, brutally ignorant of natural law, lunged at his head with a terrifying yell, her eyes blazing, jaws gaping wide, and muscles distended. He had just enough time to seize her by the throat before her teeth could crush his face. One of her claws grabbed his shoulder, ripping the fabric, while the other, aimed at his cheek, would have been fatal. Martin was old-fashioned and wore no hat, only a scapulary of the same material as his jerkin. He had thrown this over his head like a hood, and the brute's claw snagged in the loose leather.

Martin struggled desperately to keep her teeth from his face, gripping her throat with iron strength, while she tore at his shoulder. It felt like blunt reaping hooks grinding and shredding flesh. The pain was excruciating, yet instead of breaking him, it ignited his blood. He gnashed his teeth in rage almost as fierce as hers, squeezing her neck with unyielding force.

Two pairs of eyes flared at one another—the man's nearly as furious as the beast's. Realizing she was being throttled, the leopard made a wild attempt to free herself, dragging his cowl across his face and blinding him, while tearing her claw completely out of his shoulder, taking flesh and bone with it. Still, he maintained his grip with hand and arm of iron.

Presently, her long tail, previously held high, dropped.

"Aha!" cried Martin, joyfully, tightening his grasp like death. Next, her body lost its elasticity; he held a choked, powerless mass. He gripped it firmly until all motion ceased, then dashed her to the earth. Panting, he removed his cowl. The leopard lay mute at his feet, tongue protruding, with a bloody paw. For the first time, terror seized Martin.

"I am a dead man," he thought. "I have slain the Duke's leopard."

Hastily, he gathered a handful of leaves and threw them over the carcass. Shouldering the buck, he staggered away, leaving a trail of mingled blood—his own and the deer's.

He burst into Peter's house a horrifying figure, bleeding and stained, and flung the deer's carcass onto the floor.

"There," he grunted, ignoring everything else. "Broil me a steak on it, for I am faint."

Margaret did not notice his wounds; she assumed the blood belonged entirely to the deer. Busy at the fire, the stout soldier bandaged his own injury while Gerard and Margaret prepared the meal. Soon, the three of them dined royally on roasted venison. They were exceedingly merry. Gerard, displaying remarkable thoughtfulness, had brought a flask of Schiedam wine. Under its influence, Martin recovered and recounted the tale of how the venison was obtained. They all laughed heartily over the exploit.

Margaret severed a massive chunk of roasted venison and dashed to the window, hurling it outward toward the emerald-eyed beasts below. With a savage snarl, the creatures lunged at the meat; the sound of tearing flesh and crunching bone echoed sharply. Suddenly, a hound bayed so loudly and close to the house that it vibrated through the timbers, causing the three figures at the window to recoil in alarm. The leopard, fearing the loss of her meal, slipped away swiftly and silently into the shadows of the woods. Before she had vanished completely, a chaotic throng of horses, men, and dogs thundered past the window, pursuing her on a full cry.

Martin and his companions exhaled, relieved. The leopard was too fast to be cornered within a league of their dwelling. They clasped hands in relief. Seizing the brief respite, Margaret broke down sobbing softly. Gerard leaned forward and kissed the tears from her cheeks.

They returned to the table, and Gerard raised a toast to the power of female intellect. "Woman's wit is stronger than man's strength," he declared.

"Ay," Margaret replied, "but only when those she loves are in peril; otherwise, it is merely cleverness."

That night, Gerard lingered with her longer than usual, returning home feeling prouder than ever and happier than a king. A short distance from his home, beneath the canopy of ancient trees, he encountered two figures blocking his path. It was his father and mother.

"Out so late!" he thought, a chill seizing him. "What could possibly bring them here?"

He halted, staring at them. They stood grim and silent. He stammered a question.

"Why ask?" his father retorted sharply. "You know why we are here."

"Oh, Gerard!" his mother interjected, her voice thick with reproach yet undeniably tender.

Gerard's heart hammered against his ribs; he fell silent. Seeing his son's confusion, his father softened slightly. "Do not hang your head, lad. You are not the first young fool ensnared by a rosy cheek and a pair of azure eyes."

"Nay, nay!" Catherine added. "It was witchcraft. Peter the Magician is famous for such tricks."

"Come, Father," the elder man resumed, addressing his son directly. "You know you must not meddle with womenfolk. But give us your promise to cease visiting Sevenbergen, and here matters end. We shall not be harsh for one fault."

"I cannot promise that, Father."

"Not promise it, you young hypocrite!"

"Nay, Father, do not misname me," Gerard insisted, his voice trembling. "I lacked the courage to tell you what I knew would grieve you. I am deeply grateful to that good friend—whomever he be—who allowed you to know. It is a weight lifted from my soul. Yes, Father, I love Margaret. Do not call me a priest, for I shall never be one. I would sooner die."

"That we shall see, young man," his father warned sternly. "Come, deny me no more; you will learn the price of disrespecting a father."

Gerard held his tongue, and the trio walked home in a somber silence, broken only by the occasional heavy sigh of Catherine. From that hour, the small house at Tergou ceased to be a sanctuary of peace.

The following day, Gerard was summoned before the entire family. Every voice rose up against him, save for little Kate and the dwarf, who instinctively sided with him without understanding why. Cornelis and Sybrandt were more bitter than their father. Gerard was dismayed to find so many enemies and cast a longing glance at his little sister. Her eyes were filled with tears as harsh words rained down on the boy who had been everyone's favorite just yesterday. Yet, she offered no comfort. Turning her head away, she whispered, "Dear, dear Gerard, pray to Heaven to cure you of this folly!"

"What, are you against me too?" Gerard asked, his heart breaking.

With a deep sigh, he rose and fled the house, heading straight for Sevenbergen.

A quarrel between parties bound by affection yet divided by opposing interests begins innocently enough. Often, both sides hold truth in their initial arguments. It is only as the conflict drags on that a calm, judicious friend—one capable of seeing both perspectives—becomes a divine necessity. For the longer the discord persists, the wider and deeper it grows, fueled by human fallibility and irritability. These flaws exist on both sides until inevitably, both arrive at the same conclusion: both are wrong.

In this struggle, the combatants were unevenly matched. Elias was furious; Cornelis and Sybrandt were spiteful. But Gerard, possessing a broader and more cultivated mind, perceived the nuances they missed. He suffered from fits of indecision and felt not wrath, but profound unhappiness. He was utterly alone in this fight, unable to confide in anyone.

Margaret Van Eyck was a spirited girl, but he dared not burden her with his domestic torment. She might sympathize, yet she was also capable of sacrificing him to his relatives to preserve her own happiness. Though she had been a great comfort to him on other occasions, he now feared making her his confidante. Her own history was well-known: in her youth, she had rejected numerous marriage proposals to dedicate herself to art, a pursuit incompatible with the duties of wife and mother. Consequently, she remained single, painting alongside her brothers. How could he confess that he had declined the clerical position she had secured for him, doing so precisely because of the very calling she had once despised and sacrificed lightly?

At this juncture, Gerard seemed poised to surrender. However, the opposition possessed a formidable ally in Catherine, the senior. This good-hearted but uneducated woman lacked the quiet firmness of her daughter and the ability to formulate plans. Sometimes she irritated Gerard, inadvertently bolstering his resolve, for anger often sustains courage. Other moments, she would suddenly turn on her own side, undermining their efforts.

Consider one instance among many: one day, with the whole household assembled, Cornelis declared, "Shall our Gerard wed Margaret Brandt? Why, it is hunger marrying thirst!"

"And what will happen when *you* marry?" Catherine shrieked.

"Gerard can paint, Gerard can write, but what can *you* do to keep a woman, you lazy fool? Nothing but wait for your father's shoes." She laughed derisively. "We see why you and Sybrandt refuse to let the poor boy marry. You fear he will come to us for a share of our wealth. If he does, and we give it to him, it is not yours we part with, nor will it likely be yours anyway."

On such occasions, Gerard would smile slyly, regain his composure, and temporary bewilderment would fall upon Catherine's unfortunate accomplices.

But after more than six months of relentless irritation, the situation reached its climax. The father announced to the entire family that he had ordered the burgomaster to imprison Gerard in the Stadthouse rather than allow him to marry Margaret.

Gerard turned pale with rage but managed to remain silent through sheer effort. His father continued, "And before the year is out, you shall be a priest, whether you will or no."

"Is that so?" Gerard cried out.

"Then listen to me, all of you. By God and Saint Bavon, I swear I will never be a priest while Margaret lives. Since force is to decide this matter—not love and duty—try your force, Father. But force shall fail you. The day the burgomaster comes for me, I leave Tergou forever, and Holland too, and my father's house, where it seems I have been valued these many years not for myself, but for what can be extracted from me."

With that, he stormed out of the room, white-faced with anger and despair.

"There!" Catherine exclaimed. "That is the result of driving young people too hard. Men are crueler than tigers, even to their own flesh and blood. Now, Heaven forbid he should ever leave us, married or single."

As Gerard emerged from the house, his cheeks pale and his heart pounding, he met Reicht Heynes carrying a message: Margaret Van Eyck wished to see him.

He found the elderly lady seated as grimly as a judge. She wasted no time on pleasantries, asking coldly why he had not visited recently. Before he could respond, she spoke in a sarcastic tone, "I thought we had been friends, young sir."

At this, Gerard appeared the picture of doubt and consternation.

"It is because you never told her you were in love," Reicht Heynes said, pitying his confusion.

"Silence, wench! Why should he tell us his affairs? We are not his friends; we have not earned his confidence."

"Alas, my second mother," Gerard murmured, "I did not dare to tell you my folly."

"What folly? Is it folly to love?"

"I am told so every day of my life."

"You need not have been afraid to tell my mistress; she is always kind to true lovers."

"Madam—Reicht—I was afraid because I was told..."

"Well, you were told...?"

"That in your youth you scorned love, preferring art."

"I did, boy; and what is the end of it? Behold me here, a barren stock, while the women of my youth have a host of children at their sides and grandchildren at their

The legal formalities of marriage required the reading of the banns three times, a tradition much like today, save for one significant difference: in those days, they were typically proclaimed on weekdays. However, the young couple, driven by urgency and aided by their striking appearance, successfully persuaded the local priest to compress the entire process into a single twenty-four-hour period. The announcements were made on a Monday at both Matins and Vespers, and to their immense relief, not a single soul from Tergou appeared in the church to object.

The following morning, Margaret and Gerard arrived at the church, their hearts pounding with nervous anticipation. To their absolute horror, a stranger rose in the congregation and formally forbade the union. He claimed the couple were minors and lacked parental consent. Outside the church doors, the pair engaged in a trembling, near-despairing consultation. Before they could formulate a plan, the interloper approached them. He feigned regret, claiming his intention was to promote their happiness, yet explained that his livelihood depended entirely on obstructing such unions.

"The young people give me a crown, and I undo my work handsomely," he suggested. "Tell the cure I was mistaken, and all proceeds smoothly."

"A crown!" Gerard exclaimed eagerly. "I will give you a golden angel to secure this favor." The man agreed instantly, accompanying Gerard to the priest. He declared the previous objection a ridiculous error, corrected by a mere glance at the lovers. Satisfied, the priest scheduled the wedding for the next day at ten o'clock. The professional impediment to bliss departed with Gerard's golden angel.

Like many such cunning rogues, however, he proved to be a fool. He proceeded to a tavern in Tergou known for its green dedicated to archery and popular games, where he became drunk and boasted of his successful interference. Among the listeners was Sybrandt, a notorious idler and frequent visitor to the establishment. Sybrandt rushed home to report the news. Finding his father away in Rotterdam purchasing cloth from merchants, he signaled his elder brother to emerge and shared the intelligence. Black sheep exist in nearly every large family; these two brothers were Gerard's. Idleness corrupts the soul, and waiting for the demise of loved ones breeds resentment; these two single-minded curs were prepared to destroy anyone who threatened their miserable inheritance, which occupied their thoughts by day and dominated their dreams by night.

Their parents' stinginess was originally a virtue born of industry and love for their children, but in these twisted, selfish hearts, it had mutated into avarice—a fertile source of crime. They conspired not to inform their mother, whose feelings were unpredictable, but to approach the Burgomaster immediately. Shrewdly, they sensed his aversion to the match, though they could not discern the reason. Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the Burgomaster, saw through their plot instantly but allowed them to remain unaware of his insight. Hearing their tale, he adopted an air of magisterial dignity and coldness.

"Since the father of the family is absent, his duty falls upon me, the father of the town," he stated. "I know your father's mind. Leave everything to me. Above all, tell no woman a word of this, least of all the women within your own household, for chattering tongues ruin the wisest counsels." With a touch of condescension, he dismissed them, feeling somewhat ashamed of his unwitting accomplices.

Upon returning home, they discovered their brother Gerard seated on a low stool at their mother's knee. She caressed his hair, speaking kindly and promising to support him against his father's wishes. The catalyst for this sudden reversal was characteristic of the woman. In a fit of female irritation, she had previously torn Margaret's portrait to shreds. Having observed Gerard's reaction—turning pale as death, sitting motionless like a bereaved creature, tears blinding his gaze as he stared at the fragments—she had been terrified by her own action. Her heart ached with remorse, yet pride prevented her from admitting fault. Instead, she resolved silently to make amends. Her maternal instincts overwhelmed her, and her earlier violent impulse died naturally as she transferred her allegiance to Gerard just as the two "black sheep" entered the room.

Gerard, inexperienced in the complexities of female emotion, felt shame at having suspected her of destroying the picture. Overcome with gratitude for her apparent kindness, he kissed her repeatedly and retired to bed, a prince in spirit, believing his mother had fully embraced him at the critical juncture of his destiny.

The following morning at ten o'clock, Gerard and Margaret stood in the church at Sevenbergen. He radiated joy; she burned with blushes. Only Peter and Martin Wittenhaagen were present among their friends; secrecy was paramount. Margaret had refused to travel to Italy, unable to abandon her father, who was too learned and too frail to be left alone. It was agreed they would withdraw to Flanders for a few weeks until the storm in Tergou subsided.

The priest did not keep them waiting long. Soon, he stood at the altar, calling them forward. Hand in hand, the happiest couple in Holland approached him. The priest opened his book, but before uttering a single word of the sacred rite, a harsh voice commanded, "Forbear!" Constables from Tergou surged up the aisle and seized Gerard in the name of the law. Martin's long knife flashed out instantly.

"Forbear, man!" cried the priest indignantly. "What! Draw your weapon in a church? You who interrupt this holy sacrament, what manner of impiety is this?"

"There is no impiety, Father," replied the Burgomaster's servant respectfully. "This young man seeks to marry against his father's will, and his father has petitioned our Burgomaster to deal with him according to the law. Let him deny it if he can."

Gerard hung his head in silence. "We take him to Rotterdam to await the sentence of the Duke," the servant announced.

At this, Margaret screamed in despair. The formerly joyful youths collapsed into each other's arms, sobbing pitifully. Even the oppressors hesitated, ashamed by the scene, though one, acting on good nature and under the pretext of separating them, leaned toward Margaret and whispered, "Rotterdam? It is a lie. We merely take him to our Stadthouse."

Mounted on horses and taking a dozen halts via sly detours, they were led back to Tergou. Just outside the town walls, a crude vehicle covered in canvas awaited. Gerard was forced into it and, by five in the evening, was secretly conveyed to the prison of the Stadthouse.
