He was hoisted up several flights of stairs and shoved into a tiny room illuminated only by a narrow window featuring a vertical iron bar. The sole piece of furniture was a massive oak chest. Imprisonment in that era was a direct highway to death. Even in its mildest form, it entailed cold, unbroken solitude, torture, starvation, and often poison. Gerard realized he was in the hands of an enemy.

"Oh, the look that man gave me on the road to Rotterdam," he thought, despair filling his heart. "There is more here than my father's wrath. I doubt I shall see the light of day again." Kneeling, he commended his soul to God.

Soon, he rose and leaped at the iron bar of the window, clutching it tightly. By pressing his knees against the wall, he managed to peer outward for a fleeting minute—a duration precious only to a captive. Through the gap, he saw Martin Wittenhaagen's back. Martin sat quietly fishing in the brook near the Stadthouse. Gerard lunged again at the window and whistled. Instantly, Martin stopped fishing, his attention sharpening far beyond the sport. Turning hastily, he spotted Gerard, made a signal, and gathered his line and bow before departing quickly. Gerard understood his friends were not idle, yet he wished Martin had stayed; the sight of him alone was a profound comfort. Holding onto the window frame, he watched the soldier's retreating form until he could no longer see him, then slumped back heavily.

Just as he did, the rusty iron bar, held fast only by corroded nails, tore away from the stonework. Simultaneously, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten opened the door stealthily behind him. The Burgomaster's eyes fell on the broken iron, then glanced at the window, saying nothing. The window stood a hundred feet above the ground; if Gerard fancied jumping, surely he would not hesitate. The Burgomaster placed a brown loaf and a pitcher of water on the chest in solemn silence.

Gerard's first instinct was to smash the Burgomaster's skull with the iron bar and flee down the stairs. But sensing malice in the official's eyes, the Burgomaster cleared his throat, and three armed men materialized at the doorway.

"My orders are to keep you here until you swear an oath to renounce Margaret Brandt and return to the Church, to which you have belonged since infancy," the Burgomaster declared.

"Death sooner," Gerard answered.

"With all my heart," the Burgomaster replied, and withdrew.

Martin hastened to Sevenbergen, finding Margaret pale and agitated but resolute. She was finishing a letter to the Countess Charolois, appealing against Ghysbrecht's violence and treachery.

"Courage!" Martin cried upon entering. "I have found him. He is in the haunted tower, right at the very top. Yes, I know the place; many a poor soul has gone up there and come down feet foremost." He recounted spotting Gerard's face

The piercing gaze of hatred the Burgomaster had fixed upon Gerard, combined with his sudden incarceration, convinced the young man that Ghysbrecht was his implacable foe. Gliding around the corner of the tower, he expected nothing less than a cruel, treacherous trap devised by a wicked man to harm him within those walls, an escape from which would remain unknown to the world. Yet, as he moved stealthily forth, a soft yet courageous hand slipped into his own. Margaret stood beside him, prepared to share this newfound peril.

Before the haunted tower could be fully discerned, a sight struck their vision that froze them in place. Halfway up the structure, a creature with a blazing head, resembling an enormous glow-worm, climbed steadily toward the summit. Its body was shrouded in darkness, though its silhouette was visible against the glare emanating from its luminous head; the entire entity measured nearly four feet in length. At the base of the tower stood a figure clad in white, indistinguishable from a living woman. Gerard and Margaret trembled with awe.

"The rope! The rope!" Gerard gasped. "It is ascending the rope."

As they watched, the glowing form vanished into Gerard's former prison cell, its light flooding the interior and casting a crimson hue across the window. Below, the white figure remained motionless. Those who survive the initial shock of the supernatural often succumb to a peculiar, paralyzing terror—a wild, irresistible urge to throw oneself upon the terrifying object. It captivates the observer with the same hypnotic force a snake holds over a bird. The great tragedian Macready once portrayed this perfectly in *Macbeth*, during Banquo's second apparition; he threw himself backward, head averted, at the horrific shadow. This same primal impulse seized Margaret. She released Gerard's hand, standing bewildered for a moment, before suddenly letting out a wild cry and darting toward the specter.

Gerard, unaware of this natural instinct, instantly believed the Evil One was luring her to her doom. He fell to his knees. "Exorcizo vos! In nomine beatae Mariae, exorcizo vos!"

While the exorcist shrieked his incantations in extreme terror, he received infinite relief when the specter emitted a feeble cry of fear. Discovering that even Hell possessed its minor weaknesses was deeply encouraging. He intensified his prayers, and shortly thereafter, he saw the ghastly shape kneeling at Margaret's feet, begging pitifully for mercy.

Soon, Kate and Giles arrived at the haunted tower. Their surprise was immense upon discovering a new rope dangling from the prisoner's window down to the ground.

"I see how it is," remarked the younger brother, accepting the evidence before him. "Our Gerard has descended this rope. He has escaped. Now, I shall ascend and verify."

"No, Giles, no!" cried the elder, blinded by prejudice. "Do you not see this is sorcery? This rope is a line cast out by the Devil to lure you to destruction. He knows the frailties of every heart; he sees how fond you are of climbing heights. How could our Gerard procure such a rope? How could he fasten it in the sky like this? It defies nature. May the Holy Saints protect us tonight, for Hell is abroad."

"Stuff!" retorted the dwarf. "The road to Hell goes down, and this rope leads up. I have never been lucky enough to climb such a long rope. It might be years before I encounter one so conveniently prepared. Better to be knocked on the head at once than to wait forever for happiness."

With a cry of delight, akin to a cat leaping onto a table laden with fish, he sprang onto the rope. All his athletic energy ignited within him; the only concession Kate managed to extract was permission to fasten a lantern around his neck first.

"A light frightens evil spirits," she explained.

Thus, with his massive arms and legs moving with feather-like agility, Giles ascended the rope faster than his brother had descended. The lantern at the nape of his neck transformed him into a living glow-worm. His sister watched his progress with trembling anxiety. Suddenly, a female figure burst forth from the solid masonry, flying at her with superhuman speed. Kate let out a weak cry—the best effort her tongue allowed, for terror had glued it to her palate. She dropped her crutches, sank to her knees, hid her face, and moaned, "Take my body, but spare my soul!"

Margaret, panting, exclaimed, "Why, it is a woman!"

Kate, quivering, repeated, "Why, it is a woman!"

"How frightened you've made me!" Kate cried.

"I am terrified enough myself. Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"This is strange! But what about the fiery-headed thing? It was with you, and you are harmless! Why are you here at this hour?"

"Nay, why are *you* here?"

"Perhaps we are on the same mission? Ah! You are his dear sister, Kate!"

"And you are Margaret Brandt."

"Yes."

"All the better. You love him; you are here. Then Giles was correct. He has won his freedom."

Gerard stepped forward to confirm the truth, but their conversation was abruptly cut short by a horrifying, unearthly noise, sounding like a sepulcher speaking through a ventriloquist's trick:

With each repetition, the volume increased. Looking upward, they beheld the dwarf, clutching handfuls of parchments, his face alight with fiendish joy and flushed with diabolical fire. The lantern hanging from his neck rendered him a more infernal transparency than any mortal eye had ever witnessed. Simultaneously, the awful imp hurled the parchments at the stunned figures below. Documents rained down like wounded wild ducks; some collapsed immediately, others fluttered helplessly, while still others spread wide, wheeling slowly downward in airy spirals.

Hardly had these settled when the sepulchral roar returned—"Parchment! Parchment!"—and another flock of documents pattered and sailed down. Another followed, until the grass beneath them was whitened by the falling papers.

Finally, the fire-headed imp, with his luminous body and horned hands, slid down the rope like a falling star. Prioritizing business over sentiment, he approached his rescued brother to demand immediate payment for the merchandise he had just delivered.

"Hush!" Gerard warned. "You speak too loudly. Gather them up and follow us to a safer place than this."

"Will you come home with me, Gerard?" asked little Kate.

"I have no home."

"You shall not say that. Who is more welcome than you, after such cruel injustice, to your father's house?"

"Father! I have no father," Gerard replied sternly. "He who was my father has become my jailer. I have escaped his grasp; I will never set foot within his reach again."

"An enemy did this, not our father," Kate insisted, recounting what she had overheard Cornelis and Sybrandt say. Yet, the injury was too fresh to be soothed. Gerard displayed a bitterness of indignation he had previously seemed incapable of feeling.

"Cornelis and Sybrandt are two vile curs who have shown me their teeth and their hearts for a long time, but they could accomplish nothing. My father is the one who granted the Burgomaster authority, or he would not have dared lay a finger on me, a free burgher of this town. So be it. I was his son; I am his prisoner. He has played his part; I shall play mine. Farewell to the borough where I was born, lived honestly, and was imprisoned. While there remains another town in creation, I shall never trouble you again, Tergou."

"Oh! Gerard! Gerard!" Margaret whispered urgently. "Do not provoke him now. Allow his anger to cool!"

Kate turned quickly toward her. "May I look at your face?"

The inspection appeared favorable, prompting her to whisper, "It is a comely face, and not that of a mischief-maker."

"Fear me not," Margaret said in the same gentle tone. "I could not be happy without your love, as well as Gerard's."

"These are comforting words," Kate sobbed. Then, lifting her eyes, she added, "Little did I think I would like you so well. My heart is willing, but my infirmity prevents me from embracing you."

At this suggestion, Margaret gently wrapped her arms around Gerard's sister and kissed her lovingly. "Often he has spoken of you to me, Kate; and often I longed for this moment."

"You, too, Gerard," Kate said, "kiss me before you depart; for my heart grows heavy at parting with you this night."

Gerard kissed her, and she proceeded home on her crutches. The last sound they heard from her was a faint, patient sigh. Then, tears welled thick in Margaret's eyes. But Gerard was a man, and he paid no heed to his sister's sigh.

As they turned toward Sevenbergen, the dwarf nudged Gerard with his bundle of parchments and extended a concave claw. Margaret attempted to dissuade him. "Why take what does not belong to us?"

"Oh, spoil an enemy however you can."

"But might they not use this as a pretext for renewed violence?"

"How can they? Do you think I will remain in Tergou after this? The Burgomaster robbed me of my liberty

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten clutched the key to Gerard's prison within the folds of his tunic, waiting until the clock struck ten before making his descent. "A touch of hunger serves a purpose," he muttered to himself, "it softens their resolve." At the appointed hour, he ascended the stairwell, carrying a loaf of bread and a water pitcher, trailed by his loyal servant, Dierich, who moved with a weapon drawn tight against his hip. Ghysbrecht paused at the heavy oak door, listening intently. Silence answered him. A cold, grim smile twisted his features. "By now, he must be as despondent as poor Albert Koestein," he reflected.

With a slow turn of the lock, he pushed the door open. The cell was vacant. Ghysbrecht froze, his posture stiffening until he appeared carved from stone. Then, slowly, the rigidity broke; he collapsed to his knees, trembling violently. Behind him, Dierich sensed the shift in his master's demeanor and stepped forward, peering over the old man's shoulder. Upon seeing the empty cot, the discarded rope, and the removed iron bar, the servant let out a gasp of astonishment. Yet, his awe doubled when he witnessed his master, ignoring everything else, hurling himself before the hollow chest. With quivering fingers, Ghysbrecht frantically patted every inch of the wooden surface, refusing to believe his own eyes.

"Master?" Dierich ventured, bewildered. "What troubles you?"

Ghysbrecht's pale lips moved, forming words that never escaped his throat. His hands dropped limply to his sides, and he stared vacantly into the void of the chest.

"Why stare at that empty box?" Dierich pressed, frustration rising. "The boy is gone. Look! Note the cleverness of the rascal; he has removed the bar—"

"GONE!" Ghysbrecht shrieked, the word tearing from his throat. "GONE! GONE!"

"Gone? What is gone, holy saints? Has he been struck blind?"

"THIEF!" Ghysbrecht roared, spinning around to seize Dierich by the collar and shaking him with furious energy. "Do you stand there, knave, while your master is robbed? Run! Fly! Offer a hundred crowns to anyone who brings him back. No, it is futile. Oh, fool! Foolishness to leave that in the same room with him! But none ever discovered the hidden latch before. Only he could have done it. It was meant to happen. It is happening. Lost! Lost!"

As the old man's brief frenzy gave way to the weight of his age and infirmity, he slumped onto the chest, whispering brokenly, "Lost! Lost!"

"What is lost, master?" Dierich asked gently.

"My house, my lands, and my reputation," Ghysbrecht groaned, wringing his hands weakly.

"WHAT?" Dierich cried, his voice cracking with shock.

The intensity of the question, laced with genuine concern, pierced through Ghysbrecht's despair and reignited his natural cunning. "I have lost the town records," he stammered, casting a furtive glance at the servant like a fox cornered near a henhouse.

"Oh, is that all?"

"Is it not enough? How will the burghers react? What will the council do?" Suddenly, his composure returned. "A hundred crowns to whoever recovers them! Every single item contained in this box. If even one piece is missing, I pay nothing."

"'Tis a bargain, master," Dierich replied smoothly, reaching into his pouch. "The hundred crowns are right here. Do you not see those sheets of sheepskin you hold in such high regard?"

"That is true, that is true, good Dierich. My faithful Dierich. All of it, mind you, all that was in the chest."

"Master, I shall take the constables straight to Gerard's dwelling and arrest him for the theft."

"Theft? Yes, excellent. Very good. It is theft. I had forgotten. Since he is now a thief, we shall cast him into the dungeons below, among the toads and the rats. Dierich, that man must never see the light of day again. It is his own doing; he was prying. Haste, haste! Before he has time to speak, you understand—time to talk."

Within half an hour, Dierich Brower led four constables to the hosier's residence, demanding the presence of young Gerard from the terrified Catherine.

"Alas, what has he done now?" she wailed. "That boy will break my heart."

"Nay, dame, merely a youthful prank," Dierich assured her, smoothing his coat. "He has simply absconded with some parchment skins, likely in a fit of merriment. However, the Burgomaster is accountable to the city for their safekeeping, hence his anxiety. As for the youth, he will surely receive a mere reprimand."

These polished words lulled Catherine into relief, though her daughter remained wary. Her suspicions hardened further upon learning that Gerard was not at home and had not returned that evening.

"Come along," Dierich commanded roughly. "We waste precious time." He added, with vehemence, "I shall find him if he breathes air anywhere."

Affinity sharpens the intellect, often enabling the innocent to outwit the scheming. Just as Dierich prepared to depart, Kate signaled him to wait. He dismissed his men and stepped outside. She approached him quietly.

"Hush!" she whispered. "My mother knows nothing. Gerard has fled Tergou."

"How?"

"I saw him last night."

"Ay! Where?" Dierich asked, his interest piqued.

"At the base of the haunted tower."

"How did he manage to get the rope?"

"I do not know; however, I know this: my brother Gerard bid me farewell there, and he is leagues away from Tergou by now. You know the town was never worthy of him. When they imprisoned him, he swore never to return. Let the Burgomaster rest easy; he has imprisoned him, and in doing so, banished him from his birthplace and homeland. Why rob him further, and stain our own good name?"

Had this occurred at another time, Dierich might have acknowledged the logic. Yet, mortified by Gerard's escape and the potential loss of a hundred crowns, he snapped.

"Why did he need to steal?" he retorted bitterly. "Gerard did not steal the trash; he took it solely to spite the Burgomaster, who stole his freedom. But he shall answer to the Duke for it. As for these parchment skins you fuss over so much, check the nearest stream or pigsty; chances are you will find them there."

"Do you truly think so, mistress? Think you so?" Dierich's eyes flashed with sudden suspicion. "Perhaps you know the truth."

"This I know," Kate insisted, "that Gerard is too noble to steal, and too wise to burden himself with worthless rubbish on a journey."

"Then good day to you," Dierich said sharply. "Those sheepskins you despise, I value more highly than the hide of any beast in Tergou." With that, he hurried off, chasing a phantom trail.

Kate retreated indoors and pulled Giles aside. "Giles, my heart forebodes ill. Speak to no soul of what I tell you. I lied to Dirk Brower, saying Gerard has left Holland, yet I strongly suspect he is not a league from Tergou."

"Why, where is he, then?"

"Where else could he be, but with the woman he loves? But if so, he must not linger. These are deep, dark, and wicked men hunting him. Giles, I see the fear in Dirk Brower's eyes, and it makes me tremble. Oh, why cannot I flee to Sevenbergen and summon him away? Why am I not robust and agile like other maidens? God forgive me for fretting against His will; but I have never felt until now the agony of being lame, weak, and useless. But you are strong, dear Giles," she coaxed, "very strong."

"Yes, I am strong," Perpusillus thundered, then, noticing her implication, grumbled, "but I detest traveling on foot."

"Alas, alas! Who will aid me if you refuse?" she pleaded. "Dear Giles, do you not love Gerard?"

"Yes, I favor him most of all. I will ride to Sevenbergen on Peter Buyskens's mule. I shall ask him, for he will not lend it to me otherwise."

Kate protested. The entire town would follow his tracks. It would become known where he had gone, placing Gerard in greater peril than before. Giles deflected her worry by promising to ride out of the town in the opposite direction, turning the mule's head away from Sevenbergen only once he had shed the curious gaze of the populace.

Kate agreed, borrowing the mule. She entrusted Giles with a brief, significant message, drilling him to repeat it word for word until he mastered it perfectly. As Giles mounted the animal, little Kate withdrew to perform the final act in her power for her beloved brother: she knelt and prayed long and fervently for his safety.

Gerard and Margaret departed for Sevenbergen buoyant with the fresh exhilaration of regained freedom and a recent triumph. Yet, this initial elation quickly curdled into somber reflection. Gerard remained a fugitive, perpetually vulnerable to recapture and punishment; consequently, separation became inevitable. Furthermore, he harbored a deep-seated animosity toward his homeland. While Margaret urged him to depart the country temporarily, the mere prospect of him traveling to Italy caused her heart to falter. Conversely, Gerard accepted the necessity of parting from her solely because it allowed him to pursue his dream of visiting Italy. He was convinced that there he could secure wealth and renown, thereby removing every barrier to their union. He recounted to her the warnings of the Demoiselle Van Eyck, reminding her that the gold pieces bestowed upon him were specifically intended for this Italian journey. Clearly, the path ahead served his interests alone. As a craftsman and artist adrift in this uncouth region, he believed Italy would finally recognize his worth. Moved by this selfless logic, the gentle girl granted her consent, though her agreement was accompanied by torrential tears. Hand in hand until midnight, they alternated between lamenting their cruel destiny and painting vivid, hopeful visions of a shared future. In these moments, Margaret's sorrow would overwhelm her, causing even Gerard's eloquent comfort to dissolve into a sigh. By dawn, resignation had taken hold, yet neither possessed the courage to name the hour of departure—a hesitation that might well have delayed their parting indefinitely.

Then, around three in the afternoon, Giles arrived. Having ridden great distances to evade suspicion, he galloped directly to the cottage door. Both lovers rushed forth, driven by urgent curiosity. "Brother Gerard!" Giles cried in his booming voice, "Kate commands you to flee for your life! They accuse you of theft; you have handed them proof. Do not attempt explanation; seek no justice in Tergou. The parchments you seized were merely a ruse. She has seen death in the men's gazes; a bounty hangs over your head. Fly! For Margaret's sake and for all who cherish you, do not waste another moment!"

It was a thunderclap that left two pale faces staring in stunned silence at the harrowing messenger. Then, breaking from his rehearsed message, Giles added words of his own. "Every constable was at our dwelling searching for you, and so was Dirk Brower. Kate is prudent, Gerard. Listen to her counsel and run!"

"Oh, yes, Gerard!" Margaret shrieked wildly. "Flee immediately! Alas, those parchments! My instincts warned me; why did I permit you to take them?"

"Margaret, they are only a distraction," Giles insisted. "Regardless, that old villain shall never see them again. I will not leave until I have concealed his treasure where he can never find it."

After offering Giles a fervent thanks, Gerard bid him farewell, instructing him to return and tell Kate that he was gone. "For I shall be gone before you reach home," he declared. He then summoned Martin, explaining the crisis and begging him to patrol the road toward Tergou. "If you spot Dirk Brower or any of his men," Gerard instructed, "shoot an arrow into the oak tree in our garden. Upon seeing it, I will dash into the nearby forest and meet you at the Weird Hunter's Spring. There, you will guide me through the woods."

With this contingency arranged, Gerard drew a breath he scarcely knew he had been holding. Accompanied by Margaret, he stood beneath the oak tree, watching it tremble anxiously, fearing every rustle might signal an incoming shaft. Meanwhile, Gerard began digging a deep pit to bury the stolen documents. One by one, he cast them into the earth. Most were charters and records of the burgh, but one document appeared to be a private deed between Floris Brandt, father of Peter, and Ghysbrecht.

"Why, this belongs just as much to him as to you," Gerard observed. "Let me read it."

"Oh, not now, Gerard, not now!" Margaret implored. "Every second you delay fills me with dread. Look! Large drops of rain begin to fall, and the clouds darken ominously."

Yielding to her plea, Gerard slipped the deed into his breast pocket, covered the remaining parchments with soil, and trampled the earth down firmly. Just then, a bolt of lightning split the sky, followed by a roll of distant thunder, and the heavens opened in a deluge. Margaret and Gerard scrambled into the house, swiftly joined by Martin.

"The road is clear," Martin reported, "but a violent storm approaches."

His prediction proved accurate. Thunder grew louder, crashing overhead like a giant's hammer, while flashes of lightning strobed rapidly, resembling the lashes of a whip. Rain poured in torrents. Margaret buried her face, refusing to witness the terrifying display. Seizing the moment, Gerard raised the rough shutter and lit a candle. The lovers conferred quietly, and Gerard thanked the tempest for granting them a few precious hours together. The sun vanished unnoticed, swallowed by the gloom. Thunder continued its relentless pealing, lightning danced across the darkness, and rain washed the world clean.

Supper was laid out, yet neither Gerard nor Margaret could bring themselves to eat; the knowledge that this might be their final meal together choked them. As the storm subsided slightly, Peter retired to bed. However, Gerard was to depart at first light, and neither lover could afford to squander an hour in slumber. Martin lingered briefly, carefully restringing his bow, a task he performed with meticulous care. Beside him, the lovers whispered their griefs and their undying love.

Suddenly, the old man raised a hand, signaling for absolute silence. They stilled themselves and listened, hearing nothing but the wind. Then, abruptly, a footstep cracked softly upon the autumn leaves scattered across the garden behind the cottage. To those without fear, such a sound would be insignificant; to those hunted, it was a death knell. It was a foot attempting to remain unheard. Martin nocked an arrow and snuffed out the candle.

In that same instant, to their sheer horror, they heard additional footsteps approaching the front door—not quite as silent as the first, but moving with deliberate stealth—followed by a deadly pause. Their blood turned to ice.

"Oh, Kate, oh, Kate! You said to fly instantly!" Margaret wailed, wringing her hands in agony, terror, and wild remorse for having detained Gerard.

"Hush, girl!" Martin commanded in a harsh whisper.

A heavy knock resonated against the door, striking not just the timber, but the very hearts within.

As though a prearranged signal had been given, the rear door was battered down with brutal force moments later. They were trapped. Yet, amidst the terrifying clamor, Margaret seemed to regain a measure of composure. She whispered urgently, "Tell them he was here, but has fled." Seizing Gerard, she nearly dragged him up the rough stone steps leading to her father's chamber; her own room lay just beyond. The pounding on the door redoubled.

"Who dares knock at this hour?"

"Open, or perish!"

"I yield not to thieves—honest folk are all asleep."

"Open to the law, Martin Wittenhaagen, or regret it bitterly."

"Why, that is Dirk Brower's voice, I believe. What brings you so far from Tergou?"

"Open, and you shall learn."

Martin drew the bolt with deliberate slowness, and in surged Dierich and four others. They ushered in their companion who had entered through the back door.

"Now, Martin, where is Gerard Eliassoen?"

"Gerard Eliassoen? Why, he was here but now!"

"Were he here?" Dierich's face darkened instantly. "And where is he now?"

"They say he has departed for Italy. Pray, what is the urgency?"

"No matter. When did he leave? Do not tell me he vanished in such a storm!"

"There is quite a fuss over Gerard Eliassoen," Martin scoffed. He lit a candle, seated himself calmly by the fire, and began whittling a piece of fine silk around his bowstring at the spot worn thin by the arrow notch.

"I shall inform you," he said casually. "Do you know his brother Giles? A little malformed imp, all head and limbs? Well, he tore over here on a mule, shouting something—I was too distant to catch the words, only the din. In any case, he startled Gerard. Immediately after his departure, there was such weeping and embracing, and then Gerard departed. They whisper he has gone to Italy—perhaps you know where that lies, for I do not."

Dierich's expression sank deeper with every word. There was no flaw in the tale; a more cunning liar might have spun too many falsehoods and aroused suspicion. But Martin executed his duty perfectly. He uttered only the single lie commanded, inventing nothing else of his own volition.

"Folks," said Dierich, "I fear he speaks truth. I warned the burgomaster exactly how this would unfold. He encountered the dwarf galloping Peter Buyskens's mule from Sevenbergen. 'They have dispatched that imp to Gerard,' he declared, 'therefore Gerard must be at Sevenbergen.' 'Ah, master!' I replied, 'It is too late now. We ought to have considered Sevenbergen sooner, rather than wasting our time scouring the obscure corners of Tergou for those cursed parchments we shall never locate until we find the man who stole them. If he was at Sevenbergen,' I continued, 'and they sent the dwarf to him, it must have been to warn him we hunt him. He is leagues away by now,' I stated. Confound that chalk-faced girl! She has outwitted us bearded men; and so I reported to the burgomaster, yet he refused to listen. A dampened jerkin apiece—that is all reward we shall receive for this endeavor."

Martin grinned coolly in Dierich's face.

"Nevertheless," added the latter, "to satisfy the burgomaster, we shall search the house."

Martin's demeanor shifted instantly to gravity. This transformation did not escape Dierich's notice. After a moment's reflection, he ordered, "Station two of you outside, one guard on each flank of the house, ensuring no one leaps from the upper windows. The rest follow me." Taking the candle, he ascended the stairs, trailed by three comrades. Martin remained alone.

The sturdy soldier bowed his head. Everything had proceeded smoothly at first; now came this disastrous reversal! Suddenly, a thought struck him: all was not yet lost. Gerard must be in Peter's room or Margaret's; neither was excessively high from the ground. Gerard would surely leap out. Dierich had left a man below, but what then? For thirty seconds, Gerard and he would stand two against one, and in that fleeting interval, anything might occur.

Martin held the back door slightly ajar and watched. Light spilled from Peter's room.

"Curse the fool!" he muttered. "Does he intend to surrender like a maiden?"

The light drifted into Margaret's bedroom. Still, no window opened. Had Gerard planned to flee that way, he would not have waited until the intruders filled the room. Martin grasped this immediately, abandoned the door, descended to the foot of the stairs, and listened. He began to suspect Gerard had slipped away through a window while the men occupied the interior. The longer the silence persisted, the firmer this conviction became.

Then, abruptly and roughly, it was shattered. Faint cries emanated from the inner bedroom—Margaret's.

"They have captured him," groaned Martin. "They have secured him."

A flash of realization struck him: if they removed Gerard, his life would be forfeit; and if harm befell him, Margaret's heart would shatter. Casting his gaze wildly around like a wild beast seeking an exit, he formed a resolution strikingly characteristic of those iron ages and of a soldier cornered. He moved to each door in succession, mimicking Dierich Brower's tone sharply, "Guard the window!" He then quietly barred and bolted both doors. Next, he picked up his bow and six arrows; fitting one to the string, he stowed the others in his quiver. His knife he placed upon a chair behind him, hilt facing inward, and there he waited at the base of the stairs with a calm resolve to kill those four men or fall by their hands.

Two, he knew, he could dispatch with his arrows before they could approach, leaving Gerard and himself to fight hand-to-hand with the remaining pair. Furthermore, he had witnessed men paralyzed by sudden attacks of this nature. Should Brower and his men hesitate even an instant before engaging him, he would strike three instead of two, shifting the odds in their favor.

He need not wait long. Heavy footsteps echoed in Margaret's room, drawing nearer and nearer. The light advanced, accompanied by voices. Though Martin's heart, robust as it was, hammered violently hearing men march toward their deaths—and perhaps his own—it was more likely so than not. Four against one was long odds in a battlefield merely ten feet wide, and Gerard might be bound and powerless to assist. Yet this man, previously seen trembling in his boots at the mere mention of a lantern named Giles, never faltered in this terrible moment of genuine peril. He stood there, body coiled for combat, eyes blazing, equally prepared to give life or take it.

Desperate stakes! To win meant instant and lifelong exile; to lose meant dying instantly upon that very floor.

Dierich Brower and his men discovered Peter in the first stages of sleep. They ransacked his cupboards, drove knives into an alligator skin nailed to his wall, and peered beneath his bed. It was a spacious room, seemingly full of hiding places, yet Gerard was nowhere to be found.

Next, they proceeded to Margaret's room, and the sight itself was disheartening—it was small, barren, devoid of cupboards. However, a large fireplace and chimney dominated the space. Dierich's eyes fell upon these immediately. Here they found the beauty of Sevenbergen sleeping on an ancient chest barely a foot high, with no attempt made to conceal her; yet her sheets were snow-white, as was her own linen. She lay there, resembling a lily fallen into a rut.

Soon she awoke, sitting upright in bed, bewildered. Upon seeing the men, she began to scream faintly, praying for mercy. Her plea made Dierich Brower ashamed of his mission.

"This is a disturbance," he said, somewhat flustered. "We mean no harm to you, my pretty maid. Remain still, close your eyes, and dream of your wedding night while I inspect this chimney to see if Master Gerard hides within."

"Gerard! In my room?"

"Why not? They claim you and he—"

"Cruel! You know they have driven him away from me—expelled him from his homeland. This is a ruse. You are thieves; you are wicked men; you are not men of Sevenbergen, or you would know Margaret Brandt better than to seek her lover in this room above all others in the world. Oh, brave! Four great hulking men, armed to the teeth, to insult one poor honest girl! The women residing in your own homes must be worthless, or you would respect them too greatly to disgrace a girl of good character."

"There! Depart, before we hear worse," Dierich hastened, retreating quickly. "He is not in the chimney. Plaster may repair what a cudgel breaks, but a woman's tongue is a double-edged dagger, and a girl possesses her mother's milk still." With that, he beat a hasty retreat.

"I informed the burgomaster precisely how this would conclude."

Where is the woman who cannot assume a role when necessity demands it? Who will not perform it perfectly to preserve the life of the man she adores? In moments of supreme crisis, nature itself seems to intervene, granting even the most timid souls the power to blind Solomon with dust.

As soon as the soldiers withdrew, Margaret rose from her hiding place. She slipped out of bed, discarding her skirts, petticoats, and stockings, leaving only her nightgown. She pushed the heavy chest—her makeshift coffin—against the wall, then crept silently to the door to listen. The heavy tread of boots faded through her father's chamber and down the stone stairs.

Inside the room lay a secret known only to her. To conceal her presence, a section of the floorboards had been removed. When Gerard had requested repairs to make the room presentable, lacking proper timber, he had cleverly cut away just enough wood to accommodate her narrow form. Using those very planks, he had patched the hole, rendering the deception nearly invisible to any casual observer. Yet, beneath the surface, the truth remained: the "bed" she occupied actually hung suspended from the rafters, two feet above the actual floor. Visually, it appeared shallow; in reality, it was a deep crypt.

Silence reigned absolute. Margaret knelt, offering a silent prayer of gratitude to Heaven. Rising again, she moved to the edge of the trapdoor and whispered softly, "Gerard?"

There was no response. She pressed her voice slightly higher. "Gerard, all is safe. Thank God! You may rise now; but oh, be careful!"

Stillness answered her. She placed her trembling hand upon his shoulder. "Gerard!"

Nothing. Panic, cold and sharp, pierced her heart. "What is this?" she cried, her fingers flying frantically across his face and chest. She gripped his shoulders, shaking him violently, lifting him up. But he offered no resistance; he slid from her grasp, falling back not with the weight of a living man, but with the lifelessness of a corpse.

A profound dread seized her. The lid had been lowered while she slept. The men had been in the room. With a surge of frenzied strength, she ripped him free from the darkness of the chest. Carrying him in her arms, she rushed to the window and threw it wide open. The cool, fragrant night air rushed in, filling the stagnant space. She laid him within the sill, bathed in the silver light of the moon.

His complexion was the color of ash; his limbs were utterly limp and motionless. Reaching for his pulse, she found nothing. Horror struck her with the force of a physical blow. *Horror of horrors!* She had suffocated him with her own body. Her mind refused to accept such a sudden, catastrophic tragedy. Gerard, who had scrambled into that box mere minutes prior, how could he already be dead?

She called upon every endearing name her heart could conjure. She kissed him, caressed him, coaxed him, imploring him to speak. There was no answer to her words of love, phrases she had never dared utter before, yet which now poured forth uncontrollably. Trembling violently, she began to babble over his pale countenance, saying things that were both terrifying and pitiful.

"Oh, Gerard! I am so sorry you are dead. I am so sorry I have killed you. Forgive me for not allowing the men to take you; it would have been kinder than this. Oh, Gerard! I am very, very sorry for what I have done."

Suddenly, her composure shattered completely. She raved aloud. "No! No! Such things cannot be true, or there is no God! It is monstrous! How can my Gerard be dead? How could I have killed my Gerard? I love him! Oh, God, you know how I love him. He does not. If he knew my heart, he would speak to me; he would not remain so deaf to his poor Margaret. This must be a trick to make me scream and betray him! But no! I love him too deeply for that. I would rather die."

Seizing her own throat, she tried to stifle her wild urge to shriek in terror and agony. "If he would but say one word. Oh, Gerard! Do not die without speaking. Have mercy on me and scold me, but speak to me! If you are angry, curse me! I deserve it—the fool who killed the man she loved more than herself. Ah, I am a murderer. The worst in all the world. Help! Help! I have murdered him! Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah!"

She tore at her hair, emitting shrieks so wild and piercing that they rang like funeral bells throughout the house. The sounds reached the ears of Dierich Brower and his men instantly. Startled from their slumber, they sprang to their feet, staring at one another in confusion and alarm.

Martin Wittenhaagen stood at the base of the staircase, his bowstring pulled taut against his cheek and his dagger gripped firmly in his other hand. His heart hammered against his ribs until he saw the men return empty-handed, lacking Gerard. Confusion washed over his face; he slowly lowered the weapon, staring at them with wide eyes. The intruders, however, were no less baffled by his defensive posture.

"Why, mates, was the old fellow preparing to loose an arrow at us?" one asked.

"Rubbish!" Martin retorted, forcing his rough demeanor back into place. "I was merely testing my new string. There! If you doubt me so greatly, I will unstring the bow right before your eyes."

"Hmph!" Dierich grunted, his suspicion undimmed. "There is more to you than meets the eye. Put a log on the fire, and let us warm our frozen limbs before we descend further."

A roaring blaze was quickly kindled. The men clustered around the heat, hanging their wet garments and long, matted hair over the flames until the smoke curled upward. It was then that the shrill cries erupted from Margaret's chamber above. Every man leaped to his feet. One snatched a candle and bolted up the narrow stairway toward the sleeping quarters. Martin surged forward instinctively, driven by panic and a fear that the stranger's curiosity would lead to disaster. As he lunged to block the path, Dierich wrapped his arms around Martin's waist from behind, shouting for the others to hold him fast.

The candle-bearer slipped past the grapple, and instantly the group descended upon Martin. A brutal struggle ensued, sending them tumbling across the floorboards. Martin found himself pinned beneath the weight of four men, fighting desperately until they finally subdued the giant. Binding him hand and foot with a rope intended for Gerard, they left him gasping on the ground.

"Ay, gnash your teeth, you ancient rascal," Dierich panted, still catching his breath. "You shall not use them again."

"It seems our very lives hung by a thread while this old brute remained free," one of the men observed grimly.

"He makes me believe Gerard is not far behind," another added hopefully.

"No such fortune," Dierich snapped. "Listen! Jorian Ketel has been lingering in that girl's bedroom far too long. Some of us should go check on him immediately."

The crude laughter that followed this command had barely faded when the sound of hurried footsteps echoed loudly overhead.

"Oh, here he comes at last!" someone cried out. "Well, Jorian, what business have you up there now?"

Jorian Ketel hurried directly to Margaret's chamber, expecting to find the fugitive hidden away. Instead, he stumbled upon a scene of profound stillness that left him breathless. In the center of the room lay Gerard, pale and rigid, his head resting in Margaret's lap. She knelt over him, utterly silent, her form frozen in a paralysis of grief. Her eyes were wide and glassy, reflecting no light, hearing no voice, caring for nothing in the world save the white, lifeless face cradled against her breast.

Jorian stood transfixed, the candle trembling violently in his grip. "Where has he been all this time?" he cried out, but Margaret did not answer. Ignoring her, Jorian approached the open chest and examined its contents. A sudden comprehension dawned upon him, mirroring the girl's dumb, icy despair. "This is a pitiful sight," he muttered, his voice heavy with pity. "A black night's labor, all for a handful of skins! It would have been far better to have gone with us. She is past responding to me, poor creature."

He paused, then reached for a small, round mirror hanging nearby—no larger than his palm—and pressed it gently to Gerard's mouth and nostrils. Holding it there for a moment, he watched the surface closely. When he pulled it away, the glass remained dull and clouded.

"There is life in him!" Jorian whispered to himself, the realization striking him like lightning.

The word struck Margaret instantly. Though spoken softly, it shattered her stupor. As if a statue suddenly infused with spirit and passion, she sprang to her feet. She threw her arms around Jorian's neck, clutching the burly man with an intensity that bordered on fierceness. "Oh, bless the tongue that speaks this truth!" she gasped, holding him tight again and again. "Yes, yes! Lay him warm," Jorian urged gently.

In a swift movement, he lifted Gerard and placed him upon the bedclothes. Drawing a flask from his belt, he poured a generous measure of Schiedamse brandy into his hand and splashed it sharply onto Gerard's face. The pungent liquor acted swiftly; Gerard drew a faint, ragged sigh. Never had such a sound brought more relief to the human ear. Margaret rushed toward him, but halted abruptly, trembling with the fear that her touch might harm him further. Having lost all confidence in her own strength, she hovered uncertainly.

"That is correct—leave him be," Jorian instructed firmly. "Do not crowd him as you did me, or you will drive his breath back. Leave him alone; he will surely recover. He is not an old man."

Gerard sighed deeply, and a faint flush of color began to creep up his lips. Seizing the opportunity, Jorian moved toward the door. Hardly had he reached the threshold when his legs were seized from behind. It was Margaret! She curled around his knees like a clinging vine, kissing his hand and fawning upon him with desperate gratitude.

"You will not speak?" she pleaded. "You have saved his life; you possess no heart to cast him back into the grave, to undo your own good deed?"

"No, no!" Jorian reassured her warmly. "It is not the first time I have aided both of you; remember, I warned you in the church where we intended to hide him. Besides, what does Dierich Brower matter to me? I would see him hanged before I ever revealed his location. However, I must ask: where are the parchments? A hundred crowns are offered for them. That would be a wonderful fortune for my Joan and the children, you know."

"Ah! Those hundred crowns shall be yours," Jorian promised eagerly.

"What! Are the documents still in the house?" Jorian asked, leaning forward.

"Not there, but I know exactly where they are," Margaret declared, her voice rising with renewed vigor. "By God and Saint Bavon, I swear you shall have them tomorrow. Come to me whenever you wish, but come alone."

"I could not be otherwise," Jorian replied, his mind racing. "How could I share the hundred crowns with Dirk Brower? May my bones rot in my skin if I allow another soul to know the poor boy is here."

With that, he bolted from the room, fearing that any delay might arouse suspicion and draw the authorities to them. Left alone, Margaret knelt beside Gerard, quivering from head to toe, praying fervently for his recovery.

Outside, Jorian met Dierich Brower's questioning gaze. "What is to be done?" Jorian asked casually. "Well, we have frightened the girl out of her wits. She was in some sort of fit."

"We ought to go and tend to her ourselves," Dierich suggested.

"Oh, yes! And scare her right into the churchyard," Jorian retorted smoothly. "Her father is a physician, and I have already summoned him to restore her senses. Shall we check the fire instead?"

His casual demeanor disarmed all suspicion. Soon, the group concurred that the kitchen of the "Three Kings" inn was far warmer than Peter's dwelling, and they departed, having first untied Martin. At the door, Dierich Brower remarked triumphantly, "Note well, friend, that I was right and the burgomaster wrong. I predicted we would arrive too late to catch him, and indeed, we were too late."

Thus, Gerard escaped the twin terrors of prison and death. How did he achieve freedom? Not through cleverly devised hiding places nor Margaret's quick wit alone, but through a sudden impulse of kindness in one of his captors—a fragment of humanity remaining in a reckless heart—aided by a desire for profit. Human motives are often so tangled and contradictory; even our shrewdest plans are frequently shortsighted. Those whose gentle natures or fortunate lives shield them from the extreme heights of joy and depths of anguish may be the happiest of mankind, though to explain the unspeakable bliss that now settled upon these persecuted lovers to such readers would be a futile task.

To watch a beloved face return from the brink of the grave to the world, regaining health and beauty through swift, miraculous gradations; to see the roses bloom once more on the cherished cheek, the spark of love return to the beloved eye, and speech flow freely from the loved mouth—this was Margaret's joy, a happiness sufficient to balance years of sorrow. For Gerard, it was awakening from a trance to find his head pillowed on Margaret's arm, to hear the woman he adored whisper new words of eloquent love, and to receive showers of tears, tender kisses, and caresses. Until this sacred moment, he had never known the depth of her affection. He thanked his enemies.

They entwined their arms sweetly, feeling that trouble and danger belonged to a distant age. They called each other husband and wife. Had they not been solemnly betrothed? Had they not stood before the altar together? Did not the blessing of the Holy Church rest upon their union, while its curse fell upon all who sought to separate them? Yet, no woman's nerves can endure such terrible strain without consequence. Presently, Margaret grew faint and sank onto Gerard's shoulder, smiling weakly but completely unstrung.

Anxious, Gerard wished to seek help, but she held him with a gentle grasp, imploring him not to leave her even for a moment. "As long as I can place my hand upon you, I feel you are safe; otherwise, I am lost," she murmured. "Foolish Gerard! Nothing ails me. I am merely weak, dearest, but oh, so happy!"

Then it was Gerard's turn to support that dear head, with its great waves of hair flowing loosely over him. He nursed her, soothing her quivering frame on his bosom with soft, encouraging words and murmurs of love. There is no charm sweeter to a manly heart than a woman's vulnerability in moments of crisis. Poor souls! They were truly happy. Tomorrow they must part, but that thought meant nothing to them in that hour. They had stared into Death's face, and all other troubles seemed as light as air. Where there is life, there is hope; where there is hope, there is joy. Was separation for a year or two significant to two such young people who had glimpsed the grave? The future appeared bright, the present was heaven. So passed those blissful hours.

Alas, their innocence faced dangers beyond prisons and graves. Now that visible threats had vanished, they were in peril from their own hearts and their inexperience.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten knew no rest throughout the long, tempestuous night. His mind was a churning sea of dread, so consumed by anxiety that he dared not join the party scouring Peter's dwelling while the heavens roared above. Only when the final rumble of thunder faded into silence did he stir. With trembling hands, he crept down the stairs, saddled his mule, and rode swiftly toward the "Three Kings" inn at Sevenbergen.

There, he found his men slumbering in every conceivable posture—some sprawled upon chairs, others atop tables, and still more curled upon the bare floor. Ghysbrecht roused them with furious shouts, demanding answers for their failure. Their responses were a disjointed tale of unsuccessful searching, punctuated by boasts of their own zealous efforts.

"Fool!" the burgomaster barked, his voice cracking with rage. "To send you out without me! My life depends on whether he is there this very moment. Did you look beneath the girl's bed?"

"No," came the reply, hesitant and sheepish. "There was simply no room for a man there."

"How can you claim such certainty unless you looked?" Ghysbrecht snarled, his face contorted with fury. "You should have crawled under her bed, pried within its depths, and prodded every panel with your knives until something gave way. Now, rise! I will teach you the true art of searching."

Dierich Brower scrambled to his feet, shaking off the dust of his fatigue. "If you locate him, call me a beast and no man," he challenged, though his eyes betrayed a flicker of doubt.

Within moments, Peter's house stood once more encircled by the burghers. The fiery old man left his mule in the care of Jorian Ketel and, accompanied by Dierich Brower and the rest, burst through the door. Inside, the air was still and silent. Not a soul stirred—not even Peter. They ascended the stairs, hearts pounding with anticipation, but the upper rooms remained vacant.

Then, abruptly, one of the men let out a piercing cry, pointing a finger through the open window of Peter's chamber. The others turned to gaze outside. There, at a short distance, walking calmly across the golden fields alongside Margaret and Martin, stood the fugitive they had hunted so desperately.

Seizing the moment, Ghysbrecht erupted in an exultant yell. He dashed down the stairs and threw himself onto his waiting mule. With a collective surge of adrenaline, he and his men spurred their horses forward, launching themselves in hot pursuit of the fleeing trio.

Gerard, shaken by the narrowness of his escape, rose before the break of dawn and roused Martin. The old soldier stared at him in astonishment, convinced that Gerard had somehow slipped away through the window during the night. When asked for counsel on how to flee the country and evade their pursuers, Martin declared there was but one safe path. "I must guide you through the vast forest to a bridle-path I know," he said. "From there, you will reach a hostelry that lends swift horses; a single day's gallop will carry you clear of Holland. But we must depart ere the villagers stir from their beds."

Peter's home lay merely a furlong and a half from the tree line. They set out immediately. Martin carried his bow and three arrows, for it was Thursday, a holy day. Gerard bore nothing but a sturdy oak staff Peter had lent him for the journey. Margaret pinned up her kirtle and farthingale against the damp road. Peter walked them as far as his garden hedge, then, with an emotion rarely seen on his face regarding passing events, bestowed his blessing upon the young man.

As the sun crested the horizon, casting golden light across the stony fields toward the woods, disaster struck. Having crossed roughly half the distance, Margaret, who had been nervously scanning their rear, let out a piercing cry. Acting on pure instinct, she turned and sprinted toward the timber, screaming in terror. Ghysbrecht and his men were in hot pursuit. Resistance would have been suicide. Martin and Gerard followed Margaret's lead. Though the pursuers gained ground, Martin shouted encouragement: "Only win the wood! Only win the wood!" Their early start gave them hope; the great trees seemed to stretch their branches like welcoming arms, their leaves forming a protective screen.

Yet, an unforeseen danger emerged. The fiery old burgomaster threw himself onto his mule, spurred it into a gallop, and rode not just his own men but directly at the fugitives. His intent was to cut them off. The old man circled in a wide arc until he reached the forest's edge, positioning himself squarely between the fleeing group and the exit. He cared little whether the others escaped; his focus was solely on Gerard.

Margaret shrieked, clutching at Gerard in a desperate attempt to shield him, but he shook her off without ceremony. In his fury, Ghysbrecht forgot that hunted beasts often turn upon their hunters, and that two men can harbor such hatred that they long to destroy the very thing they despise. Instead of dodging as the burgomaster expected, Gerard charged straight at him, issuing a savage, triumphant cry. With all his heart, soul, and strength, he swung the oak staff. It descended upon Ghysbrecht's face with a terrifying crash, knocking the old man beneath his mule's tail. The beast beat a frantic tattoo with its hooves while Ghysbrecht lay beaten, his face streaming with blood, his collar spattered red.

In an instant, the three fugitives vanished into the tree cover. The yells of dismay and vengeance erupting from Ghysbrecht's men signaled that the chase had become a race for absolute survival.

"Why run?" Gerard gasped, breathless. "You have your bow, and I have this," adding that he brandished his bloodied staff.

"Boy!" Martin roared. "The gallows! Follow me!" He plunged deeper into the woods.

Soon, a sound like a pack of hounds closing in on prey echoed through the trees. The pursuers had entered the forest and spotted them darting among the trunks. Margaret moaned and panted as she ran; Gerard gritted his teeth, gripping his staff tightly. They soon encountered a dense thicket of hazel coppice. Martin dashed inside, pushing aside the young wood as easily as one moves through standing grain. Within fifty yards, they faced four blind paths. Martin chose one.

"Bend low," he commanded. Half-creeping, they glided forward. Their route soon intersected with other twisting trails. Taking another, it initially seemed to loop back, but soon curved again, leading them to a thick pine grove where the footing was firm and the air heavy with scent. Here, there were no paths; the young firs grew so densely that visibility dropped to mere yards ahead.

After traveling some distance, Martin sat down. Having learned in war to shed all impressions of danger amidst the danger itself, he retrieved a piece of bread and a slice of ham from his wallet and calmly ate his breakfast. The younger pair watched him with dismay.

"All Sevenbergen could not find you now," Martin remarked casually. "You will lose your purse, Gerard, long before you reach Italy; is that the proper way to carry a purse?"

Gerard looked down. Entangled by its chains to the buckle and strap of his wallet hung a large triangular purse.

"This is none of mine," Gerard said. "What lies within, I wonder?" He attempted to free it, but since passing through the coppice, it had become inextricably bound to his gear.

"It seems loath to leave me," Gerard muttered, eventually cutting it loose with his knife. Upon inspection, the purse was well-stocked with silver coins of various denominations, though its swollen appearance was largely due to numerous sheets of brown paper folded and doubled inside.

A sudden realization dawned on Gerard. "Why, it must belong to that old thief! And see! Stuffed with paper to deceive the world!"

The mystery of how the burgomaster's purse ended up on Gerard solved itself quickly. It must have been slung at Ghysbrecht's saddlebow; when Gerard rushed his enemy, he had unconsciously torn it away, thus felling his foe and robbing him in a single, fatal gesture.

Gerard was delighted by this feat, but Margaret remained uneasy. "Throw it away, Gerard, or let Martin take it back. Already they call you a thief. I cannot bear the stain."

"Throw it away? Give it back? Not a stiver!" Gerard protested. "This is spoil lawfully won in battle from an enemy. Is it not, Martin?"

"Of course," Martin agreed. "Send him back the brown paper, but the purse or the coin—that would be a sin."

"Oh, Gerard!" Margaret pleaded. "You are going to a distant land. We need the goodwill of Heaven. How can we hope for that if we take what is not ours?"

But Gerard viewed the matter differently. "It is Heaven that gives it to me by a miracle, and I shall cherish it accordingly," the pious youth declared. "Just as the favored people spoiled the Egyptians and were blessed."

"Take your own way," Margaret submitted humbly. "You are wiser than I am. You are my husband," she added softly, "is it for me to gainsay you?"

These humble words from Margaret, who until that day had held the dominant position, surprised Martin momentarily. They lingered in his mind later, surprising him even less. Gerard kissed her tenderly in reward for her wife-like docility, and they continued their journey hand in hand, with Martin leading the way into the depths of the immense forest.

The further they traveled, the more secure they felt from pursuit. Indeed, the townsfolk never ventured so far into the trackless wilderness. Impetuous natures tend to repent quickly. As soon as Gerard was entirely out of immediate danger, his conscience began to prickle.

"Martin, would I had not struck quite so hard."

"Whom? Oh! Let that pass; he was cheaply served."

"Martin, I saw his grey hairs as my stick fell upon him. I doubt they will remain visible to me for long."

Martin grunted with contempt. "Who spares a badger for his grey hairs? The greyer your enemy, the older; and the older the craftier, and the craftier the better for a little killing."

"Killing? Killing, Martin? Speak not of killing!" Gerard trembled violently.

"I am much mistaken if you have not," Martin replied cheerfully.

"Now Heaven forbid!"

"The old vagabond's skull cracked like a walnut. Aha!"

"Heaven and the saints forbid it!"

"He rolled off his mule like a stone shot from a cart. I said to myself, 'There is one wiped out,'" Martin grinned ruthlessly.

Gerard fell to his knees and began praying for his enemy's life. At this, Martin lost his patience. "Here's mummery. What! You, who set yourself up for learning, do you not know that a wise man never strikes his enemy unless to kill him? And what is all this fuss about killing old men? If it had been a young one, full of the joys of life—wine, women, and pillaging! But an old fellow at the edge of the grave, why not shove him in? Go he must, today or tomorrow; and what better place for greybeards? Now, if I should ever be so misfortunate as to live as long as Ghysbrecht did, forced to walk on a mule's legs instead of Martin Wittenhaagen's, and with a back like this"—striking the wood of his bow—"instead of this"—tapping his bowstring—"I'll thank and bless any young fellow who knocks me on the head, as you have done that old shopkeeper. Malison on his memory."

"Oh, *culpa mea*! *Culpa mea*!" cried Gerard, striking his breast.

"Look there!" Martin called to Margaret scornfully

A man's courage, much like his talent, tends to flow within a narrow channel. Push him but an inch outside his accustomed bounds, and he crumbles. Martin's bravery was absolute within the limits of his experience. Having weathered countless perils throughout his rugged existence, he faced those familiar threats with Spartan stoicism, bordering on indifference. Yet, he had never been hunted by a bloodhound, nor witnessed that beast's infallible instinct thwarted by human wit. Now, a sense of the supernatural, compounded by sheer novelty, stripped his resolve bare.

After taking a few hesitant steps, Martin leaned heavily against his bow; his vitality and hope drained away. Gerard, viewing the danger as negligible because it remained distant, pressed them toward flight. "What purpose does fleeing serve?" Martin asked, his voice heavy with sorrow. "If we manage to clear the woods, we shall die cheaply. But here, close at hand, I know a place where we might die with honor."

"Alas, good Martin!" cried Gerard. "Despair not so hastily; surely there is a path to escape."

"Oh, Martin!" Margaret interjected, her voice trembling. "What if we were to separate? Gerard's life alone is forfeit. Is there no way to draw the pursuit onto us both and allow him to go free?"

"Girl, you do not understand the nature of the bloodhound," Martin replied grimly. "It does not hunt this man or that; it hunts blood itself. They have already tracked him to where Ghysbrecht fell. From the dead man's blood to the man who spilled it, that cursed beast will lead them, whether Gerard runs through an army or swims the Meuse."

Once more, he rested his weight on his bow, his head sinking low. The hound's rich, melodic bark echoed through the forest—a cry more haunting and tuneful than any ever sounded in Crete, Sparta, or Thessaly. It was strange that beauty could be so terrifying and lethal. The eye of a boa constrictor, while mesmerizing its prey, possesses a loveliness unmatched by any royal crown; it is a ruby glowing with emerald light. Yet, the deer that beholds it loses all movement, trembling until death claims it. Similarly, this sweet, mellow sound seemed to paralyze Martin Wittenhaagen. He stood bewildered, unnerved, and uncertain. Gerard was hardly better; Martin's final words had daunted him. He had struck an old man and shed his blood, and now, via that very blood, the four-legged avenger of vengeance was upon his trail. Was not the finger of God visible in this?

While the men were numbed by dread, the woman's mind raced with frantic activity. Her beloved was in mortal peril.

"Lend me your knife," she commanded Martin.

He handed it to her. "But it will be of little use in your hands," he warned.

Margaret performed a subtle act of desperation. Stepping behind Gerard, she secretly drew the blade across her forearm, allowing it to bleed freely. Stooping low, she smeared her stockings and shoes with the flowing crimson, doing so with such dexterity that neither Gerard nor Martin noticed. As the blood continued to trickle, she applied more, ensuring the trail was altered. Then, seizing the soldier's arm, she spoke urgently.

"Come, be a man!" she urged. "Let this end. Lead us to some dense thicket where numbers will not aid our enemies."

"I am going," Martin grumbled sullenly. "Hurrying serves no purpose; we cannot outrun the hound, and the cover is nearby." Turning left, he led the way, moving as one walks toward execution.

Soon, he guided them to a thick hazel copse, similar to the one that had aided their escape earlier that day.

"There," he declared. "It is only a furlong wide, but it will suffice."

"What shall we do?" Gerard asked.

"Pass through this, and wait on the other side. As they emerge straggling, shoot three, strike two on the head, and the rest will finish us off."

"Is that all you can devise?" Gerard challenged.

"That is all."

"Then, Martin Wittenhaagen, I shall take the lead, for you have lost your wits. Come, can you obey a younger man than myself?"

"Oh, yes, Martin," Margaret cried out. "Do not contradict Gerard! He is wiser than his years."

Martin offered a reluctant agreement. "Do as I see you do," Gerard instructed. Drawing his massive knife, he chopped hazel shoots close to the ground at every step, twisting them upright behind him to form a living wall among the standing trunks. Martin mimicked the action, though with a dogged, hopeless demeanor.

As they painfully traversed most of the copse, the bloodhound's deep bay grew louder, losing its musical quality and becoming stern and fierce. Margaret trembled visibly. Martin dropped to his stomach to listen.

"I hear horses' hooves," he whispered.

"No," Gerard corrected. "I suspect it is a mule. That cursed Ghysbrecht is still alive; no one else would pursue me so relentlessly."

"Never strike your enemy unless you intend to kill him," Martin said gloomily.

"I shall hit harder this time, if Heaven grants me the chance," Gerard vowed.

Finally, they emerged from the copse into an open woodland. The trees were large but widely spaced, offering no refuge. Now, mingling with the hound's bay were dozens of voices shouting and whistling.

"The entire village has come out after us," Martin stated.

"I do not care," Gerard replied calmly. "Listen, Martin. I have smoothed the track for the dog but made it rough for the men, allowing us to engage them separately. The hound will gain on the humans. As soon as it emerges from the copse, we must kill it."

"The hound? There are several of them."

"I hear only one."

"Ay! Only one barks; the others run silent. But if the leader loses the scent, another will take up the cry. There will be at least two dogs, or devils in dog-skins."

"Then we must kill two instead of one. The moment they fall, we retreat immediately back into the copse."

"That is a fine idea, Gerard," Martin admitted, finding renewed courage.

"Hush! The men are entering the wood."

Gerard issued his orders in a whisper. "Stand with your bow by the side of the copse—right there, in the ditch. I will move only a few yards to that oak tree and hide behind it. The dogs will follow me; shoot as many as you can as they exit. I will dispatch the rest as they circle around the tree."

Martin's eyes flashed with determination. They assumed their positions. The shouts and whistles drew closer, and soon the rustling of the undergrowth was audible. Every now and then, the bloodhound let out a solitary, piercing bay. It was terrible—the branches brushing nearer, the inevitable struggle for life and death approaching minute by minute, the death knell ringing out.

A trembling hand touched Gerard's shoulder, causing him to start violently, fully strung for battle.

"Martin says if we are forced to part, make for that high ash tree we entered by," the voice whispered.

"Yes! Yes! Yes! But go back, for heaven's sake! Do not come here, exposed in the open!"

She turned to flee toward Martin, but before she could reach him, a huge dog burst from the copse, standing erect for a brief moment. Margaret cowered in terror, yet the beast ignored her entirely. Scent was to him what sight was to mankind. He lowered his nose for an instant, then, with a horrific yell, launched himself straight at Gerard's hiding spot, rolling head-over-heels dead as a stone, literally impaled by an arrow from the bow twanging beside the copse in Martin's hand.

At that exact moment, a second hound emerged, sniffing its fallen comrade. Gerard lunged at it, but before he could swing his cudgel, a streak of white lightning seemed to strike the animal. It collapsed into the dust, mortally wounded but not dead, howling pitifully. Gerard lacked the time to finish it; the copse rustled too dangerously close, seeming to breathe. Gesturing wildly for Martin to retreat, Gerard sprinted a few yards to the right and crept cautiously into the thick brush just as three men burst forth. These had gained considerable ground on their companions; the rest followed at varying intervals.

Gerard crawled back almost on all fours. Instinct taught Martin and Margaret to mimic his movements along their line of retreat. Within mere yards, pursuers and pursued passed one another on opposite paths. A loud cry signaled the discovery of the dead and wounded hounds. This was followed by a babble of voices, swelling as fresh hunters arrived.

Typically, hunters waste precious time on a surprise, while the hunted maximize their advantage.

"I hear no more hounds," Martin whispered to Margaret, regaining his composure.

"It was your turn to tremble and despair," she murmured. "Oh, why did we leave Gerard? They will kill my Gerard, and I will not be near him."

"Nay, nay! The responsibility lies not on their shoulders. Did you bid him meet us at the ash tree?"

"And so I did. Praise be to you, Martin, for thinking of that. To the ash

Speech is often the vessel through which human thought flows, yet there exist emotions so primal and overwhelming that they escape the tongue entirely, erupting instead as raw, eloquent cries. In such moments, man sheds his civilized veneer, revealing himself as nothing more than a creature of instinct; for when beasts are driven to the brink, they too emit sounds of anguish or triumph, though they lack the power of articulate speech. There was something terrifyingly feral in the roar of victory that erupted from the thicket as Dierich's men emerged upon their quarry, mirrored instantly by the piercing shriek of terror that drove Gerard and the mule forward. The pursuers' hands grasped at empty air mere feet behind them as the fugitives fled for their lives. For a fleeting second, disoriented like lions whose springs go awry, Dierich and his party hesitated, allowing Gerard and the beast to gain a ten-yard advantage. Then, weapons raised, they surged forward once more. Capture seemed inevitable; this was not the first time these groups had tested their mettle against one another. On the open plain earlier that day, the pursuers had steadily closed the gap on the trio, and now, finally, the contest would be decided solely by speed. Within moments, they covered a hundred yards, yet that crucial ten-yard buffer remained intact.

This sudden acceleration baffled Dierich Brower. The explanation lay in the dynamics of movement: when three flee together, their pace is dictated by the slowest member. From Peter's house to the forest's edge, Gerard had been forced to match Margaret's slower stride. But now, unburdened by her presence, he ran at his own natural velocity, aided by a mule capable of leaving everyone else in the dust. Furthermore, the benefits of youth and a life of chastity began to manifest. Light grew perceptibly between the hunted and the hunters. Desperate to close the distance, Dierich made a final, frantic surge, gaining two yards, only for Gerard to silently reclaim the ground within seconds. Frustration boiled over; curses rang out from the pursuing ranks. Martin heard them, and a spark of hope ignited in his eyes. "Hold fast, Gerard! Courage, brave lad! Look how they scatter!" Indeed, the formation was breaking. Dierich now led by one man, while another lagged dangerously in the rear.

They ascended a long, gentle rise where endurance proved paramount. Here, the vigor of youth, sheer grit, and the strength derived from sober living outweighed experience. Before reaching the summit, the weight of Dierich's forty years pressed upon him as heavily as forty leaden bullets. "We've lost the prize," he panted, his voice ragged. "Seize him dead if you cannot take him alive!" With those words, he abandoned the chase, trailing at a walk. One by one, Jorian Ketel and the others fell back until only a single figure remained relentless—a hound of war that refused to yield, though slowly losing ground with every step. That man was Eric Wouverman.

Pressing hard behind him, the group approached a steeper, shorter rise within the woods. "Grab the reins!" Martin shouted. Gerard complied, and the mule's powerful hooves carried them upward with a speed surpassing even his previous running pace. Witnessing this maneuver, Eric's resolve crumbled. Now nearly eighty yards behind Gerard and significantly ahead of his nearest companion, he halted abruptly. Obeying Dierich's silent command, he lowered his crossbow, aimed with deliberate precision, and, just as the trio vanished over the hill's crest, released the bolt. A cry of horror tore through the air. The next instant, as if struck by lightning, the entire party collapsed onto the earth—man and beast alike, brought low by the unseen arrow.

The result was instantaneous and miraculous, leaving even the marksman momentarily stunned. He immediately summoned his companions to assist in capturing the fugitives and spurred his horse toward the hill. However, before he could reach the midway point, the figure of Martin Wittenhaagen appeared, drawn bow taut against the sky. Upon seeing Martin poised for action, Eric Wouverman instinctively dove behind a tree, shrinking into its shadow as much as possible. Martin's mastery with the crossbow was legendary, and the dead dog lying nearby served as a grim testament to his lethal precision.

Wouverman peered cautiously around the rough bark; the arrowhead remained fixed upon him, gleaming in the sunlight. Terrified, he dared not shift his position. Minutes passed in tense silence until his reinforcements arrived in full force. With a mocking laugh, Martin vanished from sight, soon followed by the clatter of hooves as he rode away on his mule. The hunters surged forward to the high ground, which offered a commanding view of a narrow, seemingly endless glade. There, far below, they spotted Gerard and Margaret sprinting desperately across the terrain, appearing no larger than gnats. Behind them thundered Martin, driving the mule at full gallop. The hunters had been bested both in cunning and in speed.

A brief explanation clarifies Martin's strategy. While cause and effect often reveal themselves through coincidence, such alignments can sometimes be deceptive. Just as a startled hare might tumble over a thicket precisely when a shot is fired—raising hopes only to vanish unharmed moments later—so too did the burgomaster's mule stumble into a rabbit hole at the exact moment the harmless crossbow bolt whistled overhead. The beast collapsed, throwing both rider and passenger to the ground. Gerard managed to catch Margaret, but her momentum dragged him down with her, scattering the castaways across the soil.

The mule rose quickly, trembling violently. Martin dismounted, glanced around, and noted that only one pursuer remained. Seizing the opportunity, he urged the young lovers to flee on foot while he held off the remaining enemy. Once he had secured his advantage, he galloped after his charges. After a grueling chase, he finally caught up to them. Instantly, he mounted Gerard and Margaret onto the mule, then ran alongside them until exhaustion forced him to stop. Taking turns riding and running in rotation, he ensured that a fresh pair of legs was always available. Consequently, long before the lovers slowed their pace, every trace of them was hopelessly lost to Dierich and his men. Defeated and crestfallen, the hunters turned back to tend to their leader and their wounded hound.

It is a truth often overlooked until necessity strikes: life and liberty, when secure, are taken for granted, their value obscured by the very fact of their presence. It is only when threatened that we truly comprehend their worth. This sentiment mirrors the nature of sunlight; we rarely pause to admire its brilliance at high noon, when it floods the sky with undifferentiated light, but rather in the late afternoon, when it fractures into golden flakes beneath the shade of ancient elms. Though weaker then, the light is rendered more precious by the encroaching gloom, revealing a fire that contrasts sharply with the dark.

Thus, Gerard and Margaret, though trembling at every rustling leaf that sounded harsher than its neighbors, radiated with a profound joy and gratitude as they drifted through the friendly woods. Safety and deep, tranquil silence surrounded them, yet the memory of barking dogs and brutal voices still echoed in their minds. Soon, however, Gerard noticed something alarming: stains of blood on Margaret's ankles.

"Martin! Martin! Help!" he cried, panic rising in his throat. "They have wounded her! The crossbow!"

"No, no!" Margaret replied, offering a reassuring smile. "I am neither wounded nor hurt at all."

"But what is it, then?" Gerard demanded, agitated. "In Heaven's name?"

"Do not scold me!" Margaret blushed softly. "Have I ever scolded you?"

"No, dear Gerard."

"Well, then," she continued gently, "Martin told me the cruel dogs were following because of the scent of blood. So, thinking that if I could offer them just a little bit of my own blood on my shoes, they might follow me instead of letting my Gerard pass freely... I scratched my arm with Martin's knife. Forgive me! Whose else could I use? Not yours, Gerard? Ah, no. Will you forgive me?" she asked, her tone beseeching, loving, and fawning all at once.

"Let me see this scratch," Gerard choked out, overcome with emotion.

"There," she showed him. "I thought so. Just a scratch?"

Gerard shuddered. "A scratch? I call it a cut—a deep, terrible, cruel cut."

"She might have done it with her bodkin," the soldier muttered, stepping forward. "Milksop! That sickens at the sight of a mere scratch and a drop of blood."

"No, no," Gerard insisted fiercely. "I could look upon a sea of blood, but not on hers. Oh, Margaret! How could you be so cruel?"
