Margaret smiled with an ineffable tenderness. "Foolish Gerard," she murmured, "to make such a fuss of nothing." She threw her guilty arm around his neck. "As if I would not give all the blood in my heart for you, let alone a few drops from my arm."

Under the weight of their recent danger, she began to weep against his neck, driven by pity and love; he wept with her.

"And I must part from her," he sobbed, the reality crashing down upon him. "We two who love so dearly—one must remain in Holland, the other in Italy. Ah me! Ah me! Ah me!"

At this, Margaret wept anew, yet her tears were patient and silent. Instinct never sleeps, and with her selflessness lay a sharp instinct. To voice her true thoughts would only deepen Gerard's agony at their separation, so she chose silence.

Suddenly, they emerged onto a well-worn path, and Martin halted.

"This is the bridle-road I mentioned," he said, hanging his head. "And there, lying ahead, is the inn."

Margaret and Gerard exchanged terrified glances.

"Come a step with me, Martin," Gerard whispered urgently.

Drawing the soldier aside, he spoke in a broken voice. "Good Martin, watch over her for me! She is my wife, yet I leave her. Look, Martin! Here is gold—it was meant for my journey. It serves no purpose for me to ask her to take it; she would refuse. But you will take it for her, will you not? Oh, Heaven! Is this all I can offer her? Money? But poverty is a curse. You will ensure she wants for nothing, dear Martin? The burgomaster's silver is sufficient for me."

"You are a good lad, Gerard," the soldier replied warmly. "Neither want nor harm shall come to her. I care for her little finger more than for all the world; and were she nothing to me, even for your sake, I would be a father to her. Go with a stout heart, and may God be with you going and returning."

The rough soldier squeezed Gerard's hand, turning his head away with an unusual display of feeling. After a brief silence, he prepared to return to Margaret, but Gerard stopped him.

"No, good Martin; pray, stay here behind this thicket and turn your head away from us, while I—Oh, Martin! Martin!"

By this ruse, Gerard avoided having a witness to his anguish at leaving the woman he loved, and Martin spared himself the sight of their pain. He saw nothing of the poor young couple kneeling to renew before Heaven those holy vows that cruel men had interrupted. He witnessed nothing of them clinging together as one entity, struggling to part, failing, returning, and embracing again like drowning, despairing creatures. He heard only Gerard's continuous sobbing and Margaret's mournful moans.

Finally, a hoarse cry pierced the air, followed by the patter of feet on the hard road. Martin sprang up to find Gerard running wildly, both hands clasped above his head in fervent prayer, while Margaret tottered backward toward him, palms extended in pitiful supplication, her cheeks ash-gray and her eyes vacant.

He caught her in his arms and offered words of comfort, but her mind could not process them. Only the sound of his voice caused her to moan and hold him tightly, trembling violently. He lifted her onto the mule, wrapping his arm around her. Supporting her frame—which had gone from the strength of a boy to a state of total relaxation and powerlessness—he carried her slowly and sadly home.

She shed not a single tear and spoke not a single word. At the edge of the wood, he helped her down from the mule and instructed her to walk across to her father's house. She obeyed immediately. Martin turned toward Rotterdam; Sevenbergen was too oppressive for him. Gerard, severed from the love of his life, moved forward like one in a dream.

He hired a horse and a guide at the small inn and rode swiftly toward the German frontier. Yet, everything felt mechanical; his senses seemed dulled. Trees, houses, and people passed him like objects viewed through a thick veil. His companions spoke to him twice, receiving no answer. Only once did he cry out savagely, "Shall we never escape this hateful country?"

After many hours of riding, they reached the crest of a steep hill, with a small brook flowing at the bottom.

"Halt!" the guide shouted, pointing across the valley. "Here is Germany."

"Where?" Gerard asked, bewildered.

"On the other side of the stream. No need to ride down the hill, I think."

Gerard dismounted without a word and drew the burgomaster's purse from his belt. As he opened it, the guide remarked, half-sulkily, "You will soon be out of this detestable country. Perhaps the one you are heading to won't treat you any better anyway. Still, even if it is a church you have robbed, they cannot touch you once you cross that boundary."

Such words, at another time, would have merited a stern rebuke or a slap. Now, they fell upon Gerard like idle wind. He paid the lad in silence and descended the hill alone.

The brook was silvery, murmuring over tiny pebbles that glittered, varnished by the clear water. Gerard sat down and stared stupidly at them. Then he drank from the stream and washed his hot feet and hands in the icy water; the shock woke him. Rising, he took a run and leaped across the stream into Germany.

Even as his foot touched the strange soil, he turned abruptly and looked back.

"Farewell, ungrateful country!" he cried. "But for her, it would cost me nothing to leave you forever, along with all my family and kin—and the mother who bore me, and my playmates, and my little native town. Farewell, fatherland—welcome, wide world! *Omne solum fortippatra*."

With these brave words on his lips, he suddenly collapsed, arms and legs weak, sitting down to sob bitterly upon the foreign earth.

When the young exile had sat thus for some time, bowed down in grief, he rose and wiped the tears from his eyes like a man steeling himself. Casting not a single glance behind him to weaken his resolve, he stepped out into the vast unknown. His love and heavy sorrow left no room for petty doubts. Compared with the tearing apart of his soul from Margaret, walking on foot to Italy in that rough age seemed a trivial matter. All nations meet within a convent. Thanks to his good friends the monks and his own insatiable thirst for knowledge, he could speak most of the languages required for that long journey.

"I will soon reach Rome," he told himself. "The sooner, the better."

After walking a good league, he arrived at a junction where four winding country roads met, confusing many an inexperienced neighbor traveling between villages. Gerard took out the small dial Peter had

Gerard continued along the road until he encountered a small gathering. Two men in somber attire leaned casually against a horse, chatting idly. The rider, clad in a silk doublet and a vibrant green jerkin and hose of English make—glossy as polished leather—lay prone in the afternoon sun, resembling a massive lizard. His velvet cloak, a blazing yellow, was meticulously draped over the horse's flanks.

"Is anything wrong?" Gerard asked.

"Not that I know of," one of the servants replied.

"But your master lies like a corpse. Do you not feel shame letting him grovel on the ground?"

"Nonsense," the servant retorted. "The bare earth is the finest remedy for his condition. If you remain sober in bed, you wake with a splitting headache; but rising from the hard ground makes you soar like a lark in spring. Does Ulric agree?"

"He speaks truly, young sir," Ulric answered earnestly.

"What, is the gentleman intoxicated?"

The servants erupted in a rough laugh at the simplicity of Gerard's query. Suddenly, Ulric halted, scanning Gerard from head to toe before speaking with grave seriousness. "Who are you, and where born, that you do not know the Count is perpetually drunk at this hour?" Gerard immediately found himself under suspicion.

"I am a stranger," Gerard explained, "yet a truthful man who loves learning; therefore, I ask questions, not out of curiosity."

"If you are indeed a true man," Ulric said shrewdly, "then offer us a trinket for the knowledge we have shared."

Gerard looked confused but maintained a pleasant demeanor. "A trinket you shall receive, whatever my slender purse can spare, provided you explain why you removed his cloak from the man and placed it upon the beast."

Inspired by the prospect of payment, the two men instantly offered Gerard two theories. First, if the Count regained consciousness—which, being a seasoned drinker, he often did quickly—and found his horse sweating in the cold while his cloak lay idle nearby, he would undoubtedly curse and perhaps strike out. Second, and more pretentiously, a horse is a simpleton that drinks only water; it must be coaxed and kept warm externally. A master, however, is always filled with good beer, possessing an internal heat that warms him to the skin, rendering a cloak a mere symbol of vain ostentation.

Each speaker fell in love with their own theory. Both had clearly caught a bit of the madness that had infected their master's mind. Their voices soon rose so high that the green-faced sot began to growl instead of snore. Ignoring the disturbance, they continued their debate until the situation escalated.

Hans, holding the bridle with his right hand, delivered a solid cuff to Ulric with his left. Ulric returned the gesture with equal enthusiasm, his right hand free. They exchanged blows over the horse's mane, pounding one another and jostling the poor animal until it backed away and stepped heavily onto the green nobleman. Like a toad stung by a magical spear, the Count jumped up screaming, clutching his injured hand with one grip and reaching for his hilt with the other. Terrified, the servants released the reins. The horse galloped off whinnying, pursued by the men shouting in fear, while the green nobleman chased them, hurling curses, brandishing his naked sword, and bouncing wildly from hedge to hedge down the narrow lane.

Amidst this chaos, Gerard turned his back on everyone and calmly headed south. He was relieved to have saved the four tin farthings he had prepared as payment, though his heart remained too heavy to smile at their drunken folly.

The sun was nearing its setting, and Gerard, who had hoped in vain to find an inn, felt increasingly uneasy. Black clouds began to gather overhead. He quickened his pace, almost running. It was futile; torrential rain descended, soaking the bewildered traveler and seemingly extinguishing the fading sunlight.

Gerard trudged onward, dark, wet, and lost in an unfamiliar region. "Fool!" he muttered. "To leave Margaret."

Darkness thickened around him as he entered a vast forest. Massive branches blocked the narrow path, forcing the benighted stranger to navigate what felt like an endless, ink-black cave with a rugged floor. He stumbled repeatedly. Onward he went, with shivering limbs, an empty stomach, and a failing heart, until wolves rose from their dens and bayed around the woods.

His hair bristled, but he gripped his cudgel, preparing to defend his life. There was no wind, yet his agitated ears heard light footsteps patter over the fresh leaves and heard low branches rustle as creatures slipped swiftly past.

Suddenly, amidst the sea of darkness, a great fiery star appeared close to the ground. He hailed it as if it were his patron saint. "CANDLE! A CANDLE!" he shouted, attempting to run toward it. But the dark, treacherous path hindered him. The light proved farther away than expected. Finally, in the very heart of the forest, he spotted a house with lit candles and loud voices inside. He looked upward for a signboard, finding none. "Not an inn after all!" he sighed sadly. Yet, thinking, "What Christian would turn a dog out into this wood tonight?", he approached the door leading to the voices.

He opened it slowly and peered in timidly. Then, as if slapped, he withdrew abruptly, recoiling into the rain and darkness.

He had glimpsed a large, low room dominated by a huge round stove or clay oven reaching to the ceiling. Wet clothes hung to dry around it—some on lines, others more simply on rustic racks. These garments, soaked with the day's moisture and stained by travel, combined with the dampness of the season, emitted rank vapors and indescribable odors in steaming clouds.

In one corner sat a traveling family, large in number, emitting a peculiar, sickly smell of neglected children. Garlic permeated the air. With windows shut and the central furnace radiating intense heat, the breath of at least forty persons filled the space.

They had just finished supper.

Like many artists, Gerard possessed sensitive senses, and the powerful effluvia struck him with dismay. However, the relentless rain lashed him outside, while the light and fire tempted him inward.

Unable to push through the palpable perfumes all at once, he returned to the doorway repeatedly, like a singed moth. Eventually, he discerned that the various smells did not fully mix; no demon stirred them. The odor of the family dominated two corners; stewed rustic food reigned supreme in the center; and garlic prevailed among the noisy group by the window. He noted, through hasty analysis, that the garlic occupied the smallest sphere of influence, while the scent of the reeking rustic darted farthest—a flavor as if ancient goats or the ancestors of all foxes had been dragged through a river and dried by Nebuchadnezzar.

Creeping into a corner near the door, Gerard found some relief, though the solidity of the main fetors only partially isolated him. The heat and reeking vapors circulated, causing the walls to drip. The inexperienced novice felt something akin to a cold snake winding around his legs, his head turning to a heavy lump of lead. Next, he felt as though he were choking, sweetly slumbering, and dying all at once.

On the verge of swooning, he recovered with a profound sense of disgust and discouragement, resolving to return to Holland at dawn. Gathering a little courage despite his faintness, he asked one of the men smelling strongly of garlic if this was indeed an inn.

"Where do you come from, knowing not 'The Star of the Forest'?" was the reply.

"I am a stranger; in my country, inns always bear signs."

"Droll country yours! What need of a sign for a public house—a place known to every soul?"

Too tired and faint for argument, Gerard changed the subject, asking where he could find the landlord. At this fresh display of ignorance, the native's contempt rose beyond words. He pointed to a middle-aged woman seated opposite the oven and informed his companions of the outlander's presence. Immediately, the loud voices ceased one by one as the news spread. Each eye turned, pivoting silently and zoologically to follow Gerard and his movements.

The landlady sat on a chair slightly elevated above the rest, between two bundles. From the start, a huge pile of feathers and wings, she had been extracting downy plumes from quills, filling a second bundle that littered the floor ankle-deep, contributing to the room's stuffy, malarial atmosphere. Gerard asked if he could have something to eat.

She opened her eyes in astonishment. "Supper ended over an hour ago."

"But I had none, good dame."

"Is that my fault? You were welcome to your share for me."

"But I was benighted, a stranger, and sorely delayed against my will."

"What does that concern me? Everyone knows 'The Star of the Forest' serves supper from six to eight. Come before six, you eat well; come before eight, you eat as Heaven pleases; come after eight, you get a clean bed, a stirrup cup, or a horn of cow's milk at dawn."

Gerard looked blank. "May I go to bed, then, dame?" he asked sulkily. "For it is ill sitting up wet and fasting, and the saying goes, 'He sups who sleeps.'"

"The beds have not arrived yet," the landlady

Denys caught at Gerard, halting his fall, though it is doubtful whether this alone would have spared him a broken neck or shattered limb. His salvation came instead from the dying bear, whose hairy carcass bore the weight of Denys's head and shoulders. Denys tore himself free, but it was futile; the beast still panted, her limbs trembling, yet she was no match for a hare, and soon she breathed her last. The sensible Denys propped Gerard up against the soft corpse, fanning him until consciousness returned. Gerard stirred, confused, feeling the bear around him, and rolled away, screaming.

"Courage!" cried Denys. "The devil is dead."

"Is it dead? Quite dead?" Gerard asked from behind a tree. His courage was feverish, and a chill had seized him, lingering for some time.

"Behold," said Denys, pulling the brute's ear playfully, opening her jaws, and inserting his head within them amidst other insulting antics. In the midst of this, Gerard vomited violently.

Denys laughed at him. "What is the matter now? Also, why tumble off your perch just when we had won the day?"

"I swooned, I trow."

"But why?"

Receiving no answer, Denys continued, "Green girls faint as soon as they look at you, but then they choose their time and place. What woman ever fainted up a tree?"

"She sent her nasty blood all over me. I think the smell must have overpowered me! Faugh! I hate blood."

"I do believe it potently."

"See what a mess she has made of me."

"But with her blood, not yours. I pity the enemy that strives to satisfy you."

"You need not brag, Master Denys; I saw you under the tree, the color of your shirt."

"Let us distinguish," said Denys, blushing. "It is permitted to tremble for a friend."

In answer, Gerard threw his arms around Denys's neck in silence.

"Look here," whined the stout soldier, moved by this sudden display of youthful affection. "Was ever anything so like a woman? I love thee, little milksop—go on. Good! Behold him on his knees now. What new caprice is this?"

"Oh, Denys, ought we not to return thanks to Him who has saved both our lives against such fearful odds?" Gerard knelt and prayed aloud. Presently he found Denys kneeling quietly beside him, his hands crossed over his bosom in the fashion of his nation, with a face as long as his arm. When they rose, Gerard's countenance was beaming.

"Good Denys," he said, "Heaven will reward thy piety."

"Ah, bah! I did it out of politeness," said the Frenchman. "It was to please thee, little one. *C'est égal*, 'twas well and orderly prayed, and edified me to the core while it lasted. A bishop had scarce handled the matter better; so now our evensong being sung, and the saints enlisted with us—*marchons*."

Before taking two steps, he stopped. "By-the-by, the cub!"

"Oh, no, no!" cried Gerard.

"You are right. It is late. We have lost time climbing trees, tumbling off them, swooning, vomiting, and praying; and the brute is heavy to carry. And now I think on't, we shall have Papa after it next; these bears make such a coil about an odd cub. What is this? You are wounded! You are wounded!"

"Not I."

"He is wounded; miserable that I am!"

"Be calm, Denys. I am not touched; I feel no pain anywhere."

"You? You only feel when another is hurt," cried Denys, with great emotion. Throwing himself on his knees, he examined Gerard's leg with glistening eyes.

"Quick! Quick! Before it stiffens," he cried, hurrying him on.

"Who makes the coil about nothing now?" inquired Gerard composedly.

Denys's reply was indirect. "Be pleased to note," said he, "that I have a bad heart. You were man enough to save my life, yet I must sneer at you, a novice in war. Was not I a novice once myself? Then you fainted from a wound, and I thought you swooned for fear, calling you a milksop. Briefly, I have a bad tongue and a bad heart."

"Denys!"

"Plait-il?"

"You lie."

"You are very good to say so, little one, and I am eternally obliged to you," mumbled the remorseful Denys.

Before they had walked many furlongs, the muscles of the wounded leg contracted and stiffened until Gerard could barely touch the ground with his toe, doing so with immense pain. Finally, he could bear it no longer.

"Let me lie down and die," he groaned. "This is intolerable."

Denys argued that it was afternoon and the nights were now frosty; that cold and hunger were ill companions, and that it would be unreasonable to lose heart, especially since a certain great personage was notoriously deceased. Thus, Gerard leaned upon his axe and hobbled on, but presently he gave in completely, sinking helpless into the road.

Denys drew him aside into the wood. To Gerard's surprise, Denys gave him his crossbow and bolts, enjoining him strictly to lie quiet. If any ill-looking fellows found him and approached, he was to bid them keep aloof; if they refused, to shoot them dead at twenty paces. "Honest men keep the path; and knaves in a wood, none but fools do parley with them." With this, Denys snatched up Gerard's axe and set off running—not toward Düsseldorf, as Gerard expected, but back along the road they had come.

Gerard lay aching and smarting. To him, Rome, which seemed so near at the start, now appeared far, far off, despite being two hundred miles closer. Yet soon all his thoughts turned toward Sevenbergen. How sweet it would be one day to hold Margaret's hand and tell her all he had endured for her! The mere thought of her soothed him, and amidst the pain and nerve irritation, he lay resigned, smiling sweetly though faintly.

He had lain thus for more than two hours when suddenly there were shouts. The next moment, something struck a nearby tree hard and quivered within it.

He looked; it was an arrow.

He sprang to his feet. Several missiles rattled among the branches, and the wood echoed with battle cries. Whence they came he could not tell, for noises in these vast woods are so reverberated that a stranger is always at fault regarding their origin; but they seemed to fill the entire air. Presently there was a lull, then the fierce gallop of hooves, followed by louder shouts and cries mingled with shrieks and groans. Above all, strange and terrible sounds, like fierce claps of thunder, bellowed loudly before dying off in cracking echoes. Red tongues of flame shot out intermittently among the trees, and clouds of sulfurous smoke drifted over his head. Then all was still.

Gerard was struck with awe. "What will become of Denys?" he cried. "Oh, why did you leave me? Oh, Denys, my friend! My friend!"

Just before sunset, Denys returned, almost sinking under a hairy bundle—the bear's skin.

Gerard welcomed him with a burst of joy that astonished him. "I thought never to see you again, dear Denys. Were you in the battle?"

"No. What battle?"

"The bloody battle of men, or fiends, that raged in the wood a while ago," Gerard said, describing it vividly, more fully than the narrator has done.

Denys patted him indulgently on the back. "It is well," he said. "Thou art a good liminer; and fever is a great spur to the imagination. One day I lay in a cart-shed with a cracked skull and saw two hosts maneuver and fight a good hour on eight feet square, which I fairly described to my comrade in due order, only not so gorgeously as thou, for want of book learning."

"What, then, you believe me not? When I tell you the arrows whizzed over my head, and the combatants shouted, and—"

"May the foul fiends fly away with me if I believe a word of it."

Gerard took his arm and quietly pointed to a tree close by. "Why, it looks like—it is—a broad arrow, as I live!" He went close and looked up at it. "It came out of the battle. I heard it, and saw it."

"An English arrow."

"How know you that?"

"Marry, by its length. The English bowmen draw the bow to the ear; others only to the right breast. Hence the English loose a three-foot shaft, and this is one of them, perdition seize them! Well, if this is not glamour, there has been a trifle of a battle. And if there has been a battle in so ridiculous a place for a battle as this, why then 'tis no business of mine, for my Duke hath no quarrel hereabouts. So let's to bed," said the professional. With this, he scraped together a heap of leaves and made Gerard lie on them, his axe by his side. He then lay down beside him

The following afternoon found Gerard no longer dizzy, though he was plagued by irritability and strange whims. In one such mood, he demanded a lemon to suck upon. Denys, transformed from a rough soldier into a doting grandfather figure by their shared bond, rose quickly. "Do not worry," he assured him, promising lemons within moments. He rushed out to scour the local shops. Lemons were rare in the north then, and his search took a long time. Gerard grew increasingly impatient until the door finally creaked open.

However, it was not Denys who entered. Instead, a formidable figure glided softly inside: an elderly gentleman clad in a long, sober gown trimmed with rich fur, wearing cherry-colored hose and pointed shoes. A sword hung at his side in a fine leather sheath, and around his neck sat a ruff, starched so severely it was held rigid by hidden wooden stays. On his head rested a four-cornered cap bordered with fur, and a magnificent white beard framed his chin and bosom. Gerard instantly recognized the visitor's profession; barring the weapon, this was the full ceremonial attire of a physician. Trailing behind him was a boy carrying a basket brimming with phials, lint, and surgical instruments.

The old doctor approached the bedside gently, asking in a hushed tone, "How fares my son?"

Gerard replied gratefully that his wound caused little pain, though his throat felt parched and his head heavy.

"A wound!" the doctor exclaimed, feigning surprise. "They told me nothing of that. Let me inspect it. Yes, yes—a clean, deep bite. The mastiff certainly had sharp teeth capable of such a feat." His sympathy seemed to drift entirely toward the imaginary beast he had conjured. "This requires immediate cauterization, or you will find yourself unable to drink water, tumbling somersaults in bed. It is the season of rabid dogs; one has already done your business, but we shall thwart him. Boy, heat the iron."

"But, sir," Gerard protested weakly, "'twas no dog, but a bear."

"A bear!" the senior physician remonstrated sternly. "Think before you speak! It is disrespectful to joke with a healer who brings his gray hair and lifelong studies to mend you. A bear, you say? Had you dissected as many bears as I have—or even a tenth—you would know that no bear's jaw creates such a trivial wound. I tell you it was a dog. Since you force my hand, I deny it was even a large one, but merely one of those furious curs running rampant today, biting legs and bringing men low unless stopped by my blade and cautery."

"Alas, sir!" Gerard corrected him, "When did I say a bear's *jaw*? I said 'bear'; it was his *paw*, now."

"And why did you not tell me immediately?"

"Because you kept insisting otherwise."

"Never hide anything from your leech, young man," the doctor continued, proving himself a skilled speaker but a terrible listener. "Well, this is unfortunate. Claws of tigers, panthers, badgers, cats, and bears, along with deer horns and human fingernails—especially children's—are steeped in deadly poison. You would have been safer bitten by a stray cur than gored by a bull or scratched by a bear. Nevertheless, I shall apply a soothing poultice to your leg. Meanwhile, keep the body cool. Stick out your tongue—good! Fever. Let me check your pulse—good! Fever. I order bloodletting, and immediately."

"Bloodletting! Humph! Very well. If it guarantees a cure, I shall not remain idle." The doctor assured him that the procedure was infallible, particularly in this instance.

"Hans, fetch the necessary items, and I shall explain the reasoning to the patient."

The physician then lectured Gerard on the nature of disease, claiming that blood becomes hot, disturbed, and poisonous. Removing this corrupt fluid allowed Nature to replace it with pure substance. Therefore, bleeding acted as both a coolant and a purifier, curing all ailments because all diseases were essentially fevers, regardless of what other practitioners claimed.

"Do not suppose," he said earnestly, "that mere bleeding suffices. Any barber can cut a vein (though not all can stitch it closed). True art lies in knowing exactly which vein to drain for a specific illness. Recently, a man suffered from severe earache. I bled his right thigh, and his pain vanished instantly. Regrettably, he passed away shortly after. Another arrived with toothache; I bled behind his ear, relieving him immediately. He too has since died. I bled our bailiff between the thumb and forefinger for rheumatism. Soon afterward, he returned complaining of headaches and ringing in his ears, holding his hand over a basin. I smiled at his folly, bled his left ankle against his will, and cleared his head completely."

Branching off into enthusiastic tangents, the revered teacher continued:

"Know, young man, that two schools of medicine currently vie for dominance across Europe. The Arabian school, rooted in the ancient oracles of Avicenna, Rhazes, and Albucasis, revived by Chauliac and Lanfranc; and the Greek school, championed by modern figures like Bessarion, Platinus, and Marsilius Ficinus, whose original masters were medicine's supreme prophets: Apollo, Chiron, Asclepius, and his sons Podalinus and Machaon, alongside Pythagoras, Democritus, Praxagoras—who discovered the arteries—and Diocles, 'who first gave the soul to urine.' These teachings were oral until Hippocrates, the eighteenth descendant of Asclepius, appeared. From him we possess manuscripts; to him we owe the concept of the 'vital principle.' He invented the bandage, tapped chests for water, and above all, performed dissections—though only on quadrupeds, as pagan prejudice forbade cutting the human body. He was followed by Aristotle, who identified the aorta, the largest vessel in the human body."

"Certainly, sir," Gerard objected humbly, "the Almighty gave us all that exists within our bodies, not Aristotle or any Greek scholar."

"Child! Of course He created the organ," the doctor retorted, flushing slightly. "But Aristotle gave it its name. Yet, youths always chatter. The next great luminary was Galen, who studied in Alexandria, the center of science. Dissatisfied with quadrupeds, he dissected apes, believing them closer to man, and bled like a Trojan. Then came Theophilus, who revealed the nerves, lacteal vessels, and pia mater."

This troubled Gerard. "I cannot lie still hearing mortals claim they bestowed upon Adam the very parts God formed from clay and breathed into his sons."

"Is there such perversity?" the doctor snapped, his color rising. "Who truly donates a thing to man: the one who planted it secretly in the dark recesses of the body, or the learned sage who reveals it to the intellect, thereby enriching the mind? Understanding is your only true possession. Is that answer sufficient?"

"I am silenced, sir."

"That is preferable; garrulous patients are difficult to cure, especially when febrile. I maintain that Eristratus discovered the cerebral nerves and milk vessels; indeed, he invented lithotomy. Then came another whom I forget; you disturb me with your petty objections. Next was Ammonius, author of lithotrity. Here comes Hans with the basin—to curb your verbosity. Blow the charcoal, boy, and bring the basin. Excellent. Arabs, you say? What are they but a sect of yesterday who, around the year 1000, stumbled upon Greek writings and misread them, lacking their own light? Their demi-god, camel-driver Mahound—an impostor in science as in religion—strictly forbade them anatomy, even of lower animals. To separate medicine from anatomy is to remove the sun from the world, as Cicero said. Do not marvel at my fervor, good youth; when public welfare is at risk, a little warmth is civic, humane, and honorable. Lately, a pestilential Arabist has settled here, a mere empiric who despises anatomy, barely distinguishes Greek from Hebrew, yet has stolen half my patients. I tremble for the rest. Extend your ankle, and you, Hans, fan the charcoal."

Just then, Denys entered with the lemons, looking bewildered. "What sport is this?" he asked, raising his eyebrows.

Gerard blushed slightly and explained that the learned doctor intended to bleed and cauterize him—that was all.

"Yes! Indeed; and what is that imp blowing hot coals for?"

"For what else," the doctor addressed Gerard, "than to cauterize the vein once opened and the poisonous blood released? It is the only safe method. Avicenna recommends tying the vein, but he offers no instructions on how, nor did he know himself, nor any of the descendants of Ishmael. I have no faith in such tricky expedients. Take this as a safe principle: 'Whatever an Arab or Arabist says is correct, must be wrong.'"

"Oh, I see now what it is for," Denys remarked. "Are you so simple as to allow him to press hot iron against your living flesh? Have you ever kept your little finger near a candle flame for more than ten moments? This will last minutes. Are you content to burn in purgatory after death? Must you purchase a foret

The worthy physician returned home and declared to his housekeeper that he suffered agonizing pain from "a severe burn." These were his chosen words. In the realm of phlogiston, as in all else, we often cauterize our neighbor's digits while burning our own fingers. His housekeeper applied a soothing remedy, mild as milk. He submitted to her treatment like a lamb, his sole objective being recovery; meanwhile, he drafted his bill for the shattered phials and arranged immediately for the travelers' imprisonment. Before a magistrate, he swore an oath that these strangers, owing him money, had plotted instant flight from the township. Alas, it proved to be his unluckiest day. His sincere desire and honest endeavor to commit perjury were thwarted by a circumstance he had never foreseen, nor indeed deemed possible. He had spoken the truth. And IN AN AFFIDAVIT!

When the officers arrived at "The Silver Lion," they discovered the birds had flown. Descending to the river, they learned from local informants and launched a hot pursuit up the bank. This brief escape owed everything to Denys's good sense and keen observation. After a peal of laughter—so cordial it was a pleasure to hear—and after venting his watchword three times, Denys abruptly grew grave.

"Gerard Dusseldorf is no place for us," he stated firmly. "That old fellow went off unnaturally silent for such a chatterbox. We are strangers here, and the bailiff is his friend. Within five minutes, we shall lie in a dungeon for assaulting a Dusseldorf dignitary. Are you strong enough to hobble to the water's edge? It is nearby. Once there, you merely lie down in a boat instead of a bed; what is the odds?"

"The odds, Denys?" Gerard replied eagerly. "Untold, and entirely in favor of the boat. I pine for Rome; Rome is my road to Sevenbergen. Then we shall lie in the boat, but ON the Rhine—the famous Rhine! The cool, refreshing Rhine. I feel its breezes already; the mere sight will cure a little hop-o'-my-thumb fever like mine. Away! Away!"

Seeing his excitable friend in this mood, Denys hastily settled accounts with the landlord, and they hurried toward the river. Inquiry revealed to their dismay that the public boat had departed an hour prior, and no others would launch that afternoon. However, by asking numerous questions and gathering a crowd, they secured an offer of a private boat from an elderly man and his two sons. This arrangement was duly ridiculed by a bystander.

"The current is too strong for three oars," the critic noted.

"Then my comrade and I will help row," the invalid offered.

"No need," the old man replied. "Bless your silly heart, he owns the other boat."

A powerful breeze blew directly from astern. The boatmen set a broad sail and began rowing as well, propelling the vessel at a spanking pace.

"Are you feeling better, lad, for the river breeze?" someone asked.

"Much better. But indeed, the doctor did me good."

"The doctor? You wouldn't accept any of his cures."

"No, but I mean—you will say I am nothing—but knocking the old fool down somehow soothed me."

"Amiable dove! How thy little character opens more and more every day, like a rosebud. I read thee all wrong at first."

"Nay, Denys, mistake me not. I trust I bore with his idle threats, though in sooth his voice pierced my poor ears. Yet he was an infidel, or next door to one, and such I have been taught to abhor. Did he not as good as say we owed our inward parts to men with long Greek names, and not to Him, whose name is but a syllable, yet whose hand is over all the earth? Pagan!"

"So you knocked him down forthright—like a good Christian."

"Now, Denys, you will still be jesting. Do not take an ill man's part. Had it been a thunderbolt from Heaven, he would have met his due; yet he took but a sorry bolster from this weak arm."

"What weak arm?" Denys inquired, with twinkling eyes.

"I have lived among arms, and by Samson's hairy power, I never saw one more like a catapult. The bolster wrapped around his nose, and the two ends kissed behind his head. His forehead resounded, and had he been Goliath or Julius Caesar instead of an old quacksalver, down he would have gone. St. Denys, guard me from such feeble opposites as thou! And above all, from their weak arms—you diabolical young hypocrite."

The river wound in many turns, occasionally bringing the wind against their side rather than from astern. Then they all shifted to the weather side to prevent the boat from heeling dangerously. All except a child of about five years old, the grandson of the boatman and his darling. This urchin had slipped aboard at the moment of departure; being too light to affect the boat's trim, he existed above, or rather below, the laws of navigation.

They sailed merrily on, little conscious that they were pursued by a whole posse of constables armed with the bailiff's writ, and that their pursuers were closing in. For if the wind was strong, so was the current. Suddenly, Gerard remembered that this was a fine route to Rome, but not to Burgundy.

"Oh, Denys," he said, with an almost alarmed look, "this is not your road."

"I know it," Denys replied quietly. "But what can I do? I cannot leave thee until the fever leaves thee; and it remains with thee, for thou art both red and white by turns. I have watched thee. I must e'en go on to Cologne, I doubt, and then strike across."

"Thank Heaven," Gerard exclaimed joyfully. He added eagerly, with a touch of self-deception, "'Twere a sin to be so near Cologne and not see it. Oh, man, it is a vast and ancient city such as I have often dreamed of, but ne'er had the good luck to witness. Me miserable, by what hard fortune do I come to it now? Well then, Denys," he continued, less warmly, "it is old enough to have been founded by a Roman lady in the first century of grace, sacked by Attila the barbarian, and afterwards sore defaced by the Norman Lothaire. And it possesses a church for every week in the year, besides chapels and churches innumerable, convents, and nunneries. Above all, there is the stupendous Minster, yet unfinished, and therein, within their own chapel, lie the Three Kings who brought gifts to our Lord: Melchior with gold, Gaspar with frankincense, and Balthazar, the black king, who brought myrrh. Over their bones stands the shrine, the wonder of the world. It is of ever-shining brass, brighter than gold, studded with images fairly wrought and inlaid with exquisite devices, brave with colors. Two broad stripes run to and fro, of jewels so great and rare, each might adorn a crown or ransom its wearer at need. Upon it stand the three kings curiously counterfeited: two in solid silver, richly gilt; these are bareheaded. But he of Ethiopia, ebony-skinned, bears a golden crown. And in the midst, our Blessed Lady, in virgin silver, holds Christ in her arms. At the corners, in golden branches, four goodly waxen tapers burn night and day. Holy eyes have watched and renewed that light unceasingly for ages, and holy eyes shall watch them in saecula. I tell thee, Denys, the oldest song, the oldest Flemish or German legend, found them burning, and they shall light the earth to its grave. And there is St. Ursula's Church, a British saint's, where lie her bones and all the other virgins, her fellows. Eleven thousand were they who died for the faith, being put to the sword by barbarous Moors on the twenty-third day of October, two hundred and thirty-eight. Their bones are piled in the vaults, and many of their skulls are in the church. St. Ursula's is in a thin golden case and stands on the high altar, but shown to humble Christians only on solemn days."

"Eleven thousand virgins!" cried Denys. "What babies German men must have been in days of yore. Well, would all their bones might turn to flesh again, and their skulls to sweet faces, as we pass through the gates. 'Tis odds but some of them are wearied of their estate by this time."

"Tush, Denys!" said Gerard. "Why wilt thou, being good, still make thyself seem evil? If thy wishing-cap be on, pray that we may meet the meanest she of all those wise virgins in the next world, and to that end let us reverence their holy dust in this one. And then there is the Church of the Maccabees, and the cauldron in which they and their mother Solomona were boiled by a wicked king for refusing to eat swine's flesh."

"Oh, peremptory king! And pig-headed Maccabees! I had eaten bacon with my pork liver rather than change places at the fire with my meat."

"What scurvy words are these? It was their faith."

"Nay, bridle thy choler, and

Good and evil possess a strange, universal tendency to balance one another. The steel bow of the arbalest strapped to the rescuer's back had nearly drowned him; yet, because of its position, it fell directly into Gerard's grasp—one hand clutching the limb, the other wedged securely between the bow and the cord. With a desperate, jerking pull, Denys was hauled from the depths until half his torso broke the surface.

"Now, grip me not! Grip me not!" Gerard screamed, terrified of the fatal error of letting go. "Pas si bete," Denys gurgled through the water. Realizing the strength of his companion, Gerard's fear instantly transformed into hope and command.

"On thy back!" he ordered sharply. Seizing the heavy weapon, he stroked forward, aiding the swimmer's ascent. "Hand on my shoulder! Slap the water with the other—no, use a downward motion. Do exactly as I bid."

Gerard had seized Denys's long hair, twisting it firmly and biting the end between his teeth. Using the powerful muscles of his youthful neck, he held the soldier's head aloft while striking out vigorously against the current. For a fleeting moment, he hesitated, unsure which direction to take, unaware of the terrible significance of that choice. Then, spotting the western bank slightly closer, he steered toward it rather than drifting helplessly downstream toward prison. Thus, he created a new adventure.

Driven by the river's force, they angled heavily. Covering nearly a hundred yards, Denys murmured anxiously, "How much longer?"

"Courage," rumbled Gerard. "Whatever a duck knows, a Dutchman knows; you are as safe as in bed."

Suddenly, they found themselves in shallow water and waded onto solid ground. Once on terra firma, they stared at one another from head to toe as if eyes alone could consume them. Then, in a single impulse, they threw arms around each other's necks, panting with hearts too full for words. In that sacred moment, life tasted sweeter than heaven, especially for the exiled lover who had just saved his friend. Oh, the joy to whose heights has any poet yet soared? To save a human life—and specifically, a beloved one—is worth living for, yes, sixty years and ten.

Calmer now, they joined hands and walked along the bank like sweethearts, scarcely caring where they went. The boatmen remained safe on their overturned vessel, Herr Turnip-face, the "Inverter of things," standing in the center. The chaos seemed to have done nothing to disrupt his peculiar habits; he greeted them as they entered the Rhine and again as they emerged.

"Shall we wait for them to right the boat?"

"No, Denys. Our fare is paid; we owe them nothing. Let us move briskly."

Denys agreed, noting they could walk the entire way to Cologne along this bank.

"I am not going to Cologne," came the calm reply.

"Why, then, whither?"

"To Burgundy."

"To Burgundy? Ah, no! That is too good to be true."

"It is true, and sensible besides. What matters to me how I travel to Rome?"

"Nay, nay; you say that merely to please me. The change is too sudden. Think not me so ill-hearted as to deceive you. Did I not see your eyes sparkle at the wonders of Cologne—the churches, the images, the relics?"

"How dull art thou, Denys. That was when we were to enjoy them *together*. Churches! I shall see plenty, wherever I go. The bones of saints and martyrs... alas, the world is full of them. But a friend like thee, where on earth's face shall I find another? No, I will not turn thee farther from the road leading to thy dear home, nor to her who pines for thee. Nor will I rob myself of thee by leaving you behind. Since I drew you from the Rhine, I love thee better than before. Thou art my pearl; I fished thee, and I must keep thee. Gainsay me not, or thou shalt bring back my fever. Cry courage, lead on, and hey for Burgundy!"

Denys gave a joyful leap. "Courage! Va pour la Bourgogne! Oh, soyez tranquille! Cette fois il est bien décidément mort, ce coquin-là."

Turning their backs on the Rhine, they began their journey. As this decision crystallized, a commotion arose among the group watching from the opposite bank. They had not taken many steps before a voice echoed over the water.

Gerard turned, seeing one of the four figures holding out a badge of office and a parchment scroll. His heart sank; he was a good citizen, accustomed to obeying the voice of the Law calling him back to Düsseldorf. Denys shared none of these scruples. A Frenchman, he despised every other nation, its laws, inhabitants, and customs alike. A soldier, he viewed the situation militarily: superior force ahead, a river barrier, and an open rear. Retreat was easy. Spotting the boat drifting in mid-stream and noting the lack of a nearer ferry, he decided immediately.

"I shall beat a quick retreat to that hill," he declared, "and once out of sight, quick step."

They sauntered off casually.

"Halt! In the bailiff's name!" cried a voice from the shore.

Denys spun around, ostentatiously snapping his fingers at the bailiff, and continued walking.

"Halt! In the archbishop's name!"

He snapped his fingers at the prelate and pressed on.

"Halt! In the emperor's name!"

He snapped his fingers at the sovereign himself and marched onward. Gerard watched this unnecessary pantomime with regret. As soon as they cleared the brow of the hill, he said, "There is now but one course: we must run to Burgundy instead of walking."

He set off, running the best part of a league without stopping. Denys was thoroughly winded and inquired what had become of Gerard's fever.

"I begin to miss it sadly," he replied dryly.

"I dropped it in the Rhine, I trow," Gerard answered.

Soon they reached a small village, where Denys bought a loaf of bread and a massive bottle of Rhenish wine.

"For," he explained, "we must sleep in some hole or corner. If we lie at an inn, we shall be taken in our beds."

This was prudent caution. The official net for catching lawbreakers, particularly common ones, was tight in that age, though public cooperation was virtually non-existent on the Continent. Innkeepers were under constant surveillance regarding their travelers, for whom they bore significant responsibility, seemingly more for their actions than their suffering. Both friends were relieved when sunset arrived, and delighted when, after a long trudge under the stars—for the moon did not rise until about three in the morning—they reached a large barn belonging to a distant estate. Barley had recently been threshed there; the heap of straw on one side of the floor stood almost as high as the unthreshed grain on the other.

"Here be two royal beds," Denys remarked. "Which shall we lie on, the mow, or the straw?"

"The straw for me," said Gerard.

They sat upon the heap, eating their brown bread and drinking their wine. Then Denys covered his friend in straw, piling it high above him, leaving only a small opening for air.

"Water is said to be death to fevered men," Denys noted. "Yet I shall make warm water anyway."

Gerard told him to ease his mind. "These few drops from the Rhine cannot chill me. I feel enough heat within my body now to parch a kennel or boil a cloud if I were in one."

With this epigram, his consciousness faded rapidly; he truly might be said to "fall asleep." Denys, lying awake for a while, heard something that made him nestle closer. Horse hooves rang out from Düsseldorf, and the wooden barn vibrated as they rattled past, howling in a manner too familiar to fifteenth-century ears, yet as alien to modern Europe as a Red Indian's war-whoop. Denys shuddered where he lay. Gerard slept like a top. The sound swept by, and the troop and their cries died away. The sturdy soldier drew a long breath, whispered a whistle, closed his eyes, and slept like a top as well.

At dawn, Denys sat up and reached out to wake Gerard. His hand touched the young man's forehead and found it quite wet. In his role as nurse, Denys refrained from waking him.

"It is ill to check sleep or sweat in a sick man," he muttered. "I know that far, though I never mimicked an ape or a gallows-bird."

After waiting an hour, feeling desperately hungry, he turned and went to sleep again in self-defense—a maneuver to which the poor fellow had often been driven during his hard life. At high noon, he was awakened by Gerard moving. He found the young man sitting up, surrounded by smoking straw. Animal heat versus moisture.

Gerard called him "a lazy loon." Denys quietly grinned.

They set out, and the first thing Denys did was return Gerard's arbalest and equipment, then climb a high tree on the roadside.

"Coast clear to the next village," he instructed, and they moved on.

As they approached the village, Denys halted and suddenly asked Gerard how he felt.

Gerard stood stunned by this abrupt revelation, his complexion shifting rapidly between pale fear and flushed anger. He ground his teeth in fury, recalling how even the most spirited man resembles a wild boar: he may flee a superior force simply because he lacks the stupidity to stand his ground, yet if pursued relentlessly and driven to desperation, he will turn upon his pursuers with terrifying ferocity. Resolving to fight, Gerard squared his shoulders for battle; but instantly, his resolve crumbled, and he whispered with anguish, "But my axe lies in the Rhine."

Together, they conferred. Prudence urged them to bypass the village entirely, while hunger demanded they procure food. Though hunger's voice was loud, Prudence argued with greater conviction. They agreed to cut across the open fields instead. Halting beside a haystack, they secured two bundles and lay concealed within a dry ditch, hidden from view though stinging by nettles. Taking turns, they ventured forth and returned with turnips, gnawing on these meager provisions at intervals throughout their flight until sunset arrived.

Soon, they crawled out, shivering violently into the driving rain and encroaching darkness, emerging onto the road beyond the village. It was a night of dismal gloom, black as pitch, with winds howling fiercely. They were invisible, unheard, and unable to see or hear anything; indeed, they might have slipped past their enemies like ghosts, unnoticed amidst the natural terrors of the stormy night. In this chaos, where they seemed to claw their way through solid black marble, they nearly forgot the presence of their foes. When the moon finally rose, they had traveled leagues away from Düsseldorf, yet they continued trudging onward.

Eventually, a massive structure loomed before them. "Take courage!" cried Denys. "I believe I recognize this convent. Yes, it is so! We are now within the diocese of Jülich; Cologne holds no sway here." With those words, they crossed the threshold and found themselves safe within the sanctuary walls.

Gerard formed a friendship with a monk renowned for his ingenuity, a man who had crafted the great sundial in the prior's garden, a wheel for lifting water, and a winnowing machine for the grain. Always occupied with some clever contrivance, he had previously built several psalteries and two dulcimers, and was currently working on a set of regalles—small organs intended for the choir.

Gerard played the humble psaltery modestly, but the monk struck the instrument with divine skill, demonstrating to Gerard how green he truly was in matters of music. While the monk excelled in illumination, he admitted he could not match Gerard's calligraphy. Comparing their talents against the earnest simplicity of an era where skills reflected genuine natural aptitude, the young traveler and the aged monk quickly grew like brothers. Gerard was warmly invited to remain that night.

He consulted Denys, who agreed with a rueful shrug. Gerard explained his destination to his old companion, recounting their recent adventures while carefully softening the blow of their troubles. "Alack!" exclaimed the good old man. "I have traveled extensively in my day, yet no one ever molested me." He advised Gerard to avoid inns entirely, which were always infested with rogues and rowdiers, warning that his soul could suffer harm even if his body escaped injury. Instead, he urged him to plan daily journeys so as to rest within the peace of a monastery. Suddenly, the tone shifted; the old man looked at Gerard with the sharp intensity of a needle and asked, "How long has it been since you were shriven?"

Gerard flushed and replied weakly, "More than a fortnight."

"And thou an exorcist! No wonder perils have overtaken thee. Come, thou must be absolved immediately."

"Yes, father," said Gerard, "with all my heart." He sank to his knees, joining his hands, but the monk stopped him, half-fretful. "Not to me! Not to me! Not to me! My soul is as filled with worldly cares as yours or anyone living in it. My entire devotion lies in these wooden pipes and sorry leather stops, which will perish along with those whose minds are fixed on such vanities."

"Dear father," pleaded Gerard, "these instruments are for the service of the Church, and surely that sanctifies the labor spent upon them?"

"That is precisely what the devil has been whispering in my ear lately," the monk retorted, placing one hand behind his back and shaking his finger at Gerard with a mixture of threat and playfulness. "He was so kind and thoughtful as to remind me that Solomon built the Lord a house with rare hangings, and that this act was deemed gracious and sinless. Oh! He can quote Scripture remarkably well. But I am not so simple a monk as you think, my lad," he cried, suddenly defiant, addressing not Gerard but the empty space beside him. "This one toy finished, vigils, fasts, and prayers await me. Prayers standing, prayers lying on the chapel floor, and prayers in a right good tub of cold water."

He nudged Gerard and winked knowingly. "Nothing he hates and dreads more than seeing us monks at our devotions up to our chins in cold water. For *corpus domat aqua*. So now, go confess your little trivial sins—pardonable in youth and secular life—and leave me to mine, which are sweeter to me than honey and require equal expiation."

Gerard bowed his head but could not resist asking, "Where shall I find a confessor more holy and merciful?"

"In each of these cells," the monk replied simply, gesturing toward the corridor. "Go to Brother Anselm, yonder."

Gerard moved toward the indicated cell, but the doors stood close together, and he appears to have mistaken his mark. Just as he raised his hand to knock, he heard his old friend calling out in an agitated whisper, "Nay! Nay! Nay!"

Turning, he found the monk pacing anxiously before his own cell door, beating the air with both hands like a woodcutter swinging an axe. Gerard genuinely feared the room he sought housed some dangerous wild beast, or perhaps the very personage whose presence in the convent had been so clearly announced. Looking back questioningly, he proceeded to the next door.

Then, his old friend nodded vigorously, his face breaking into a blissful expression before shooting back into his sanctuary. He took his hourglass, flipped it, and returned to his work on the regalles. Often, he would glance upward and mutter to himself, "Well-a-day, how swiftly the sands run when a man bends over earthly toys."

Father Anselm was a venerable monk with an ample head and a countenance radiating dignity and love. Consequently, as Gerard confessed to him and answered his gentle yet probing questions, he couldn't help thinking, "Here is a head! Oh dear, oh dear! I wonder if you will allow me to sketch it once the confession is concluded." In his distraction, his own mind grew confused, and he forgot certain crimes. Nevertheless, he did not soften his account this time, nor was he so dishonest as to diminish the pagan nature of his previous embellishments.

The penance assigned was brief: Gerard was to enter the convent church, prostrate himself, and kiss the lowest step of the altar three times. Then, kneeling on the floor, he was to recite three Our Fathers and a Creed. Upon completion, he was to return instantly.

Having performed this short mortification, Gerard returned to find Father Anselm mixing plaster. "After the soul comes the body," the monk said. "Know that I serve as the surgeon here, lacking a better option. This is for your leg; to cool it, not to burn it—the saints forbid."

During the treatment, the monastic physician, having taken a keen interest in the Düsseldorf episode of Gerard's confession, leaned slightly toward Denys regarding the merits of "bleeding."

"We Dominicans seldom bleed nowadays," the monk remarked. "Lay surgeons claim it stems from timidity and lack of skill; indeed, we have long found that simples cure most ailments that can be cured at all. Furthermore, they never kill in capable hands, whereas other remedies strike like thunderbolts. As for blood, the Vulgate explicitly states it is the life of a man. And in medicine, as in law or divinity, to be wiser than the All-Wise is to be a fool. Moreover, simples are mighty. Consider the small four-legged creature that kills the poisonous snake; if bitten itself, it finds an herb powerful enough to quell that venom, acting faster and stronger than any human remedy. Taught by her wisdom and our own traditions, we still search and test the virtues of the plants God has scattered across this earth—some to feed the body, others to heal it. Only in desperate cases do we mix heavenly with earthly virtue, steeping the hair or bones of deceased saints in the medicine to achieve marvelous cures."

"Do you believe, father, it is due to the relics? For Peter a Floris, a learned physician and certainly no pagan, denies this stoutly."

"What does Peter a Floris know? And what do I know? I do not presume to claim we can command the saints, nor that they will, regardless of our wishes, draw corporeal virtue from their blessed remains. Yet, I observe that patients drinking thus in faith are often improved as if by a charm. Doubtless, faith in the recipient plays a large role in all such cures. Indeed, it always has. A sick woman whom all Jewish physicians failed to heal merely touched Christ's garment and was cured instantly. Had she not touched that sacred cloth, she would never have recovered. If she had worn it to her grave without faith, I suspect she would have gained nothing. But we do ill to investigate these matters too curiously. Everything around us demands faith. Have then a little patience. We shall soon know all. Meanwhile, I, your confessor for this occasion, strictly forbid you, under penalty to your soul's health, from listening to learned laymen on religious matters. Arrogance is their ruin; with it, they shut heaven's open door in their own faces. Mark my words: learned laics. Unlearned ones have often been my masters in humility and may become yours. Your wound is tended; in three days, it will be but a scar. May God speed you, and may the saints make you as good and happy as you are thoughtful and gracious."

Gerard hoped there was no need to depart, as he was scheduled to dine in the refectory. However, Father Anselm told him, with a barely perceptible trace of regret, that he would not leave his cell that week, being himself in penitence. With this, he gently held Gerard's head in both hands, kissed him on the brow, and almost before the cell door had closed, he was back to his pious duties.

Gerard left feeling chilled to the heart by the isolation of monastic life, deeply saddened. "Alas!" he thought. "Here is a kind face I shall never behold again on earth; a kind voice lost forever from my ear and my heart. There is nothing but meeting and parting in this sorrowful world. Well-a-day! Well-a-day!"

This pensive mood was broken by a young monk who fetched him and led him to the refectory. There, he found several monks seated at a table, while Denys stood like a sentinel, being questioned about the towns he intended to pass through. The friars pooled their knowledge, mapping out the safest route and

Gerard felt a fervor for this promised land nearly matching Denys's own. The soldier was a relentless evangelist for Burgundy; at every minor inconvenience, he would chant its virtues, contrasting them sharply with their current surroundings. Above all, he exploited Gerard's fastidious nature by promising the pristine linens of that polished nation. "Ask no more!" Denys would declare, shaking his head in disgust. "To think I haven't slept in a bare bed since leaving home! When I gaze upon their linen, I wish nothing more than to close my eyes and nose forever." His patriotism bordered on the absurd; he had once walked twenty leagues in shoes that had long since disintegrated rather than purchase provisions from a German merchant, whom he deemed a churlish obstacle to civilization, citing the man's incivility and poor fare as reasons enough to endure the journey.

As the sun began to dip below the horizon, they found themselves equidistant between a small town and a monastery, the latter situated slightly off the main road. Denys favored the inn; Gerard preferred the convent. Denys relented, however, on one condition: once within Burgundian borders, they were to lodge exclusively at inns. Gerard agreed all too readily, for his chart of monasteries concluded precisely at this point. Turning off the road toward the cloister, Gerard soon voiced his bewilderment regarding Denys's sudden aversion to places that had previously offered them free board and lodging.

The soldier hesitated, hemming and hawing, before finally letting his grievances spill forth. It emerged that this was not a fleeting dislike but an ancient, abiding horror, suppressed until now only through sheer effort and politeness. "I suspected they had poisoned your drink," Denys confessed, "so I held back. But since this is our final stop, why not clear my conscience? Consider me a fish out of water in all those great dungeons. You rise immediately with some old friar, so you never witness my purgatory."

"Forgive me," Gerard said, feeling guilty. "I have been selfish."

"Ay, ay, I forgive thee, little one; 'tis not thy fault," Denys replied dismissively. "Art thou not the first fool ever priest-ridden and monk-hitted? Yet, I shall not forgive *them* my misery." With a grim determination, he launched into his indictment, recounting tales from a century prior to Henry VIII.'s commissioners.

"These gloomy piles are all constructed identically," Denys raged. "Inns vary, but here everything is monotony. A great gate, a little gate, countless steps leading to a gloomy cloister. There is the dormitory, then the vast, freezing refectory where one must sit silent, or at least inaudible, if one wishes to keep one's thoughts private. 'And then,' I say, 'no one is truly human here; everyone is a slave. Slaves to what? To a peevish, tinkling bell that knows no sleep.' If it were a trumpet blowing constant alarms, it might not freeze a man's heart quite so thoroughly. *Tinkle, tinkle, tinkle*, and yet you must sit down to eat with perhaps no appetite whatsoever. Before your food has settled in your stomach, *tinkle, tinkle!* and you must rush to church with perhaps no desire for devotion. I am no hog at prayers, I assure you. *Tinkle, tinkle,* and now you must retire to bed with your eyes wide open. By the time you manage to shut them, some restless spirit of darkness has seized the bell-rope, and *tinkle, tinkle!* compels you to recite a prayer in the dark, knowing none or caring less. If they could hear the sort of prayers I mutter while they shatter my rest with their *tinkling*! Well, you drift off again, gaining perhaps an hour's sleep: lo, it is *tinkle, tinkle* once more for Matins."

"And the only clapper you seem to cherish is a woman's," Gerard interjected, speaking with half-contempt.

"Because there is some music in her voice, even when she scolds," Denys retorted stoutly.

"And then to be constantly corrected. If I merely touch my finger in the salt-cellars, instantly I hear, 'Have you no knife with which to handle the salt?' And if I dare wipe my blade on the cloth to save time, the rebuke comes: 'Wipe thy knife dirty on the bread, and clean upon the cloth!' Oh, small souls! These petty, pedantic trivialities fall upon good fellowship like tiny icicles melting down from thatched eaves."

"I hold cleanliness to be no mere pedantry," Gerard countered firmly. "Thou shouldst learn better manners once and for all."

"Nay; 'tis they who lack manners. They stop a fellow's mouth at every word."

"At every other word, you mean; every obscene or blasphemous one."

"Exaggerator, go to! At the very last of these dungeons, I found poor travelers sitting chilled and mute around a single friar, looking like rogues awaiting execution. To cheer them, I cried out, 'Courage, tout le monde, le diable—'"

"Connu! What happened?" Gerard interrupted.

"Marry, this. 'Blaspheme not!' quoth the executioner. 'Plait-il?' I replied. Didn't he wheel and wryte at me in a sort of Alsatian French, turning all the P's into B's? I had much ado not to laugh in his face."

"Being thyself unable to speak ten words of his tongue without error," Gerard noted dryly.

"Well, all the world ought to speak French. What avail so many jargons except to erect frontiers between men's hearts?"

"But what did he say?"

"What signifies it what a fool says?"

"Oh, not all the words of a fool are folly, or I should not listen to you."

"Well, then, he declared, 'Those who begin by making free with the Devil's name end by doing it with all the names in Heaven.' 'Father,' I said, 'I am a soldier, and this is but my consigne, my watchword.' 'Oh, then it is just a custom?' he asked. Not seeing through the old fox, and hoping to clear myself, I answered, 'Ay, it was.' 'Then that is ten times worse,' he replied. ''Twill bring him about your ears one of these days. He still comes where he hears his name often called.' Observe! No gratitude for the tidings which neither his missals nor his breviary had ever revealed to him. Then, deigning further instruction, he informed me that soldiers commonly commit the crimes for which all other men are broken on the wheel—a savoir murder, rape, and pillage."

"And is't not true?" Gerard asked cautiously.

"True or not, it was ill manners," Denys replied guardedly.

"And so does this courteous host of mine add, 'Being the foes of mankind, why make enemies of good spirits into the bargain by still shouting the names of evil ones?' and a host of similar nonsense."

"Well, but, Denys, whether you heed his advice or scorn it, why blame a man for raising his voice to save your soul?"

"How can his voice save my soul when he keeps twisting his P's into B's?"

Gerard was staggered. Before he could recover from this thunderbolt of Gallicism, Denys veered triumphantly onto another tangent, stigmatizing all monks as hypocrites. "Do but look at them," he sneered. "How they creep about, unable to eye you like honest men."

"Nay," Gerard said eagerly, defending his order. "That modest, downcast gaze is part of their discipline; it is *custodia oculorum*."

"Cussed toads eating *hoc hac horum*? No such thing! Just so looks a cutpurse. Cannot meet a true man's eye. Doff the cowl, monk, and behold a thief; don the cowl upon the thief, and lo, a monk. Tell me not they will ever be able to look God Almighty in the face when they cannot even look a true man in the face down here. Ah, here it is, black as ink! Into the well we go, comrade."

Just then, the sound approached. "Misericorde, there goes the tinkle already," Denys announced. "'Tis the best of tinkles though; 'tis for dinner: stay, listen! I thought so: the wolf in my stomach cried 'Amen!'"

He confirmed this last statement with two oaths and marched forward like a victorious gamecock into the convent, believing that by Gerard's silence he had won the argument, unaware of the profound disgust he had actually instilled in his companion.

In the refectory, conversation at the table where Gerard sat drifted toward the sudden demise of the monk who had volunteered to transcribe fresh copies of the monastery's charter and rules. Seizing the opportunity, Gerard timidly proffered his services. A pause ensued, which he misinterpreted as rejection.

"Nay, not for hire, my lords, but for love," Gerard hastened to explain, "and as a humble token of gratitude for the many comfortable nights the brethren of your order have granted to a poor wayfarer."

One monk smiled with approval but gently suggested that the deceased brother was a master of the pen, implying that such delicate work required a peer to continue it. Undeterred, and with a tremor in his hand, Gerard withdrew a vellum deed from his wallet. He had carefully cleaned its reverse side and inscribed it as a specimen.

Upon seeing the document, the monk jumped slightly, then hurried up the hall to the high table. Bending his knee until it merely grazed the fifth and tenth steps, he presented the deed to the Prior, offering enthusiastic commentary. Instantly, a dozen discerning eyes locked onto the page, and a murmur rippled through the assembly. Soon, Gerard watched the Prior point repeatedly, while the monk returned, beaming with pride, bearing a savory *crustade royale*—a game pie spiced and gravy-drenched—for Gerard, alongside a silver grace cup brimming with rich pimentum.

Gerard accepted the cup. Bowing low first to the distant Prior, then to his fellow diners, he drained it and passed it around. Immediately, to his astonishment, the entire table hailed him as one of their own. "Are you not convent-bred? Deny it not!"

He affirmed it, giving thanks to Heaven, for without this revelation, he felt as uncouth and ignorant as his companions, Sybrandt and Cornelis.

"But 'tis passing strange how you could know," one monk remarked.

"You drank with the cup in both hands," two others chimed in simultaneously.

For some time, voices had grown raucous around a table at the far end of the hall, but now erupted into a burst of boisterous laughter so prolonged and loud that the Prior dispatched the Sub-Prior down the aisle to quell the disturbance and assign penance to the offenders. Gerard's face burned with shame; amidst the chaotic revelry, his ear had caught the word "courage" and the distinctive trumpet tones of Denys of Burgundy.

Soon after, Gerard was settled in Feu Werter's cell, illuminated by wax lights, equipped with a tilting writing frame, and supplied with all necessary calligraphic materials. Yet, the task proved too arduous for a single evening. The question arose: how could he ask Denys, the self-proclaimed monk-hater, to remain longer? He posed the query, submitting to whatever decision Denys chose. To his delight, Denys replied graciously, "A day's rest will harm neither of us. Write thy script, and I shall pass the time as best I may."

Gerard's work garnered immense admiration; they concurred that the monastery's records had suffered greatly under the late Brother Werter's stewardship. The Sub-Prior pressed a rix-dollar into Gerard's hand along with several brushes and pigments from the convent's ample stores. Warm-hearted and buoyed by this auspicious sign—that the pen might indeed secure his fortune and help him win back his beloved—Gerard resumed his journey.

"Come, Denys," he said cheerfully, displaying his gifts, "see what the good monks have bestowed upon me. Now, strive to be fairer to them; for, to speak plainly, hearing you call my benefactors 'hypocrites' chilled my friendship for a fleeting moment."

"I recant," Denys declared instantly.

"Thank you! Thank you! Good Denys!" Gerard exclaimed.

"I was a scurrilous vagabond," Denys confessed.

"Nay, nay, say not so!" Gerard protested.

"But we soldiers are rough and hasty. I condemn myself, and I offer my highest esteem to all whom I misunderstood. 'Tis unjust that thousands should be defamed for the hypocrisy of a few."

"Now you reason wisely," Gerard noted. "Have you pondered my words?"

"Nay, it was their own doing," Denys shrugged.

Gerard crowed inwardly, for everyone enjoys being vindicated, and listened intently as Denys recounted the tale of his conversion.

"Well then," Denys began, "at dinner on the first day, a young monk beside me opened his jaws and laughed aloud, most musically. 'Good,' I thought, 'at last I have fallen upon a man and not a shorn ape.' To test him further, I slapped his broad back and delivered my customary salute. 'Heaven forbid!' he cried. I stared. For the dog looked as sorrowful as Solomon; a better mimic you never saw, even at a mystery play. 'I see war is no sharpener of the wits,' he said. 'What are the clergy for but to fight the foul fiend? And what else are monks for? When the fiend is dead, the friars are sped. You may plough up the convents, and we poor monks shall have nothing to do—but turn soldiers, and so bring him to life again.' Then there was a great laugh at my expense. 'Well, you are the monk for me,' I said. 'And you are the crossbowman for me,' he replied. 'And I'll be bound you could tell us tales of the war that would make our hair stand on end.' 'Excusez-moi,' I retorted, 'the barber has put that out of the question,' and then I had the laugh."

"What wretched ribaldry!" Gerard observed pensively.

The candid Denys immediately admitted he had witnessed merrier jests born of less cacophony. "'Twas a great matter to have dispelled hypocrisy," he continued. "'So,' I said, 'I can grant you the chair of the rooster, if that pleases you.' 'That we shall see,' was the cry, and a signal went round."

Then Denys burst forth with glee, recounting how at bedtime he was ushered not to the great dormitory but into a private cell, strictly forbidden to sleep. To aid his vigil, a book of pictures depicting a hundred merry adventures of monks pursuing female laity was lent him. Shortly thereafter, he was led barefoot down to the parlour, where a supper fit for a duke awaited, attended by twelve jovial friars—the roughest, rowdiest boys he had ever encountered in peace or war.

How the stories flowed, how toasts were raised, how jests were exchanged, and how wine cups circled! Some played cards with a gorgeous deck where Saint Theresa and Saint Catherine, adorned in gold, served as the four queens, while black, white, grey, and crutch-bearing friars acted as the four knaves. They staked their very rosaries, swearing like troopers when they lost. About midnight, a sly monk slipped out, only to be cunningly followed by others into the garden, where he was seen thrusting his hand into the ivy and pulling out a rope ladder. With this, he scaled the ten-foot wall; though broad, it yielded quickly, and a russet kirtle appeared from the outside world as fast as he. Thus began a scene of billing and cooing that struck Denys as more feline than ecclesiastical, drawing from him the apt comment of a "mew!"

The monks joined the musical chorus, and the lay visitor shrieked in discomfort, but Abelard merely cried, "What, are ye there, ye jealous miauling knaves? Ye shall caterwaul to some tune tomorrow night. I'll fit every man-jack of ye with a fardingale!"

This brutal threat convinced him to stay another day—at Gerard's request. Gerard groaned internally. Meanwhile, unable to disconcert such a brazen monk, and the damsel beginning to whimper, they danced a caterwauling circle before bestowing a solemn benediction on the two "wall-flowers," then retired to the parlour. There they found a pair lying dead drunk and two others weeping affectionately. They promptly carried off the unconscious ones, dragged the tearful ones kicking and screaming into their respective cells, and left them there in "measureless content."

Gerard was disgusted and voiced his displeasure. Denys chuckled and proceeded to describe the following day's exploits: how he and the young monks had drained the fish ponds, secreting pike, carp, tench, and eels for their own use. Then, in the dead of night, he was taken shoeless by crooked paths into the chapel—a ghostly, dark place—and down steps into a crypt beneath the floor, where suddenly paradise burst upon him.

"'Tis there the holy fathers retire to pray," Gerard interjected.

"Not always," Denys corrected. "Dozens of wax candles were lit, and princely cheer awaited. Fifteen soups *maigre*, with marvelous flavors of venison, grouse, and hare, plus twenty different kinds of fish (for it was Friday), cooked with wondrous art. Each dish was flanked by two buxom lasses, and each lass by two lads in cowls—all except me. To think I had to woo by interpreter! I doubt the intermediary spoke three words for himself and one for me; if he didn't, hang him for a fool

They encountered the innkeeper in the hallway.

"Welcome, gentlemen," he said, lifting his cap with a low bow.

"Come, we are not in Germany," Gerard remarked.

In the main hall, they found the mistress, a robust woman of about forty. She curtseyed to them and offered a warm smile. "Take the trouble to sit down, fair sir," she said to Gerard, dusting two chairs with her apron—though they hardly needed it.

"Thank you, dame," Gerard replied. *Well,* he thought, *this is a polite nation: the trouble of sitting down? I shall endure it with singular patience; and shortly thereafter, the labor of eating, the toil of digestion, and finally, by Hercules' aid, the strain of retiring to bed, and the struggle of sinking fast asleep.*

"Why, Denys, what are you doing? Ordering supper for only two?"

"Why not?"

"What? Can we dine without waiting for forty others? Burgundy forever!"

"Aha! Courage, comrade. Le dia—"

"C'est convenu."

It seemed the Salic Law had not yet penetrated French inns. In this establishment at least, the wimple and kirtle reigned supreme; doublets and hose were few in number and lacked vigor. The landlord himself wandered aimlessly, eternally tipping his hat to people out of sheer habit; and the women, passing him in turn, shoved him quietly aside without looking at him, as one removes a live twig while rushing through a forest.

A maid brought in the supper, and the mistress followed her, empty-handed.
