"Fell to, my masters," she said cheerfully. "You have but one enemy here; and he lies beneath your knife." (I shrewdly suspect this is merely a formula.)

They began to eat. The mistress pulled her chair slightly closer to the table and provided companionship as well as food, chatting genially with them like old friends. Once this initial exchange was finished, the busy dame departed quickly, sending in her daughter instead—a beautiful young woman of about twenty, who took the vacant seat. She was not quite so broad and genial as the elder, but gentle and cheerful, showing a tender concern for Gerard upon learning the distance the poor boy had traveled and the journey he still faced. She remained nearly half an hour, and when she left, Gerard remarked, "Is this an inn? Why, it feels like home."

"Qui fit François, il fit courtois," said Denys, bursting with gratified pride.

"Courteous? Nay, Christian; to welcome us like home-guests and old friends, mere vagrants here today and gone tomorrow. But truly, who deserves pity and kindness more than the weary traveler far from his kin? Hola! Here comes another."

The newcomer was the chambermaid, a woman of about twenty-five, with a prominent nose, a large laughing mouth, and a sparkling black eye, alongside a bare arm that was stout but not particularly shapely.

The moment she entered, one of the travelers passed a somewhat bold jest regarding her; instantly, the entire company erupted in laughter at his expense, thanks to her practiced tongue. Just as, in a fencing match between a novice and a master, foils clash—the novice gets pinked—so too did another, and then another, attempt to break a lance with her. But Marion planted her great arms upon her hips and held the whole room in play. This country girl possessed in perfection that rough, ready humor which may appear mean and vulgar on paper but carries all before it when spoken aloud: not the rapier of wit, but its bludgeon. Nature had done much for her in this regard, and daily practice in an inn completed the rest.

Yet, I shall not photograph her, but indicate her feebly; for it was just four hundred years ago, the raillery was coarse, she returned every stroke in kind, and though a virtuous woman, she said things without blinking that no decent man of our day would utter even among men.

Gerard sat gaping in astonishment. This was almost a new variety of *homo* to him. He whispered, "Denys, now I see why you Frenchmen say 'a woman's tongue is her sword,'" just as she leveled another assault against an opponent. Meanwhile, the chivalrous Denys, to console and support "the weaker vessel"—the iron kettle among the clay pots—administered his counter-stroke: "Courage, ma mie, le—" etc.

She turned on him immediately. "How can he be dead as long as there is an archer left alive?" (General laughter at her ally's expense.)

"It is 'washing day,' my masters," she declared with sudden gravity.

"Afterward? We travelers cannot strip and go bare while you wash our clothes," objected a peevish old fellow by the fireside, who had kept mum during the raillery but crept out into the sunshine of common sense.

"I aimed not your way, ancient man," replied Marion superciliously. "But since you ask me" (she scanned him slowly from head to foot), "I trow you might take a turn in the tub, clothes and all, and no harm done" (laughter). "But what I spoke for, I thought this young sire might like his beard starched."

Poor Gerard's turn had come; his chin crop was thin and silky.

The loudest of all the laughers this time was the traitor Denys, whose beard was of good length and singularly stiff and bristly; so that Shakespeare, though he never saw him, hit him in the bull's-eye.

"Full of strange oaths, and bearded like the pard." —*As You Like It*.

Gerard bore the Amazonian satire with remarkable calm. He possessed little personal vanity. "Nay, 'chambermaid,'" he said with a smile, "mine is all unworthy of your pains; take you this fair growth in hand!" pointing to Denys's vegetable.

"Oh, time for that, when I starch the besoms."

While they all shouted over this palpable hit, the mistress returned, and in no more time than it took her to cross the threshold, our Amazon transformed into a seeming Madonna, meek and mild.

Mistresses are wonderful subjugators. Their like, I think, breathes not on the globe. Housemaids, decide! It was a waste of histrionic ability, however, for the landlady had heard and did not at heart disapprove the peals of laughter.

"Ah, Marion, lass," she said good-naturedly, "if you laid me an egg every time you cackle, 'Les Trois Poissons' would never lack an omelet."

"Now, dame," said Gerard, "what is to pay?"

"For what?"

"Our supper."

"Where is the hurry? Cannot you be content to pay when you go? Lose the guest, find the money—that is the rule of 'The Three Fish.'"

"But, dame, outside 'The Three Fish' it is written—'Ici-on ne loge'"

"Bah! Let that flea stick on the wall! Look hither," and she pointed to the smoky ceiling, which was covered with hieroglyphics. These were accounts, vulgo scores; intelligible to this dame and her daughter, who wrote them at need by simply mounting a low stool and scratching with a knife so as to show lines of ceiling through the deposit of smoke. The dame explained that the writing on the wall was placed there to frighten moneyless folk away entirely, or to be acted upon at odd times when a non-paying face should enter and insist on being served. "We cannot refuse them outright, you know. The law forbids us."

"And how do you know mine is not such a face?"

"Out fie! It is the best face that has entered 'The Three Fish' this autumn."

"And mine, dame?" said Denys; "do you see no knavery here?"

She eyed him calmly. "Not such a good one as the lad's; nor ever will be. But it is the face of a true man. For all that," she added drily, "if I were ten years younger, I'd as lief not meet that face on a dark night too far from home."

Gerard stared. Denys laughed. "Why, dame, I would but sip the night dew off the flower; and you needn't take ten years off, nor ten days, to be worth risking a scratched face for."

"There, our mistress," said Marion, who had just come in, "did I not tell you the other day you could make a fool of them still, if you were properly minded?"

"I dare say you did; it sounds like some daft wench's speech."

"Dame," said Gerard, "this is wonderful."

"What? Oh, no, no, that is no wonder at all. Why, I have been here all my life; and reading faces is the first thing a girl picks up in an inn."

Marion. "And frying eggs the second; no, telling lies; frying eggs is the third, though."

The Mistress. "And holding her tongue the last, and modesty the day after—never at all."

Marion. "Alack! Talk of my tongue. But I say no more. She under whose wing I live now deals the blow. I'm sped—it's but a chambermaid gone. Catch what's left on't!" And she staggered and sank backward onto the handsomest fellow in the room, which happened

"Here be we!" roared a chorus of voices, followed immediately by a blessing. "God bless you all! God bless you!"

In response, there was a frantic rush toward the staircase. Half a dozen hands, calloused yet warm with genuine friendship, shot out to grasp the rescuers.

"You've saved our lives, lads!" cried Denys, his voice thick with relief. "You've saved our lives this very night!"

The scene that greeted the eyes of the rescued pair was one of chaotic brilliance. Torches flared wildly, casting dancing shadows against the walls. The room was filled with the glittering breastplates of the archers, their faces bronzed by firelight and exertion. Bound tightly were the pale, terrified faces of the thieves, and lying amidst the gore was the bleeding giant, his life extinguished by the hard men who now stood guard over him.

Gerard moved among the archers, shaking each hand with glistening eyes. In turn, they kissed him—a gesture of profound gratitude—and this time, he returned their kisses. Turning to a handsome archer of his own age, Gerard spoke softly. "Prithee, good soldier, watch over me. A strange drowsiness overtakes me. Let no one cut my throat while I sleep—for pity's sake."

The archer laughed, assuming Gerard was merely jesting, and promised faithfully. Almost immediately, Gerard sank into a deep slumber. Denys watched, surprised but unmoving; the unconscious state suited his immediate needs perfectly.

Elsewhere, two archers were examining the Abbot's body, kicking it gently to turn it over as they asked, "Which of the two cast this enormous rogue from an upper storey like that? We would fain learn the trick of his arm."

Denys initially scoffed, unwilling to boast. *That young vagabond,* he thought, *will claim it was the finger of Heaven or some divine intervention, making me look like a fool.* But seeing Gerard lost in dreams, Denys decided to provide the necessary account.

"Well, then, comrades," Denys declared loudly. "I ran my sword through this one to the hilt. Others swarmed around me, so I needed my blade or I must die. Thus, I planted my foot against his stomach, gave a tug with my hand, sprang with my leg, and sent him flying straight to kingdom come! He died in mid-air, and his carcass rolled down among you without ceremony—making quite a jump, I warrant. But pikes and pillage! What avail does prattle serve once the deed is done? *Buvons, camarades, buvons!* (Drink, friends, drink!)"

The archers noted that saying "drink" was easy when no liquor was present, though doing so was harder.

"Nay, I shall soon find you liquor," Denys insisted, his nose twitching with alacrity. "My nose is naturally gifted at scenting wine. Follow me, and I will lead the way with this torch!"

They descended a short flight of stone steps into a large, low, and damp cellar. The air was close and dank; the walls were encrusted here and there with what looked like cobwebs but proved to be saltpetre oozing from the moist stones and crystallizing.

"Oh! The fine mouldy smell," Denys murmured. "In such places, good wine often still lurks. Advance your torch."

Suddenly, a cry broke the silence. "Diable! What is that in the corner?"

Gathering around the torchlight, they discovered a figure crouching on a heap in the gloom, pale as ash and shivering violently.

"Why, it is the landlord," Denys identified. "Get up, thou craven heart!" shouted one of the archers. "The thieves are bound, and we are thirsty. Rise and show us your wine; for no bottles are visible here."

"What? Are the rascals bound?" the pale landlord stammered, his voice trembling. "Good news... wine? Yes, yes, I will, honest sirs."

He rose with unsteady joints, offering to guide them to the wine cellar. But Denys interjected sharply. "You are all in the dark, comrades. He is in league with the thieves."

"Alack, good soldier! Me in league with the accursed robbers? Is that reasonable?" the landlord protested.

"The girl said so anyway," an archer retorted.

"The girl! What girl? Ah! Curse her, traitress!" the landlord spat.

"Well," another archer added, "the girl is not here; she has gone to the bailiff. Let the burghers decide if this coward is guilty or not. We caught him not in the act, so let him simply draw us our wine."

"One moment," Denys said shrewdly. "Why did he curse the girl? If he were a true man, he should bless her as we do."

"Alas, sir!" the landlord pleaded. "I have but my good name to live by. I cursed her to you because you accused her of bellying me."

"Humph! I trow thou art a thief, and where is the thief who cannot lie with a smooth face?" Denys countered. "Therefore, hold him, comrades. A prisoner can draw wine even if his hands are not bound."

Offering no objection, the landlord agreed to show them his stock, hoping they would pay, noting sadly that his rent was overdue by two months. The archers smiled grimly at his simplicity. One laid a firm hand on his shoulder while the other led the way with the torch.

As they reached the threshold, Denys cried, "Halt!"

"What is it?"

"Here be bottles in this corner. Advance your light."

The torch-bearer stepped forward, removing his scabbard to probe the heap where the landlord had just crouched.

"Nay, nay!" cried the landlord. "The wine is in the next cellar. There is nothing here."

"Nothing is mighty hard, then," Denys replied, reaching into the heap and pulling out an object. It was a bone. He tossed it onto the floor, where it rattled.

"There is naught there but the bones of the house," the landlord insisted. "Just now it was empty. Now that we have found something, it is nothing but bones. Here is another."

Denys handed the bone to an archer named Philippe, who held it up to the light, turning it over and over.

"Well?" Denys prompted.

"Well, if this were a battlefield," Philippe mused, "I should say it was the shankbone of a man. No more, no less. But this isn't a battlefield, nor a churchyard; it is an inn."

"True, mate; but that knave's ashen face is as good a lantern as any battlefield. Read the bone by it. Bring that face nearer, I say. When the chine is missing and the house dog won't look at you without his tail tucked between his legs, who was the thief? Good brothers, my mind misgives me. The deeper I thrust, the more there seems to be. Perhaps if these bones could speak, they would make true men's flesh creep."

"Alas, young man, what hideous fancies are these!" the landlord shrieked. "These are bones of beef, sheep, and kids, not of men and women! Holy saints preserve us!"

"Hold thy peace! Thy words are air. Thou hast not got burghers by the ear who cannot distinguish a veal knuckle from their grandsire's ribs; but soldiers-men who sought their comrades and found their bones picked clean by crows, perhaps as thoroughly as these by thee and thy mates." Denys thrust a small object suddenly into the landlord's face. "Men and women, didst thou say? Prithee, when did I speak of women's bones? Thou wouldst make a child suspect thee. Field of battle, comrade! Was not this house a field of battle half an hour ago?"

Dragging the landlord closer, Denys demanded, "Read his face now. What is this, thou knave?"

The landlord stared, paralyzed. "Alas! I know not."

"I would not swear either," Denys admitted, drawing his sword. "But it looks too much like the thumb bone of a man's hand. Mates, my flesh creeps. Churchyard! How do I know this is not one?"

Raking the heap of earth, broken crockery, and bones onto the floor, Denys searched relentlessly. The landlord assured him he was wasting time.

"We poor innkeepers are sinners," the wretch sobbed. "We give short measure and baptize the wine; we are fain to do these things because the laws are so unjust to us. But we are not assassins. How could we afford to kill our customers? May Heaven's lightning strike me dead if there be any bones here but those used for meat. 'Tis the kitchen wench who flings them here. I swear by God's holy mother, by Saint Paul, by Saint Dominic, and by Saint Denys, my patron saint..."

Denys held out a bone under the landlord's eye in dead silence. It was a bone that no man, however ignorant or lying, could confuse with those of sheep or oxen. The sight silenced the lying lips and palsied the heartless heart. The landlord's hair rose visibly on his head like spikes,

Gerard awoke to find Denys gazing upon him with evident unease. "It is you still asleep!" the friend exclaimed. "'Tis already high noon."

"It was a blessed slumber," Gerard replied softly. "Methinks Heaven itself bestowed it upon me. It seemed to draw a veil between my spirit and that dreadful nightmare. To consider that we sit here, breathing and whole... how terrifying the vision must have been!"

"Indeed, lad," Denys answered wisely. "Once such horrors are behind us, one must view them merely as dreams or fleeting shadows. Eat your breakfast, and soon enough, the memory will fade. Furthermore, I promised to escort you into the town by midday to present yourself before His Worship."

Gerard dipped a piece of coarse rye bread into a cup of red wine and consumed it to break his fast. He then accompanied Denys across the battlefield where the recent skirmish had occurred. Returning from the scene, he walked with a shudder still clinging to his frame. As they set out again toward the town, he kept glancing anxiously through the hedgerows, expecting ambushes that reason dictated were impossible, until they finally arrived at the gates of the little settlement.

Denys led him straight to "The White Hart."

"No need to fear highwaymen here," Denys assured him confidently. "I have known this hostler for years. He is a respected burgess, likely serving as bailiff. In fact, this is where I intended to lodge last night. But we lost precious time, and darkness overtook us—until—"

"And there stood a woman at the door," Gerard interrupted, his voice trembling slightly. "She proved far wiser than a mere Jeanneton, telling us that all danger had passed."

"Why, what saved our lives if not that woman?" Denys asked earnestly. "Yes, she risked her own safety to aid us."

"That is true, Denys," Gerard admitted. "Though I hold little regard for women generally, I am compelled to seek out this poor girl, to thank her and reward her, even if I must part with every penny in my purse to do so. Would you not agree?"

"By God's grace, yes," Denys responded without hesitation.

"But where might we find her?"

"Perhaps the alderman can inform us. We must visit him first."

The alderman received them with a countenance of such singular and inexplicable gravity that they paused in surprise. Yet, after a brief moment of reflection, his face softened into a grim smile. He immediately began to question Gerard, taking careful note of every answer given. Once finished, he declared that they must remain in the town until the thieves were tried, warning them that failure to appear as witnesses would result in heavy fines and imprisonment.

They stared at him, bewildered by the severity of the command.

"Nevertheless," the magistrate continued, "it will not be long; the culprits were caught red-handed." He added casually, "And you should know, under any circumstances, you could not leave this place for the coming week."

Denys fixed him with a stare of confusion, while Gerard smiled inwardly at the old gentleman's naivety. Could a provincial town in Burgundy truly possess such allure as to detain a man bound for Rome and longing for Margaret?

Now, however, Gerard turned his attention to the matter nearest both their hearts.

"Your Worship," he said respectfully, "we are unable to locate our benefactress within the town."

"Nay, but pray tell, who is this benefactress?" the alderman inquired.

"Who? Why, the virtuous maiden who approached you last night," Gerard explained, his heart swelling with gratitude. "She saved our lives at great peril to herself. Oh, sir, our hearts burn within us to offer thanks and blessings; where may we find her?"

"In prison, sir? Good heavens, for what crime?"

"She is a witness, and may prove essential to the case."

"Why, Messire Bailiff," interjected Denys, "you do not lay all your witnesses by the heels, I trow."

The alderman, pleased to be addressed as bailiff, grew conversational. "In cases involving bloodshed, we detain all testimony likely to secure bail and thus frustrate justice; hence, we still hold the womenfolk. A man may hide in one mindset for a week, but a woman, if she fulfills her duty to the realm on Friday, will undo it before Sunday comes around. Could you see that wench now, you would find her sobbing bitterly over having betrayed five men to the gallows. Had they been women, we might have trusted a subpoena; they despise one another, showing some sense in that regard. But now, thinking on it, there were other reasons for holding this one captive. Hand me those depositions, young sir." He adjusted his spectacles. "Ah! She was implicated; she was one of the band."

A loud denial erupted simultaneously from Denys and Gerard.

"No need to deafen me," retorted the alderman. "It is written in black and white. 'Jean Hardy (one of the thieves), when questioned, confessed that—hmm? Yes, here it is. 'And that the girl Manon was the decoy, and her sweetheart was Georges Vipont, one of the band; and hanged last month; and that she has been depressed ever since, openly blaming the band for his death, stating that if they had not been rank cowards, he would never have been caught, and it is his opinion she betrayed them out of pure spite—and'

"His opinion!" cried Gerard indignantly. "What significance does the opinion of a cutthroat hold, one burning for revenge against her who delivered him to justice? If you accept that, what value is his testimony? Is a thief never a liar? Is he not always a liar? And here is a motive to lie: revenge, which is the strongest of all passions. Oh, sir, what madness to question a detected felon and listen to him fabricating an honest life—as if he were a truthful man swearing in broad daylight with his true hand on the Gospel!"

"Young man," said the alderman, "restrain your heat in the presence of authority! By your tone, I find you are a stranger. Know then that in this land we question everyone. We are not so weak as to hope to discover the truth by closing either our left or right ear."

"And so you would listen to Satan bellying the saints!"

"Nonsense! The law deals only with men and women, and these cannot utter a story entirely composed of falsehoods, however hard they try. Therefore, we do not shut the barn door against any man's grain. Once admitted, we merely winnow and sift. Who told you I swallowed the thief's story whole like clear water? Not so. I credited only as much as was corroborated by superior evidence."

"Superior evidence?" Gerard looked bewildered. "Who else but the thieves would speak against her?"

"Marry, herself."

"Herself, sir? Did you question her as well?"

"I tell you we question everyone. Here is her deposition; can you read? Read it yourself, then."

Gerard glanced at Denys and read Manon's statement aloud.

"I am a native of Epinal. Two years ago I left my home due to misfortune; I could not tolerate the man they forced upon me. My father beat me, and I fled. I went into service, but left when the mistress became jealous of me. Her stated reason for dismissing me was my sauciness. Last year, I stood in the marketplace seeking employment alongside other girls. The landlord of 'The Fair Star' hired me. I remained with him for eleven months. A young man courted me, and I loved him. I discovered that travelers arrived and never departed again. I informed my lover. He commanded silence and threatened me. I learned my lover belonged to a band of thieves. When travelers were to be robbed, the landlord would leave and signal the band to emerge. Then I wept and prayed for the travelers' souls. I never spoke. A month ago, my lover died.

"The soldier reminded me of my lover; he was bearded just like the one I lost. I cannot say whether I should have intervened had he lacked a beard. I regret telling now."

The paper nearly slipped from Gerard's grasp. For the first time, he perceived that Manon's life hung by a thread. Knowing the rigid laws and the relentless men who enforced them, he threw himself suddenly to his knees before the alderman. "Oh, sir! Consider the difference between those cruel men and this poor, weak woman! Could you have the heart to send her to the same death as them? Could you condemn us to watch her slaughter, someone who, risking her life for ours, is now in peril? Alas, sir! Show me and my companion some pity, if you have none for her, poor soul. Denys and I are true men, and you will rend our hearts if you kill that innocent, simple girl. What can we do? What remains for us but to cut our throats at the foot of her gallows?"

The alderman was tough yet mortal; Gerard's prayers and agitation first astonished, then touched him. He displayed this in a peculiar manner, becoming peevish and fretful. "There, rise, do," he said. "I doubt anyone would speak so many words for me. Ho, Daniel! Fetch the town clerk."

As the functionary entered from an adjacent room, the alderman continued, "Here is a foolish lad fretting about that girl. Can we stretch a point? Shall we admit her to bear witness and question her favorably?"

The town clerk was a man of strict impossibilities.

"Nay, sir, we cannot do that; she was not involved in this affair. Had she been an accessory, we might have offered a pardon in exchange for her testimony."

Gerard burst in, "But she did better! Instead of being an accessory, she stayed the crime; she volunteered as a witness by rushing here with the tale."

"Tush, young man, 'tis a matter of law." The alderman and the clerk engaged in a lengthy debate, the one insisting, the other denying, that she stood legally as sound as if she had been an accomplice to the attempted robbery of the travelers. Fortunately for Manon, the alderman, irritated by the clerk's repetition of his inability to act, declared, "I will show him I can do whatever I choose."

Consequently, he released Manon, securing her release through the landlord of "The White Hart" acting as her bondsman, and Denys depositing five gold pieces. The girl promised, though requiring some coaxing from Denys, to appear as a witness. The alderman liberated her but eased his conscience by explaining his leniency in his own terms: "The town must buy a new rope for every execution and present it to the executioner, or compensate him in money; in his estimation, she was not worth this municipal expense, whereas determined characters like her late confederates certainly were."

Thus, Denys and Gerard escorted her away. Gerard danced around her in joy, while Denys bolstered her spirits by assuring her of the removal of a troublesome figurehead. Yet, she wept ominously. On the road to "The White Hart," the public discovered her story through the archers, who naturally recounted it passionately in her favor. Following her with cheers and encouragement, the crowd swelled until, backed by numbers, she regained her courage. The landlord of the inn, seeing at a glance that her presence would attract custom, received her politely and assigned her an upper chamber. There, she secluded herself, and being alone, she poured out her tears anew.

Poor little mind, it was like a ripple, up and down, down and up, endlessly oscillating. Bidding the landlord treat her kindly while keeping her effectively imprisoned without her realizing it, the friends departed. Lo! As they stepped onto the street, they witnessed two processions approaching from opposite directions. One was large, accompanied by noise, howls, and those indescribable cries by which rough natures occasionally reveal a kinship to the beasts of the field and forest—a connection usually hidden. The other procession was sparse, attended by a few nuns and friars, moving slowly and silently.

These were the prisoners heading to exposure in the marketplace, and the gathered bones of the victims being transported to the churchyard.

The two met in the narrow street nearly at the inn door, unable to pass each other for a considerable time. The bier bearing the relics of mortality became wedged against the cart carrying the men who had turned those bones into what they were, destined within hours to meet the same fate themselves. The mob lacked the quick wit to immediately grasp the grim irony of this encounter. Finally, a woman cried out, "Look at your work, ye dogs!" The crowd seized upon this like wildfire, erupting in a horrific yell. The culprits groaned, attempting to hide their heads on their chests, but failed, their hands bound. They stood there, images of pale, hollow-eyed despair, gazing longingly at the bier and envying those they had sent ahead on the dark path they were now forced to tread.

The two men responsible for both processions stood and watched gravely. Even Manon, hearing the commotion, crept to

The condemned criminals were ordered to stand bound in the marketplace for two hours, a precaution intended to allow any witnesses to identify them or testify to other crimes they might have committed. They endured the full duration, yet no new accusations arose against them. This lack of evidence was hardly surprising; they were creatures of the night, vampires who preyed upon weary travelers in the darkness, mostly strangers.

However, just as the officers prepared to release them, a terrible scream erupted from the crowd. A woman pointed a trembling finger at one of the prisoners, her eyes nearly popping from their sockets. Before she could utter a word, she collapsed, fainting dead away.

Men and women surged around her, driven by a mix of concern and morbid curiosity. As consciousness slowly returned to the victim, speculation filled the air.

"'Twas at him she pointed!" someone cried.
"Nay, 'twas at this one!" countered another.
"No, no," insisted a third, "she meant that hound with the hair hanging loose around his neck."

These guesses were abruptly silenced. The moment the woman regained her senses, she lunged at the man with the dangling hair like a lioness. "My child! Man! Man! Give me back my child!" she screamed, seizing the glossy golden locks the officers had tied around his neck. She tore the hair from him and buried her face in it, kissing it desperately. Only then did her confused mind clear; realizing through this tragic token that her lost daughter was dead, she sank to the ground, shrieking and sobbing uncontrollably.

Instantly, the crowd turned savagely toward the accused man. With a unified roar, they rushed upon him. In those days, every citizen carried a weapon at their girdle, and justice was swift. His life ended quickly. Yet, Denys drew his sword immediately, shouting, *"A moi, camarades!"* (To me, comrades!), holding the mob at bay. "Who touches him dies!" he commanded. Several archers backed him up, struggling with difficulty to protect the man from the frenzied throng while Denys pleaded with the rioters.

"What sort of vengeance is this?" Denys shouted, trying to reason with them. "Are you so mad as to rob the wheel and give the vermin an easy death?"

The archers' steel kept the mob passive; Denys's words had little effect, and the crowd continued to growl with flashing eyes. Seeing the danger, the municipal officers gathered around, forming a guard with the archers, and prudently escorted the accused back to jail.

The mob hooted at both the prisoners and the guards indiscriminately. Once Denys confirmed the others were safely locked away, he headed for "The White Hart" inn, expecting to find Gerard there.

On his way, he spotted two girls working at a first-floor window. He saluted them, and they smiled warmly. Conversation ensued; their manners were easy, their complexions radiant. Invited them to dine at the inn, they initially refused, but eventually relented. Surrounded by this charming company, Denys momentarily forgot his worry for Gerard, who was currently being carted off to prison.

Meanwhile, Gerard, having partially recovered his wits, suddenly halted and demanded to know by what authority he was being arrested.
"By the vice-baillie's," the constable replied.
"The vice-baillie? Alas! What have I, a stranger, done to offend such a magistrate? This charge of sorcery must be a pretext. I am no sorcerer, but a poor, truthful youth far from home."

Such vagueness disgusted the officer. "Show him the *capias*, Jacques," the constable ordered.

Jacques held out the writ, keeping it about a yard and a half from Gerard's face. Simultaneously, the large constable pinned Gerard down. Both officers were on edge, fearing the prisoner might snatch the document, to which they attributed superstitious power.

But the poor prisoner had no such thoughts. He merely craned his neck to read it. To his infinite astonishment, he discovered the vice-bailiff who had signed the writ was the very friendly alderman he had spoken to earlier. Taking courage, Gerard assured his captors that there must be an error and requested to be taken before the alderman.

"What say you to that, Jacques?"
"Impossible. We have no orders to take him before his worship. Read the writ!"
"Nay, but good kind fellows, what harm can it be? I will give you each an ecu."
"Humph!" Jacques hesitated. "I say we have no orders *not* to take him to his worship. Read the writ!"
"Then let us take him to prison via his worship's residence."

Agreed. They accepted the money and warned Gerard that they were doing him a favor. He sensed they desired gratitude as much as silver. He attempted to appease their greed, but the words stuck in his throat; feigning was not his strength.

Entering the alderman's presence, Gerard's heart hammered in his chest. Begging with a faltering voice, he asked what offense he had committed since leaving that very room with Manon and Denys.
"Nought that I know of," the alderman replied calmly.
When the writ was produced, the alderman admitted signing it at dawn. "I am growing old, and my memory fails me. I entirely forgot your specific offense amidst discussions regarding the girl... but I recall now. All is well. You are the one I committed for sorcery. Wait! Before you go to gaol, you shall hear what your accuser has to say. Run and fetch him."

The accuser proved difficult to locate immediately. Growing impatient, the alderman explained the core charge: that Gerard had set a dead body ablaze with diabolical fire that burned without consuming. "And if this is true, young man, I am sorry for you, for you will assuredly burn with the fire of good pine logs in the marketplace of Neufchâteau."

"Oh, sir, for pity's sake, let me speak with his Reverence, the Cure!" Gerard pleaded.
The alderman advised against it. "The Church is harder upon sorcerers than the corporation."
"But, sir, I am innocent!" Gerard protested, sounding part snarl, part whimper.
"Oh, if you believe yourself innocent—officer, escort him to the Cure, but ensure he does not escape you. 'Innocent,' quotha?"

They found the Cure in his doublet, repairing a wheelbarrow. Gerard recounted everything, appealing pitifully to him. "For merely using a little phosphorus in self-defense against cutthroats, they intend to hang me."

It proved fortunate for the magician that he had already confessed his tale fully to the Cure. Thus, the shrewd cleric held the situation by the right end of the stick, whereas the corporation gripped it by the handle. The Cure looked exceedingly grave and declared, "I must question you privately on this untoward affair." He led Gerard into a private room, ordering the officer to stand guard outside and remain ready to enter if summoned. The massive constable stood trembling outside the door, expecting the room to vanish and leave a stench of brimstone.

Instantly alone, the Cure's expression relaxed, and he became himself again.
"Show me the trick," he said, his curiosity piqued.
"I cannot, sir, unless the room is darkened."
The Cure swiftly blocked out the light with a wooden shutter. "Now, then."
"But on what shall I perform it?" Gerard asked. "There is no dead face here; that is what made it seem so dreadful."
The Cure groped around the room. "Good; here is an image. It is my patron saint."
"Heaven forbid! That would be profanation!"
"Pshaw! Will it rub off?"
"Ay, but it goes against me to take such liberties with a saint," the sorcerer objected.
"Fiddlesticks!" exclaimed the divine.
"To be sure, performing it on his holiness will demonstrate to your reverence that it is no Satanic art."
"Perhaps that is why I proposed it," the Cure said subtly.

Encouraged, Gerard ignited the eyes and nostrils of the image, causing the Cure to jump. He then lit patches of the hair until the entire face glowed like a glowworm.
"By'r Lady!" shouted the Cure. "'Tis strange, and small my wonder that they took you for a magician, seeing a dead face thus fired. Now, come along with me!"

Donning his grey gown and great hat, the Cure led Gerard within minutes to the alderman. Standing beside the alderman, poisoning his mind, was the accuser—a singular figure in red hose and red shoes, wearing a black gown with blue bands and a cocked hat.

After saluting the alderman, the Cure turned to the accuser with good humor. "So, Mangis, at your work again, babbling away honest men's lives! Come, your worship, this is the old tale! Two of a trade can never agree. Here is Mangis, who professes sorcery and would sell himself to Satan tonight, but Satan is not so weak as to buy what he can have gratis. This Mangis, who wishes to be a sorcerer, is actually only a quack doctor, accusing a true lad of magic who merely used a secret of chemistry well known to me and all churchmen."

"But he is no churchman

Outside the town walls, the road was blanketed in fresh snow, heavily trampled by the countless tracks of wolves. Every footprint bore witness to their passage.

"We acted wisely in following the old man's counsel, Denys," Gerard remarked.

"Aye, we did," Denys agreed. "In fact, reflecting on it now, I recall hearing them last night—scuttling beneath our window, howling and whining for human flesh in that marketplace. Yet, not a single fat burgher showed pity for those poor vagrants, dropping a coin from their windows."

Gerard offered a smile, though it carried an air of distant abstraction. Together, they trudged on in silence.

"What are you contemplating so deeply?" Denys asked eventually.

"Your goodness," Gerard replied.

Denys bristled at the answer. While he possessed a remarkable tolerance for genuine impudence due to his naturally good humor, he would occasionally flare up where no just cause for anger existed.

"A civil inquiry deserves a civil response," he said dryly.

"Alas, I meant no offense," Gerard protested.

"Then why feign that you were pondering my goodness, knowing full well there is no goodness beneath my skin?"

"If anyone else had spoken thus, I would have retorted, 'Thou liest.' But to thee, I say, 'Thou hast no eye for men's virtues, only for women's.' Once more, I defy your unreasonable temper and affirm I was thinking of your kindness shown last night. You intended to marry me to the 'Golden Head'—or rather, the 'Golden Veal Head'—thereby leaving yourself entirely solitary."

"Oh, are you there, lad?" Denys recovered his cheerfulness instantly. "Truth be told, I meant that not out of goodness, but out of friendship and true fellowship. Let me confess, my young master, that my conscience pricks me even now for allowing you to turn your back on fortune and peaceful days. A truer friend might have intervened and somewhat hindered you. Then perhaps you would have lain smarting at the 'Golden Head' for a month or two; that skittish lass would have tended to you tenderly, and all would have been well. I had the blade ready to strike, but remembering how you detest pain—even a mere scratch—my cowardly heart failed me at the critical moment."

Denys wore an expression of humble apology for his lack of virtuous resolve when the path of duty appeared so clear. Gerard raised his eyebrows in astonishment at this monstrous yet thoroughly characteristic revelation; however, this new and delicate aspect of their friendship—the question of whether one ought to sever the Achilles' tendon of a beloved friend in acts of love—was never discussed. An incident soon intervened.

"Behold, one approaches from behind riding upon his neighbor's mule!" Denys shouted.

Gerard turned around.

"And how do you know it is not his own, pray?"

"Oh, blind fool! Because he rides it with no discretion." Indeed, the man came galloping like a fury. What astonished the friends most was that upon reaching them, the rustic rider's eyes widened saucer-like, and he jerked the reins so violently and abruptly that the mule planted its forelegs and slid between the pedestrians like a four-legged table on castors.

"I believe you are from the 'Golden Head,'" the rider called out.

They assented.

"Which of you is the younger?"

"He who was born later," Denys winked at his companion.

"Mercy upon the news!" the rider exclaimed. "Come, divine then!"

"And shall we?"

"Certainly. Your beard is ripe; your companion's is green. He shall be the younger. Here, youngster." He extended a paper packet. "You left this at the 'Golden Head,' and our mistress sends it to you."

"Nay, good fellow, I think I left nothing," Gerard said, feeling his pouch.

"Would you make our burgess a liar?" the rustic reproached, demanding a tip even more sharply before charging off ventre à terre.

"Nay, you shall receive a tip," Gerard said, handing him a small coin.

"A la bonne heure!" cried the clown, his features beaming with disproportionate joy. "The Virgin go with you! Come up, Jenny!" He returned "stomach to earth," as his countrymen phrase it.

Gerard untied the packet. It was roughly six inches square, containing another packet, which held yet another, and so on. By the fourth layer, he hurled the entire bundle into the snow.

Denys retrieved it and rebuked his petulance, excusing himself on the grounds of his hatred for affectation. Denys testified, "'The great toe of the little daughter of Herodias'—there was no affectation here, only a woman's clever wit. Doubtless the wrappings concealed something that, out of delicacy or her sex's cunning, she would not wish her hind to see bestowed upon a young man: namely, your garter."

"I wear none," Gerard noted.

"Then hers; or perhaps a lock of her hair. What is this? A piece of raw silk, fresh from the cocoon. Well, of all the love tokens!"

"Now, who but you ever dreamed she was so base as to send me love tokens? I saw no harm in her—save for her hands."

"Wait, here is something hard lurking within this soft nest. Come forth, I say, little nestling! Saints and pikes! Look at this!"

It was a gold ring featuring a large amethyst, glowing and sparkling with a full color yet pure as crystal.

"How lovely!" Gerard said innocently.

"And here is something written; read it yourself! I do not read as fluently as others when I know not the subject beforehand."

Gerard took the paper. "'Tis a posy, and fairly written." He read the lines, blushing like a maiden. They were remarkably naive, easily translated into English:

"The little dove!" Denys cooed. "The great owl! To risk her good name thus. However, thank Heaven she has played this prank with an honest lad who will never expose her folly. But oh, the perversity! Could she not bestow her affection upon thee?"

Denys sighed and shrugged. "Upon thee, who art as ripe for folly as she herself?"

Denys admitted that his young friend had echoed his very thoughts. It was strangely paradoxical to him that a damsel with eyes in her head should pass by a man and bestow her affections upon a boy. Still, he could not deny the bounty of Nature. Boys were human beings after all, and but for this occasional caprice of women, their lot would be too terrible; they would remain forever in the shade, blighted and achieving nothing, since only the fair could transform such unpromising material as a boy into a man.

Gerard interrupted this flattering discourse to request Denys's acceptance of the lady's ring. Denys refused it flatly, insisting that Gerard return immediately to the "Golden Head," ring and all, acting like a man and not allowing a poor girl to hold out her arms in vain.

"Her hands, you mean?"

"Her hand, holding the 'Golden Head'."

Failing in this, Denys attempted to place the ring on his friend's finger. Gerard declined.

"I wear a ring already."

"What, that sorry trinket? Why, 'tis pewter or tin at best; whereas this is virgin gold, besides the jewel."

"Yes, but Margaret gave me this one, and I value it above rubies. I will neither part with it nor give it a rival," Gerard declared, kissing the base metal and bidding it fear nothing.

"I see the owl has sent her ring to a goose," Denys said sorrowfully. Nevertheless, he persuaded Gerard to fasten it inside his bonnet. To this, the lad readily consented. For sovereign qualities were universally attributed to certain jewels, and the amethyst ranked high among these precious talismans.

Once this was arranged, Gerard earnestly requested his friend to let the matter drop, noting that speaking of the opposite sex to him made him pine so desperately for Margaret that he felt almost unmanned by the thought that each step took him farther from her.

"I am no general lover, Denys. There is room in my heart for one sweetheart and one friend. I am far from my dear mistress; and my friend, a few leagues more, and I must lose him too. Oh, let me drink your friendship pure while I may, and not dilute it with any of these foolish females."

"And shalt, honey-pot, and shalt," Denys said kindly.

"But regarding my leaving you at Remiremont, do not count on that! For" (swearing three times consecutively) "if I do... Nay, I shall propose to you to stay forty-eight hours there, while I kiss my mother and sisters, and the females generally, and then we shall proceed together to the sea."

"Denys! Denys!"

"Neither Denys nor me! 'Tis settled. Gainsay me not! Or I shall go with you to Rome. Why not? His Holiness the Pope always has some

Rough travel holds a peculiar charm for us Englishmen, and so do the chronicles of our journeys—even if the traveler is no pilgrim seeking love. Antique friendships possess at least the fascination of fossils. Yet, since the true heart of this narrative beats in Holland, it is time to return there, to those ordinary people and events that constitute the fabric of life across all ages.

Jorian Ketel arrived at Peter's house to claim Margaret's promise. However, Margaret lay ill in bed. Upon hearing his purpose, Peter affronted him, warning him to leave the premises immediately. Those standing nearby were ready to dunk him in water, for both father and daughter were beloved figures, and the entire saga was the talk of everyone. The citizens of Sevenbergen were in a state of heated, indiscriminate agitation—a symptom of intense popular sympathy. Humiliated, Jorian departed in a fury, regretting his earlier kindness. Such repentance is not uncommon and carries the merit of sincerity.

Meanwhile, Dierich Brower, caught at "The Three Kings" attempting to buy information about Martin Wittenhaagen's location by getting a chatterbox drunk, was seized and thrown into a horse pond. He was threatened with further punishment should he ever appear in the borough again. Furthermore, fueled by municipal jealousy, the burgomaster of Sevenbergen sent a formal letter to the burgomaster of Tergou. The missive reminded the latter that he had overstepped legal boundaries and requested that any future complaints—real or imagined—against Sevenbergen's townsfolk be directed solely to Sevenbergen's authorities.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, suppressing his rage at this rebuke, replied with a polite message. He claimed the individual he had pursued to Sevenbergen was a Tergovan named Gerard who had stolen the town records. Since Gerard had fled abroad, likely taking the documents with him, the matter was concluded. Thus, Ghysbrecht made a virtue of necessity. In truth, however, his composure was merely a mask. Baffled by the situation in Sevenbergen, he shifted his focus elsewhere. He dispatched emissaries to the household at Tergou to discover where Gerard had fled. To his infinite surprise, they knew nothing. This uncertainty gnawed at him, fueling a fear that Gerard was merely hiding in the neighborhood, waiting to make a discovery and exact terrible revenge.

From this point forward, Dierich and others noted a marked deterioration in Ghysbrecht's demeanor. He became moody and irritable, haunted by a dread that settled heavily upon him. His eyes darted furtively, as if expecting a blow from an unknown direction. Having made others wretched had failed to bring him happiness, for rarely does inflicting pain on others bring joy. The small family at Tergou, which might otherwise have resolved its differences without exiling *spem gregis* (hope of the flock) to a distant land, retained its outward appearance but lost its inner simplicity. Little Kate knew the extent of Cornelis and Sybrandt's role in banishing Gerard. Though she feared causing further strife and thus never spoke to her mother, there were moments when she shuddered at their mere presence and blushed at their hypocritical expressions of regret.

Catherine, possessing a woman's keen vigilance and subtlety, observed these shifts. She said nothing aloud but pondered quietly, watching for more evidence. The "black sheep" of the family, in their attempts to share the general gloom, managed to deceive their father and Giles temporarily. Yet, the demure satisfaction lurking beneath their actions could not escape the sharp eyes of the women—"Noting all, seeming to note nothing." Consequently, mistrust and suspicion replaced the memory of Gerard's intelligent face, which had once brightened the entire circle, remaining unnoticed until it vanished.

As for the elderly hosier, his pride had been wounded by his son's disobedience. He bore his grief stiffly, striving never to utter Gerard's name. Beneath his stoic exterior, however, Nature tugged insistently at his heartstrings. One anxiety he never feigned to hide: "If I but knew where the boy is, and that his life and health remain safe, my cares would be minimal," he would often say, followed by a deep sigh. One wonders if Gerard had simply walked through the door at that moment; surely, there would have been many tears and embraces, and few, if any, reproaches.

Then came a shock to the old couple: publicity. Within a week of Gerard's departure, his adventures were the subject of every conversation. Worse still, public sympathy rallied fervently behind the lovers and against Gerard's "cruel" parents and the meddling burgomaster who had intruded into a private affair.

"Mother," Kate said softly, "it is known throughout the town that Margaret is suffering from a fever—a burning fever. Her father fears greatly for her."

"Margaret? Which Margaret?" Catherine asked, feigning a treacherous calm.

"Oh, Mother! Who else could I mean? Why, Gerard's Margaret."

"Gerard's Margaret!" Catherine shrieked. "How dare you speak such a word to me? I warn you never to mention that hussy's name in this house, that she has defiled. She is the ruin of my poor boy, the flower of my flock. Because of her, he is not a holy priest among us but roams the world, leaving me a desolate, broken-hearted mother. Do not cry, my child; I act wrongly speaking so harshly. But oh, Kate! You do not understand a mother's heart. I endure before you all; I must swallow my fears. Yet at night, I see him in my dreams, always plagued by some trouble. Sometimes he is torn by wild beasts; other times held captive by robbers, their cruel knives raised to strike his pale face—one thinks it would move a stone. Oh! When I recall that, while I sit here in comfort, perhaps my poor boy lies dead in some savage land because of that girl... her very name is poison to me. I tremble whenever I hear it."

"I shall say nothing and do nothing to grieve you further, Mother," Kate replied tenderly, though she sighed deeply.

She whose name was so fiercely forbidden within these walls was widely discussed and even pitied outside. All of Sevenbergen felt sorry for her. Young men and maidens cast pitying glances as they passed the small window where the village beauty lay "dying for love." In this familiar phrase, they underestimated her spirit and selflessness. Gerard was alive, and she remained too loyal to doubt his constancy. Her father was dear to her and helpless. Had she not been physically weak, her love for Gerard would not have prevented her from fulfilling her duties, though she might have performed them with a drooping head and heavy heart.

However, physical and mental strain had triggered a violent fever. Only her youth and strong constitution saved her. The illness eventually subsided, leaving her in a terrifying state of debility where life felt like a burden. It is during such times that love and friendship become mortal angels, offering comfort in their voices and healing in their hands. But this poor girl had to find a way back to life and vigor on her own. For many days, she lay alone as heavy hours rolled over her like leaden waves. In her weakened state, existence seemed burdensome, and life felt like something already passed. Unable to strive for recovery, she often lay motionless for hours, tears gently welling in her eyes.

One day, awakening from an uneasy sleep, she found two women in her room. One was a servant; the other, distinguished by deep fur lining her collar and sleeves, appeared to be a person of consequence. A narrow band of silvery hair, spared by her hairstyle, indicated she was past the age when sensible women conceal their years. Both women looked kind and friendly. Margaret attempted to rise, but the older lady gently pressed a hand against her.

"Lie still, sweetheart. We come not to disturb you, but to comfort you, God willing. Cheer up a bit, and tell us, who do you think we are?"

"Nay, madam, I know you, though I have never seen you before. You are Dame Van Eyck, and this is Reicht Heynes. Gerard has often spoken of you and your goodness to him. Madam, he has no friend like you near him now," she said, sinking back onto the pillows as tears instantly filled her eyes.

The kindly Reicht Heynes began to weep for companionship, but her mistress chided her gently. "Well, you are a pretty one for a sickroom," she remarked, employing a wealth of innocent artistry to cheer the patient, achieving some modest success. An old woman who has witnessed life's trials is a sovereign blessing beside a sorrowful young woman. Knowing what to say and what to avoid, how to soothe and engage, she soon had Margaret resting her head on her shoulder rather than the pillow, her soft eyes gazing upward with gentle gratitude.

"Ahh! This is hair," the old lady murmured, running her fingers through it. "Come, Reicht, let us admire it!"

Reicht approached, handling it and praising it sincerely. The poor owner, not entirely immune to flattery due to her proximity to death, admitted, "In truth, madam, I used to think it hideous; but he praised it, and ever since, I have been almost vain of it, saints forgive me. You know how foolish those are who love."

"They are greater fools who do not," the old lady replied sharply.

Margaret opened her lovely eyes to seek

Some grow old in heart at forty, others remain young at eighty. Margaret Van Eyck's heart was an evergreen tree, blooming perpetually. She loved her young namesake, Margaret Brandt, with a fervor that defied her age. Yet, this affection was no fleeting caprice; Margaret possessed a sharp insight into character, recognizing in the younger woman a genuineness rare among women of her own sex. Beyond this sterling quality, Margaret Brandt held a powerful ally in the bosom of the elder artist: human nature itself.

It is strange, perhaps, to the unobservant, that our hearts warm more readily toward those whom we have benefited than toward our benefactors. Ancient Greek philosophers noted this tendency, but the British bard Homer has etched it into immortal verse:

> "I heard, and thought how side by side
> We two had stemmed the battle's tide
> In many a well-debated field,
> Where Bertram's breast was Philip's shield.
> I thought on Darien's deserts pale,
> Where Death bestrides the evening gale,
> How o'er my friend my cloak I threw,
> And fenceless faced the deadly dew.
> I thought on Quariana's cliff,
> Where, rescued from our foundering skiff,
> Through the white breakers' wrath I bore
> Exhausted Bertram to the shore:
> And when his side an arrow found,
> I sucked the Indian's venom'd wound.
> These thoughts like torrents rushed along
> To sweep away my purpose strong."

Mark this! An assassin's hand is stayed not by memories of favors received, but by recollections of kindnesses conferred.

Margaret Van Eyck had shown wonderful kindness to Margaret Brandt. She had broken her own rigid habits to visit the sickbed, nursing, soothing, and petting the young woman until she was cured more effectively than any physician could have hoped. Consequently, her heart opened fully to the recipient of her goodness. She now loved Margaret with a tenderness surpassing even her love for Gerard, though initially, her visits had been motivated solely by concern for Gerard's welfare.

Thus, when she beheld the roses blooming on Margaret's cheek and read the fragment of parchment that caused such color, she surrendered her own plans without a murmur.

"Sweetheart," she said gently, "I had wished for him to remain in Italy for five or six years, returning wealthy and, above all, a finished artist. But your happiness comes first. Seeing you cannot live without him, we must hasten his return home."

"Oh, madam! You see my very thoughts!" the young woman replied, hanging her head and blushing. "But how shall I let him know? That exceeds my skill. He has departed for Italy, though I know not which city he frequents. Wait! He named the places he must visit: Florence and Rome." Yet hesitation clouded her resolve. Being a sensible girl, she feared a letter addressed simply "My Gerard—Italy" might miscarry. She looked imploringly at her friend for counsel.

"You have come to the right place, and at the right time," declared the old lady. "Hans Memling was with me today; he departs for Italy no later than next week, 'to improve his hand,' he claims. Not before necessity demanded it, I assure you."

"But how shall he find my Gerard?"

"Why, he knows your Gerard, child. They have dined here more than once, fitting like hand and glove. Since their trades are identical, they will traverse the same lands, and sooner or later, they must meet. Therefore, compose a long letter, copying this pardon into it, and I vouch for the messenger. Within six months at the latest, Gerard shall receive it. When he does, he will kiss it, press it to his bosom, and fly home. What are you smiling at? And why do your cheeks burn? What are you stifling? I cannot guess. Yes! Happy days approach for my little pearl."

Meanwhile, in the kitchen, Martin sat before the black-jack while Reicht Heynes spun beside him. Alas, she pumped him that night with relentless questions.

Hans Memling was indeed an old pupil of Jan Van Eyck and his sister. Though Margaret had sneered at his profession, he was a painter nonetheless—a good soul with one distinct flaw. He loved the "nipperkin, canakin, and the brown bowl" more than they deserved. This peculiar penchant prevented him from accumulating wealth, causing him frequently to seek meals and occasionally a groat from Margaret Van Eyck. Yet, this dependence created a bond; she knew he would not slight any commission she entrusted to him.

The letter was duly written and left with Margaret Van Eyck. The following week, as promised, Hans Memling returned from Flanders. Margaret handed him the letter and a piece of gold for his travel expenses. He seemed eager to depart.

"All the better," remarked the old artist; "he will reach Italy the sooner."

However, just as some horses burn with rage to start, only to require a whip after the first few yards, so too did Hans's haste cool into inertia once he reached the principal inn of Tergou and spotted two of his boon companions in the bay window. He entered for a farewell drink. When he offered to pay, they refused; he was embarking on a long journey, and they insisted on treating him—the landlord included.

The result was that his tongue loosened as if the wine were oil. Confiding in his jovial circle, he boasted of showing the Italians how to paint, recounted his exploits in battle having wielded a pike, and detailed his amorous conquests with women present to contradict their versions. In short, *plenus rimarum erat*; he flowed hither and thither with tales. Among the miscellaneous matters leaking forth, he blurted that he carried a letter to a townsman of theirs, one Gerard, a fine fellow. Adding, "You are all good fellows," he slapped Sybrandt on the back so heartily that he nearly drove the air from the man's lungs.

Sybrandt circled the table to evade this muscular greeting but listened intently, learning for the first time that Gerard had gone to Italy. Determined to verify the news, he feigned skepticism.

"My brother Gerard is never in Italy."

"Ye lie, ye cur!" roared Hans, instantly turning irascible and failing to recognize that the man sitting opposite was the very individual he had just praised and punched. "If he is ten times your brother, he is in Italy. What proof do you offer? Read this inscription!" He flung the letter onto the table.

Sybrandt picked it up, examined it gravely, and finally set it down, claiming he could not read. However, by some immense fortuity, one of the company could. Proud of this rare skill, he seized the document and read aloud:

"To Gerard Eliassoen, of Tergou. These by the hand of the trusty Hans Memling, with all speed."

"'Tis excellently penned," observed the reader, scrutinizing every letter.

"Ay!" declared Hans bombastically. "No wonder: it is written by a famous hand—that of Margaret, sister of Jan Van Eyck. Blessed and honored be her memory! She is an old friend of mine, Margaret Van Eyck."

Afterward, Miscellaneous Hans diverged into forty different topics.

Sybrandt slipped out of the gathering and sought Cornelis. Together, they pondered the news. Italy was a vast distance. Could they keep him there?

"Keep him there? Nothing would hold him long from his Margaret."

"Curse her!" spat Sybrandt. "Why didn't she die when she was about it?"

"She die? She would outlive the plague to vex us." Cornelis raged at her selfishness in refusing to die and thereby obliging them.

These two black sheep continually plotted, poisoning each other's minds until their corrupt hearts devised a scheme to keep Gerard in Italy forever, thus securing his share of their father's estate. Yet, planning was easy; execution required talent, and iniquity stalled. Suddenly, as if Satan whispered simultaneously into their ears, they both shouted:

They approached Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, who received them immediately. The man tortured by suspense clutches eagerly at any knowledge. Certainty is often painful, but rarely, like suspense, is it intolerable.

"You have news of Gerard?" he asked eagerly.

They recounted the letter and Hans Memling. He listened with restless eyes. "Who wrote the letter?"

"Margaret Van Eyck," they replied, assuming the content matched the signature.

"Are you sure?" Ghysbrecht moved to a drawer and produced a paper written by Margaret during negotiations for her house. "Was it written thus?"

"Yes. 'Tis the same hand," Sybrandt affirmed boldly.

"Good. Now, what do you desire of me?" Ghysbrecht asked, his heart pounding beneath a mask of feigned carelessness so convincing it staggered them. They fumbled with their bonnets, stammered, hesitated, and beat about the bush, gradually revealing their intent: they wanted a letter drafted to keep Gerard in Italy, proposing to substitute it for the one in Hans Memling's wallet. As they fumbled with their hats and their wicked schemes, vacillating between respect for the burgomaster and suspicion that he was as rogue as themselves, conflicting thoughts raced through the old man's mind.

Denys was positioned squarely between his companions, a precaution taken to prevent him from attempting flight in his state of madness. Carried away by an agony of grief and remorse, he raged against heaven and earth, alternating these outbursts with a silence so gloomy and sinister that his guards felt compelled to keep him disarmed. His torment stemmed from a bitter realization: for Denys's sake, Gerard had forsaken the direct German road to Rome, leaving the young man stranded alone in the heart of Burgundy. "He has parted from me," Denys cried, his voice breaking, "and now he may finally listen to reason. Poor wretch, he cannot bear solitude!"

They intercepted their leader just outside the town, halting the entire cavalcade at the *Tête d'Or*. The young landlady, though preoccupied with the Count and the Bastard, spotted Denys and asked anxiously where his young companion had gone. With a sudden burst of sorrow, Denys recounted everything, imploring her to send someone after Gerard. "Now that he is separated from me, he might heed my counsel," he pleaded. "Poor creature, he hates loneliness." The landlady merely tossed her head. "I fear I have shown him too much kindness already," she replied, turning slightly red. "Will you not?" "Certainly not," Denys snapped. Whereupon he unleashed a torrent of curses upon her. She turned her back, retreating while whimpering that she was unused to such treatment, yet she immediately ordered her stablehand to saddle two mules.

Denys rode north with his troop, silent and dejected, unaware that the truthful young woman had donned riding gear in less than forty minutes. Driven by urgency, she spurred away with her servant in the exact opposite direction. That evening, after a grueling march, the Bastard and his men arrived at "The White Hart." Their entry created a commotion, and it took some time before Manon recognized her old friend amidst the crowd. Upon spotting him, her face flushed, yet she offered no greeting, her demeanor suggesting a growing disdain for the humble origins through which she had risen. Denys observed her coldness but could not muster a smile; the inn itself seemed to mirror the memory of Gerard.

Before nightfall, the wind shifted. Manon entered the room and beckoned him with a single finger. Rising sullenly, accompanied by his guards, Denys followed her to a secluded corner. "I wish to speak privately," she whispered. "Do you grant me a favor?" he asked aloud, his tone sharp. "No," he declared loudly. "I am not in the mood to show kindness to man or woman. If I were to bestow a kindness, it would be upon a dog—and even then, only if I could help it."

"Alas, good Archer," Manon said humbly, "you once did me a great service, you and your pretty companion." "Indeed, Dame, you did," Denys conceded. "Well then, for his sake—what is required?" "You know my tale," she continued. "I was once unfortunate, but now I am respected. Yet today a woman spat in my face regarding him. As long as he hangs there, this shame will remain. I desire him taken down; alas!"

"With all my heart," Denys replied. "But I dare ask no one else. Will you do it?" "Not I, even were I not a prisoner," he stated firmly. At this stern refusal, the gentle Manon sighed, clasping her hands in despair. Denys reassured her, noting that there were soldiers of lesser standing who would accept half a crown for such a task. It was merely a matter of coin; if she could provide two angels, he would find two men to perform the dirty work at "The White Hart." Though the offer was unpalatable, Manon reflected that soldiers were transient creatures, drunk tonight and likely dead by tomorrow. Softly, she agreed, instructing him to send them out. "But pray tell them it is for a friend," she urged. "Let them not believe it is for me; I should sink into the earth. Times have changed."

Denys readily found warriors eager to earn an angel each so easily. He dispatched them and, dismissing the matter with contempt, sat brooding over his lost friend. Soon, Manon and the hired men reached an agreement, save for the timing of payment. The armored men itched for their reward, seeking it in advance. Manon hesitated, doubting their word. "Come with us, or watch from a distance," she suggested. "Me?" Manon exclaimed in horror. "I would sooner die than witness it." "Yet you would have done so," Denys countered gently. "Yes, for my need is great. Times have changed."

She had already forgotten her earlier advice to Denys. An hour later, the unpleasant reminder of their caterpillar-like existence ceased to mar the respectable matron's public life, and the grim eyes of the past stopped casting malignant glares over the white hind's clover field. In total, she secured herself an average husband and a fine house-dog, outliving everyone around her.

When the troops returned from executing the medieval maxim "Off with the old love," they were shocked to find the marketplace thronged with people—a stark contrast to its emptiness an hour prior. Conscience struck them hard; this was the result of meddling with the dead. However, the bolder of the two, emboldened by the darkness, crept forward alone. He stealthily merged into a gathering crowd and soon returned to his companion, speaking in a tone of reproach that lacked strict logic: "You foolish fool, it is nothing but a miracle."

Letters of fire had recently burned themselves onto the church wall, asking with a look of genuine curiosity why no Mass was being celebrated for the Duke during this time of turmoil. Many had witnessed this supernatural protestation before it gradually faded, leaving the crowd staring in stunned silence. Ultimately, the city council, unwilling to lag behind divine power in their loyalty to a temporal ruler, generously funded the required services. From this arrangement, our old friend, the Cure, derived a substantial sum in hard cash—a fact he certainly appreciated given his refined tastes. Yet, I would not detain you with such a trivial occurrence as a minor miracle if Denys had not urgently requested his weapons.

"Why disgrace him as well as break his heart?" someone asked. "Then swear on the cross of your sword not to leave the Bastard's service until the sedition is crushed." Reluctantly yielding to necessity, Denys recited three solemn vows, thereby recovering his arms and his liberty.

The troops halted at an inn known as "The Three Fish." Upon seeing Denys, Marion exclaimed, "I'm out of luck; who would have thought to see you again?" Noticing his somber expression rather than amusement at her blunt greeting, she inquired what troubled him. He confessed his plight. Marion, however, adopted a bright outlook. Gerard was too handsome and well-mannered to suffer harm; moreover, the women would invariably support him. Things must either go well or ill for him. In the former case, he would join a noble company bound for Rome; in the latter, he would return home, perhaps arriving before his friend, for he had fighting to do in Flanders by all accounts. She then produced her gold pieces, steadfastly refusing to accept a single coin despite his repeated urging.

Denys was somewhat persuaded by her logic, partly because she aligned with his own desires and partly because her honesty cheered him. It made him reflect more kindly on the world in which his poor little friend wandered alone. Meanwhile, foot soldiers in small groups were already heading toward Flanders, many penniless, relying on bailiffs to secure food and lodging from innkeepers along the way. Anthony of Burgundy intercepted numerous stragglers, gathering them under his banner until he entered Flanders at the head of six hundred men. Crossing the frontier, he was met by his brother Baldwyn, who brought men, arms, and provisions. Anthony organized his entire force and marched through several towns in battle array, receiving great acclaim and facing no impediments.

This display of loyalty drew mixed reactions. "This rebellion of ours is a joke," growled a soldier named Simon, who had chosen to be Denys's companion. Denys remained silent, yet he made a private vow to Saint Mars to kill Anthony of Burgundy should this rebellion—which had cost him Gerard—prove to be nothing more than a sham.

That afternoon, they spotted a heavily fortified town. A whisper spread through the small army that it was a disaffected stronghold. However, upon arrival, the great gate stood wide open, and the flanking towers were manned by only a single sentinel each. The advancing force relaxed their formation and marched carelessly. Just as they approached within a furlong, the drawbridge across the moat rose slowly and creakingly until it stood vertically against the fortress. The very moment it settled into this defensive position, the portcullis slammed down at the gate, and the towers and curtain walls bristled with lances and crossbows. A stern murmur rippled through the Bastard's front lines and spread to the rear.

"Halt!" Denys cried. The command echoed down the line, and they stopped. "Herald to the gate!" A pursuivant spurred out, halted twenty yards from the entrance, raised his bugle with the herald's flag trailing behind it, and blew a summons. A tall figure in brazen armor appeared over the gate. Fiery words exchanged between him and the herald were inaudible, yet their meaning was clear: the herald blew a single, keen, threatening note at the walls and galloped back with war in his eyes.

The Bastard stepped out of the line to meet him. Their heads had not been together for two seconds before the stranger turned in his saddle and shouted, "Pioneers, to the van!" Instantly, hedges were leveled, the force took the field, and encamped just out of range of the walls. Mounted officers flew south, east, and west to friendly towns to gather catapults, palisades, mantelets, raw hides, tar barrels, carpenters, provisions, and all materials necessary for a siege.

This bright prospect greatly cheered the drooping spirits of the soldiers. At the first clang of the portcullis, eyes brightened and temples flushed. When the herald returned with battle in his gaze, the effect was immediate; for the first time in many days, a soldier cried out, "Courage, everyone, the devil is dead!" If that great warrior heard, he surely would have grinned.

The besiegers established camp a furlong from the walls and began constructing roads. Pikemen remained in camp ready for assault, while sappers, pioneers, catapultiers, and crossbowmen moved forward. They opened the siege by filling the moat, mining, and attempting to breach the walls. Since much of this work had to be done under heavy fire from arrows, quarrels, bolts, stones, and rocks, these artisans needed a hundred eyes. They operated with a vigilance, individual intelligence, daring, and skill that made the siege fascinating, even amusing, to observers.

First, they advanced their carpenters behind rolling mantelets to erect a high, strong stockade on the very edge of the moat. Some lives were lost, but not many, thanks to a strong force of crossbowmen—including Denys—who rolled their mantelets up and rained shots over the workers' heads at every defender showing his nose or opening a loophole. Protected by this concentrated fire, they soon raised a high palisade between themselves and the ordinary missiles from the pierced masonry.

However, the besieged anticipated this. At night, they ran out wooden penthouses atop the curtains. The curtains featured square holes near the top designed to receive beams supporting these structures—the true defense of medieval forts. From here, the defenders delivered missiles with far greater freedom and range than could be achieved through the oblique, immovable loopholes of the curtain or the sloping crenelets of the higher towers. In response, the besiegers brought up mangonels to hurl huge stones at these woodworks, battering them to pieces. Simultaneously, they constructed a triangular wooden tower as high as the curtain, keeping it ready just out of shot. This was a terrifying sight for the defenders.

These wooden towers had captured many towns. The besiegers began mining beneath the section of the moat where the tower loomed, preparing a warm reception. They also mined elsewhere, aiming to undermine the square barbican and bring it crashing down. Throughout this, Denys stayed behind his mantelet alongside another arbalestrier, protecting the workmen and taking excellent shots. These actions eventually earned him the respect of an unseen archer, who periodically sent a winged compliment quivering into his mantelet.

One arrow struck within an inch of the narrow slit through which Denys was peering. "Peste!" he cried. "You shoot well, my friend. Come forth and receive my congratulations! Shall merit such as yours hide its head? Comrade, it is one of those cursed Englishmen with his half-ell shaft. I won't die until I've had a shot at London Wall."

On the side of the besieged, a figure soon attracted great attention by promenading under fire. It was a tall knight clad in complete brass, carrying a light but prodigiously long lance, which he used to direct the movements of the defenders. Whenever disaster befell the besiegers, this tall knight and his long lance were invariably involved.

"My young reader will ask, 'Why did not Denys shoot him?' Denys did shoot him; every day of his life. Other arbalestriers shot him; archers shot him. Everyone shot him. He was there to be shot, seemingly. But the abomination was that he did not mind being shot. Worse still, he became so demoralized he seemed unaware when he was hit. He walked his battlements under fire, like a sturdy captain pacing his deck in a storm, calmly oblivious to the April rain falling on his woolen armor."

Finally, the besiegers grew spiteful and refused to waste good steel on him, cursing his impenetrable coat of mail instead. He took those missiles like the rest. Gunpowder has since ruined warfare; war was always detrimental to humanity's solid interests. But in old times, it served a purpose: it painted well, sang divinely, and furnished Iliads. Invisible butchery under a pall of smoke a furlong thick leaves no one better off. A poet with a notebook may repeat, *"Suave etiam belli certamina magna tueri"* (It is pleasant even to behold the great contests of war), but the sentiment is hollow and smells of cuckoo. One cannot *tueri* (behold) anything but a horrid row. He didn't say, *"Suave etiam ingentem caliginem tueri per campos instructam"* (It is pleasant to behold vast darkness spread over fields). They managed better in the Middle Ages.

This siege was a small affair, yet a writer or minstrel could witness it and earn an honest penny by singing of it; thus, the sport was reasonable and served an end.

It was a bright, clear day, though not quite frost

A profound transformation seized Margaret Brandt. Her movements became mechanical, performed as if she were drifting through a dream. Should Peter utter a word with any suddenness, she would start violently, her gaze locking onto him with eyes wide with terror. Her visits to her beloved friend, Margaret Van Eyck, dwindled until they ceased entirely. When forced to endure the old lady's presence, Margaret offered no warmth in return; instead, she stood passive and trembling, occasionally shrinking back as though burned by the affection she could not reciprocate. Yet, the strangest alteration of all was her refusal to venture outdoors during the light of day. She would only emerge after dusk, wandering toward Tergou, and would not return until the dawn broke. She avoided even the sanctuary of morning prayers.

Eventually, Peter, though usually oblivious to such shifts, grew suspicious. "Why do you avoid the world?" he asked. "It seems to me the people stare at you."

One afternoon, Margaret Van Eyck pressed her gently. "Child, what troubles you? Why hide your sorrow from me?"

Margaret cast a frightened glance upward, and tears flooded her cheeks. "I have no sorrows, madam, save those I have wrought myself. I am treated with kindness I do not deserve, especially within this household."
