"Then why not visit me more often, my dear?"

"I come more often than I ought," she sighed heavily. "Reicht is crying for you," the old lady interrupted, pointing toward the nursery. "Go, child—what on earth is wrong?"

Speculating on the cause, Margaret Van Eyck concluded that Margaret must be humiliated by the disdain shown to her by Gerard's family. Resolved to intervene, she decided to reprimand the women of the house. The following day, donning her hood and cloak, accompanied by the weeping Reicht, she marched to the hosier's residence.

Catherine received her with great deference, thanking her profusely and shedding tears for her past kindness toward Gerard. Encouraged by this reception, Margaret Van Eyck turned her attention to Margaret Brandt. Instantly, Catherine's demeanor hardened; her eyes lost their moisture, her lips thinned, and her expression settled into the rigid, unyielding mask typical of obstinate, ignorant women. To try to soften such a visage was futile, akin to attempting to melt a brick wall. Margaret Van Eyck persisted, but in vain. Unable to tolerate being thwarted, the old lady grew provoked. With an abrupt, truncated curtsey, she turned and departed, leaving behind a Catherine who returned the gesture with an air of defiant insolence.

Stepping outside, Margaret Van Eyck encountered Reicht speaking quietly with a pale girl leaning on crutches. Pushing past them with flushed cheeks and a scornful toss of her head directed at the entire family, she was suddenly halted. A small, delicate hand slipped timidly into hers. Looking around, she met two eyes like doves', filled with tears that pleaded and thanked her simultaneously. Reading the complex depth of that gaze, Margaret's anger dissolved instantly. She stopped, kneeling to kiss Kate's forehead.

"I understand," she said softly. "Leave it to me."

Returning home, she recounted her journey. "I visited a house today where I witnessed both the commonplace and the miraculous. I saw a foolish, stubborn woman, and beside her, an angel incarnate—a face so beautiful that if I held my paints here, I would wish to repaint the world just to capture its likeness."

Little Kate lived up to this high praise. Seizing a quiet moment later, she shared what she had learned from Reicht Heynes: that Margaret had given her very blood for Gerard in the woods.

"Look, Mother, how she loves him!" Kate exclaimed.

"Who would not love him?"

"Oh, Mother, consider it! Poor creature."

"Aye, wench. She carries her own burdens, just as we do. I cannot bear the sight of blood, let alone my own."

Though this concession was gained, Kate's attempts to pursue the subject were abruptly cut short. Approximately a month later, a soldier of the Dalgetty clan, returning from service in Burgundy, delivered a letter to the hosier's house. Though he was away on business, the family gathered for supper. The soldier placed the envelope upon the table beside Catherine, declining any reward before departing for Sevenbergen.

Curiously, though none possessed the skill to decipher the script, they all knew immediately whose handwriting it was.

"And your father must be absent," cried Catherine. "Are you not ashamed? Not one among you can read your brother's letter."

While the words remained hieroglyphics to them, the message transcended language. There exists an art that speaks without words, unbound by the limitations of ink, capable of slipping directly from the eye into the heart and mind of both scholar and peasant. It crosses seas and frontiers losing nothing, needing no translator, for it writes a universal tongue. Upon seeing the drawing Gerard had sketched between the two brief paragraphs—a pair of hands clasped tightly—they understood instantly. Gerard was bidding them farewell.

As they gazed upon the simple sketch, recognizing his manner in every curve and line, Gerard seemed present, saying goodbye. The women wept until the image blurred before them. Giles whispered, "Poor Gerard!" in a tone foreign to his usual boisterous self. Even Cornelis and Sybrandt fell silent, overcome by a fleeting remorse and gloom.

But how could they obtain the written words without exposing their ignorance and vulnerability to a stranger?

"The Dame Van Eyck?" Kate suggested timidly.

"Yes, I shall go to her, Kate. She possesses a good heart. She loved Gerard dearly; she will be glad to hear of him. I was harsh with her when she visited, but I shall make my submission, and then she will tell me what my poor child wishes to convey."

She arrived swiftly at Margaret Van Eyck's home. Reicht ushered her into a room, announcing, "Wait a moment; she is praying."

Inside, a young woman sat pensively by the stove. Rising gracefully, she made way for the visitor.

"Thank you, young lady; these winter nights are biting, and your fire is a blessing," Catherine remarked, warming her hands while casting furtive glances from head to toe. The girl wore a plain wimple, yet her gown was trimmed with fur—a mark of superior rank in those times. What struck Catherine most, however, was the candor and modesty radiating from the young woman's face. Feeling certain of sympathy from such a noble countenance, she launched into conversation.

"Now, what brings you here, young lady? It is a letter! A letter from my poor boy, far away in some savage land. I blush to admit that none of us can read it. Do you perhaps know how?"

"Yes."

"You can? That is much to your credit, my dear. I fear she will not be long, but every minute feels like an hour to a longing mother."

"I will read it to you."

"Bless you, my dear! Bless you!"

In her genuine eagerness, Catherine failed to notice the suppressed urgency with which the hand extended to retrieve the letter, nor the tremor that gripped the fingers as they closed around it.

"Begin, then, please. I am wearying for it."

"The first words are, 'To my honoured parents.'"

"Ay! He always honored us, poor soul."

"'God and the saints have you in His holy keeping, and bless you by night and by day. Your one harsh deed is forgotten; your years of love remembered.'"

Catherine laid her hand over her bosom and sank back in her chair, letting out a long, shuddering sob.

"Then comes this, madam. It speaks for itself: 'a long farewell.'"

"Ay, go on. You give me sad comfort, but still, it is comfort."

"'To my brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt—Be content; you will see me no more!'"

"What does that mean? Ah!"

"'To my sister Kate. Little angel of my father's house. Be kind to her—for poor Gerard's sake: since grief to her is death to me.'"

Nature, rebelling against the girl's desperate struggle to maintain composure, finally broke. She collapsed from her chair, lying insensible with the letter clutched in her hand and her head resting on Catherine's lap.

Experienced women possess a calmness that frightens neither themselves nor others when a comrade faints; they do not hastily conjure supernatural explanations, but rather attribute the collapse to known physical causes, having witnessed them too often. Catherine moved with brisk efficiency. She laid the girl flat upon the floor, ensuring her head rested squarely on the ground, flung open the casement to admit fresh air, and loosened the tight bodice of the unconscious form. Only after these measures were complete did she turn to the door and call out, her voice ringing clearly through the room: "Come here, if you please."

Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht entered promptly, finding the young woman prone and Catherine striking her palms together in a rhythmic beat. "Oh, my poor girl!" cried Margaret, her face pale with alarm. "What have you done to her?"

"Me?" retorted Catherine, her tone sharp with irritation. "What has happened, then?"

"Nothing, madam," replied Margaret, though her cheeks flushed with rising anger. "Nothing more than is natural in her situation."

"You do well to speak so coolly," Margaret snapped, "especially since you are the cause of her situation."

"That I am not," declared Catherine bluntly. "Nor is any woman born."

"What!" exclaimed Margaret, indignant. "Was it not you and your husband who kept them apart? And now he has gone to Italy all alone. 'Situation' indeed! You have broken her heart amongst you."

"Why, madam? Who is it then? In Heaven's name! To hear you speak, one would think this was my Gerard's own lass. But that cannot be. This fur never cost less than five crowns the ell; besides, this young gentlewoman is a wife—or ought to be."

"Of course she ought," Catherine agreed coldly. "And who is the cause that she is none? Who came before them at the very altar?"

"God forgive them, whoever it was," said Catherine gravely. "It was not me, nor my man."

"Well," said the other, her anger softening slightly as she observed the girl stirring, "now that you have seen her, perhaps you will not be quite so bitter against her, madam. She is coming to, thank Heaven."

"Bitter against her?" laughed Catherine. "No, that is all over. Poor soul! Trouble behind her and trouble ahead of her; and to think of my setting her, of all living women, to read Gerard's letter to me! Ay, and that was what made her go off, I'll be sworn. She is coming to. What, sweetheart! Be not afraid; none are here but friends."

They raised the girl into an easy chair. As color began to creep back to her cheeks and lips, Catherine drew Margaret Van Eyck aside into a corner.

"Is she staying with you, if you please?" asked Catherine.

"No, madam."

"I wouldn't let her go back to Sevenbergen tonight, then."

"That is as she pleases. She still refuses to spend the night there."

"Ay, but you are older than she is; you can make her stay." Catherine leaned close, putting her mouth to Margaret Van Eyck's ear. It seemed hardly long enough to whisper a single word, let alone a full sentence. Yet, on certain topics, females can exchange thoughts between them with the speed of lightning. The old lady started, her eyes widening, and whispered back urgently:

"It is false! It is a calumny! It is monstrous! Look at her face. It is blasphemy to accuse such a countenance."

"Tut! Tut! Tut!" dismissed the other impatiently. "You might as well say this is not my hand. I ought to know; and I tell you it is exactly so."

Then, to Margaret Van Eyck's utter astonishment, she stepped forward, took the girl by the neck, and kissed her warmly. "I suffered for Gerard," she declared, her voice trembling with emotion, "and you shed your blood for him. His own words show me that I have been to blame—the very words you have read to me. Ay, Gerard, my child, I have held aloof from her; but I shall make it up to her once I begin. You are my daughter from this hour."

Another warm embrace sealed this hasty compact, and the impulsive woman departed. Margaret lay back in her chair, a feeble smile stealing over her face. Gerard's mother had kissed her and claimed her as a daughter; yet the next moment, she saw her old friend regarding her with a deeply vexed expression.

"I wonder you let that woman kiss you," Margaret murmured, half reproachful.

"Mother, or no mother, you would not have let her touch you if you knew what she whispered in my ear about you," Margaret continued softly.

"About me?" Margaret asked faintly.

"Ay, about you, whom she has never seen until tonight."

The old lady hesitated, choosing her words carefully, preparing to share her indignation, when an unexpected interruption silenced her. The young woman slid from her chair to her knees and began to pray piteously for pardon. From the fervor of her words and the anguish of her manner, a bystander would have gathered that she had inflicted some cruel wrong, some intolerable insult, upon her venerable friend.

The small gathering at the hosier's dwelling remained seated around the table, still digesting the weight of recent events, when the mother entered. Her gaze swept sharply across the circle before her, then she placed a folded letter flat upon the wood. Without hesitation, she began to recite its contents from memory, tracing the invisible lines with her finger—a futile gesture intended to convince herself, and perhaps the room, that she could truly read the words she was speaking. Suddenly, she lifted her head, fixing a piercing stare upon Cornelis and Sybrandt. Their eyes immediately lowered. In that instant, the tempest that had been simmering beneath the surface erupted fully upon them.

Catherine seemed to inflate with rage, ruffling her feathers like an agitated hen. From her lips poured a torrent of wisdom and nonsense alike, a mixture of grand invectives and petty insults that no man, however eloquent, could ever muster in a single stream, let alone a woman. A fair, albeit condensed, sample of her speech follows, delivered in one relentless breath:

"I have long harbored suspicions that you kindled the fire between Gerard and your father, setting that old rogue, Ghysbrecht, to stoke it. Now, here are Gerard's own written words confirming your treachery. You have driven your own flesh and blood into a distant land, robbing the mother who bore you of her darling—the pride of her eye, the joy of her heart. Yet, you remain consistent from end to end. When we were all boys together, my other children were a comfort; you were a curse—mischievous and cunning. You required a woman half a day merely to mend your torn garments. Why? Because labor wears cloth, but play tears it apart. Prudence arrives with the beard, but it never came to you. You were always the last to retire and the last to rise; for honesty sleeps early, and industry wakes at dawn. Where there are two 'lie-in-beds' in a household, there are inevitably a pair of ne'er-do-wells.

Often I have sat watching your conduct, wondering whence you sprang. You bear no resemblance to your father, nor are you akin to me—not even as a wasp resembles an ant. Surely, you were altered in the cradle, or perhaps a cuckoo dropped you upon my floor, for you possess neither our hands nor our hearts. Of all my offspring, none but you have ever mocked those whom God afflicted. Frequently, with my back turned, I heard you sneer at Giles for lacking size, and at my lily Kate for not being as sturdy as a Flemish mare. If you can rob a church, you certainly can do worse! For in the eyes of Him who created both Kate and Giles, and in mine who suffered for them—as I suffered for you—you are paltry, unfeeling, treasonous curs!

No, I will not hush, my daughter; they have filled the cup to overflowing. It requires a great deal to turn a mother's heart against the sons she has nurtured upon her knees. Many times I have closed my eyes to avoid seeing too clearly, biting my tongue lest your father discover what I knew; he would have cast you out instantly. But now, the cup is full. And where did you acquire all this wealth? For the past month, you have rolled in riches. You earned not a penny of it. May I never hear from another mouth how you obtained it! It is since that night you stayed out so late, returning with a swollen head, Cornelis. Sloth and greed are a terrible mismatch, my masters. Those who love money must either sweat or steal. Well, if you plundered any poor soul of it, it was surely some woman, I swear it; for a man would drive you off with his bare hand.

It matters not; it serves one purpose. It reveals how you intend to manage our affairs once they become yours. I have observed you, my lads, for some time. You have squandered a groat between you today. Meanwhile, I spend scarcely a groat per week and sustain you all, good and bad. Very well, I cease waiting for the shoes that might follow your coffins. This shop and this house shall never belong to you. Gerard is our heir—poor Gerard, whom you have exiled and done your utmost to destroy. Never address me as 'mother' again! Yet, you have made him tenfold dearer to me. My poor lost boy! Soon I shall see him again, hold him in my arms, and place him upon my knees. Yes, you may stare! You are too clever, yet not clever enough. You severed the stalk, but you left the seed—the seed that shall outgrow you and outlive you. Margaret Brandt is swift, and the child within her is Gerard's. What belongs to Gerard belongs to me. I have prayed to the saints that it might be a son, and it shall be—it must be.

Kate, when I discovered the truth, my very bowels yearned over her unborn child as if it were my own. He is our heir. He will outlive us. You will not; for a corrupt heart within a carcass is like a worm in a nut, quickly reducing the body to dust. So, Kate, retrieve Gerard's bib and tucker from the drawer you know of. One of these days, we shall carry them to Sevenbergen. We will borrow Peter Buyskens' cart and go to comfort Gerard's wife under her burden. She is his wife. Who is Ghysbrecht Van Swieten? Can he stand between a couple and the altar, and sever those united by God and the priest? She is my daughter, and I am as proud of her as I am of you, Kate. As for you, stay out of my sight for a while, for you are like the black dog in my eyes."

Cornelis and Sybrandt caught the implied dismissal and slipped away, wracked with remorse, impenitence, and hatred. They avoided her gaze whenever possible, and for many days thereafter, she said not a word—good, bad, or indifferent—to either of them. Thus, she freed her spirit.

Catherine was a model housewife, yet she rarely ventured beyond the walls of her home for a single day; whenever she did, something invariably went awry. Acutely aware of this tendency, she watched for a lull in domestic affairs to delay her planned visit to Sevenbergen. Day after day she postponed the trip until, finally, on an afternoon when it might have been accomplished, Peter Buyskens' mule happened to be out of the way.

Eventually, Eli confronted her before the entire family. "Catherine," he asked, "have you truly considered visiting Margaret Brandt?"

"Ay, my man," she admitted.

"Then I forbid it."

"Oh, do you?" she challenged, flushing slightly.

"I do."

"There is nothing more to be said, I suppose," she murmured.

"Not a word," Eli replied sternly.

Once alone with her daughter, Catherine turned her severity inward, directing it not at Eli but at herself. "I behaved like a cat stalking a robin," she lamented. "That was entirely my fault. I am like a silly hen that cannot lay an egg without cackling and summoning the whole flock to rob her of it. Next time either of us desires anything, let us act immediately and silently. If questioned later, we can claim ignorance, say we meant no harm, and wonder why anyone would suspect us. For two pins, I would have gone to Sevenbergen despite their teeth."

Such wild defiance staggered Kate. "Nay, mother, with patience Father will change his mind."

"And so will Michaelmas, but when? I was so determined you should see the girl. Then we could have pooled our thoughts regarding her. As they say, one cannot judge a body or a beast without seeing it with one's own eyes. Were I to have fifty more sons, I would never thwart one's fancy until I had seen the object of their desire and verified the truth. I should have thought of that before condemning Gerard's choice. Yet, life is a school, and the lessons are endless; we put down one fault only to pick up another, stumbling here and there until we stumble into our graves, and that is the end."

"Mother," Kate whispered timidly.

"Well, what brings this look upon your face? No good news, surely. What in the world has made the poor wretch so terrified?"

"She has an avowal to make," Kate faltered.

"Now, there is a noble word for you," Catherine declared proudly. "Gerard taught you that, I'll give you my oath. Come then, speak your mind."

"Well then, to tell the truth, I have seen her."

"And?"

"And I spoke with her as well."

"And you never told me? After this, miracles are mere dust."

"Mother, you were so angry against her. I waited until I could tell you without provoking you further."

"Ay," Catherine said, half sadly, half bitterly. "Like mother, like daughter; cowardice is our ruin. Sometimes I buffet the others, or how would the house fare? But did you, Kate, ever receive a harsh word or look from your poor mother that you— Nay, I will not have you cry, girl. Ten to one you had your reasons. Rise up, brave heart, and tell me everything. Better late than never. First and foremost, when and how did you go to Sevenbergen with your poor crutches, and I not knowing?"

"I never was there in my life," Kate replied gently. "And, mammy dear, to say that I never wished to see her, yet I never went nor sought to see her."

"There now," Catherine argued, "did I not say it was unlike my girl to seek her out secretly? Come, I am all agog."

"It was thus. It came to my ears, no matter how, and pray, good mother, do not ask me on my knees how I learned it, that Gerard is a prisoner in the Stadthouse tower."

"Ah."

"Under the pretext of Father's command."

Catherine sighed, a sound bordering on a moan. "Blackness deeper than I imagined," she muttered faintly.

"Giles and I went out at night to encourage him. And at the foot of the tower stood a brave maiden, quite strange to me, I swear, on the same mission."

"Lookee there now, Kate."

"At first, we properly frightened one another due to his notorious reputation and our minds filled with devils; we turned pale in the moonlight. But the next moment, she said, 'You are Margaret.' And I replied, 'And you are Kate.' Think on it!"

"Did one ever? 'Twas Gerard! He must have been talking backwards and forwards between you two."

In return for this revelation, Kate bestowed upon Catherine one of nature's finest gifts—the composite kiss. She pressed a single kiss to her mother's cheek, conveying simultaneously: *Quite correct; Good, clever mother for guessing so quickly; How sweet for us to be of one mind again after such estrangement; etc.*

"Now then, speak thy mind, child. Gerard is not here. Alas, what am I saying? Would to Heaven he were."

"Well then, mother, she is comely and resembles her portrait but little."

"Eh, dear; listen to the youth! I care for good deeds, not good looks. Does she love my boy as he deserves to be loved?"

"Sevenbergen lies farther from the Stadthouse than we do," Kate reflected. "Yet she was there before I arrived."

Catherine nodded knowingly.

"Nay, more; she had freed him before I came. Ay, down from the captive's tower."

Catherine shook her head in disbelief. "The highest tower for miles! It is not feasible."

"'Tis true nonetheless. She and an old man she brought used means and wit to lower him via a rope. There he hung from his prison, and our Giles climbed it. When I first saw the rope hanging, I said, 'This is magic.' But when the frank lass's arms embraced me, and her bosom beat against mine, and her cheeks grew wet, then I said, 'Tis not magic: 'tis love.' For she is not like me, but strong and capable. And, dear heart, even I, poor frail creature, sometimes feel as though I could move the world for those I love: I love you, mother. And she loves Gerard."

"God bless her for it! God bless her!"

"But..."

"But what, lamb?"

"Is her love truly certain honesty? 'Tis most strange; but that very quality which warms your heart has somewhat cooled mine toward her; poor soul. She is no wife, you know, mother, when all is said and done."

"Humph! They stood at the altar together."

"Ay, but they departed as they arrived: maid and bachelor."

"The parson says so?"

"Nay, for that I know not."

"Then I will take no man's word but his own in such a tangled skein." After some reflection, she added, "Nevertheless, you are right, girl. I shall go to Sevenbergen alone. A wife I am, but not a slave. We are all in the dark here. She holds the key. I must question her, and no one else, least of all you. I will not carry a lily to a house with a stain, no, not to a palace of gold and silver."

As Catherine pondered this conversation, she felt increasingly drawn to Margaret. Moreover, she was "all agog" with curiosity—a potent passion shared by all, and nearly omnipotent for those like Catherine who do not satisfy it with books. Finally, one fine day after dinner, she whispered to Kate, "Keep the house from falling apart if you can," donned her best kirtle and hood, her scarlet clocked hose, and her new shoes, and briskly trudged off to Sevenbergen, troubling no man's mule.

Upon arrival, she inquired where Margaret Brandt lived. The first person she asked shook his head, replying, "The name is strange to me." She walked a short distance further and asked a girl of about fifteen standing at a doorway. "Father," the girl called out into the house, "here is another after that magician's daughter." The man emerged and informed Catherine that Peter Brandt's cottage was just outside the town on the east side. "You may see the chimney from here," he pointed out. "But you will not find them there, neither father nor daughter; they have left the town this week, bless you."

"Do not say so, good man, and me walking all the way from Tergou."

"From Tergou? Then you must have met the soldier."

"What soldier? Yes, I did meet a soldier."

"Well, then, that soldier was here seeking that very same Margaret."

"Yes, and he wasn't mad with us because she was gone?" the girl interjected. "His long beard and her cheek are no strangers, I warrant."

"Say no more than you know," Catherine snapped sharply. "You are too young to slander your elders. Wait! Tell us more about this soldier, good man."

"Nay, I know no more than that he came here seeking Margaret Brandt. I told him she and her father had made a moonlight flight two nights ago, and some thought the devil had flown away with them, being magicians. 'And,' he said, 'the devil fly away with thee for thy ill news;' that was

The following morning, Denys seized an opportunity to reveal the remainder of his tale to Mother and Daughter, carefully excluding Cornelis and Sybrandt from the conversation. "It is not for me to cast a shadow upon them," he explained with his characteristic reserve regarding his comrades. "They are of good stock. Yet, Gerard views them as enemies in this affair. Since I am Gerard's companion, it is a soldier's code to withhold information from the foe—unless, of course, one chooses to speak lies."

Catherine sighed deeply but offered no rebuttal. The adventures Denys recounted stirred within them a tumult of agitation and profound grief; they wept bitterly at the memory of the friends' separation, a scene Denys could barely describe without faltering. Yet, eventually, their sorrow merged into a joyful hope and eager anticipation of Gerard's swift return. Denys shared this confidence wholeheartedly, though he gently reminded them that Gerard's final wishes and instructions must not be neglected simply because of their optimism. Indeed, should Gerard return next week only to find no sign of Margaret, what manner of figure would he cut?

For the first time, Catherine felt a kinship toward the absent Margaret that surpassed her usual demeanor. She was equally anxious to locate her and offer kindness before Gerard's arrival, yet she could not align with Denys's belief that abandoning their neighborhood to search for her was wise. "She must have confided in someone where she was going," Catherine argued. "These are not dishonest folk fleeing the country; they owe not a single stiver in Sevenbergen. And, dear heart, Denys, you cannot hunt the entire province of Holland for one girl."

"Can I not?" Denys asked grimly. "We shall see." After a moment of reflection, he proposed a division of labor: she should remain here with her eyes and ears wide open, while he would scour every town in Holland if necessary. "However," he noted, "she will not be many leagues distant. Three do not fly as fast, nor travel as far, as one."

"That is sound logic," Catherine conceded, though she insisted he visit the Demoiselle Van Eyck immediately. "She and our Margaret were bosom friends. She knows where the girl has gone, if she will but tell us."

Denys was ready to depart instantly, but Catherine, seizing the moment, smoothed her dress slightly and took him with her. They were received graciously by the elderly lady seated in a richly furnished chamber. Upon hearing their news, the tapestry slipped from Margaret Van Eyck's hands.

"Gone? Gone from Sevenbergen without telling me? The thankless girl!"

This reaction startled the visitors. "What? You know nothing? When was she last here?"

"Perhaps ten days ago," the old lady replied. "I had taken out my brushes, after so many years, to paint her portrait. I did not finish it, though; for certain reasons."

Catherine remarked on the strangeness of her departure—"bag and baggage"—without permission or explanation. "Was there ever anything so untoward? Just when our hearts were warm toward her, and here comes Gerard's mate from the ends of the earth with comfort for her, only to find her missing, while Gerard himself awaits... What am I to do? Surely she did not leave without cause. Can you not give us a clue, my good demoiselle? Prithee, speak."

"I have no clue to give," the elder lady said, her tone growing peevish.

"Then I can provide one," declared Reicht Heynes, appearing in the doorway with flushed cheeks. "Have you been listening all along, Mistress?"

"What are my ears for, mistress?"

"True. Very well, then, shed the light of your wisdom upon this dark matter."

"There is no darkness here that I perceive," Reicht stated firmly. "If you call it a two-ply cord, and the ends of it are lying right here in this room, you will not be far astray. Oh, Mistress, I wonder at you sitting there pretending ignorance."

"Marry, come up," the mistress commanded, her own cheeks flushing nearly as red as the servant's.

"So it was I who drove the foolish girl away," Reicht admitted.

"You did your share, Mistress. How did you greet her the last time she visited? Do you think she could fail to notice it, especially when she was all alone and friendless? And you said, 'I have changed my mind about painting you,' turning your nose up at her."

"I did not turn up my nose," the mistress retorted sharply. "My nose is not shaped like yours for gazing heavenward."

"Oh, all noses can follow the heart's inclination, for that matter. Poor soul. She did come to my kitchen. 'I am not to be painted now,' she said, tears in her eyes. She spoke no further. But I knew exactly what she meant. I had seen you."

"Well," said Margaret Van Eyck, "I confess as much and make you the judge, Madam. Understand that young girls can accomplish nothing of their own volition; they are merely adept at mimicking their sweethearts. Your Gerard is reasonably skilled in many things, including the illuminator's craft. Margaret was his pupil, a patient one. Is it a marvel? Having a woman's eye for color and a lover to emulate? 'Tis a trick I despise in my heart. For through it, the great art of color—which should be royal, aspiring, and free—becomes a poor slave to the petty crafts of writing and printing. It is fettered, imprisoned, and diminished, both body and soul, to match the littleness of books, destined to go to church in a rich fool's pocket."

"Naturally, affection governs us all," she continued, her voice softening. "When the poor wench brought me her thorn-leaves, lilies, ivy, dewberries, ladybirds, and butterfly grubs—all the scum of Nature stuck fast in gold-leaf like wasps in a honey-pot—and alongside her daily book, showing she had poured over each singular page for a hundred, or perhaps two hundred hours, certainly I was wroth. That an immortal soul, countless hours of labor, and much manual skill should be flung away on Nature's trash, leaves, insects, and grubs, and on barren letters. Yet, having bowels, I restrained myself, damming my better feelings, and looked kindly at the work to suggest improvements. I said, 'Since Heaven, for our sins, has doomed us to spend time, soul, and color on great letters and tiny beetles, omitting such small fry as saints and heroes—their acts and passions—why not present the scum naturally?'

'I told her,' I said, 'that the grapes I saw hanging in the air did not stick to walls; and even these insects, and Nature's slime in general, do not pass their noxious lives wedged miserably in metal prisons like flies in honey-pots and glue-pots, but crawl or hover at large, infesting the air.'

'Ah, my dear friend,' she replied, 'I see now whither you drive; but this ground is gold; upon it we may not shade.'

'Who said so?' I asked.

'All teachers of this craft,' she answered; and (to make an end of me at once, I suppose) 'Gerard himself!'

'That for Gerard himself,' I retorted, 'and all the gang; give me a brush!'

'So I chose to shade her fruits and reptiles with a color false in nature, but true relative to that monstrous background of glaring gold. In five minutes, out came a bunch of raspberries, stalk and all, almost flying into your mouth; likewise a butterfly grub she had so truly rendered it might turn the stoutest stomach. My lady threw her arms around my neck and exclaimed, 'Oh!'"

"Did she now?"

"The little love!" observed Denys, finally managing to interject.

Margaret Van Eyck stared at him, then smiled. She proceeded to explain how, step by step, she had been led to promise to resume the art she had abandoned with a sigh upon her brothers' deaths—to paint the Madonna once more, using Margaret as her model. Incidentally, she even revealed how girls are transformed into saints.

"Thy hair is adorable," I said.

"Why, 'tis red," she replied.

"Ay," I said, "but what a red! How brown! How glossy! Most hair is not worth a straw to us painters; thine is the artist's very hue. But thy violet eyes, which currently smack of earth—being now languid for lack of one Gerard, now aflame with hope for the same Gerard—I will lift to heaven in fixed and holy meditation. And thy nose, which already somewhat aspires that way (though not so piously as Reicht's), I will debase a trifle, and somewhat enfeeble thy chin."

"Enfeeble her chin? Alack! What may that mean? You go beyond me, Mistress."

"'Tis a resolute chin. Not a jot too resolute for this wicked world; but when you come to a Madonna? No, thank you."

"Well, I never. A resolute chin," Denys interjected.

"And now comes the rub. When you told me she was... the way she is, it gave me a shock; I dropped my brushes. Was I going to turn a girl, who could not keep her lover at a distance, into the Virgin Mary, at my time of life

Catherine felt a sudden chill of dismay as she realized that Gerard must return home within a week, or at the latest, another month. The prospect terrified her. How could she possibly explain to him that she had failed even to cast a glance toward his betrothed? Beyond her own guilt lay the agonizing uncertainty of the girl's fate, a shadow that occasionally coalesced into something sickening in her mind. "Oh, Kate," she groaned aloud, "what if she has already thrown herself into the river?"

"Mother, she would never be so wicked!" Catherine protested.

"Ay, my lass, but you know not the folly of young maidens who lack a mother's hand to guide them," her mother replied, her voice heavy with experience. "They will cast themselves into the water for a man whom the next stranger they meet could cure of in a single week. I have seen them leap in like iron one moment and scream for rescue the next, unable to comprehend their own desires regarding such trifles. Worse still, there are times when their bodies ache in ways unknown to any other creature, tightening their nerves until their patience snaps. In those moments, reason vanishes, and they plunge into the depths. That is why I say men are monsters."

"Monster?" Catherine cried out.

"Monsters, and no less," her mother insisted. "They dig these very canals merely to lure poor women in. They know we cannot cut our throats, for we hate the sight of blood and value our skins and lives too highly. As for hanging, once the noose is fixed, there is often time to change one's mind. But a jump into a canal is no different than jumping into bed; the water washes everything clean, whether you wish it or not. Look at me, the mother of nine—was I not eager enough to make a hole in our canal just now?"

"Nay, Mother, I shall never believe that of you."

"You may believe it, though. It happened in the first year we kept house together. Eli had not yet discovered my frailty, nor I his; so we fell into a quarrel, pulling in opposite directions. Overcome with sorrow, I ran to tell some gossiping fool what I ought to have kept between myself and the saints. There stood one of those precious canals before me. Do they take us for ducks? Oh, how inviting it looked! I told myself, 'Since he has driven me from his door in anger, let him find me drowned next; then he will surely change his tune. He will weep bitterly, and I shall gaze down from heaven—and perhaps another world—to see him mourn, and oh, that would be sweet indeed.' My feet were already lifting, ready to step in, but then I hesitated. I had a new gown nearly finished, after all. Instead, I turned back home. What was Eli's first word? 'Let that flea stick in the wall, my lass,' he said. 'None of what angered you was true, save this: I love you.' Those were his exact words, spoken during our first fight. I threw my arms around his neck, sobbing slightly, thinking of the cold water, but he was no colder to me than I to him, being both a man and a youth. That warmth was far sweeter than lying in the river, ruining my wedding dress and my fine new shoes made by Old John Bush, his uncle who runs the shop today. And what was my grief compared to hers?"

Little Kate clung to the hope that Margaret loved her father too deeply to abandon him, especially given his advanced years. "He is father, mother, and everything to her, you know," she whispered.

"Nay, Kate," her mother countered gently. "In a moment of despair, when the sky turns utterly black, people forget all such duties. I place greater trust in the unborn than in the ripe for the grave to keep a soul from mischief. Indeed, it goes sore against nature to die when a tiny innocent pulls at one's heartstrings, feeding on our very veins."

"Well, then, keep your heart high, Mother," Catherine urged, adding privately that these fears were likely exaggerated. She concluded by earnestly begging her mother to stop insisting on naming the sex of Margaret's baby. It was bad luck, all the town gossips claimed. "Dear heart, as if there were not as many girls born as boys."

Though this reflection was reasonable, it met with immediate clamor. "Do you have the cruelty to threaten me with a girl?!" her mother exclaimed. "I want no more daughters while I have you. What use would a lass be to me? Can I hold her on my knee and see my Gerard again as I can with a boy? Tell thee, it is settled."

"How can that be?" Catherine asked.

"In my mind," her mother declared firmly. "And if I am to be disappointed in the end, it is not for you to disappoint me beforehand by telling me it is not a child, but merely a girl."

Margaret Brandt had always kept herself aloof from the bustling life of Sevenbergen, a reserve that the townsfolk interpreted as arrogance. Whispers of jealousy and malice swelled within their hearts, waiting for the moment her hidden condition could no longer be concealed. The prospect terrified her; she feared how they would triumph over her once the truth broke the surface. This dread gnawed at her every hour, driving her to seclude herself within the house and shunning the daylight whenever possible.

Yet, in her secret conscience, Margaret possessed a clear moral compass. Though unfamiliar with the intricate nuances of contemporary law, she understood that a betrothal was a binding contract, as irrevocable as any signed agreement. She knew that marrying another would likely result in annulment by both Church and State, and that children born of such unions were often deemed legitimate. However, what crushed her simple medieval spirit was the absence of proof. Instead of holding the document, Gerard had taken it, and he was now far away across the sea.

She loathed herself for the careless oversight that left her vulnerable to public scorn. While she had never heard of Horace's famous line regarding how things that strike too close to home cause the greatest irritation, she shared the same practical intelligence characteristic of her sex when emotion does not cloud judgment. She measured the world's perception with precision. With her "marriage lines" missing and located in Italy, they could never counterbalance the undeniable evidence of her pregnancy and the future sight of her child. How could she silence slanderous tongues with the plea, "I possess my marriage certificate, but I cannot show it to you"? Who would believe a woman, especially one known for her extreme modesty? Here, slander wore the mask of truth. In an era before the concept of the "strong-minded woman," Margaret—though naturally capable and accustomed to managing a household—found herself weak as water against the weight of public opinion. Like the delicate leaves of the elite class, she trembled at every gust of societal judgment.

There is profound misery in few words. Few descriptions could convey the agony of a virtuous woman anticipating her parade as a sinner, whereas a woman of frail heart might have simply worn the label with defiance. Margaret was pure as snow, watching the darkness approach. She sat listlessly for hours, consumed by internal anguish. Often, during conversations with her father, her face would flush with sudden heat, leaving the old man wondering what words had offended her. Nothing had changed; his voice was mere air to her. It was the constant, suffocating dread that painted her cheeks with the color of shame, regardless of the topic at hand.

This fear reached its zenith after returning from Margaret Van Eyck's home that evening. Her condition had been discovered by women of her own sex. While the secluded old artist might remain silent, Catherine—a gossip at the center of a large family and a tight-knit neighborhood—posed a grave threat. A single spark of hope lingered: Catherine had spoken with kindness, even affection. The situation allowed for no middle ground; roused, Catherine would either become Margaret's staunchest ally or her fiercest enemy. Waiting in agonizing anxiety for news, Margaret received none. Hope faded. Convinced that Catherine intended to expose her due to a lack of genuine compassion, a wild, desperate desire to flee the neighborhood took root.

But how could she persuade her father to abandon his home? Old people clung to their roots, and Peter was infirm. The only path lay in making him her confidant, rather than fleeing alone. Caught between an overwhelming urge to hide and flight, and an insurmountable aversion to speaking to a man—even her father—she existed in a state of torturous suspension. Then, one day, the fatal thought arrived: *an end to it all.*

Catherine's frequent fears regarding the city's canals tempted this wavering girl. Standing by the bank one afternoon, she gazed into the calm, deep water, which seemed a mirror of the peace she craved. "No more trouble. No more fear of shame," she whispered. If Gerard had not loved her, she might have jumped then. But as it was, kneeling by the water's edge, she prayed fervently to God to purge such wicked thoughts from her soul.

"Oh, selfish wretch!" she cried aloud. "To leave thy father! Oh, wicked wretch, to kill thy unborn child and deprive poor Gerard of the pain and peril he undertook for thy sake! I will tell my father everything, yes, before this sun sets."

With eager haste, lest her resolve dissolve, she rushed home. In domestic matters, the learned Peter was as simple as a child, while Margaret, since the age of sixteen, had governed the house gently but absolutely. It was therefore strange to see her young, despotic mistress addressing him with such faltering hesitation.

"Father, I would speak to thee."
"Speak on, girl."
"Wilt you listen? And—and—not try to excuse my faults?"
"We all have our faults, Margaret, no more than the rest; but fewer, unless parental feeling blinds me."
"Alas, no, father. I am a poor, foolish girl who wishes to do well but has done ill, most ill, and most unwisely. Now I must bear the shame. But, father, I love you, with all my faults, and will you not forgive my folly and still love your motherless daughter?"
"That ye may count on," Peter replied cheerfully.
"Oh, well, do not smile. How can I speak and make you sad?"
"Why, what is the matter?"
"Father, disgrace is coming upon this house; it stands at the door. And I am the culprit. Oh, father, turn your head away. I—I... father, I have let Gerard take away my marriage lines."
"Is that all? 'Twas an oversight."
"'Twas the deed of a mad woman. But woe is me! That is not the worst."

Peter interrupted her. "The youth is honest and loves you dearly. You are young. What is a year or two to you? Gerard will assuredly return and keep his troth."
"And meanwhile, do you know what is coming?"
"Not I, except that I shall be gone first."
"Worse than that. There is pain worse than death. Nay, for pity's sake, turn away your head, father."
"Foolish wench!" Peter muttered, turning his face aside.

She trembled violently, her cheeks aflame, beginning to stammer, "I looked upon Gerard as my husband—we were betrothed—and he was in such dire danger, and I thought I had killed him, and I..." Tears welled in her eyes. "If you were but my mother, I might find courage; you would question me. But you say not a word."
"Why, Margaret, what is all this commotion? Why are thy cheeks crimson, speaking to no stranger, but to thy old father?"
"Why are my cheeks on fire? Because—because—father, kill me! Send me to heaven! Bid Martin shoot me with his arrow! And then the gossips will come and tell you why I blush this day. And then, when I am dead, I hope you will love your girl again for her mother's sake."

"Give me thy hand, mistress," Peter said, somewhat sternly.
She extended her trembling hand. He took it gently, his face anxious as he checked her pulse.
"Alas, nay," she whispered. "'Tis my soul that burns, not my body, with fever. I cannot, will not, abide in Sevenbergen." Wringing her hands impatiently, she added, "Be calm now," he soothed, "nor torment thyself for naught. Not abide in Sevenbergen? What need to stay a day, as it vexes thee and brings on a fever? For fevered thou art, deny it not."
"What!" cried Margaret. "Would you yield to go hence, and—and ask no reason but my longing to depart?" Suddenly throwing herself to her knees beside him in a fervor of supplication, she clutched his sleeve, then his arm, then his shoulder, imploring him to leave immediately without asking why.
"Alas! What need? You will soon see. And I could never say it. I would sooner die."
"Foolish child, who seeks thy girlish secrets? Is it I, whose life has been spent searching Nature's mysteries? And for leaving Sevenbergen, what keeps me here if you are unwilling? Is there respect for me here, or gratitude? Am I not called a quack-salver by those who avoid me, and a wizard by those I heal? Do they not give the reward and honor denied to me to the empirics who slaughter them? Besides, what matters where we sojourn? Choose thou, as thy mother did before thee."

Margaret embraced him tenderly, weeping upon his shoulder. She was reprieved. Yet, as she wept, she almost wished she had possessed the courage to tell him everything. Eventually, nothing satisfied him but her acceptance of a medicine he fetched. Taking it submissively to please him, she drank the composing draught. Administered under a common misconception, it did her more good than harm; she awoke calmed by a long sleep. That very day, she began her preparations.

The following week, they departed for Rotterdam, bag and baggage, lodging above a tailor's shop in the Brede-Kirk Straet. Only one person in Tergou knew their destination: the Burgomaster. He locked the information

Amidst strangers, Margaret Brandt found a comparative happiness. Soon, however, a new and unforeseen source of joy emerged. A civic dignitary, plagued by illness and prone to fanciful notions, consulted doctor after doctor until he teetered on the brink of death. Desperate, he summoned Peter. Upon arrival, Peter discovered the patient reduced to nothing by excessive bleeding and purging. With decisive action, Peter threw a battalion of bottles out the window, leaving the casement open; he whisked egg yolks in neat Schiedam, administering them in measured doses, followed by meat stewed in red wine and water, seasoned with mild febrifuge herbs that caused no harm. Eventually, the patient recovered mobility, appearing somewhere between a man and a pillowcase. Being a voluble dignitary, he spread Peter's fame through every street. Thus, the artist, who had long deserved a reputation in vain, gained one rapidly by sheer luck.

Things appeared bright. The old man's pride was finally cheered, and his purse began to swell. Yet, he squandered much of his earnings on sovereign herbs and choice drugs, intending to invest them all in such remedies. Margaret, however, intercepted a portion. The victory came too late; the happy excitement proved fatal.

One evening, as he bid her goodnight, his voice sounded strangely inarticulate. By the next morning, he was found speechless, barely conscious. Margaret, who had served as her father's attentive pupil for years, immediately recognized the signs of a paralytic stroke. Trusting no one, she fetched a physician—one of those who had not been obstructed by Peter and thus had not harmed the previous dignitary. The doctor cheerfully confirmed her diagnosis and proposed bleeding the patient. Margaret refused. "He was always against bleeding," she stated firmly, "especially the elderly."

Peter survived, but he was never the same man again. His memory deteriorated significantly, rendering him unfit to prescribe. Several patients, eager to be cured by the new doctor exclusively, waited for his recovery. Misery stared Margaret in the face. Resolved to seek counsel and comfort, she turned to her cousin, William Johnson, from whom she had previously kept aloof due to pride and poverty.

She found him and his servant in the same room, neither improved by liquor. Mastering her surprise, she greeted them and announced she had come to discuss a family matter, glancing quietly at the servant as a subtle hint. The woman took the cue, but not as expected.

"Oh, you can speak before me, can she not, my old man?"

At this familiarity, Margaret flushed deeply. "I cry you mercy, mistress. I knew not my cousin had adopted the customs of this town. Well, I must take a fitter opportunity," she said, rising to depart.

"I wot not what ye mean by custom o' the town," the woman retorted, springing up. "But this I know: 'tis the duty of a faithful servant to keep her master from being preyed upon by his beggarly kin."

"You are too modest, mistress," Margaret replied sharply. "Ye are no servant. Your speech betrays you. 'Tis not till the ape hath climbed the tree that she shows her tail so plainly. Nay, there sits the servant; God help him! While this remains true, fear not that his kin will ever be so poor in spirit as to come where the likes of you can flout their charity." Casting a look of mute reproach at her cousin for remaining passive and silent throughout the exchange, Margaret turned and exited haughtily, refusing to shed a single tear until she reached home. There, she faced the grim reality: two men to lodge and feed, plus another mouth arriving into the world.

Yet, this last addition, though the most helpless, was their greatest ally. Nature was strong within Margaret Brandt—the same instinct that drives beasts, birds, and insects to cunningly provide food and shelter for their future progeny. Stimulated by this primal force, she sat and brooded, thinking intensely on how to preempt destitution. Even with five gold pieces remaining, she foresaw starvation approaching with inevitable steps.

When women deviate from custom to think with male intensity, they achieve results just as effective as men. Margaret rose, ordered Martin to move Peter to another room, tidied her own space meticulously, polished a glass globe, and suspended it from the ceiling. She dusted a crocodile figure and nailed it to the exterior wall. After instructing Martin, she assigned him to act as a lounging sentinel at the street door, telling passersby that a great, aged, and learned alchemist resided within, who occasionally amused himself during recreation by curing mortal diseases.

Patients soon arrived, demanding to see the leech. "That is impossible," Margaret declared. "He is deep in study, seeking the grand elixir; not even princes may speak with him. You must describe your symptoms and return in two hours." And oh, the mysterious power of suggestion! When they returned, the drug or draught was invariably ready. For worshipping patients, she would carefully examine their faces, check pulses and skins, and listen to their tales. Retreating softly to Peter's room, she would ponder how her father, whose methods she had long observed, would treat the case. She would then scribble an illegible note filled with cabalistic letters, bring it down reverently, and present it to the patient. "Can you read that?" she would ask. Usually, the response was either, "I am no reader," or, with admiration, "Nay, mistress, I understand naught of it."

"Ay, but I can. 'Tis sovereign. Consider yourself cured!" If she possessed the necessary materials—and she was too economical not to favor medicines from her own stock—she would sometimes allow the patient to watch her compound them, anxiously consulting the sacred prescription lest Great Science suffer in her hands. Thus, she sent them away relieved of cash but burdened with medicine, their minds filled with faith and humbugged to their hearts' content. *Populus vult decipi* (the people want to be deceived). Once they departed, she retrieved two small boxes Gerard had crafted for her. On one she wrote "Today" and on the other "Tomorrow." She placed smaller coins in the "Today" box and larger ones in the "Tomorrow" box, alongside whatever gold pieces survived the journey from Sevenbergen and the costs of housekeeping in a foreign land. In this manner, she met current expenses while saving for the rainy days she anticipated, mixing drugs with simples and vice with virtue. Conscience pricked her sorely regarding this last practice. After each day's performance, she knelt and prayed God to forgive her "for the sake of her child." Yet, lo and behold, reports of cures continued to pour in; consequently, her conscience began to harden.

Martin Wittenhaagen had recently become a dead weight on her hands. Like many who had endured great hardships, he had stiffened abruptly. Though less supple, he remained as strong as ever and could have carried the doctor herself around Rotterdam at his own pace. Instead, he carried her slops.

In this new enterprise, Martin displayed the qualities of a soldier: unreasoning obedience, punctuality, accuracy, dispatch, and drunkenness. Falling among "good fellows," the blackguards plied him with Schiedam; he babbled and bragged.

Doctor Margaret had risen very high in his estimation. All this brandishing of a crocodile as a standard, ambushing a dotard, disposing of slops, and exchanging good money struck him not as science, but as something far superior: Strategy. He boasted in his cups and before mixed companies how "me and my General we are a biting of the burghers."

When this revelation had time to ferment through the city, his General, Doctor Margaret, received a summons from the constables. They took her, trembling and begging her subordinate machines to forgive her, before the burgomaster. Standing by his side were real physicians—a terrible row—in long robes and square caps, accusing her of practicing unlawfully on the bodies of the Duke's subjects. Initially, she was too terrified to speak. A novice, the mere name of "Law" paralyzed her. But questioned closely, yet not as harshly as if she were ugly, she told the truth. She had long been her father's pupil and merely followed his system, noting that she had cured many. "And it is not for myself, truly, sirs," she pleaded, "but I have two poor, helpless, honest men at home upon my hands. How else can I keep them? Ah, good sirs, let a poor girl make her bread honestly; you hinder them not from making it idly and shamefully. Oh, sirs, ye are husbands, ye are fathers; ye cannot but see I have reason to work and provide as best I may." Ere these words left her lips, she wished she could recall them, standing with one hand upon her heart and the other covering her face, hiding it but not the tears trickling beneath. Unfortunately, her appeal went to the wrong address. Perhaps a female bailiff might have yielded to such arguments, permitting her to practice medicine and break the law until her child was weaned.

"What have we to do with that," said the burgomaster, "save and except that if thou pledgest thyself to break the law no more, I will remit the imprisonment and exact but the fine?"

Upon this, Doctor Margaret clasped her hands and vowed most penitently never, never, never to cure body or beast again. Dismissed with the constables to pay the fine

Margaret stood at the crossroads of fortune, a day that tasted both like honey and gall. For within its span, she had gained a soulmate in Denys, yet lost the hope of her husband's return. The truth settled heavy upon her: Gerard was utterly alone in that distant, strange land. Reason whispered that he should have arrived weeks ago; logic dictated he would be here by now. Yet, Denys remained her anchor. He was her "she-comrade," a constant source of cheer who banished despair with his wit and formulas. She doted on him, treating him with the affection usually reserved for kin, while simultaneously lecturing him with a feigned sternness. She would not allow him to take a single meal outside her roof, nor suffer any foul language from his lips.

"It ruins you, Denys," she chided, watching his transformation from rough sailor to polite gentleman. "Good heavens, to hear such ugly words escape such a handsome head! Desist, or I shall be truly angry; behave yourself." Instantly, Denys fell into line, the comedy of his struggle between innate courtesy and acquired ruffianship playing out before her eyes.

But as the saying goes, when it rains, it pours. Soon, others sought her friendship. She had penned a humble missive to Margaret Van Eyck, acknowledging her own diminished status in the household and promising to keep her distance, yet pleading that she could not erase the memory of past kindness. In the letter, she recounted her desperate battles for survival and implored secrecy regarding her whereabouts—especially from Gerard's former associates. "I loathe them," she wrote, her bitterness seeping through the ink. "They drove him from me. Even after his departure, their hearts did not turn toward me as they might have, had they repented their cruelty."

Van Eyck was perplexed by this sudden shift. Eventually, she confided in Reicht, who carried the secret like water through a pipe straight to Catherine.

"Indeed, has she turned so bitter against us?" the good woman asked, her voice tinged with sorrow. "She stole our son from us, and now she hates us for failing to rush into her embrace. Still, it is a blessing she lives and remains no further than Rotterdam."

Meanwhile, across the northern duchy, a spectacle unfolded. The English Princess, now Countess Charolois, made a grand procession accompanied by her stepdaughter, Marie de Bourgogne, the young heiress. The old Duke, Europe's most magnificent prince, displayed his full splendor. Armies of dazzling knights and crowds of gorgeously dressed ladies trailed behind them, joined by minstrels, jesters, bards, musicians, actors, and tumblers. Fencing matches, dances, and merriment filled every town they visited. Richart summoned all his people to Rotterdam to witness the pageant.

Days passed in Rotterdam before fate intervened again. Denys encountered Catherine by chance in the street. After a warm exchange of greetings, he urged her to rejoice, boasting that he had finally located her "she-comrade." But Catherine quickly cooled his enthusiasm, pointing out that he would have found her sooner had he stayed quietly at Tergou rather than wandering aimlessly through Holland.

"And now that we are found, does it make things better?" she asked cynically. "Her heart is set firmly against us."

"Oh, let that flea bite," Denys dismissed lightly. "Come with me to her house."

"No," Catherine refused flatly. "Those who arrive unbidden remain seated nowhere." She suggested instead that Denys act as a mediator to smooth things over. He accepted the task with great pomp and confidence, trotting off to Margaret.

"She-comrade," he announced, "I met today a friend of yours."

"You looked into Rotterdam, then, and saw yourself?" Margaret retorted sharply.

"Nay, 'twas a woman seeking your regard. 'Twas Catherine, Gerard's mother."

"Oh, was it?" Margaret replied coldly. "Then you may tell her she has come too late. There was a time I yearned for her, yes; but she kept aloof during my hour of greatest need. So now, let us remain exactly as we were."

Denys attempted to soften her resolve, coaxing and pleading, but she remained bitter and sullen, unmoved. Finally, he scolded her, and the pressure broke her; she erupted into hysterics. Terrified by the result of his own eloquence and caught off guard, Denys stumbled into a solemn promise never to raise the subject again.

He returned to Catherine crestfallen and delivered the news. Catherine flared with anger, recounting the reception of his overtures to the family. The feud ignited, burning hotter with each passing day. Only little Kate dared offer excuses for Margaret.

The following day, another visitor arrived. The household appeared enslaved and degraded: Martin was buried to his elbows in soap suds, Denys ironing with clumsy ineptitude, and Margaret plaiting ruffs, though her eyes held the sharp gaze of a mistress surveying her raw recruits. Into this scene stepped an old man. Venerable at first glance, he revealed himself upon closer inspection to be keen-eyed and wizened.

"Ah!" cried Margaret.

Swiftly turning her back, she hid her face with invincible revulsion. "Oh, that man! That man!"

"Nay, fear me not," said Ghysbrecht gently. "I come on a friend's errand. I bring you a letter from foreign parts."

"Do not mock me, old man," she snapped, turning slowly around.

"Nay, see;" he held out an enormous envelope.

Margaret lunged forward, snatching the letter with trembling hands and glistening eyes. It bore Gerard's handwriting.

"Oh, thank you, sir, bless you for this! I forgive you all the ill you ever wrought me."

Clutching the letter to her bosom with one hand, she glided swiftly from the room, leaving Ghysbrecht behind. He departed without returning, casting a final scowl at Martha. Margaret was left alone with her letter for hours, the weight of the world suspended in those few folded pages.

Upon returning downstairs, Margaret was a transformed figure. Though her eyes remained glistening with unshed tears, the storm within her had settled into a profound, quiet calm. The sharp edges of her bitterness and the feverish intensity of her recent emotions had melted away entirely.

"Denys," she whispered, "I have received my instructions. I am to read my lover's letter aloud to his family."

"You will never do that?" Denys asked, disbelief coloring his voice.

"I shall," she replied firmly.

He stared at her, searching for some sign of hesitation. "I perceive that something in the letter has softened your heart toward them."

"Not a single jot, Denys, not a single jot," she insisted gently. "Yet, even if I hated them as one hates poison, I would not disobey my love. Denys, how sweet it is to obey! But sweeter still is to obey a soul who stands afar, unable to enforce my duty through force, but who must rely solely upon the fidelity of my love. Ah, Gerard, my darling... had you stood before me, close at hand, I might have slighted your commands, repelled by your kin as I have every reason to despise them. But now, if you were to bid me wade into the raging sea to read your sweet letter to the very sharks, I would go without a second thought. Therefore, Denys, tell his mother I possess the letter. If she and her kin are willing to listen, I am their humble servant. Let them set the hour, and I will seat them as comfortably as possible and welcome them with all the warmth I can muster."

Denys departed immediately to deliver this news to Catherine. He found the family gathered around the dinner table. Announcing that a lengthy letter from Gerard had arrived, he watched the joy ripple through the room. Then, amidst the celebration, he added, "Her heart has also been softened; she intends to read it to you personally. You may choose whatever time suits you best."

"What makes her believe no one else can read it but her?" Catherine queried.

"Let her send the letter, and we shall read it ourselves," someone suggested.

"Nay, but, Mother," interjected little Kate, "perhaps she cannot bear to part with it from her own hands; she loves him so dearly."

"Doth she think we intend to steal it?" Cornelis offered a cynical suggestion, implying that Margaret sought merely to wedge herself into the household through this token of affection.

Denys shot a look of pure scorn at the speaker. "That speaks a base heart," he declared. "Comrade La hates you all like venom. Do not mistake me, Dame; I offer no defense for her nature—so it is indeed—but despite her spleen at a mere word from Gerard, she offers to read you his letter with her own delicate lips. She possesses a voice like honey—it is certainly a fair proposal."

"'Tis true, my honest soldier," the father, Eli, agreed warmly. "It merits a courteous response. Hold your tongues, you women, and allow me to answer. Tell her that I, her son's father, setting aside all ancient grudges, accept this grace with gratitude. Should she desire double thanks, let her dispatch my son's letter through your faithful hand. I shall read it to his flesh and blood, and thereafter, I will return to her with such surety and faithfulness as I am Eli, a Dirick, a William, a Luke—a free burgher of Tergou, following in the footsteps of my ancestors, and like them, a man of my word."

"Ay, and a man who keeps his word better than most," cried Catherine, adding, "The only one I have ever encountered worthy of such praise."

"Hold thy peace, wife!" Eli commanded sharply.

"Are you a man of sense, Eli, a Dirk, a Chose?" Denys shouted, using local dialect terms for a person of substance. "The female comrade will be delighted to obey Gerard while avoiding facing you all, whom she detests as wormwood, save for your presence. Blessed be, the world has changed; today she is pure submission. 'Obedience is honey,' she proclaimed. In truth, it is a sweetmeat she cannot resist, though she eats sparingly of it. With her fair face and mellifluous tongue, coupled with her tendency to fly into fits that terrify us soldiers to death when thwarted, and her alternating between scolding us like children and petting us like lambs, she has turned two of the most crawling slaves imaginable out of two honest swashbucklers. I am the ironing rogue, and the other washes."

"What comes next?" Eli asked.

"Why, whenever the brat enters the world, I shall rock the cradle, and the other knave shall wash the tucker and bibs. So, I shall fetch the letter instantly. Will you permit me to stay and hear it read?"

"Unless our hearts are blacker than coal," Catherine assured him.

Denys rushed forth to retrieve the letter but returned crestfallen. "She will not yield it to me, nor to you, nor to any living soul."

"I knew she would not," Cornelis muttered.

"Hush! Hush!" Eli commanded. "Let Denys recount his tale."

"Nay," Denys reported, "'Be ruled by me.' 'Not I,' she retorted. 'Well then,' I pressed, 'consider that honeyed Obedience you spoke of.' 'You are a fool,' she said. 'Obedience to Gerard is sweet, but obedience to any other mortal, whoever they may be, holds no sweetness for me.'"

"At last, she seemed to soften slightly and handed me a written note for you, Mademoiselle. Here it is."

"For me?" Little Kate blushed, reaching out.

"Give it here!" Eli took the paper and scanned the handwriting, speaking almost in a whisper. "These are words from the letter. Hearken!"

"And, sweetheart, if these lines should journey safely to thee, make trial of my people's hearts as well. Perhaps they are somewhat turned toward me, being far away. If so, they will demonstrate it to thee, since they may not do so openly to me. Read, then, this letter! Yet, I strictly forbid thee to let it from thy hand. And if they still hold aloof from thee, then say nothing, but let them think me dead. Obey me in this; for, if thou dost disrespect my judgment and my will herein, thou lovest me not."

A heavy silence fell upon the room as Gerard's words, transcribed by Margaret, were passed around and examined.

"Well," Catherine finally said, "that is another matter entirely. But methinks it falls to her to come to us, rather than us going to her."

"Alas, Mother! What difference does that make?" little Kate exclaimed.

"Much," Eli replied gravely. "Tell her we are too numerous to visit her, and bid her come hither, the sooner the better."

Once Denys had withdrawn, Eli confessed privately that it was a bitter pill to swallow. "When that girl crosses my threshold, all the mischief and misery she has wrought here will seem to tumble into one heap at my doors. But what choice did I have, wife? We must hear the news of Gerard. I saw it in thine eyes, and felt it in my own heart. She is backed by our undutiful yet still beloved son, making her stronger than we, bringing our noses down to the grindstone, that sly, cruel jade. But never mind. We shall hear the letter; and then, let her depart unblessed, just as she arrived unwelcome."

"Make your mind easy," Catherine soothed him. "She will not come at all." A tone of deep regret lingered in her voice.

Shortly thereafter, Richart arrived from Amsterdam, as had been hourly anticipated. Grave and dignified in his burgher's robes, adorned with a gold chain, ruff, and fur-lined cap, he was received not merely with affection but with profound respect. For he had risen a step higher than his parents, and in medieval society, such ascents were marked as visibly as new flights of stairs.

Admitted to the family council in due course, Richart displayed plainly, though without overt discourtesy, that his pride was deeply wounded by their decision to deign treating with Margaret Brandt.

"I see the temptation," he remarked dryly. "But which of us has not, at times, wished one thing and done another?"

This remark cast a considerable chill over the older members of the family. Little Kate quickly stepped forward to diffuse the tension. "Vex not thyself, dear Richart. Mother says she will not come."

"All the better, sweetheart," Richart replied coolly. "I fear me, if she does arrive, I shall hasten back to Amsterdam."

Just then, Denys poked his head through the doorway. "She will be here at three o'clock on the great dial."

They all exchanged looks, falling into a silent, tense contemplation.

"Nay, Richart," Catherine finally said, "for Heaven's sake, let not this one sorry wench set us all by the ears; hath she not made ill blood enough already?"

"In very deed she hath," Richard replied. "Fear me not, good mother. Let her come and read the letter of the poor boy she hath bewitched by devilish arts, and then let her go. Give me your word to show her no countenance beyond decent and constrained civility; less we may not, being in our own house. And I will say no more."

On this understanding, they waited for the foe. For her part, Catherine prepared for the interview in a spirit little less hostile. When Denys brought word that they would not come to her, but would receive her, her lip curled, and she bade him observe how in them every feeling, however small, was larger than the love for Gerard.

"Well," she said, "I have not that excuse; so why mimic the pretty burgher's pride, the pride of all unlettered folk? I will go to them for Gerard's sake. Oh, how I loathe them!"

Thus, poor, good-natured Denys was bringing into one house the materials of an explosion.

Margaret made her toilet in the same spirit that a knight of her day dressed for battle—he to parry blows, and she to parry glances. Glances of contempt at her poverty, or of irony at her extravagance. Her kirtle was of English cloth, dark blue, and her farthingale and hose of the same material, but a glossy roan, or claret color. Not an inch of pretentious fur about her, but plain snowy linen wristbands, and curiously plaited linen from the bosom of the kirtle up to the commencement of the throat; it did not encircle her throat, but framed it, being square, not round. Her front hair still peeped in two waves, much after the fashion which Mary Queen of Scots revived a century later; but instead of the silver net, which would have ill become her present condition, the rest of her head was covered with a very small, tight-fitting hood of dark blue cloth, hemmed with silver. Her shoes were red; but the roan petticoat and hose prepared the spectator's mind for the shock, and they set off the arched instep and shapely foot.

Beauty knew its business then as now.

And with all this, she kept her enemies waiting, though it was three by the dial.

At last she started, attended by her companion. And when they were halfway, she stopped and said thoughtfully, "Denys!"

"Well, she-general?"

"I must go home" (piteously).

"What? Have ye left something behind?"

"What?"

"My courage. Oh! oh! oh!"

"Nay, nay, be brave, she-general. I shall be with you."

"Ay, but wilt thou keep close to me when I be there?"

Denys promised, and she resumed her march, but gingerly.

Meanwhile, they were all assembled, waiting for her with a strange mixture of feelings. Mortification, curiosity, panting affection, aversion to her who came to gratify those feelings, yet another curiosity to see what she was like, and what there was in her to bewitch Gerard and make so much mischief.

At last Denys came alone and whispered, "The she-comrade is without."

"Fetch her in," said Eli. "Now, whisht, all of ye. None speak to her but I."

They all turned their eyes to the door in dead silence. A little muttering was heard outside—Denys's rough organ and a woman's soft, mellow voice. Presently that stopped; and then the door opened slowly, and Margaret Brandt, dressed as I have described, somewhat pale but calm and lovely, stood on the threshold, looking straight before her.

They all rose but Kate, and remained mute and staring.

"Be seated, mistress," said Eli gravely, and motioned to a seat that had been set apart for her.

She inclined her head and crossed the apartment; and in so doing, her condition was very visible, not only in her shape, but in her languor. Cornelis and Sybrandt hated her for it. Richart thought it spoiled her beauty. It softened the women somewhat.

She took her letter out of her bosom and kissed it as if she had been alone; then disposed herself to read it, with the air of one who knew she was there for that single purpose.

But as she began, she noticed they had seated her all by herself like a leper. She looked at Denys, and putting her hand down by her side, made him a swift, furtive motion to come by her. He went with an obedient start, as if she had cried "March!" and stood at her shoulder like a sentinel; but this zealous manner revealed to the company that he had been ordered thither, and at that she colored. And now she began to read her Gerard, *their* Gerard, to their eager ears, in a mellow, clear voice, so soft, so earnest, so thrilling that her very soul seemed to cling about each precious sound. It was a voice as of a woman's bosom set speaking by Heaven itself.

*"I do nothing doubt, my Margaret, that long ere this shall meet thy beloved eyes, Denys, my most dear friend, will have sought thee out, and told thee the manner of our unlooked-for and most tearful parting. Therefore I will e'en begin at that most doleful day. What befell him after, poor faithful soul, fain, fain would I hear, but may not. But I pray for him day and night next after thee, dearest. Friend more stanch and loving had not David in Jonathan, than I in him. Be good to him, for poor Gerard's sake."*

At these words, which came quite unexpectedly to him, Denys leaned his head on Margaret's high chair and groaned aloud. She turned quickly as she sat, found his hand, and pressed it. And so the sweetheart and the friend held hands while the sweetheart read.

*"I went forward all dizzied, like one in an ill dream; and presently a gentleman came up with his servants, all on horseback, and had liked to have ridden o'er me. And he drew rein at the brow of the hill, and sent his armed men back to rob me. They robbed me civilly enough and took my purse and the last copper, and rode gaily away. I wandered stupid on, a friendless pauper."*

There was a general sigh, followed by an oath from Denys.

*"Presently a strange dimness came o'er me; I lay down to sleep on the snow. 'Twas ill done, and with store of wolves hard by. Had I loved thee as thou dost deserve, I had shown more manhood. But oh, sweet love, the drowsiness that did crawl o'er me desolate, and benumb me, was more than nature. And so I slept; and but that God was better to us than I to thee or to myself, from that sleep I ne'er had waked; so all do say. I had slept an hour or two, as I suppose, but no more, when a hand did shake me rudely. I awoke to my troubles. And there stood a servant girl in her holiday suit. 'Are ye mad,' quoth she, in seeming choler, 'to sleep in snow, and under wolves' noses? Art weary o' life, and not long weaned? Come, now,' said she, more kindly, 'get up like a good lad;' so I did rise up. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' But I stared at her as one amazed. 'Why, 'tis easy of reply,' quoth she. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' Then I gave a great, loud cry; that she did start back. 'Am I rich, or am I poor? Had ye asked me an hour ago, I had said I am rich. But now I am so poor as sure earth beareth on her bosom none poorer. An hour ago I was rich in a friend, rich in money, rich in hope and spirits of youth; but now the Bastard of Burgundy hath taken my friend, and another gentleman my purse; and I can neither go forward to Rome nor back to her I left in Holland. I am poorest of the poor.' 'Alack!' said the wench. 'Natheless, an ye had been rich ye might ha' lain down again in the snow for any use I had for ye; and then I trow ye had soon fared out o' this world as bare as ye came into it. But, being poor, you are our man: so come wi' me.' Then I went because she bade me, and because I recked not now whither I went. And she took me to a fine house hard by, and into a noble dining-hall hung with black; and there was set a table with many dishes, and but one plate and one chair. 'Fall to!' said she, in a whisper. 'What, alone?' said I. 'Alone? And which of us, think ye, would eat out of the same dish with ye? Are we robbers o' the dead?' Then she spied where I was born. 'At Tergou,' said I.
