Says she, 'And when a gentleman dies in

Margaret woke to find her head resting upon Catherine's arm. Around her stood the sturdy, honest members of the household, speaking in rough, homely tones of encouragement. Giles, in particular, roared that she must not take on so; Gerard was alive and well, for he could not have penned such a magnificent letter—the largest, most beautiful, and most moving document the world had ever seen.

"Ay, good Master Giles," Margaret sighed weakly. "He was alive. But how can I know what has happened since? Oh, why did he leave Holland to wander among strangers fierce as lions? And why did I not drive him from me sooner, rather than part him from his own flesh and blood? Forgive me, you who are his mother!"

She gently dislodged Catherine's arm and made a feeble attempt to slide from the chair onto her knees. After a brief struggle against gravity, she found herself settled on Catherine's lap. Then, holding out the letter to Eli, she spoke faintly but sweetly, "I will entrust it to your hands now. Truly, I am unfit to read any more—and loath to leave this comfort." She wound her free arm tightly around Catherine's neck.

"Read it, Richart," Eli instructed. "Thine eyes are younger than mine."

Richart accepted the letter. "Well," he remarked, "I have never seen such handwriting. It is written with a needle's point, and remarkably clear. Why is he not in my counting-house in Amsterdam instead of vagabonding abroad?"

"When I regained consciousness," Margaret began, "I was seated in the litter, with my good merchant holding my hand. I babbled nonsense, then shivered in silence for a while. He pressed a horn of wine to my lips."

"Bless him! Bless him!" Catherine exclaimed.

"Quiet," Eli whispered.

"I told him what had befallen. He examined my leg; it was severely sprained and swollen at the ankle. All my stays were broken; I could scarcely keep my hose up. I said, 'Sir, I fear I shall be but a burden to you, and can offer you no harmony now; my poor psaltery is broken.' I grieved deeply over my broken instrument, a companion of so many weary leagues. But he patted my cheek, bidding me not fret. He propped my leg on a pillow and tended me like a kind father."

"January 19.—I sit all day in the litter, for we push forward with haste. At night, the good, kind merchant sends me to bed and forbids me to work. Strange! Whenever I encounter men like fiends, the very next moment God sends me some good man or woman, lest I despair of humanity. Oh, Margaret! How strangely mixed they are, and how aged I feel compared to who I was three months ago. Lo! If good Master Fugger has not already bought me a new psaltery."

"Eli, my man," Catherine interrupted, "if that merchant passes our way, let us buy a hundred ells of cloth from him, and haggle not."

"That will I do," Eli promised, "take your oath on it!"

As Richart prepared to continue reading, Kate glanced at her mother. With a faint blush, she withdrew a piece of embroidery from under her apron and began to sew, her head bowed slightly lower than necessary. Her mother followed suit, drawing a similar project from her pocket. As Richart read aloud, both women worked diligently. The specimens they displayed were baby caps, more than half finished, telling a silent tale. Horror! They resembled little monk's cowls in shape and delicacy.

"January 20.—Confined to the litter, nearly blind, yet limping along, the Lombardy plains burst upon me. Oh, Margaret! A land flowing with milk and honey; all sloping plains, noble rivers, cheerful meadows, delightful orchards, and blooming gardens. Though winter, it looks warmer than poor beloved Holland at midsummer, making the wanderer's face shine and his heart leap for joy to see earth so kind and smiling. Here are vines, cedars, olives, and cattle in abundance, though there are three goats to every sheep. The draft oxen wear white linen on their necks, and standing beside dark green olive trees, each one is a picture. The folk, especially the women, wear delicate straw hats adorned with flowers and leaves faithfully imitated in silk, mixed with silver. Today we crossed a river prettily in a chained ferry-boat. On either bank stood a windlass, and a single man, by turning it, drew our entire company to shore—a sight I admired greatly as a stranger. Some country folk passed with us. An old woman, watching a young girl, hid her face with her hand and held out her crucifix like a knight brandishing a sword in a tournament, fearing the evil eye."

"January 25.—Safe at Venice. A place whose strange and surpassing beauty is well known to thee by the reports of our mariners. Do you remember how Peter would often fill our ears with tales of this sea-enthroned, peerless city, shaped like a bow, with its great canal and palaces built on piles, its watery ways plied by dozens of gilded boats? Of that marketplace of nations, *orbis*, non *urbis*—St. Mark's Square, his place? His statue with the peerless jewels in his eyes, and the lion at his gate? But I, lying at my window in pain, can see none of these beauties yet, only a fairly paved street which seems dull, and houses covered with oiled paper and linen instead of glass, which appears crude. The passers-by, with their habits and gestures, seem superfluous. Therefore, to avoid missing my daily comfort of whispering to thee, I will turn my eyes inward and bind my sheaves of wisdom reaped by travel. For I love thee so that no treasure pleases me unless shared with thee; and what treasure is so good and enduring as knowledge? Thus, Sir Footsore, I have learned that each nation possesses its proper wisdom and its proper folly. Methinks, could a great king or duke tramp like me and see with his own eyes, he might pick the flowers and eschew the weeds of nations, returning home to set his own folk upon Wisdom's hill. The Germans in the north are churlish, yet frank and honest; in the south, they are kindly and honest too. Their general failing is drunkenness, carried even to a dislike and contempt of sober men. They commonly say, 'If you cannot drink and eat, you cannot serve any lord well.' In England, the vulgar drink as deeply, but the respectable regard excess as a reproach, drinking a toast or two for courtesy, not gluttony, and always sweetening the wine. In their cups, the Germans use little mirth or conversation, but ply their business seriously, crying 'Seid frolich!' Their finest drunken sport is 'Kurlemurlehuff,' a method of drinking involving deftly touching the glass, the beard, and the table in turn, interspersed with whistlings and finger snaps so curiously ordered that it is a labor of Hercules, yet to the observer, right pleasant and merry. Their drinkers, advised by German physicians, sleep with pebbles in their mouths. For, as the lid of a boiling pot must be set ajar, so with these fleshy wine-pots, to vent the heat of their inward parts; despite which, many die suddenly from drink. Yet it is a matter of religion to gloss over this, attributing it to some innocent disease. Still, it is more a custom than nature, for their women mingle among the tipplers, standing but a moment to kiss the wine-cup, and are indeed most temperate in eating and drinking, and of all women, the most modest, virtuous, and true spouses and friends to their mates; far surpassing our Holland lasses, who, as maids, ask the men questions, and as wives, rule over them. Indeed, there is a wife in Tergou, not far from our door. One came to the house seeking her husband. She said, 'You will not find him: he asked my leave to go abroad this afternoon, and I granted it.'"

"'Tis true! 'Tis true!" Catherine declared. "'Twas Beck Hulse, Jonah's wife. This comes of a woman marrying a boy."

"In the south, where wine flows, the gentry drink themselves bare; but not in the north: for with beer, a nobleman will sooner burst his body than melt his lands. They are quarrelsome, but it is the liquor, not the mind; for they are not vindictive. When they have made a bad bargain drunk, they stand by it sober. They keep their windows bright and judge a man by his clothes. Whatever fruit, grain, or herb grows by the roadside, gather and eat. The owner, seeing you, will say, 'Welcome, honest man.' But if you pluck a wayside grape, your very life is in jeopardy. 'Tis eating of that Heaven gave to be drunken. The French are much fairer spoken, yet not nearly so true-hearted. Sweet words cost them nothing. They call it *payer en blanche*."

"Those scoundrels!" Denys laughed.

"Nevertheless, courtesy resides in their hearts, yes, in their very blood. They commonly say, 'Give yourself the trouble of sitting down.' Such strands of speech reveal how the wind blows. Also, at a public show, if you wish to leave your seat without losing it

About two months prior to this scene, in a small coastal town nestled between Naples and Rome, the locals gathered on the beach, their gaze fixed seaward upon a vessel laboring against a fierce gale that blew directly onto the shore. Moments passed where the ship appeared poised to withstand the storm, prompting cheers from the crowd; then, the wind and surf would drive her visibly closer to destruction, granting the spectators a secret, unspoken satisfaction. *Non quia vexari quemquam est jucunda voluptas / Sed quibus ipse malis careas quia cernere suave est.* (It is not pleasant to see anyone tormented, but it is sweet to behold someone free from such evils.) Though not scientifically designed for open-sea navigation, the ship was marvelously constructed for grounding itself, boasting an extravagant stern that caught the wind and lines resembling a cocked hat turned upside down. To the observers on the sand, the battering wooden frame seemed alive, a creature struggling against death with a panting heart. Yet, had they been transported to her deck, they would have discovered she possessed not one beating heart but many, and not a single nature, but a dozen distinct ones revealed clearly in that terrifying hour.

The mariners stumbled wildly across the deck, hauling ropes as each deemed fit, alternating between curses and prayers. Passengers huddled around the mainmast—some seated, some kneeling, others lying prostrate—clutching the bulwarks as the vessel rolled and pitched in the towering waves. Among them stood a handsome young man, his ashen cheeks contrasting with compressed lips that betrayed a fierce internal battle between terror and self-respect. He gripped a shroud tightly, wincing with every swell. This was the ill-fated Gerard.

Meanwhile, cries of prayer and vow rose from the trembling crowd amidships. Listening to them, one might think there were nearly as many deities present as there were humans. The sailors, however, placed their faith in a single Goddess, varying only her titles: "Queen of Heaven," "Star of the Sea," "Mistress of the World," "Haven of Safety." Among the landlubbers, polytheism raged. Even those who accidentally invoked the same deity did not summon the same version of that divinity. An English merchant vowed a pile of gold to Our Lady of Walsingham; a Genoese merchant promised a silver collar worth four pounds to Our Lady of Loreto; a Tuscan noble pledged ten pounds of wax candles to Our Lady of Ravenna. With equal fervor for diversity, they pledged allegiance not merely to the True Cross, but to specific iterations of the Cross associated with various modern cities.

Suddenly, a gust more violent than any before caught the sail at a fatal angle. The rotting shrouds snapped, and the mainsail tore away with a deafening crack. It descended the windward side, shrinking and darkening until it fluttered into the sea half a mile offshore, looking like a discarded sheet of paper. Before the helmsman could adjust the ship's heading, a massive wave struck her quarter, soaking the miserable crew to the bone and offering them a foretaste of chilling death.

In response, vows erupted anew. One man vowed to become a Carthusian monk if Saint Thomas would save him; another promised to pilgrimage barefoot and bareheaded to Santiago de Compostela, clad only in a coat of armor, if Saint James intervened. Others invoked Saints Thomas, Dominic, Denis, and especially Catherine of Siena. Two small Neapolitan traders stood shivering. One shouted at the top of his lungs, "I vow a waxen image of Saint Christopher at Paris, weighing exactly as much as myself, if I reach land safely!" The other nudged him, whispering, "Brother, brother, beware your vow. If you sold all your worldly possessions at public auction, it would not buy enough wax to match his weight." "Hold your tongue, you fool!" retorted the first. Then, in a whisper, "Do you think I am joking? Let me but reach land safely, and I will not give him a single straw dipped in wax."

Others lay flat, praying to the ocean itself. "O most merciful sea! O sea most generous! O bountiful sea! O beautiful sea! Be gentle, be kind, preserve us in this hour of peril!" Others wailed and moaned in pure animal terror each time the doomed ship rolled or pitched more violently than before; she was now a mere toy in the grasp of tremendous waves.

A Roman woman of humble means sat with her child at her half-exposed breast, silent amidst the wailing throng. Her cheek was ashen, her eyes calm, and her lips moved occasionally in silent prayer, yet she neither wept nor lamented nor bargained with the gods. Whenever the ship seemed truly vanishing beneath their feet, causing bearded men to squeak, she simply kissed her child. That was all. Thus, she sat patiently, nursing him in the jaws of death. Why should he lose any joy she could offer him while dying? *Moribundo?* Indeed, I believe Antiquity sat among these medieval figures. Sixteen centuries had not tarnished the old Roman blood flowing in her veins; the instinct of a race she had perhaps scarcely heard of taught her to meet death with decent dignity.

A gigantic friar stood on the poop with feet planted wide apart, like the Colossus of Rhodes, defying the peril surrounding him less than ignoring it. He recited verses from the Song of Songs with a loud, unwavering voice, inviting the passengers to confess. Some knelt before him; he listened and laid his hands upon them, absolving them as if he were in a cozy sacristy rather than a perishing ship. Gerard drew nearer and nearer to him, driven by the instinct that pulls the wavering toward the side of the impregnable. In truth, the courage of heroes facing physical odds might have paled beside that of the gigantic friar and his even more monumental composure. Here, even in extremity, two were found maintaining the dignity of their race: a woman, tender yet heroic, and a monk steeled by religion against mortal fears.

Now, with the sail gone, the sailors cut down the useless mast a foot above the deck, and it crashed over the side with its remaining rigging. This seemed to relieve the strain slightly. However, the hull, no longer propelled by canvas, could no longer outrun the sea. It struck her repeatedly on the poop, delivering tremendous blows that seemed inflicted by a rocky mountain rather than liquid water. The captain abandoned the helm and moved amidships, pale as death.

"Lighten her!" he cried. "Throw everything overboard, or we shall founder before we strike, losing the only slim chance we have of life."

While the sailors executed this order, the captain, pale himself and surrounded by equally pallid faces demanding to know their fate, spoke unlike any English skipper in similar peril. "Friends," he said, "last night, when all was fair, too fair, alas! a globe of fire approached the ship. When a pair appear, it is good fortune, and nothing can drown her on this voyage. We mariners call these fiery globes Castor and Pollux. But if Castor comes without Pollux, or Pollux without Castor, she is doomed. Therefore, like good Christians, prepare to die."

These words were met with a loud wail. When asked tremblingly how long they had to prepare, the captain replied, "She may, or may not, last half an hour; beyond that, it is impossible; she leaks like a sieve. Bustle, men, lighten her!"

The poor passengers seized everything on deck and flung it overboard. Soon, they grabbed a heavy sack; an old man lay upon it, seasick. They pulled it from underneath him. It rattled. Two men dragged it to the side; the owner sprang up with an unearthly shriek and pounced on it. "Holy Moses! What would you do? 'Tis my all! 'Tis the fruit of my entire journey! Silver candlesticks, silver plates, brooches, chalices—"

"Let go, thou hoary villain!" cried the others. "Shall all our lives be lost for thy ill-gotten gear?"

"Fling him in with it!" shouted one. "'Tis this Hebrew whom Christian men are drowning for."

Numbers quickly wrested the object from him and heaved it over the side. It splashed into the waves. Its owner let out one cry of anguish, standing glaring with white hair streaming in the wind, ready to leap after it if it had floated. But it sank, gone forever. He staggered to and fro, tearing his hair, cursing them, the ship, the sea, and all the powers of heaven and hell alike.

Then the captain cried out, "See, there is a church in sight. Steer for that church, mate, and you, friends, pray to the saint, whoever he may be."

So they steered for the church and prayed to the unknown god it honored. A tremendous sea swamped them, breaking the rudder and jamming it immovable, flooding the deck. Wild with superstitious terror, some turned to Gerard.

"Here is the cause of all!" they cried. "He has never invoked a single saint. He is a heathen; here is a pagan aboard."

"Alas, good friends, say not so," Gerard stammered, his teeth chattering with cold and fear. "Rather call these heathens, who lie praying to the sea. Friends, I honor the saints—but I dare not

Gerard secured a modest lodging on the west bank of the Tiber and set out daily in pursuit of employment, carrying samples to every workshop known to execute such commissions. The reception he met was uniformly frosty. "Our letters are cut somewhat thinner than this," remarked one merchant. "Your ink is far too dark," observed another. Yet the prevailing complaint echoed through the shops: "Of what avail is this? Latin script is scarce in Rome nowadays. Cannot you write Greek?" "Yes, but not nearly as well as Latin." "Then you shall never earn your bread in Rome."

Desperate, Gerard borrowed a beautiful Greek manuscript at a steep price, returning home with a hollow purse but unbroken spirit. Within a fortnight, he had mastered the Greek characters with remarkable speed. Eager to capitalize on this skill, he worked until noon and spent the afternoon hunting clients. When he finally presented a Greek specimen surpassing anything they owned, the traders merely shrugged, informing him that both Greek and Latin were unsellable; the city was flooded with works from across Europe. He should have arrived a year prior.

In his frustration, Gerard purchased a psaltery. His landlady, charmed by his appearance and demeanor, frequently offered him kind words. One day, she invited him to dine with her and, to his surprise, asked what had dampened his spirits. He confessed his struggles. She offered her interpretation: "Those sly traders," she declared, "have writers in their pay for whom they pay nobly, paying you poorly. No wonder they blow cold on you. It seems you write too well. How do I know? Perhaps because you do not lock your door like the churl Pietro, and women are naturally curious. Yes, yes, you write too well for them."

When Gerard sought clarification, she explained, "Your fine work might blind those trying to sell theirs." He sighed. "Alas, dame, you read people on the wrong side, yet you are so kind and frank yourself." "My dear little heart," she replied, "these Romans are a subtle race. As for me, I am a Siennese, thanks to the Virgin."

"My mistake was leaving Augsburg," Gerard admitted. "Augsburg?" she retorted haughtily. "Is that a place comparable to Rome? I have never heard of it." She then assured him he would make his fortune despite the booksellers. "Seeing you as a stranger, they lie without sense or discretion. All the world knows our great families are bitten by the writing spider; they pour out their money like water, turning good land and houses into sheets of parchment to store in chests. God help them through this frenzy, sending them safely as He has through countless others. Since scribbling became popular, there has indeed been less cutting and stabbing among rival factions, less vindictive devouring of opponents' livers, minced and fried. I can name two: His Eminence Cardinal Bassarion and His Holiness the Pope himself. These are a pair who could keep a scribe working day and night. I shall speak to Teresa; she hears the court gossip."

The following day, she returned with news that Teresa had spoken of five additional signors afflicted by the writing spider. Gerard recorded their names, purchased parchment, and spent several days preparing new specimens. He left copies with his name and address at each of their doors, awaiting results in hopeful anticipation. None came. Days turned into weeks, leaving him heartsick. Strangely, this was the same period when Margaret fought desperately against overwhelming odds to feed her male dependents in Rotterdam, only to be arrested for practicing medicine without a license rather than killing with it. Gerard felt ruin staring him in the face.

He spent his afternoons learning canzonets and laying in playing cards to color, managing to strike off a meal a day. This final stroke of genius brought fresh trouble. In these *camere locande*, the landlady prepared all meals, though the lodgers supplied the provisions. Her hostess quickly detected his deception. With a brusque opening that made him blush and look frightened, she pacified herself instantly and appealed to his common sense: Was adversity something to be faced on an empty stomach?

"Patienza, my lad! Times will mend; meanwhile, I will feed you out of love for heaven." (Italian for "gratis.")

"Nay, hostess," Gerard protested, "my purse is not yet entirely void, and it would add to my trouble if honest folk suffered loss through me."

"Why, you are as mad as your neighbor Pietro with his single bad picture."

"Why, how do you know it is a bad picture?"

"Because nobody buys it. There is one who lacks talent; he must don a helmet and carry his panel as a shield."

Gerard pricked up his ears. She continued, explaining that Pietro had arrived from Florence with money and an unfinished painting, renting an unfurnished room opposite hers and furnishing it neatly. Upon completion, Pietro received visitors and offers—offers she deemed generous—which he refused so disdainfully that he made enemies. Since then, he had often taken the painting out to sell but always returned it. Over the past month, she had watched furniture disappear from his room; now he wore only one suit and slept on a large chest. She had discovered this only by peering through the keyhole, noting how vigilantly he locked his door whenever he left.

"Is he afraid we shall steal his chest or his picture, that no soul in all Rome is foolish enough to buy?"

"Nay, sweet hostess; do you not see it is his poverty he wishes to hide?"

"And the greater fool he! Should we not all give him a trial?"

"How you speak of him! His case is mine; besides, he is your countryman."

"Oh, we Siennese love strangers. Is his case yours? Nay, quite the contrary. You are the comeliest youth ever lodged in this house, with hair like gold; he is a dark, sour-visaged lunatic. Moreover, you know how to win over a woman, whereas he does not. Nevertheless, I wish he would not starve to death in my house, giving me a bad reputation. Anyway, one starving guest is enough in any household. You are far from home, and it falls to me, as the mistress here, to count your meals—for me and the Dutch wife, your mother, who is far away. We two women shall settle that matter. Mind your own business, being a man, and leave cooking and such tasks to us, who are in the world for little else but roasting fowl, suckling men at birth, and sweeping up the cobwebs of grown-ups."

"Dear kind dame, truly you often remind me of my mother, who is far away."

"All the better; I shall remind you more before I am done with you." The honest soul beamed with delight.

Gerard, neither an egotist nor blinded by female affection, recognized his own sorrow in the plight of poor, proud Pietro. The more he pondered it, the more resolved he became to share his humble means with the unfortunate artist; Pietro's sympathy would surely repay him. He attempted to intercept Pietro but failed. One day, he heard a groan inside the room. Knocking yielded no answer, but a second knock elicited a gruff command to enter. Obeying somewhat timidly, Gerard stepped into a garret furnished simply with a chair, a picture facing the wall, an iron basin, an easel, and a long chest where a haggard young man sat coiled. Nothing resembled a cobra ready to strike the first intruder more vividly.

"Good Signor Pietro," Gerard began, "forgive me that, weary of my own solitude, I intrude on yours. I am your nearest neighbor in this house and think your brother in fortune. I am an artist too."

"You are a painter? Welcome, signer. Sit down on my bed." Pietro leaped up and gestured grandly to the vacant space. Gerard bowed and smiled, though hesitating slightly.

"I may not call myself a painter. I am a writer, a calligrapher. I copy Greek and Latin manuscripts when I can find patrons."

"And you call that an artist?"

"Without offense to your superior merit, Signor Pietro."

"No offense, stranger, none. Only, it seems to me an artist is one who thinks and paints his thought. Now a calligrapher merely traces in black and white the thoughts of another."

"'Tis well distinguished, signor. But then, a writer can record the thoughts of the great ancients and matters of pure reason, such as no man may paint: yea, and the thoughts of God, which angels could not paint. But let that pass. I am a painter as well, albeit a sorry one."

"The better thy luck. 'They will buy thy work in Rome.'"

"But seeking to commend myself to one of thy eminence, I thought it wise to call myself a capable writer rather than a scurvy painter."

At that moment, footsteps sounded on the stairs. "Ah! 'Tis the good dame!" cried Gerard. "Hostess, I am conversing with Signor Pietro. I dare say he will allow me to have my humble dinner here."

The Italian bowed gravely. The landlady brought in Gerard's dinner, steaming and savory. She placed the dish on the bed with a face devoid of expression and departed. Gerard began to eat but stopped midway.

"I am an ill-mannered churl, Signor Pietro. I never eat to my satisfaction when dining alone. For our Lady's sake, put a spoon into this ragout with me; 'tis not uns

About a week later, the two friends sat side by side at their easels, yet their spirits were worlds apart. Pietro worked with erratic bursts of energy; he would dash wildly at his canvas, accomplishing wonders in mere moments before collapsing into silence, only to resume his labor in a fresh rage and finally lay down his brush with a heavy groan. Throughout this tempestuous display, the shrewd Dutchman remained unflappable, working steadily and offering calm smiles. Plainly speaking, Gerard—who possessed the rare ability to master every man he befriended—had successfully harnessed Pietro's Onagra. Together, they were painting playing cards, a venture designed to "boil the pot."

Once finished, the indignant master took up his completed work to begin his daily rounds in search of customers. Gerard earnestly begged him to take the deck of cards as well, urging him to attempt a sale. Pietro initially struck a pose of pure venom, but eventually, embracing Gerard in the traditional Italian style, he relented. First, however, he dried the freshly finished cards in the fierce sunlight of that blessed climate. Left alone, Gerard sketched a few Greek letters and then repaired a small tear in his hose.

His landlady discovered him thus occupied and inquired with ironic amusement whether there were no women in the house. "When you have finished that," she said, "come and speak with Teresa, my friend—the woman I mentioned to you, whose husband is hardly worth much, yet boasts of his acquaintance with the great."

Gerard descended the stairs, and who should meet him but the Roman matron. "Ah, Madam," he exclaimed, "is it you? The good lady told me nothing of this. And the little fair-haired boy—is he well? Is he none the worse for his voyage in that strange boat?"

"He is well," replied the matron.

"Why, what are you two discussing?" asked the landlady, staring alternately at them with wide eyes. "And why do you tremble so, *Teresa mia*?"

"He saved my child's life," Teresa declared, struggling to compose herself.

"What!" cried the landlady. "My lodger? And he never told me a single word of such a thing! Are you not ashamed to look me in the face?"

"Alas, speak not harshly to him," the matron interceded gently. She then turned to her companion and poured forth a glowing account of Gerard's conduct. During this recital, Gerard stood blushing like a maiden, scarcely recognizing his own actions, as gratitude painted them in hues far more beautiful than reality.

"And to think you should ask me to serve your lodger, of whom I knew nothing save that he bore your good word, oh, Fiammina! That was sufficient for me. Dear youth, in serving you, I serve myself."

It was followed by an eager discourse between the two women regarding how to breach the thick wall of fees, commissions, and legal cunning that separated the patrons of art from an unknown artist in the Eternal City. Teresa smiled sadly at Gerard's naivety in having left specimens of his skill at the doors of the mighty without proper introduction.

"What!" she exclaimed. "Without promising the servants a share—and without even tipping them to allow the Signori to view your merchandise! It might as well have been cast into the Tiber."

"Well-a-day!" sighed Gerard. "Then how is an artist to find a patron? For artists are poor, not rich."

"By seeking a city nobler and less greedy than this," Teresa advised. "*La corte Romana non vuol' pecora senza lana*" (The Roman court does not want sheep without wool). She fell into thought and promised to return the next day. The landlady congratulated Gerard immediately upon her departure. "Teresa has something in her mind," she remarked.

Scarcely had Teresa gone when Pietro returned with his picture, looking black as thunder. Gerard exchanged a glance with the landlady and followed him upstairs to offer comfort.

"What! Have they allowed you to bring home your masterpiece?"

"As heretofore."

"More fools they, then."

"That is not necessarily the worse."

"Why, what is the matter?"

"They have bought the cards!" Pietro shouted, hammering the empty air furiously left and right.

"All the better," Gerard replied cheerfully.

"They flew at me for them! They were enraptured. They tried to conceal their longing, but could not. I saw, I feigned, I pillaged; curse those simpletons!" He threw a dozen small silver coins onto the floor, leaped upon them, dancing with basilisk-like eyes, kicking them assiduously until they spun and scattered across the room.

Down went Gerard on his knees, following the maltreated innocents directly, transferring them tenderly into his purse.

"You should rather smile at their ignorance and put it to profit," he said.

"And so I will," Pietro answered, his voice thick with concentrated indignation. "The brutes! We shall paint a pack a day; we shall set the whole city gambling and ruining itself, while we live like princes upon its vices and stupidity."

"There was one of the Queens, though," Pietro continued, his tone softening. "I had fain have kept back. 'Twas you who painted her, brother. She had lovely red-brown hair and sapphire eyes, and above all, a soul."

"Pietro," said Gerard softly, "I painted that one from my very heart."

The quick-witted Italian nodded, his eyes twinkling mischievously. "You love her so dearly, yet you leave her."

"Pietro," Gerard replied, "it is precisely because I love her so dear that I have wandered this weary road."

This poignant conversation was abruptly interrupted by the landlady calling from below, "Come down, you are wanted." He descended, and there stood Teresa once more.

"Come with me, Ser Gerard."

Gerard walked in silence beside Teresa, his mind wandering in the contemplative fashion of an artist, speculating on what she intended to do with him, rather than daring to ask. Finally, she broke the quiet herself. "A friend has informed me," she explained, "of a goldsmith's wife seeking a writer. Her shop is nearby; you will not travel far."

Soon they arrived at the goldsmith's dwelling. "Madam," Teresa began politely, "Leonora tells me you require a writer. I have brought you a most handsome one—a man who saved my child from drowning at sea. Pray, look upon him with favor."

The goldsmith's wife, Madama Olympia, complied in spirit if not in action. She fixed her gaze upon Gerard's fine features and seemed unable to look away. Yet her verbal response was disappointing. "Nay, I have no need for a writer," she declared. "Ah! Now I recall, it was my gossip, Claelia the sausage-maker, who requested one. She told me, and I passed the message to Leonora."

Teresa offered a graceful excuse and withdrew. Claelia lived some distance away, and by the time they reached her home, she was absent. Teresa remained calm. "I shall wait for her return," she stated, sitting so still, so dignified, and so statuesque that Gerard began to sketch her furtively. Just then, Claelia returned.

"Madam, I hear from the excellent Olympia that you seek a writer," Claelia announced, taking Gerard's hand and leading him forward. "I have brought you a beautiful one; he rescued my child from the cruel waves. For the sake of our Lady, look kindly upon him."

"My good dame, my fair Sir," Claelia replied, "I have no use for a writer. But now that you remind me, it was my friend Appia Claudia, a tailor living in the Via Lepida, who asked for one recently."

Teresa retreated quietly. "Madam," Gerard interjected gently, "this seems likely to become a tedious pursuit for you."

Teresa opened her eyes wide. "What has ever been accomplished without a measure of patience?" she added softly. "We shall knock at every door in Rome, and you shall receive justice."

"But, Madam, I believe we are being shadowed," Gerard whispered. "I noticed a man following us, sometimes at a distance, sometimes close behind."

"I have seen him too," Teresa said coolly, though her cheeks flushed slightly. "It is my poor Lodovico." She paused, turned, and beckoned with her finger. A figure approached them, moving somewhat reluctantly. Upon reaching them, she stared him directly in the face; he looked sheepish.

"Lodovico mio," she said, "acquaint yourself with this young Sir, of whom I have often spoken. Know him and love him, for he is the one who saved your wife and child."

At these final words, Lodovico, who had previously bowed and grinned artificially, instantly transformed into an expression of heartfelt gratitude and embraced Gerard warmly. Yet, there was something unsettling in his initial demeanor—his secret following of his wife—that made Gerard uneasy under this affectionate greeting. Nevertheless, he asked, "Shall we have your company, Sir Lodovico?"

"No, Signor," Lodovico replied, "I do not go that way across the Tiber."

"Then addio," Teresa said meaningfully.

"When shall you return home, Teresa mia?" Lodovico asked.

"When I have completed my errand," she answered.

They continued their journey in silence. Teresa now wore a somber, almost gloomy expression. Briefly, Appia Claudia proved merciful; she did not send them further across the river, but merely directed them a hundred yards down the street to Lucretia, who kept a glove shop. Lucretia also required a writer, though Appia Claudia could not imagine why.

Lucretia was a cheerful little woman who welcomed them warmly. "I do not need a writer myself," she exclaimed, "for I keep all my accounts in my head. But it was for my confessor, Father Colonna. He is utterly mad for them!"

"I have heard of his Excellency," Teresa remarked. "Who has not?"

"But, good dame, he is a friar; he has taken a vow of poverty. I cannot allow this young man to write without payment. He saved my child at sea."

"Did he now?" Lucretia asked, casting an approving glance at Gerard. "Well, rest assured; a Colonna never lacks funds. The good father need only speak the word, and the princes of his lineage will pour a thousand crowns into his lap. Such a confessor, dame! The finest in Rome. His mind is leagues and leagues away constantly; he pays no heed to what you say. Indeed, I confess my sins to him no more readily than I would tell them to that wall. Once, to test him, I confessed alongside everyone else that I had murdered my lodger's little girl and baked her in a pie. Well, when my voice ceased confessing, he startled out of his trance, gathered a grave expression, and said, 'My erring sister, recite three Paternosters and three Ave Marias kneeling, abstain from butter and eggs next Wednesday, and pax vobiscum!' Then off he went with his hands behind him, looking as though I did not exist."

Teresa waited patiently, then calmly steered this digressive lady back to the point. "Would you be so kind as to accompany this worthy youth to the friar and plead his case?"

"Alas! How can I leave my shop? And what necessity is there? His door is always open to writers, painters, scholars, and all such creatures. Why, once he refused to receive the Duke of Urbino because a learned Greek scholar was closeted with him. The friar's head and the scholar's were pressed so closely together over a dusty parchment newly arrived from Greece that you could fit both cowls over the pair. That is what Onesta told me. She mostly comes here for a chat when running errands."

"This is the man for you, my friend," Teresa said. "All you need do," she continued, "is go to his lodgings (my boy will show you) and tell Onesta you come from me and are a writer. She will take you up to him. If you place a piece of silver in the wench's hand, it will do you no harm; it stands to reason."

"I have silver," Teresa said warmly.

"But wait," Lucretia interrupted. "Mind one thing. Whatever this young man claims he can do, he must be capable of doing it, or let him avoid the good friar like poison. He is a very wild beast against all bunglers. Why, just the other day, someone brought him an ill-carved crucifix. He said, 'Is this how you present the *Salvator Mundi*, who died for you in mortal agony? And you dare grudge Him careful work? This slovenly trinket, a crucifix? But since it is a crucifix of some sort, and I am a holy man, I would dust your jacket with your crucifix!' Onesta heard every word through the keyhole. So beware."

"Have no fears, Madam," Teresa said loftily. "I will answer for his ability; he saved my child."

Gerard was not subtle enough to grasp this conclusion. Far from sharing Teresa's confidence, he begged for a delay. "Pray, let me postpone this until tomorrow. Would it not be better?"

"Here is a coward for you," Lucretia chided.

"No, he is not a coward," Teresa snapped, flaring up; "he is modest."

"I am afraid of this high-born, fastidious friar," Gerard admitted. "Consider, Dame Teresa, he has seen the work of every writer in Italy. If you would only let me prepare a superior piece of work than I have thus far produced, then tomorrow I will face him."

"I consent," Teresa agreed.

They walked home together. Not far from Gerard's lodging was a shop selling vellum. A magnificent white skin hung in the window. Gerard gazed at it wistfully, knowing he could not afford it, so he hurried past. However, he quickly decided where to procure vellum. Parting ways with Teresa at his own door, he ran hastily upstairs, retrieved the bond he had carried all the way from Sevenbergen, and laid it on the table with a sigh. He then prepared his chemicals to erase the old writing; but since this was his last chance to read it, he overcame his deep aversion to illegible script and proceeded to decipher the deed despite its detestable abbreviations.

The document revealed that Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was to advance money to Floris Brandt regarding a piece of land, to be repaid from the rent. On this discovery, Gerard felt it would be imprudent and improper to destroy the deed. Instead, he vowed to decipher every word at his leisure. Descending the stairs, he resolved to buy a small sheet of vellum with his share of the card-money.

At the foot of the stairs, he found the landlady and Teresa conversing. At the sight of him, the former cried out, "There he is! You are caught, Donna Mia. See what she has purchased for you?" She whipped out from under her apron the very sheet of vellum Gerard had longed for.

"Why, Dame! Why

Happy is the man who possesses two anchor cables: Merit, and Women. Oh, that I, like Gerard, might possess a "chain of ladies" to haul myself up by! Then I should become a prose laureate, or a professor of the spasmodic style, or whatever fashionable title suits the moment, in no time at all. Until then, let us sketch the portrait of Fra Colonna.

The true revivals of ancient learning and philosophy were largely the work of two fictional writers—Petrarch and Boccaccio. Their labors were not immediately crowned with great public triumph; rather, they sowed a good seed that never perished. Instead, it quickened in the soil, waiting patiently for the sunshine. From their era onward, Italy was never devoid of a native scholar or two versed in Greek; indeed, any learned Greek who landed there was received with fraternal warmth. Before the fourteenth century closed, we saw the births of Poggio, Valla, and the elder Guarino. Early in the fifteenth century, Florence under Cosimo de' Medici became a nest of Platonists. Headed by Gemistus Pletho, a man born Greek, this group began around 1440 to critique Aristotle. For few minds are broad enough to be fair to great A without becoming unjust to capital B. Theodore Gaza defended that great man with moderation; George of Trebizond attacked him with acerbity, retorting sharply against Plato. Then Cardinal Bessarion, another man born Greek, resisted George and his idol in a tract titled *Adversus calumniatorem Platonis*. Whether wise or not, pugnacity is a form of vitality.

Born without this controversial bile in such a zealous epoch, Francesco Colonna, a young Florentine nobleman, devoted himself entirely to the arts. At twenty, he took the habit of a Dominican friar. His objective was quiet study. He retired from idle company and faction fights—the humming and stinging of the human hive—to seek refuge with Saint Dominic and the Nine Muses. An eager student of languages, pictures, statues, chronology, coins, and monumental inscriptions, he found that these latter objects loosened his faith in popular histories. He traveled many years in the East, returning laden with spoils: masterpieces of manuscripts, and fluent in Greek, Latin, Hebrew, and Syriac. He discovered that his homeland had not stood still. Besides Cosimo, other lettered princes had risen, including Alfonso, King of Naples, and the Este brothers. Above all, his old friend Thomas of Sarzana had been made Pope, lending a mighty impulse to letters. He had accumulated five thousand manuscripts in the Vatican library and commissioned Poggio to translate Diodorus Siculus and Xenophon's *Cyropaedia*, Laurentius Valla to translate Herodotus and Thucydides, Theodore Gaza to tackle Theophrastus, and George of Trebizond to render Eusebius and certain treatises of Plato.

The monk found Plato and Aristotle under a truce, but Poggio and Valla at loggerheads over verbs and nouns, burning with *odium philologicum*. All this was heaven to him, and he settled down in his native land, his life a rosy dream. None are happier than the versatile, provided they do not need to earn their daily bread through it. And Fra Colonna was the very definition of versatility. He knew seven or eight languages and a touch of mathematics; he could write, paint, model, sing, and play the lute. Furthermore, he possessed the rare ability to relish superior excellence in all these branches. For this trait alone, he deserved to be as happy as he was. Gauge the intellects of your acquaintances, and you will find few whose minds are not deaf, blind, or dead to some great art or science—"wisdom at one entrance quite shut out." Those who are both conceited and stupid will parade these holes rather than blush for them.

A zealot in art, the friar was a skeptic in religion. In every age, there are a few men who hold the opinions of another age, past or future. Being a lump of simplicity, his skepticism was as naive as his enthusiasm. He pretended to view the religious ceremonies of his day as mere models, while regarding the worship of gods and departed heroes as mummeries fit only for the populace. Yet, his mind drew an unconscious, droll distinction: whatever Christian ceremony his learning taught him was of purely pagan origin, and thus he respected it out of reverence for antiquity. Had he, with his turn of mind, been a pagan living in that era, he would have scorned it from his philosophical heights.

Fra Colonna was charmed by his new artist, Gerard. Having access to half the palaces in Rome, he sounded Gerard's praises until the painter was soon summoned to resign his current post. "But I am so happy with you, Father," objected Gerard. "Fiddlesticks about being happy with me!" replied Fra Colonna. "You must not be happy; you must be a man of the world. The grand lesson I impress upon the young is to be a man of the world. Now, these Montesini can pay you three times as much as I can, and they shall too—by Jupiter!" And the friar slapped a terrifying price on Gerard's pen. It was acceded to without a murmur. Much higher prices were being commanded for copying than authorship ever achieved for centuries under the printing press. Gerard received three hundred crowns for Aristotle's treatise on Rhetoric. The great are mighty sweet to their pets while the fancy lasts; and in the rage for Greek manuscripts, the handsome writer soon became a favorite, caressed by nobles of both sexes like a lapdog. It would have turned a vain fellow's head, but the canny Dutchman saw the steel hand beneath the velvet glove and did not presume.

Nevertheless, it was a proud day for him when he found himself seated with Fra Colonna at the table of his present employer, Cardinal Bessarion. They were perhaps a mile below the summit of that social table, but never mind—they were there. Gerard had the distinct advantage of seeing roast pheasants served with all their feathers intact, as if they had just flown out of a coppice rather than off the spit, alongside chickens cooked in bottles, tender as peaches. But the grand novelty was the napkins, surpassingly fine and folded into cocked hats, bird's wings, and fans, instead of lying flat. This electrified Gerard, though my readers may have witnessed this dazzling phenomenon without tumbling backward out of their chairs.

After dinner, the tables were split apart and carried away, revealing underneath another table spread with sweetmeats. The signoras and signorinas fell upon them and gorged themselves, while the signors eyed them with reasonable suspicion. "But, dear father," objected Gerard, "I see not the bifurcated daggers with which men say his Excellency arms the left hand of a man." "Nay, 'tis Cardinal Orsini who invented that peevish instrument for his guests to fumble their meat withal. One, being in haste, did skewer his tongue to his palate with it, I hear; *O tempora, O mores!* How would the ancients, reclining godlike at their feasts, have spurned such pedantries?"

As soon as the ladies had disported themselves among the sugar-plums, the tables were suddenly removed, and the guests sat in a row against the wall. Then entered, ducking and scraping, two ecclesiastics with lutes. They knelt at the Cardinal's feet and sang the service of the day before retiring with a deep obeisance. In answer, the Cardinal fingered his skullcap as our late Iron Duke once tipped his hat. The company dispersed, and Gerard had dined with a Cardinal and one who had thrice just missed being Pope.

But greater honor was in store. One day, the Cardinal sent for him. After praising the beauty of his work, he took Gerard in his coach to the Vatican, leading him up a private stair to a luxurious little room with a great oriel window. There were inkstands, sloping frames for writing, and all the instruments of art. The Cardinal whispered to a courtier, and presently the Pope's private secretary appeared with a glorious, grimy old manuscript of Plutarch's *Lives*. Soon, Gerard was seated alone, copying it, awe-struck yet half-delighted at the thought that His Holiness would handle his work and read it.

The papal inkstands were all glorious externally, but within, the ink was vile. Gerard, however, carried ever good, home-made ink in a dirty little inkhorn. Praying on his knees for a firm and skillful hand, he set to work. One side of his room was nearly occupied by a massive curtain divided in the center, but its ample folds overlapped. After a while, Gerard felt drawn to peep through that curtain. He resisted the impulse. It returned. It overpowered him. Leaving Plutarch, he stole across the matted floor, took the folds of the curtain, and gently gathered them up with his fingers. Putting his nose through the chink, he ran it against a cold steel halbert. Two soldiers, armed cap-a-pie, held their glittering weapons crossed in a triangle. Gerard drew swiftly back; but in that instant, he heard the soft murmur of voices and saw a group of persons cringing before some hidden figure. He never repeated his attempt to pry through the guarded curtain, but he often eyed it. Every hour or so, an ecclesiastic would pe

Fra Colonna possessed unrestricted access to the Pope's library, and occasionally, he would cease his labors at the same hour Gerard did, choosing instead to walk the city with him. On these occasions, the fortunate artist perceived everything *en beau*, enveloped in the majestic splendor of Rome—its churches, palaces, and ancient ruins.

The friar, however, reserved his praise solely for the ruins, dousing the enthusiasm regarding the living city with a bucket of cold water.

"This place, Rome?" he declared. "It is merely the tomb of mighty Rome." He guided Gerard's gaze downward, pointing out that twenty or thirty feet of the old triumphal arches lay buried beneath the soil, while the modern thoroughfares ran directly over ancient palaces and the severed tops of columns. Coupling the relatively cramped boundaries of the contemporary city with the colossal remnants of antiquity that peeked sporadically above ground, he struck a striking comparison. "Listen to me," he said. "This village they call Rome is but as one of those swallow's nests you will see constructed upon the eaves of a decaying abbey."

"Old Rome must indeed have been magnificent then," Gerard remarked.

"Judge for yourself, my son," the friar replied. "You see the great sewer, a work of the Romans in their infancy, destined to outlast even Mount Vesuvius. You see the fragments of the Temple of Peace. Imagine how you would appear if you could behold the Capitol with its twenty-five temples! Observe this Monte Savello; what is it, if you please, but the ruins of the ancient Theatre of Marcellus? And as for Testaccio, one of the highest hills in modern Rome, it is nothing more than an ancient refuse heap; the women of old Rome discarded their broken pots and pans there, and lo—a mountain was formed."

*"Ex pede Herculem; ex ungue leonem"* ("From the foot judge the Hercules; from the claw, the lion").

Gerard listened with respectful attention, but when the holy friar proceeded by analogy to suggest that the moral superiority of the pagan Romans was proportional to their grandeur, Gerard resisted stoutly. "Has the world then suffered loss through Christ's coming?" he asked, though he immediately blushed, sensing he was reproaching his benefactor.

"Saints forbid!" exclaimed the friar. "'Twere heresy to say otherwise." Having made this direct concession, he gradually evaded the implication through subtle circumlocution until they reached the forbidden door via a spiral back staircase. Along the way, they encountered a church crowded with people in the porch, where a demon was being exorcised.

Fra Colonna had a peculiar habit of emitting a curious, low moan whenever facts that Zeno or Epicurus would reject were presented to him as truth. To those accustomed to hearing it, this moan conveyed not only strong dissent but also a profound pity for human credulity, ignorance, and error—especially when such blindness obscured the merits of paganism.

The friar sighed deeply and said, "Then let us depart."

"Nay, Father, pray stay! Pray stay! I have never seen a devil cast out."

The friar accompanied Gerard into the church, though he first gave a hearty shrug. Inside, they found the demoniac forced to his knees before the altar, a scarf tied tightly around his neck, by which the officiating priest held him fast like a dog on a leash.

Few people were present, for rumor had spread that the last demon expelled from that church had fled no further than into the lap of someone in the congregation: *"As a coney ferreted out of one burrow runs to the next."*

Upon their arrival, the priest seemed to deem the audience sufficient and commenced the ritual. Facing the demoniac, breviary in hand, he first sought to identify the entity possessing the man.

"Come out, Ashtaroth. Oh ho! Not you then. Come out, Belial. Come out, Tatzi. Come out, Eza. No; he trembles not. Come out, Azymoth. Come out, Feriander. Come out, Foletho. Come out, Astyma. Come out, Nebul. Aha! Have I found you? 'Tis thou, thou reptile, at thy old tricks. Let us pray!"

"Oh, Lord, we pray thee to drive the foul fiend Nebul out of this Thy creature: out of his hair, his eyes, his nose, his mouth, his ears, his gums, his teeth, his shoulders, his arms, his legs, his loins, his stomach, his bowels, his thighs, his knees, his calves, his feet, his ankles, his finger-nails, his toe-nails, and his soul. Amen."

The priest then rose, turned to the onlookers with quiet geniality, and announced, "Gentlemen, we have here as obstinate a devil as you might see in a summer day." Turning back to the patient, he addressed him with great severity, alternating between speaking to the man and the fiend. They answered in turn through the same mouth, now claiming hatred for the holy names the priest invoked, now complaining of internal misery.

It was the priest who first confused the victim and the culprit in the minds of the observers, assaulting the former physically—he cuffed him, kicked him, and spat repeatedly in his face. He then lit a candle, dimmed it, and burned it until it scorched his fingers, dropping it quickly thereafter. Next, he displayed the *Corpus Domini* to the patient. He lit another candle, handled it more cautiously, and finally spoke civilly to the demoniac in his human guise, dismissing him and accepting the congratulations of the assembly.

"Good father," Gerard observed, "you seem to know their names by heart. Our northern priests possess no such exquisite knowledge of the hellish squadrons."

"Aye, young man, here we know all their names, and their ways, the reptiles. This Nebul is a bitter hard one to track down."

He then recounted several of his experiences to the company in the most affable manner, concluding with a feat from the previous day when he drove a great, hulking fiend out of a woman through her mouth, leaving behind nails, pins, and a tuft of hair. The creature had cried out in anguish, *"It is not thou that conquerest me. See that stone on the windowsill. Know that the angel Gabriel, descending to earth, once alighted upon that stone; 'tis that which has done my business."*

The friar moaned. "And you believed him?"

"Certainly! Who but an infidel would discredit a revelation so precise?"

"What, believe the father of lies? That pushes credulity beyond endurance."

"Oh, a liar does not always lie."

"Aye, he does whenever he begins with an improbable story and produces a holy relic, arming you against the Satanic host. Fiends—if any exist—are not so simple. You should have answered him from antiquity: *'Timeo Danaos et dona ferentes'* ('I fear Greeks bearing gifts'). Some blackguard chopped his wife's head off on that stone, young man; you take my word for it."

With that, the friar hurried Gerard away.

"Alack, Father, I fear you have abashed the good priest."

"Aye, by Pollux," the friar chuckled, "I blistered him with a single touch of 'Socratic interrogation.' What modern mind can parry the weapons of antiquity?"

One afternoon, after completing his daily work, a fine lackey summoned Gerard to the Palazzo Cesarini. Upon entering a noble apartment, he found a girl seated, working on a tapestry. She rose, excused herself, and returned shortly with news that her mistress wished to see him.

Gerard waited a good hour in the vast room, beginning to fret. "These nobles think nothing of a poor fellow's time," he muttered. Just as he resolved to slip out, the door opened, and a superb beauty entered, followed by two maids. It was the young Princess of the House of Cesarini. She entered talking loudly and haughtily to her attendants, but upon seeing Gerard, her voice softened to a very feminine tone.

"Are you the writer, Messer?"

"I am, Signora."

"'Tis well."

She seated herself; Gerard and the maids remained standing.

"What is your name, good youth?"

"Gerard, Signora."

"Gerard? Body of Bacchus! Is that the name of a human creature?"

"It is a Dutch name, Signora. I was born at Terghou, in Holland."

"A harsh name, girls, for so well-favored a youth. What say you?"

The maids assented warmly.

"What did I summon you for?" the lady inquired with lofty languor. "Ah, I remember. Sit down, Ser Gerardo, and write me a letter to Ercole Orsini, my lover—at least, he insists I do."

Gerard seated himself, prepared paper and ink, and looked up for instructions.

The Princess, seated on a high chair that resembled a throne, looked down at him with inquiring eyes.

"Well, Gerardo."

"I am ready, Your Excellence."

"Write, then."

"But I await the words."

"And who, do you suppose, is to provide them?"

"Who but Your Grace, whose letter it is to be?"

"Gramercy! What

Princess Claelia commanded a full-length portrait of herself. Gerard suggested employing his acquaintance, Pietro Vanucci.

She declined immediately. "Let it be time to cast a slight upon the Gerardo when his work displeases me," she replied.

Then Gerard, acknowledging he was an excellent draftsman but lacking skill as a colorist, begged her to pose as a Roman statue. He demonstrated how closely he could mimic marble upon paper. She agreed initially, but hesitated when the enthusiastic artist explained she must don the tunic, toga, and sandals of antiquity.

"Why, I'd rather be presented in my smock!" she exclaimed with medieval frankness.

"Alack, signorina," Gerard protested, "have you never observed the ancient custom? It is so free, so ample, so simple, yet so noble—and most becoming to your Highness, to whom Heaven has granted Roman features, a shapely arm, and a graceful hand, albeit in modern attire."

"What? Can you flatter like the rest, Gerardo?"

"Well, grant me time to consider. Return on Saturday, and then I shall decide."

During this respite, the garments were crafted and tried on within her chambers to determine if they compensated sufficiently for being a millennium out of date.

Hurrying toward this appointment, Gerard was suddenly halted, rooted to the spot before a shop window. His keen eye had spotted an open copy of Lactantius. *"That is beautifully written, at least,"* he thought.

He scrutinized it a moment longer with all his senses.

It was not written at all. It was printed.

Gerard groaned inwardly. *"I am undone; my enemy stands at the door. The press is in Rome."*

Entering the shop, he feigned nonchalance and inquired how long the printing press had been in the city. The merchant claimed there was no such thing in Rome. "Oh, regarding the Lactantius; that was printed atop the Apennines."

"What? Did the printing press tumble down from the moon there?"

"Nay, messer," the trader laughed, pointing to the title page. "It sprang up from Germany. Look!"

Gerard snatched the book eagerly and read the inscription: *Opera et impensis Sweynheim et Pannartz Alumnorum Joannis Fust. Impressum Subiacis. A.D. 1465.*

"Shall I sell it, messer? Observe how fair and even the letters are. Few remain who can write thus, and it costs but a quarter of the price."

"I would dearly love to possess it," Gerard said sadly, "but my heart forbids it. Know that I am a calligrapher, and these disciples of Fust pursue me worldwide, stealing the bread from my mouth. Yet I wish them no ill. Heaven forbid!" He hurried away.

*"Dearest Margaret,"* he whispered to himself, *"we must lose no time. We must make hay while the sun shines. One month more, and an avalanche of printer's type shall roll down from those Apennines onto Rome, destroying us, the writers."*

He nearly ran to the Princess Claelia.

He was ushered into an apartment unfamiliar to him. Though not large, it was luxuriously appointed; a fountain bubbled in the center, and the floor was paved with panther skins, dressed with hair, ensuring silence underfoot. The room served as an antechamber to the princess's boudoir, separated only by a magnificent tapestry curtain.

Left alone, Gerard grew uneasy, doubting if he had been led astray. These fears vanished quickly.

A light step approached swiftly behind the curtain. It parted, revealing a figure the heathens might have worshipped. She was neither quite Venus nor quite Minerva, but a blend of both—nobler than Venus, more feminine than Jupiter's daughter. Toga, tunic, sandals; nothing was modern. As for beauty, it transcended all eras.

Gerard sprang upright, the artist within him flushing with delight.

"Oh!" he cried innocently, gazing in rapture.

This added the final charm to his model: a light blush tinged her cheeks, her eyes brightened, and her mouth curved in delicious self-satisfaction at this genuine tribute to her allure.

They stared at one another for some time until she realized his eloquence consisted merely of gasps and staring. Finally, she spoke. "Well, Gerardo, you see I have fashioned myself an antique monster for you."

"A monster? I doubt Fra Colonna would fall to his knees and adore your Highness, seeing you thus attired."

"Nay, I care not to be adored by an old man. I prefer to be loved by a young one... of my own choosing."

Gerard produced his pencils, arranged his canvas covered with sturdy paper, and began to work. So absorbed was he that he spared no mercy for his model. After nearly an hour in one position, she spoke faintly, "Gerardo, I can stand no longer, even for you."

"Sit down and rest awhile, Signora."

"I thank you," she replied, sinking into a chair, turning pale and sighing.

Gerard was alarmed, realizing his inconsideration. He fetched water from the fountain, intending to splash it on her face, but she raised a white hand in supplication. "Nay, hold it to my brow with your own hand; pray, do not fling it at me!"

Timidly, Gerard applied his wet hand to her forehead.

"Ah!" she sighed, "that is refreshing. Again."

He repeated the action. She thanked him and requested he ring a small handbell on the table. A maid arrived promptly and was dispatched to Floretta with orders to bring a large fan.

Floretta soon appeared with the fan. The moment she drew near the Princess, the lady's aristocratic nostrils flared wide like a racehorse's. "Wretch!" she shrieked. Rising instantly with renewed vigor, she seized Floretta by the left hand, twisted it violently into her hair, and with her right hand delivered three severe boxings to her ears.

Floretta screamed and sobbed, receiving no mercy. The antique toga, now loose, revealed a bare arm that seemed as powerful as it was beautiful. It rose and fell like the piston of a steam engine, delivering heavy slaps one after another against Floretta's shoulders. The final blow drove her sobbing and screaming through the curtain, where she continued to wail bitterly for some time.

"Saints of heaven!" cried Gerard. "What is amiss? What has she done?"

"She knows perfectly well. 'Tis not the first time. That filthy toad! I shall teach her to approach me smelling of the musk-cat."

"Alas, Signora, 'twas a minor fault, I believe."

"A minor fault? Nay, 'twas a grievous offense." With an astonishing drop from anger to humility and sweetness, she added, "Are you angry with me for beating her, Gerardo?"

"Signora, it ill becomes me to discipline you; yet I deem those Heaven appoints to govern others should govern themselves."

"That is true, Gerardo. How wise you are, being so young." She summoned the other maid and handed her a small purse. "Take this to Floretta and tell her 'the Gerardo' has interceded for her; therefore, I must forgive her. There, Gerardo."

Gerard blushed crimson at the compliment but, unsure how to respond equally, asked if he should resume her portrait.

"Not yet; beating that hussy has somewhat agitated me. I shall sit awhile, and you shall converse with me. I know you can talk, if you choose, as rarely as you draw."

"That would be easily done."

"Do it then, Gerardo."

Gerard was taken aback. "But, signora, I know not what to say. This is sudden."

"Speak your true mind. Say you wish you were anywhere else."

"Nay, signora, that would not be truthful. I desire one thing, however."

"Yes, and what is that?" she asked gently.

"I wish I could have drawn you as you were beating that poor lass. You were awful, yet lovely. Oh, what a subject for a Pythoness!"

"Alas! He thinks only of his art. And why all this fuss about my beauty, Gerardo? You are far fairer than I. You resemble Apollo more than I do Venus. Moreover, you possess lovely hair and lovely eyes—but you know not how to use them."

"Yes, I do. To draw you, signora."

"Ah, yes; you can see my features with them, but you cannot perceive what any Roman suitor saw long ago in your place. Yet surely you have noticed how welcome you are to me, Gerardo?"

"I perceive your Highness is always exceedingly kind to me, a poor stranger."

"No, I am not, Gerardo. I have often been cold to you; rude, even. And you are so simple you fail to understand the cause. Alas, I feared for my own heart. I feared to become your slave. I, who have hitherto made slaves. Ah! Gerardo, I am unhappy. Since you arrived, I have lived upon your visits. On the day you come, I am radiant; on other days, I am listless, wishing them gone. You are not like the Roman gallants. You make me hate them

Gerard regained consciousness, only to plunge immediately into a abyss of despair. By the second day, fever ravaged his mind, leaving him delirious. Beside his bed lay his once-rich auburn locks, now matted and long as a woman's. One fatal symptom swiftly followed another. On the fifth day, the leech withdrew, declaring the case hopeless. That very evening, as the sun dipped below the horizon, he sank into a profound slumber. Rumors circulated that he would awaken only to meet his end. Yet, an elderly gossip, a woman of considerable standing due to her experience as a professional nurse, offered a glimmer of hope. "His youth may yet save him," she declared, "if he can endure twelve hours of uninterrupted sleep."

Encouraged by this counsel, his aged landlady swept the room clear and tended to him alone. She lit a wax candle before the image of the Virgin Mary, vowing one prayer for every hour he remained asleep. Twelve hours passed. The good woman rejoiced, falling to her knees to thank the Mother of God. Twenty-four hours elapsed. Doubt crept back into the hearts of the caregivers. At the thirty-hour mark, she summoned the physician. "Thirty hours!" she exclaimed anxiously. "Shall we wake him?"

The doctor examined him closely for some time. "His breathing is steady, his hands remain moist," he observed. "I know that learned physicians would wake him merely to inspect his tongue, finding nothing amiss; but we, being women, possess the wisdom to allow nature its course. When has sleep ever harmed a fractured mind or a shattered heart?"

By the forty-eighth hour, whispers spread through the neighborhood, and the curious pressed against the doors. They were finally admitted only Fra Colonna and his companion, the towering Friar Jerome. These two men took over from the women, sitting in silence. Fra Colonna gazed upon his young friend with tears streaming down his cheeks, while Friar Jerome held his rosary beads, staring at the sleeper with the same calm detachment he had shown when Gerard and he faced the raging sea together.

It was around the sixtieth hour of this extraordinary vigil that the landlady nudged Fra Colonna with her elbow. He glanced over. Gerard had opened his eyes with the gentleness of one merely dozing. He stared blankly, drawing himself slightly upright in the bed. He reached up to touch his head and discovered his hair was gone. Noticing his friend Colonna, he broke into a smile of relief. But midway through the expression, his face froze, seized instantly by anguish beyond description. A loud cry tore from his throat, and he turned his face firmly toward the wall.

His landlady wept openly; she knew the sorrow of waking bereft. Friar Jerome began chanting psalms and reading prayers from his breviary. Gerard curled himself tightly within the bedclothes. Fra Colonna approached, whispering comfortingly, reminding him that all was not lost. "The divine Muses are immortal," he urged. "You must transfer your affection to them; they will never betray you nor fail you like fragile creatures of clay." Overcome by emotion, the simple friar hurried away.

Friar Jerome remained. "Young man," he said softly, "the Muses exist only in the minds of pagans and dreamers. The Church alone offers rest to the heart on earth and happiness to the soul in the hereafter. Has the earth deceived you? Has passion broken your heart after tearing it apart? The Church opens her arms: consecrate your gifts to her! The Church is peace of mind." He spoke these words solemnly at the threshold and departed the moment they were spoken.

"The Church!" Gerard shrieked, springing up in fury and shaking his fist at the retreating friar. "Curses on the Church! Were it not for the Church, I should not lie broken here, while she lies cold, cold, cold in Holland. Oh, my Margaret! Oh, my darling! My darling! And I must flee from you during the few months you had left to live. Cruel! Cruel! Those monsters allowed her to die. Death does not come without warning signs; the blind, selfish fools saw them not, or cared not; but I would have seen them, I who loved her. Oh, had I been there, I would have saved her, I would have saved her! Fool! Fool! To leave her for a single moment!"

He wept bitterly for a long time. Then, suddenly bursting into fresh rage, he cried out vehemently, "The Church! For whose sake I was driven from her! May my malediction fall on the Church! And on the hypocrites who invoke it against my broken heart. Accursed be the world! Ghysbrecht lives; Margaret dies. Thieves, murderers, harlots live forever. Only angels die. Curse life! Curse death! And whoever shaped them into such things!"

The friar did not hear these mad and wicked words, catching only the roar of rage hurled after him. It was fortunate, for had he heard them, he would have ordered the late shipmate burned in the public square with no more hesitation than roasting a kid.

The old landlady, having accompanied Fra Colonna downstairs, heard the raised voice and returned in alarm. She found Gerard dressing, sobbing uncontrollably. She protested. "What avail lies in remaining here?" he asked gloomily. "Can I find here what I seek?"

"Saints preserve us! Is he distracted again? What do you seek?"

"Oblivion."

"Oblivion, my little heart? Oh, but you are too young to speak thus."

"Young or old, what else have I to live for?"

He donned his finest garments. The good lady remonstrated. "My dear Gerard, know that it is Tuesday, not Sunday."

"Oh, Tuesday is it? I thought it had been Saturday."

"Nay, you have slept long. You never wear your fine clothes on working days. Consider."

"What I did when she lived, I did. Now I shall do whatever I once refused. The past is the past. There lies my hair, and with it my former way of life. I have served one Master as well as I could. See my reward. Now I will serve another, and give him a fair trial."

"Alas!" sighed the woman, turning pale. "What do these dark words mean? And what new master is this whose service you wish to try?"

With this terrible declaration on his lips, the wretched creature walked out. His cap and feather were set jauntily askew on one side, his limbs weak, his face sinister and pale as ash, drawn down as if by premature age.

A profound darkness descended upon a noble mind. For years, his pure and unrivalled love for Margaret had served as his guiding star, illuminating his path through the storms of life. Now, that light was extinguished. He drifted aimlessly across a grey, hopeless sea. Yet, his torment was not merely despair; it was compounded by a searing exasperation. Every act of self-denial, every display of fortitude, every peril endured, and every sacrifice of virtue felt like salt rubbed into a fresh wound. The bitter irony gnawed at him: had he remained lazy and selfish at home, guarding his own comfort, he might have saved Margaret's life.

These twin poisons—despair and rage—raging together in his youthful blood, drove him mad. He rushed headlong into pleasure, seeking escape. In those days, as always, pleasure was indistinguishable from vice. Wine, women, gambling, and anything capable of procuring an hour of excitement or a moment of oblivion became his obsession. He plunged into these vices with the desperate frenzy of a man fleeing toward enemy bullets because he has grown weary of living. The substantial savings he had accumulated for Margaret now funded his debauchery. Soon, he became the leader of a circle of loose companions he had previously shunned. His heart rotted alongside his morals. He sulked violently against his old landlady for offering gentle advice, eventually moving to a different quarter of the city simply to escape her remonstrance and the painful memories associated with her home.

As the weeks wore on, his hand grew unsteady; he could no longer write with the precision required to satisfy his artistic standards. Furthermore, his patience eroded as the habits of indulgence took root. Abandoning drawing, he attempted portraiture in color, but even this craft was neglected whenever his idle cronies summoned him. Thus, he dove deeper into foul waters, searching desperately for that hollow shell known as "Oblivion."

It is not my purpose to paint a graphic picture of the coarse vices in which this unfortunate young man now immersed himself. However, it is essential to state the undeniable truth: he was a rake, a debauchee, and a drunkard—one of the most reckless and wicked youths in Rome. Those who wish to conceal or soften the wickedness of the virtuous, thereby robbing despairing sinners of hope, are not lovers of truth or humanity. Suffice it to say, the man was not born to do things halfway. Neither was his vice.

His former humble female friends began to gossip about him. His old landlady confided in Teresa that Gerard was going to ruin, praying Teresa to locate him. Teresa relayed the sad news to her husband, Lodovico, instructing him to search for the young man's current whereabouts.

"Shall I recognize his face, Lodovico mio?" Teresa asked.

"Teresa, a man in my line of work never forgets a face, especially that of a benefactor," Lodovico replied. "But you know I rarely venture abroad during the daylight hours."

Teresa sighed. "How long must this continue, Lodovico?"

"Until some cavalier drives his sword through me," Lodovico answered grimly. "They will not allow a poor fellow like me to pursue any honest trade."

Pietro Vanucci, who bore prosperity far worse than adversity, was one such figure. After being ignominiously ejected from their lodging by the landlady for keeping late hours, he encountered Gerard in the street. He greeted him warmly and shortly thereafter secured quarters in the same house. He arrived accompanied by a lad named Andrea, who ground his colors, served as his pupil, and acted as his model—a youth of striking beauty and sharp wit. Pietro had not entirely forgotten the old days and professed a fervent friendship for Gerard. Gerard, however, in whom all warmth of feeling seemed extinguished, accepted the other's friendship with icy reserve. It was a strange alliance: Pietro was a libertine, half-misanthrope, and an open infidel, while Gerard was a broken spirit. They walked together, bound by little in common.

One day, having undermined his health, stripped the bloom from his face, and squandered most of his fortune, Vanucci organized a boisterous party to ascend the Tiber in a boat pulled by buffaloes. Lorenzo de' Medici had introduced these beasts to Florence roughly three years prior, but they were novelties in Rome. Nothing satisfied Vanucci, the "beggar on horseback," except to be drawn upstream by the brutes. The plan was simple: each libertine was to bring a lady, who must be handsome, or face a fine. The guest contributing the most beautiful partner would be crowned with laurel and declared a public benefactor—a twisted interpretation of *vir bonus est quis?* ("Which man is good?").

They secured a magnificent galley and twelve buffaloes. Gradually, the libertines and their female companions assembled at the embarkation point. But Gerard was absent. They waited, first patiently, then with growing impatience. Vanucci offered an excuse. "I heard him say he forgot to procure a fardingale [a protective skirt]. Comrades, the good lad is hunting for a beauty worthy to stand among these peerless dames. Consider the difficulty, ladies, and be patient!"

Finally, Gerard appeared in the distance, holding a woman's hand.

"She is long enough," one woman critiqued from afar.
"Gemini! Look at her stride," said another.
"Oh! It is wise to hurry into good company," Pietro muttered as an excuse.

When the pair approached, all pretense of satire vanished. Gerard's companion was a peerless beauty; she eclipsed the entire boatload like the rising sun extinguishes the stars. She was tall but not towering, straight as a dart yet supple as a young panther. Her face was a perfect oval, her forehead pale, her cheeks a rich olive tone flushed with eloquent blood, and her glorious eyes framed by long, thick silken lashes seemingly designed to sweep away sensitive hearts. Her lips were saucy red, her teeth the whitest ivory.

The women were visibly depressed by this wretched sight; the men were in ecstasy. They welcomed her with loud shouts and waving caps.
