One enthusiast even knelt on the boat's gunwale, hailing her of divine origin. But as his mistress pulled his hair and the goddess delivered a playful kick, he collapsed supine. The peerless creature skipped over his body without deigning him a glance, taking her seat at the prow.

Pietro Vanucci sat in a sort of collapse, glaring at her with his mouth agape like a dying fish. The drover cracked the reins, the ropes tightened, and the buffaloes moved upstream.

"What do you think of this new beef, mesdames?" Vanucci asked.

"We have never seen such vile monsters," a lady retorted. "With their nasty horns that make one afraid, and their foul nostrils held high to the sky. They are holes, not nostrils."

"Signorina, the cattle are a gift from Florence the Beautiful. Would you look a gift horse—or beef—in the mouth?"

"They are so dull," objected a lively lady. "Last time I ascended the Tiber twice as fast with just five mules and an ass."

"Nay, that can easily be remedied," cried a gallant. Jumping ashore, he drew his sword. Despite the drivers' protests, he charged down at the dozen buffaloes, prodding them. The animals snorted and whipped their tails but refused to move faster. The passengers laughed loudly. Finally, he goaded a patriarchal bull, which instantly turned on the sword. With a furious jerk of its prodigious neck, it sent the man flying over his head. He described a bold parabola and fell sitting, unconscious, waving his glittering blade, directly into the yellow Tiber.

The laughing ladies screamed and wrung their hands, except for Gerard's companion. She uttered something resembling an oath, seized the helm, and steered the boat away. The gallant surfaced, sputtering, gripping the gunwale, and was dragged aboard dripping wet. He glared around him, confused and peevish.

"I do not understand that," he said, puzzled and clearly discontented. Realizing he had survived, though his doublet was perforated rather than his body, they began to laugh even louder.

"What are you cackling at?" the spark demanded. "I demand to know how it is that one moment a gentleman is out there pricking African cattle, and the next—"

Gerard's lady interjected. "Disporting in his native stream."

"Tell him not," cried Vanucci. "Let him solve his own riddle."

"Confound you all! I might puzzle my brains until Doomsday and never find it out. Also, where is my sword?"

"Ask the Tiber!" she replied. "Your best course, Signor, is to do it again. In short, keep pricking Africa's cattle until your mind receives enlightenment. Only then will you comprehend the matter, degree by degree, as lawyers climb to heaven and buffaloes cross the Tiber."

At this, a chevalier remarked that the speaker surpassed the sons of Adam in wit as much as she exceeded the daughters of Eve in beauty. At these compliments—and indeed at all of them—Pietro Vanucci's behavior became peculiar. He ceased his gaping stare and now sat coiled like a snake, muffling his mouth while fixing two bright, twinkling eyes on

In a chamber adorned with opulence, where the floor was carpeted with the striped and spotted hides of beasts, a lady sat in a posture of intense distress. Her arms were stretched forward, her fingers half-clenched, mirroring the turmoil that ravaged her countenance. Her face alternated between deadly pallor and violent flushing, while her foot tapped restlessly against the ground. Suddenly, the heavy curtains parted as a servant entered. The lady's brow burned crimson at the intrusion.

"Altezza," the maid whispered, her voice trembling with awe, "the man is here."

With a sharp command, the lady signaled for admission. Seizing a small black mask from the table, she pressed it over her face. Instantly, her color drained away entirely, leaving a stark, terrifying contrast between the jet-black fabric and her marble-pale cheeks. A man stepped into the room, bowing and scraping with servile deference. He was a common figure, one easily lost in a crowd—short-haired, with a roundish head and plain, though respectable, attire. His features were neither handsome nor repelling, save for a pair of eyes that possessed a singular, unsettling restlessness.

After a flurry of bows, he stood opposite her, awaiting her instruction.

"They have informed you of the purpose of your summons?" the lady asked.

"Yes, Signora."

"Did those who sent you confirm the payment agreed upon?"

"Yes, Signora. It is the full price, securing the greater vendetta. Unless, through your benevolence, you wish to accept the lesser."

"I do not understand you," she replied coolly.

"Ah, this is the Signora's first commission," the man explained smoothly. "The lesser vendetta claims only the life of the body. We might watch our mark emerge from a church or seize him during an innocent hour, ending his physical existence thus. But in the greater vendetta, we wait until he is caught hot from some unpardoned sin, striking down both his soul and his flesh. However, such opportunities are rarer now than they were a few years past."

"Silence his tongue, man," the lady commanded, her voice hardening. "Let his treasonous heart cease beating. But his soul... with that, I hold no feud."

"So be it, Signora," the assassin nodded. "He who hired me knows nothing of the man's name or dwelling. From whom shall I learn these details?"

"From myself," she answered.

At this, the man displayed the first flicker of genuine anxiety. "Pray, be cautious and precise in this matter."

"Fear me not," she dismissed his worry. "Listen well. He is a young man, tall of stature, with auburn hair and dark blue eyes—a face of such honesty it could deceive a saint. He resides in the Via Claudia, at the corner house belonging to the glover. Within that home dwell but three males: himself, a painter of short stature and swarthy complexion, and a slender youth. The betrayer is a stranger, fair-complexioned, and taller than you."

The bravo leaned in, listening intently.

"It is sufficient," he murmured. "Does he frequent any secluded places where I might intercept him?"

"My spy reports that lately he has visited the banks of the Tiber after dusk," she said, her tone sharpening. "Doubtless to meet his light love, who calls herself my rival. Slay him there! Let my rival discover his corpse; let the smooth, heartless, insolent traitor find his end amidst the waters."

"Be calm, Signora," the man advised gently. "He will betray no other ladies."

"I cannot guarantee that," she retorted. "He wears a sword and is skilled with it. He is young and resolute."

"None of these things will avail him," the assassin assured her confidently.

"Do you truly trust your aim? What weapons do you employ?"

The bravo produced a steel gauntlet. "We strike with such ferocity that protection is mandatory for our hands. This is our mallet." He then unbuttoned his doublet, revealing a coat of mail beneath, and finally placed a glittering stiletto upon the table with a flourish.

The lady shuddered initially, but soon reached out with her pale hand to test the weapon's edge against her finger.

"Beware, madam," the bravo warned.

"What? Is it poisoned?"

"Saints forbid! We do not steal lives with drugs. We claim them with the cold steel alone. But it is freshly ground, and I feared the Signora's delicate skin might suffer."

"His skin is as white as mine," she remarked suddenly, a fleeting gleam of pity crossing her features. It vanished almost instantly. "But his heart is black as soot. Tell me, is it not right to remove a traitor who slanders me?"

"The Signora must settle that question with her confessor," the man replied, adopting a humble tone. "I am merely a tool in noble hands, much like this stiletto."

The princess seemed deaf to his words. "Oh, how I could have loved him—to the death—as I now hate him! Fool! He will learn to toy with princes, to spurn them only to fawn upon them later, preferring the scum of the streets to nobility, turning them into a byword for fools."

She lifted her gaze. "Why loiterest thou here? Haste thee, and avenge me!"

"It is customary to pay half the price beforehand, Signora."

"Ah, I forgot; thy revenge is purchased. Here is more than half," she said, pushing a heavy bag across the table toward him. "Come for the remainder when the blow is struck."

"You will see me again soon, Signora."

With another series of bows, he withdrew.

The princess remained seated, still masked, her arms rigidly extended before her, the nails of her clenched hand digging into the wood of the table. She sat like the fabled Sphinx, or perhaps like a crouching tigress. Yet, a chilling sensation began to creep over her now that the assassin had departed. Moody misgivings rose within her chest, harbingers of vain remorse.

Gerard and Margaret were far too young for such darkness. This was the true medieval world: proud, amorous, vindictive, generous, foolish, cunning, impulsive, unprincipled—and ignorant as the dust itself. Power was the curse of such a creature. Had she been forced to commit her own crimes, the natural weakness of her nerves would have tempered the violence of her passions, making her bark worse than her bite. But power granted a feeble, furious woman access to male instruments. The result was as terrible as the union was unnatural. In this specific instance, it had sharpened an assassin's dagger for a poor, forsaken wretch who was already contemplating suicide.

It came to pass, two days following the events previously recounted, that Gerard, while traversing one of Rome's most sordid thoroughfares, was caught in the grip of a violent thunderstorm. Seeking shelter, he entered a humble tavern. Ordering a measure of wine, and with the deluge persisting without abatement, he soon succumbed to a stupor, dozing heavily with his head resting upon his folded arms and his elbows propped upon the rough wooden table.

As time wore on, the establishment grew crowded, and the raucous clamor of the coarse patrons roused him from his slumber. It was then that his attention was drawn to two figures seated nearby, engaged in hushed conversation. One was clearly a pardoner; the other, though dressed neatly and modestly enough to resemble a respectable tradesman, betrayed his true nature by the manner in which he had pulled his cap low over his brow, obscuring every feature save his beard. Such a gesture marked him as one who deliberately avoids the gaze of upright citizens and the scrutiny of the law. These two were negotiating a transaction within the black market of sin. Under a celestial contract common to that era, the crime for which forgiveness was sought had not yet been committed.

The man with the lowered cap grumbled about the exorbitant cost of pardons. "If prices rise further," he lamented, "we poor souls shall be barred from Heaven entirely." The pardoner dismissed this fear with a flat denial. "Indulgences have never been more affordable for prudent stewards of their coin," he retorted. His companion asked whom these bargains typically favored. "Why, those who sin openly, like rational beings," the pardoner replied. "But should you be so obstinate as to select a transgression the Pope has explicitly condemned, look to your own conscience, not mine!"

To demonstrate that sins of various magnitudes were accessible even to the lowest strata of society (save perhaps the absolute dregs), the pardoner unrolled a parchment and recited the fee schedule. It was a peculiar list, too dark for print in this volume, yet readily available in any grand library. To alter its contents would be to distort history. Suffice it to say that the price for the murder of a layman was far less than many offenses my readers might consider trivial. This fact sealed the deal; with minor concessions from both sides, the agreement was finalized. Money changed hands, and the aspiring soul was absolved in advance for the future killing of a civilian. Yet, the term "assassination" was never spoken aloud between them.

All this murmured business filled Gerard's ears, though he remained buried beneath his arms, listening with a vacant stare. Only when the conspirators rose and departed did he lift his head slightly, fixing a glare upon the retreating figure of the buyer.

"If Margaret lived," he muttered, his voice thick with drunken despair, "I would seize you by the throat and crush you, you cowardly murderer! But she is dead—dead, dead. Let the whole world perish; it matters nothing to me now. So let us all die together; I shall be among the first."

Upon returning home, Gerard found a man wearing a slouched hat pacing briskly back and forth on the opposite side of the street. "There goes that cur again," Gerard thought. Yet, in his shattered mental state, the sight left no mark upon his consciousness whatsoever.

Two nights following this event, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea waited expectantly for Gerard's arrival at supper. As the clock struck past nine, Pietro's patience began to fray, his demeanor growing increasingly irritable. Finally, driven by desperation, he dispatched Andrea to Gerard's chamber, hoping against hope that the scholar had slipped inside unnoticed. Andrea merely shrugged, accepting the mission, and departed.

He returned empty-handed regarding Gerard's presence but clutching a folded scrap of paper. In that era, scholars possessed the literacy that modern charity schools still strive to instill; yet Andrea, though illiterate, possessed a sharp eye and had long since memorized the distinctive handwriting of Pietro Vanucci. "That is for you, I believe," Andrea declared, puffing slightly with pride at his deduction.

Pietro snatched the note and read aloud, his tone laced with biting satire. "'Dearest Pietro, dearest Andrea, life is a burden too heavy to bear.'"

"Indeed it is, my boy," Pietro retorted dryly, "but that offers no excuse for wandering the streets during supper hour. Supper itself is no burden."

"'Wear my religious habits!'"

"Said the poplar tree to the humble juniper bush," Pietro mocked.

"And you, Andrea, take my amethyst ring; and keep me in both your hearts for a month or two."

"Why, Andrea?" Pietro pressed, sensing a shift in the wind.

"For my body, before you finish reading this, it shall lie within the Tiber," Andrea recited, his voice trembling. "Do not trouble yourself to search for it; it is not worth the effort. Alas, what an unfortunate day it was when I was born! Oh, blessed night that finally frees me from its grasp!"

"'Farewell! Farewell!'"

"The broken-hearted Gerard."

"This is a lamentable jest from a peevish fool," Pietro muttered aloud, attempting to mask his own pallor and the violent tremors shaking his teeth. Yet, his composure crumbled under the weight of the news.

Andrea, overcome with panic, filled the household with shrill cries. "Oh, wretched day! Oh, catastrophe upon catastrophe! Gerard, my friend, my beloved patron! Help! Help! He is destroying himself! Oh, good souls, aid me in saving him!"

His alarm spread rapidly through the house, but once outside, his pleas fell on deaf ears. A crowd gathered, yet Andrea's frantic energy failed to stir their lethargy. "Shall we descend to the river?" someone suggested cautiously. "Nay," another replied, "that is none of our concern." "But which part of the river?" "It is folly to venture near the water after sunset; too many ghosts haunt those banks throughout the night."

However, a servant who had lingered opposite the house volunteered to accompany Andrea. His involvement drew three or four other youths, forming a small, eager band. They hurried toward the riverbank. The servant questioned Andrea, who, weeping bitterly, recounted the letter and Gerard's recent melancholy. Unbeknownst to them, this servant was a spy for Princess Claelia. Their Italian conversation flowed swiftly until they approached the Tiber.

Yet, the mere scent of the cool spring breeze and the mist rising from the stream silenced them instantly. The moon hung high and clear in a cloudless sky, casting a stark light upon the scene. Their footsteps echoed loudly and ominously on the stones, while their voices died away completely.

Suddenly, rounding a corner, they encountered a solitary figure. The man paused, hesitating at their sudden appearance. He was bareheaded, his hair dripping wet and gleaming in the moonlight. Upon closer inspection, his garments were soaked through.

"He is here!" cried one of the youths, who did not recognize Gerard's face.

The stranger spun around and bolted. The group pursued him with renewed vigor, Andrea leading the charge, followed closely by the princess's agent. Andrea closed the distance momentarily, but the fugitive twisted sharply into a narrow alleyway. Unable to stop his momentum, Andrea shot past, leaving the others behind. Soon, the pursuers found themselves lost in a labyrinthine maze, chasing a swift-footed escapee who knew every shadowed inch of the path, audible only by the sound of his fleeing breath.

They returned to their companions, who remained frozen at the spot where the man had vanished, utterly bewildered. "Are you certain?" they asked. "He had just emerged from the river. By the saints' names, why did he flee at our approach?"

Then spoke Vanucci: "Friends, I perceive this has nothing to do with the man we seek. What shall we do, Andrea?"

At this point, the spy interjected. "Let us follow the trail to the water's edge to confirm. Observe; he came dripping all the way."

This counsel was accepted, and with ease, they retraced the man's steps. However, a new puzzle presented itself almost immediately. Having traveled barely fifty yards, the trail of moisture veered away from the river, directing them instead toward the gate of a large, gloomy structure—a monastery. They halted before the dark edifice, staring into its shadows, feeling as though it concealed some terrible secret.

Suddenly, Andrea let out a triumphant shout. "Behold! The drops return!" he cried. "And this path leads straight to the river."

Resuming their pursuit, it quickly became evident that the wet footprints were guiding them homeward. The trail grew progressively deeper and wetter until they reached the very brink of the Tiber. There, on the muddy bank, lay a puddle clearly formed by a bucketful of water dumped directly from the stream.

Initially, they shouted in triumph, believing they had solved the mystery. But reflection soon set in; the evidence meant little. Undoubtedly, a man had entered the water and emerged safely, yet that individual was not Gerard. One suggested knowing a fisherman nearby equipped with nets and drags. They located the fisher and paid him generously to cast nets downstream and haul them upstream and downstream, promising a golden reward if he discovered the body.

Thus, they wandered uselessly along the riverbanks, which flowed with a strange, voiceless calm, guarding a thousand such mysteries.

Suddenly, Andrea burst back toward the house, his face alight with a cry of false hope. He returned in less than thirty minutes.

"No," he groaned, wringing his hands in despair. "What hour is it?" the spy asked.

"It is four hours past midnight," Andrea whispered.

"My pretty lad," the spy said solemnly, "pray say a mass for your friend's soul; for he is no longer among the living."

Morning broke over a silent city. Exhausted by fatigue, Andrea and Pietro trudged home, their hearts heavy with sickness. Days rolled on, as mute and indifferent as the Tiber, offering no answer to the fate of Gerard.

It would indeed have been strange if, possessing such scanty clues, those men could have deciphered the message written upon the riverbank. Yet, right there on that very spot, an event had just transpired—one that stands alone in human history, likely destined to remain unmatched until the final hour. This tale shall be recounted briefly, woven together from the voice of the narrator and the memory of a participant within the drama itself.

Gerard, having penned his short farewell to Pietro and Andrea, slipped away toward the river as twilight deepened. Driven by a grim determination common among those resolved upon their own undoing, he took careful measure. He stuffed his pockets with every piece of silver and copper he owned, intending to weigh his descent more surely. Thus equipped, he hastened to a secluded stretch of the stream he had previously noted as rarely traversed. Some, particularly women, glance around to ensure a witness is nearby; but this desperate soul scanned the shadows solely to confirm solitude. To his irritation, however, he spotted a solitary figure leaning against the corner of an alleyway. Pretending to wander aimlessly, he moved away, only to return moments later. There stood the same figure once again, emerging from a side street and lingering with deliberate slowness.

"Does he watch me?" Gerard wondered, his heart pounding. "Does he know my purpose? Impossible!" He strode briskly onward, navigating two streets before making a wide circuit, yet when he returned, the man had vanished. But as Gerard cast a wary gaze around to verify the emptiness of the path, there he was—just a few paces behind, seemingly adjusting his shoe. Realizing he was being shadowed, Gerard suddenly noticed in the pale moonlight a steel gauntlet resting in the sentinel's hand. Instantly, the truth struck him: this was an assassin. Strangely, it never crossed his mind that his own life was the intended target. After all, he knew of no enemies in the world.

Turning directly toward the bravo, Gerard approached with frantic urgency. "Good friend," he cried out breathlessly, "buy my arm! Strike a single blow! Behold, here is all I possess;" forcing his coins into the assassin's grasp. "Oh, pray thee! Pray thee! Do one act of mercy and release me from this hateful existence!" As he spoke, he unfastened his doublet, exposing his bare chest. The man stared silently at him. "Why do you hesitate?" Gerard shrieked, his voice cracking. "Do you lack compassion? Is it too great a pain to raise your arm and deliver the blow? Or is it because I am poor and cannot offer gold? Wretched creature, you can only strike a man from behind; you dare not meet his gaze! Very well, then, merely turn your head and keep silent!" With a snarl of utter contempt, Gerard fled from him and threw himself into the water.

"Margaret!"

At the heavy splash of his body hitting the stream, the bravo seemed to shake off a sudden stupor. He sprinted to the bank, issuing a strange cry, before plunging in after the man seeking death. What ensued next shall be related by the assassin himself.

A woman bears her own burdens, much as a man carries his, yet we male authors often fail to articulate the specific sorrows of the fairer sex. We sketch the outlines, leaving the flesh and blood to the imagination of our female readers, who must supply the missing details. Thus, I shall merely hint at the trials Margaret Brandt endured until that fateful day when she stepped into Eli's house as an adversary, discovered her lover's letter, and emerged as a friend. Now, however, approached the greatest test of a woman's life, while Gerard remained distant.

She made scant use of Eli's newfound kindness toward her. For this reserve, she always possessed a plausible excuse, though she never revealed the true cause: within that household dwelt two men whose mere presence instilled in her an instinctive dread. In their countenances, she had read wickedness and hatred, along with the silent signals of clandestine plotting. Consequently, she preferred receiving Catherine and her daughter within her own humble dwelling. Catherine visited daily, consumed by anticipation of the coming birth. Catherine belonged to that order of women whose sole purpose seems to be multiplication—those who, once they cease expelling infants from every corner of the house, hover protectively around the "fair scourges" still in full bloom, clucking and fussing as if multiplying by proxy.

It was in this spirit that she implored Eli to remain in Rotterdam while she returned to Tergou. "That poor lass has no soul about her who understands a thing," she argued. "What good are a pair of soldiers? Such cattle ought to be cast out at the first sign of trouble." One need not add that this reassurance brought Margaret immense relief. Poor soul, she was filled with anxiety as the appointed hour neared. She feared death, and she mourned Gerard's absence. Yet, fate balances its scales. Her poverty and her father's frailty, struggles that had nearly broken her before, now served her well. Adversity's iron grip had compelled her to fight against the lethargy that often overtakes wealthy women, forcing her to stand constantly upon her feet, laboring without ceasing. She maintained this vigilance until the end, following Catherine's counsel.

So it came to pass that one beautiful evening, just as the sun dipped below the horizon, she lay weak as water, yet safe. By her side rested a tiny face, and the vast, tender sky of maternity opened above her.

"Why dost thou weep, sweetheart? All of a sudden?"

"He is not here to witness it."

"Ay, well, lass. He will be here ere he is weaned. Meanwhile, God has been as kind to thee as to any woman born. Consider how it might have been a girl; did not my very own Kate threaten me with one? And here we have secured the bonniest boy in all Holland, a sturdy lad indeed. Praise the saints for it!"

"Yes, mother. I am but a sorry, ungrateful wretch to weep. If only Gerard were here to behold it. It is strange; I bore him well enough to be parted from me in my sorrow, yet oh, it feels so hard that he should not share my joy. Pray, pray, come to me, Gerard! Dear, dear Gerard!"

She stretched forth her feeble arms. Catherine bustled about, deliberately avoiding Margaret's gaze, unable to suppress her own tears at hearing her own absent child thus passionately invoked. Soon, turning around, she found Margaret regarding her with a peculiar expression.

"Didst hear nothing?"

"No, my lamb. What?"

"I cried out for Gerard, but now..."

"Ay, ay, surely I heard that."

"Well, he answered me."

"Tush, girl; speak not such nonsense."

"Mother, as certain as I lie here with his boy beside me, his voice returned to me: 'Margaret!' Yes. Yet, it seemed not his joyful tone. Perhaps that was due to the distance. All voices grow sad and faint when carried afar. Why art thou not happy, sweetheart? And I am so happy this night? Mother, I seem never to have felt a pain or known a care."

Her sweet eyes turned, glowing with love, fixed silently upon the infant's small face.

That very night, Gerard threw himself into the Tiber. And in that same hour, she heard him speaking her name, crying aloud between the jaws of death and despair.

"Margaret!"

Let those who can account for it explain the mystery. I cannot.

In a guest cell within a Dominican convent, a solitary stranger lay prostrate, his strength utterly spent by waves of violent nausea that had finally receded, leaving him as frail as Margaret had been in Holland. Upon the hearth, a massive wood fire crackled warmly, while the patient's garments hung suspended nearby. Beside the bed sat a towering friar, his voice resonating softly as he read aloud from his breviary. At intervals, the sufferer opened weary eyes to gaze at him, listening intently; at others, he shut them tight and emitted low moans. The monk knelt, his forehead pressed to the floor in fervent prayer, before rising to bid his charge farewell. "Day breaks," he declared. "I must prepare for Matins." "Blessed Father Jerome," the stranger whispered, "before you depart, tell me—how did I arrive here?" "By the hand of Heaven," the friar replied gravely. "You cast aside God's gift; yet He has restored it to you. Reflect upon this! You have tested the world and found only bitterness. Now test the Church! The Church offers peace. *Pax vobiscum*." With those words, he departed.

Gerard remained reclining, lost in meditation and wonder, until exhaustion claimed him and he drifted into a doze. When consciousness returned, he found a new attendant seated by his side—a layman whose eyes were small and perpetually restless, a stark contrast to the calm majesty of Friar Jerome. The man inquired anxiously about his condition. "Extremely weak," Gerard murmured. "Where have I encountered you before, sir?" "No worse for my gauntlet?" the other asked, his tone laced with concern. "Indeed, I nearly struck you down, or both of us might now lie at the bottom of the Tiber." Gerard fixed him with a stare. "What? Was it you who saved me? How?" "Well, signor, I happened to be by the banks of the Tiber on some errand, though it matters little now. You approached me, begging desperately for a dagger stroke. Yet, even as you spoke, I recognized you—the nobleman who spared my wife and child upon the sea." "It is Teresa's husband... and an assassin?!" "At your service. Next, you threw yourself into the river, commanding me to stand aside." "I recall that clearly." "If it had been anyone else, I would have obeyed instantly, moving not a muscle. My enemies are many; they might all hang on a single rope or drown in one river for my sake. But when you sank in the Tiber crying out 'Margaret!'..." "Ah!" "My heart screamed 'Teresa!' How could I return home and face her if I allowed you to die, especially since your death was the very act that saved hers? So I entered the water. Fortunately for us both, I swim like a duck. Seeing me approach, intent on mutual destruction, you attempted to seize me, hoping to end us together. Instead, I circled you and, accepting my apologies, struck the nape of your neck with my steel-gloved fist. You lost consciousness. With great effort, fighting the strong current, I dragged your body ashore, insensible and filled with water. I carried you on my back to my home, thinking, 'Teresa will nurse him, and she will be pleased with me.' Hard by this monastery, a holy friar—the largest I have ever seen—intercepted us and questioned the situation. I recounted everything. He scrutinized you closely. 'I know this face,' he stated. 'It is Gerard, a fair youth from Holland.' 'The same,' I confirmed. Then His Reverence said, 'He has friends among our brothers. Leave him with us! This is charity, our divine office.' He also informed me that the convent possessed far greater means to tend to you than I did, which proved true enough. Thus, I merely negotiated permission to visit daily, and here you are." As he spoke, the man cast a look upon Gerard that blended strangeness with deep affection. Gerard offered no response. He felt as though a serpent coiled within the room. Closing his eyes, he dismissed the visitor, who retreated on tiptoe, promising his daily return.

Gerard lay with his eyes shut, awake but profoundly contemplative. Saved from death by an assassin—was this not the finger of Heaven pointing toward salvation? That Heaven he had once insulted, cursed, and defied. A shudder ran through him at the memory of his blasphemies. He attempted to pray, finding the words formed on his lips, yet his spirit refused to engage in true devotion. "I am doomed eternally," he cried out in despair, "doomed, doomed." Suddenly, the organ of the convent church erupted in rich, solemn harmony, followed by the soaring voices of the choir chanting a full service. Amidst the chorus, one voice seemed to float above the rest, ascending toward heaven itself—a sweet boy's tone, full, pure, and angelic. Gerard closed his eyes and listened. The melodies transported him back to the innocent days of his boyhood. There was no earthly dross, no foul, fierce passions tearing and corrupting the soul. Only peace—sweet, balmy peace. "Yes," he sighed, "the Church brings peace of mind. Until I left her embrace, I knew neither sorrow nor sin." Tears streamed down the face of the poor, broken creature. Just as he wept, the radiant and reverend visage of someone he had not expected to see again appeared before him. It was Father Anselm. The good priest had arrived at the convent only the previous night. Gerard recognized him instantly and called out, "Oh, Father Anselm! You healed my wounded body in Juliers; now heal my injured soul in Rome! Alas, you cannot." Anselm sat beside the bed, placing a gentle hand upon Gerard's head to soothe him with tender words. Having learned the circumstances of Gerard's arrival, he spoke kindly yet solemnly, helping the young man confront his crime, urging repentance, and expressing gratitude to the Divine Power that had thwarted his will to save his soul. "Come, my son," Anselm said, "first cleanse your bosom of its heavy burden." "Ah, father," Gerard replied, "in Juliers I could; then I was innocent. But now, an impious monster that I am, I dare not confess to you." "Why not, my son? Do you think I have not sinned against Heaven in my time, and deeply? Oh, how deeply! Come, poor laden soul, pour forth your grief, pour forth your faults; withhold nothing! Do not lie oppressed and crushed by hidden sins." Soon, Gerard knelt at Father Anselm's feet, confessing every sin with sighs and groans of profound penitence. "Your sins are grave," Anselm acknowledged. "Yet your temptation was equally terrible, exceedingly so. I must consult our worthy Prior." The good father kissed Gerard's brow and withdrew to confer with the superior regarding the appropriate penance. Immediately, Gerard found the power to pray. He prayed with all the fervor of his heart. The phase through which this remarkable mind now passed can be summarized in a single word: Penitence. He turned away from the world with terror and revulsion, begging passionately to remain within the convent walls. To him, the monastery was like a wounded, weary bird returning to its gentle nest. He spent his novitiate in prayer, self-mortification, pious reading, and meditation.

Meanwhile, the Princess Claelia's spy returned home to report that Gerard was undoubtedly dead, though the manner of his passing remained unknown. She stood literally stunned. After a long silence, when she finally gathered the breath to speak, her words were filled with lamentation over her fate, cursed with such treacherous instruments. "So swiftly," she sighed, "these monsters commit evil deeds. They come to us in our hour of passion, first tempting us with their mercenary daggers, then executing the mortal acts we never imagined unless provoked by them." Within mere hours, her pity for Gerard and her hatred for his killer boiled into a feverish intensity, fueled by her own foolish rage. "Poor soul! I cannot call you back to life. But he shall never live, that traitor who slew you so treacherously." She immediately placed armed men in ambush, keeping them on guard throughout the day. Her plan was clear: when Lodovico arrived to collect his payment, they would spring from hiding in a specific antechamber and hack him to pieces. "Strike at his head," she commanded, "for he wears a private coat of mail beneath his robes. If he escapes alive, your own heads shall answer for it." Thus, she sat weeping for her victim, simultaneously pulling the strings of deadly machines designed to spill the blood of a second man, solely because he had served as her instrument to kill the first.

One of Novice Gerard's self-imposed penances was to treat Lodovico with kindness, though inwardly he regarded the man as nothing less than a slimy serpent. Yet, there is a peculiar virtue in such self-denial; like many rational acts of contrition, it quickly ceases to feel like a burden. Initially, the pride and complacency with which the former assassin gazed upon the single life he had spared were almost comical in their absurdity, yet undeniably pathetic. However, opening a good door in a human heart often invites a cascade of goodness. Finding the act of preserving life strangely sweet, Lodovico gradually grew averse to taking it. From this aversion sprang remorse, and from remorse, something resembling true penitence.

Teresa played her part in this transformation, repeatedly threatening to withdraw her protection unless he vowed to live honestly. The good fathers of the convent supported her efforts, and eventually, Lodovico and Teresa were dispatched by sea to Leghorn, where Teresa possessed friends. There, the assassin settled down, trading his blade for the heavy load of a porter. At first, he found the labor miserably dull and complained openly. Yet, I believe this quiet, plodding existence suited him far better than the fleeting, violent excitement of being hacked to pieces by the Princess Claelia's mercenaries. His exile had saved the unconscious penitent from that gruesome fate. Meanwhile, the Princess, thwarted in her quest for revenge, descended into a profound melancholy. She dismissed her old confessor and sought out a new one renowned for his piety—a man named Fra Jerome—to whom she poured out what she termed her "griefs."

Fra Jerome proved to be the perfect instrument for her redemption. He listened to her grievances with grim patience before seizing her by the shoulders and shaking the delusions from her mind with the roughness one might reserve for a kitchen maid. For this hard monk, the path of duty meant fearing neither the wrath of princes nor the fury of the sea. In a few sharp words, crackling with thunder and lightning, he stripped away her pretenses. "Thou art the devil," he declared, "who, with thy gold, tempted one man to slay his fellow, and then, blinded by self-love, instead of condemning and punishing thyself, art now thirsting for the blood of others—men perhaps not so guilty as thou."

At first, she resisted, claiming she was unaccustomed to such harsh scrutiny from her confessors. But Fra Jerome overpowered her objections. He threatened her with the Church's eternal curses, tore the scales from her eyes, and thundered against her until she sank to the floor, groveling in remorse and terror at the feet of this giant Boanerges. "Oh, holy father," she cried, "have pity on this poor, weak woman and help me save my guilty soul! I was lost in darkness, lacking guidance like yours, good father. I wake now as from a nightmare."

"Doff thy jewels," commanded Fra Jerome sternly.
"I will. I will," she replied.
"Remove thy silk and velvet; clad yourself in garments humbler than those worn by thy meanest servant, and hasten instantly to Loretto."
"I will," said the princess, her voice faint.
"No shoes; but a bare sandal."
"No, Father!"
"Wash the feet of every pilgrim, both arriving and departing. To any who are holy friars, reveal thy sin and await their admonition."
"Oh, holy father, let me wear my mask!"
"Humph!"
"Oh, mercy! Consider! My features are known throughout Italy."
"Aye. Beauty is a curse to most of you. Very well, thou mayst mask thine eyes, but no further."

On this concession, she reached out to seize his hand, intending to kiss it, but he snatched it away rudely. "What would you do?" he asked sharply. "That hand touched the Eucharist but an hour ago; is it fit for one such as thou to handle it?"
"Ah, no. But oh, do not depart without granting your penitent daughter your blessing."
"There is ample time to ask it when you return from Loretto."

Thus, that marvelous event by the banks of the Tiber left its indelible mark upon all involved, much as legends say prodigies do. The assassin, softened by the miracle of having saved a life rather than taken one, turned from the stiletto to the company of porters. The Princess went barefoot to Loretto, weeping over her crimes while washing the feet of low-born men. As for Gerard, carried from the Tiber into the convent as a near-suicide, he was now hailed as a young saint within its walls. Eyes loving yet experienced watched over him. Upon a probation shorter than usual, he was admitted to the priesthood. Soon thereafter, he took the monastic vows and became a friar of St. Dominic. Dying to the world, the monk renounced even the name by which he had once lived, thereby severing the final link to his earthly past. Here ended the tale of Gerard, and the story of Brother Clement began.

"As is the race of leaves, so is that of men." In this same year, while a great soul budded quietly within a humble tailor's home in Rotterdam, a mighty figure met his end with spectacular fanfare. Philip, Duke of Burgundy, Count of Holland, and many other titles, lay dying in his bed at Bruges.

In those days, the poor fell ill and recovered according to nature's whims; but for the wealthy, sickness was often a sentence of doom. In an era where one physician could kill three patients before curing a single one, the stakes were terrifyingly high. The Duke's ailment, unnamed in its time though we now know it as diphtheria, was a ravaging disease. Given the Duke's advanced age, the attending physicians—led by the learned Dr. Remedy—decided upon bloodletting. The Duke grew pale and icy, a sign that the treatment was failing.

Desperate, Dr. Remedy turned to the arcane remedies of science. "Alas!" he cried. "We must skin an ape immediately and affix it to the Duke's chest!" State officials scrambled frantically through the city, hunting for the specific primate required to counteract the madness of humanity. Yet, perdition awaited them; there were no apes to be found. Buffaloes, lizards, Turkish captives, and leopards abounded, but none possessed the requisite qualities.

"Why, there used to be an ape about here," one official noted hopefully. "I saw him standing just outside." Indeed, there had been one, until a hungry mastiff had torn the lively creature apart over a stolen meal—a grim fulfillment of the ancient human adage: *Be of your own generation.*

Faced with this emergency, the Seneschal cast desperate eyes around the room, and soon hope returned to his countenance. "Look here," he whispered urgently. "Surely this will suffice; it is entirely ape-like, save for the doublet and hose."

"Nay!" protested the Chancellor irritably. "Princess Marie would have us hanged. She holds this creature in high regard." At that moment, our friend Giles strutted through the room, unconscious of the gravity of the situation, dressed in shimmering cloth of gold.

Impatient with the delay, Dr. Remedy ordered a dog skinned instead. "A dog is the next best thing to an ape," he declared, adding the strict condition, "but it must be a dog of a single color." Thus, a liver-colored dog was skinned and pinned, still beating, against the Sovereign's chest. The Duke drew his final breath.

Philip the Good, thus dispatched by the methods of his time, left behind thirty-one children. Somehow, one emerged legitimate to reign in his stead. The benevolent Duke had provided for nineteen of the others; the remainder were forced to make their own way. Flemish chronicles claimed the deceased prince descended from the Kings of Troy through Thierry of Aquitaine and the ancient rulers of Franconia. Yet, such lineage offered no true distinction; I cannot find a single prince of that day who did not trace his roots back to Troy. To call someone "of Priam" was merely medieval code for "son of Adam."

The Duke's body was transported to Burgundy and interred in a magnificent black marble mausoleum at Dijon. Holland mourned his passing, unaware that within its borders, something far more famous was being born that very year. That judgment has since been overturned. Today, people gaze with reverence at the tailor's house where the great birth of the fifteenth century occurred. No one knows, nor does anyone care, in which house the good Duke breathed his last. Why? For Dukes like Philip the Good come and go, leaving mankind neither richer, nor wiser, nor better than they found it.

But when, once every few centuries, a child such as Margaret's son is brought into the world, behold! A human torch ignited by heavenly fire; and let there be light, thundering from pole to pole.

The Dominicans, once the most formidable order in Europe, were now fading into obscurity. Their bitter rivals, the Franciscans, were sweeping across the continent, overtaking them everywhere—even in England, that land of wealth and piety where the "Black Friars" had formerly held sway. Consequently, the wise leaders of the Order grew increasingly desperate to recruit able and zealous souls, dispatching them abroad to reclaim the world for their cause.

It became evident that Clement possessed talents of rare distinction, particularly his command of languages—he spoke Latin, Italian, French, and both high and low German fluently. He was destined for travel and preaching in England, serving as a correspondent to the Roman center. Yet, Jerome, who held the superior's ear, obstructed this plan.

"Clement," Jerome argued, "still carries the milk of the world in his veins; he retains its feelings and its weaknesses. Let not his newborn zeal or his humility lead us to discard our ancient wisdom. Test him first, temper him, lest one day we find ourselves leaning on a reed for a staff."

"It is sound advice," agreed the Prior. "Take him in hand yourself."

Thus, following the counsel of antiquity, Jerome undertook the task of testing Clement. One day, he led him to a field where young men played the games of the day—a haunt of Clement's former friends. Soon, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea passed by, casting a casual glance at the two friars. They failed to recognize their deceased companion in the shaven monk.

Clement gave a slight start, lowered his eyes, and recited a *paternoster*.

"Would you not speak with them, brother?" Jerome probed.

"No, brother; yet it was good for me to see them. They remind me of the sins I can never repent enough."

"It is well," Jerome replied, filing a cold report in Clement's favor.

Next, Jerome subjected Clement to the grim realities of death. He took him to numerous dying beds, then dragged him into noisome dungeons where darkness was appalling and the stench loathsome and pestilential. There, men resembling wild beasts lay coiled in rags and filth, consumed by despair. The trials tested Clement's body severely, yet his soul gathered all its strength to comfort the wretched souls who remained capable of solace. In this crucible, Clement shone brilliantly. Jerome reported that Clement's spirit was willing, though his flesh was weak.

"Good!" declared Anselm. "His flesh is weak, but his spirit is willing."

Yet, a far greater trial awaited.

I shall recount it as witnessed by others. One morning, a principal street in Rome was choked with crowds, the avenues blocked by heads pressing together. It was an execution—not for a common crime, nor upon a vulgar victim.

The Governor of Rome had been found slaughtered in his bed at dawn. His hand, presumably raised in self-defense, lay severed at the wrist beside him; his throat was cut, and his temples bruised by a blunt instrument. The murder was traced to his servant, who was to face retribution that very morning.

Italian executions were rarely cruel in general, but this regicide demanded exact and bloody justice. The criminal was brought to the house of the murdered man and fastened to the wall for half an hour, a foretaste of legal vengeance. Then, his left hand was hacked off, mirroring his victim's. A newly killed fowl was slit open and pinned around the bleeding stump—the purpose of this macabre gesture remains obscure, perhaps a quirk of the era's medical mysticism. Thus mutilated and bandaged, the murderer was hurried to the scaffold. There, a young friar prayed with him most earnestly and affectionately, holding the crucifix close to his eyes.

Suddenly, the executioner shoved the friar aside roughly. With a heavy blow of a mallet, he felled the culprit, then slashed his throat from ear to ear. A cry of horror erupted from the crowd. The young friar swooned. A gigantic monk strode forward and carried him off like a child.

Brother Clement returned to the convent deeply discouraged. Confessing to the Prior with tears of regret, he was met with encouragement.

"Courage, son Clement," the Prior said. "A Dominican is not made in a day. You shall undergo another trial. And I forbid you to approach it fasting."

Clement bowed in obedience. He need not wait long. A notorious robber, a monster of villainy who had slain men in wanton cruelty after stripping them, was brought to the scaffold. Clement spent his final night in prison with the convict, accompanying him to the platform. He prayed for the hardened ruffian with such intensity that the man shed tears and embraced him. Though Clement's flesh quivered with repugnance, he reciprocated the embrace, holding the crucifix firmly before the criminal's eyes. As the rope tightened and the dark spirit departed, Clement prayed aloud and earnestly. The moment the man was dead, the hangman raised his hatchet and quartered the body on the spot. The crowd, having watched the living man die with indifference, even satisfaction, uttered piteous cries at each stroke of the axe upon the corpse, which could feel nothing. Clement shuddered but stood firm, like a rock that vibrates yet cannot be toppled.

Suddenly, Jerome's voice rang in his ear: "Brother Clement, mount that cart and preach to the people! Quickly! Strike with all your force against this iron while it is hot, while souls may yet be saved."

Clement's color drained and returned; he breathed heavily. Yet, he obeyed. With uncertain steps, he climbed onto the cart and delivered his first sermon to the first crowd he had ever faced. That sea of upturned faces! His throat felt parched, his heart hammered, his voice trembled.

Soon, however, the magnitude of the occasion, the sight of the eager faces, and the fire of his own zeal ignited the pale monk. He recounted the robber's history, fresh from his own lips in the prison cell, using the example to illustrate the gradations of folly and crime, warning them solemnly not to place their foot on the first rung of that fatal ladder. Alternately thundering against shedders of blood and moving the crowd toward charity and pity, his tremors vanished. He felt strung tight like a lute, gifted with an unsuspected force. He mastered the listening crowd, sensing their very pulse, playing sacred melodies upon them as if they were his psaltery. Sobs and groans testified to his power over the mob, already stirred by the preceding tragedy. Jerome stared, astonished, like one watching a rocket launch. After a while, catching Jerome's look of surprise and finding no approval in it, Clement abruptly stopped and descended.

"It was my first endeavor," he apologized. "Your command came upon me like a thunderbolt. Am I qualified? Did I succeed? Oh, correct me, and aid me with your experience, Brother Jerome."

"Hmph!" Jerome responded dubiously. After long reflection, he added somewhat sullenly, "Give the glory to God, Brother Clement; my opinion is that you are an orator born."

He reported this at headquarters with half-reluctance, for he was an honest friar, albeit a disagreeable one.

Later, Julio Antonelli was accused of sacrilege. Three witnesses swore they saw him emerge from the church where candlesticks had been stolen, at the precise moment of the theft. Other witnesses provided a positive alibi. Caught in this contradiction, Antonelli was granted the trial by water—hot or cold. By the hot trial, he would plunge his bare arm into boiling water fourteen inches deep and retrieve a pebble; by the cold trial, his body would be submerged in eight feet of water. The clergy, believing him innocent, recommended the hot water ordeal, which, to those favored, did not seem as terrifying as it sounded. But Antonelli lacked the nerve and chose the cold ordeal.

This presented Jerome with another opportunity to steel Clement. Antonelli received the sacrament, was then stripped naked on the banks of the Tiber, and tied hand and foot to prevent struggling, which would cause a man to sink. He was gently lowered into the stream, floating momentarily with only his hair above the surface. A simultaneous roar from the crowds on either bank proclaimed him guilty. But the next instant, the ropes—which happened to be new—got wet and slackened, allowing him to settle deeper. Another roar declared his innocence. They left him at the bottom for the appointed time, slightly longer than half a minute, before drawing him up, gurgling, gasping, and screaming for mercy. After the requisite prayers, he was dismissed, cleared of the charge.

Throughout the experiment, Clement prayed earnestly on the bank. When it ended, he thanked God in a loud but slightly quavering voice. Later, he asked Jerome if the man ought not to be compensated.

"For what?" Jerome asked.

"For the pain, the dread, the suffocation. Poor soul, he lives, but has tasted all the bitterness of death. Yet he committed no ill."

"He is rewarded sufficiently in being cleared of his fault."

"But being innocent of that fault, he has nonetheless drunk Death's cup, though not to the dregs; while his accusers, less innocent than he, suffered naught."

Jerome replied sternly, "It is not in this world that men are truly punished, Brother Clement. Unhappy are those who sin yet suffer not. Happy are those who endure such ills as earth can inflict; it is counted to them above, y

Catherine is rarely a blessing within a stranger's home; her influence is often a force of domination rather than harmony. Hardly had she crossed the threshold before the household began to shift under her invisible command: the utensils gleamed with renewed vigor, the hearth swept itself clean, the windows seemed to draw in extra sunlight, and the very spirit of the house appeared animated by a giant, energetic cricket. Yet, this cricket was a "Busy Body"—a formidable character lacking all discernment. It sought to correct everything and everyone indiscriminately. While many things improve when set aright, not everything does; indeed, nothing stands correction quite as poorly as living arrangements, save perhaps for the silence of the grave.

Consequently, Catherine rearranged every chair and table in Margaret's house, likely for the better, yet she felt compelled to disturb the living furniture as well. As Margaret's confinement approached, Catherine deceitfully summoned Denys and Martin to assist. When the simple-minded men earnestly inquired how they might be of service, she suggested they find utility by taking a short walk. She added, with a lack of tact, that extending this stroll into the following week would be even preferable. This was hardly an invitation designed to endear her to them.

The dynamic of the strong yielding to the whims of the weak might have appeased a she-bear with three cubs, one of whom was already sickly. Instead, Denys and Martin generally slipped out at dawn and returned like thieves at night. If caught inside, they moved with the caution of cats walking on walls lined with broken glass, wearing expressions of awe and a demeanor of total subjugation. Yet, this was insufficient. Their mere presence was ill-timed and grated on Catherine's nerves. Perhaps instinct whispered that a pair of depopulators had no place in a house destined to multiply twice over. No breastplate offers armor against a woman's tongue, and Catherine drove countless pins of sharp speech into them.

In summary, when Margaret descended the stairs, she discovered the kitchen stripped of its defenders. Martin, old and stiff, had retreated no further than the street, returning with the honors of war: he had commandeered a stool and sat upon it in mid-air. Margaret noted he was exposed to the sun but failed to realize he had become a fixture suspended in the luminary. Seeking Denys, she declared, "Good, kind Denys; he will be delighted to see me again."

Catherine, wiping a bowl with exaggerated vigor, informed her that Denys had departed for his friends in Burgundy. "High time!" Margaret observed, noting he hadn't visited in three years. "What, gone without bidding me farewell?" she asked, lifting two tender eyes like full-blown violets.

Catherine flushed. This new perspective pricked her conscience, yet she dismissed it with a toss of her head. "Oh, you were asleep at the time; I would not have woken you."

"Poor Denys," Margaret sighed, and visible dew gathered on her violet eyes. Watching from the corner of her eye, Catherine, without acknowledging the sentiment, turned her attention to the remaining soldier. Suddenly, she lavished hospitality and tenderness upon Martin, who was old and stiff in more ways than one.

"No, thank you, dame," Martin replied. "I have grown accustomed to being outdoors. I dislike changing and changing. I shall meddle with no one here, nor let anyone meddle with me."

"Oh, you nasty, cross old wretch!" Catherine shrieked, shifting instantly from syrup to vinegar, before flouncing back into the house.

On calm reflection, she allowed herself a small cry, then half-reconciled her actions by vowing that Margaret would never miss her "plagues of soldiers" again. Still feeling uneasy, she dispelled her regrets with a simple, sovereign act: she took the child and washed him. From head to foot, she bathed little Gerard in tepid water, and concerns regarding heroes and their grievances dissolved like dust in an ocean of soap and suds.

While this celestial ceremony continued, Margaret could not remain silent. Hovering around the fortunate washerwoman, she insisted on having an apparent, if not real, hand in the task. She dipped her finger into the water—presumably to pave the way for her son, though she surely knew Catherine would not permit her to judge the temperature. Kneeling opposite the infant, she chattered with remarkable fluency, careful not to speak as adults do, lest a cherub find their pronunciation ridiculous.

"I wish you could wash out *that*," she said, fixing her gaze on the baby's hand.

"What?"

"What? Have you not noticed? On his little finger."

Granny leaned in. There, indeed, was a tiny brown mole. "Eh, but this is wonderful!" she exclaimed. "Nature, my lass, you are strong—and meddling to boot. Have you noticed such a mark on anyone else? Tell the truth, girl!"

"On him? Nay, mother, not I."

"Well, then he has; and on the very spot. And you never noticed that much. But, dear heart, I forgot; you haven't known him from child to man as I have. I've had him hundreds of times on my knees, just as this, and washed him from top to toe in lukewarm water." Swelling with conscious superiority, she looked up at Margaret as a woman beyond comparison.

Catherine gazed down from her dizzying height of moral authority and mused. Unlike other busybodies, she occasionally reflected—not deeply, certainly, for she would ensure such thoughts remained unprinted. "It is strange," she remarked, "how things circle back. Life is but a whirligig. At least, for us poor women, our lives follow one pattern. Wasn't I, in my youth, trying to wash out my Gerard's mole? 'Oh, fie! Here's a foul blot!' I'd say, scrubbing until the poor wight cried, before deciding it was time to desist. And now you tell me, 'Mother,' 'try to wash out my Gerard's finger.' Think on it!"

"Wash it out?" Margaret cried. "I wouldn't for all the world! It is the sweetest part of his little body. I'll kiss it morning and night until the owner returns to us three. Oh, bless you, my jewel of gold and silver, for bearing a mark like your own daddy, to comfort me."

She kissed the mole, but could not stop there. Soon, she had little Gerard sprawled across her lap, kissing his back repeatedly, seeming to worry the lamb as a wolf might. Catherine watched, smiling. She had witnessed many such savage onslaughts in her day. This brief sketch indicated the tenor of Margaret's life for several months.

A few minor events occurred during this period that deserve mention, though I reserve them for another thread, as one string can hold many glass beads. However, while her boy's father endured those terrifying storms of the soul, culminating in the dead calm of the monastery, Margaret's existence could be summarized in one great, blissful word: Maternity.

To those who understand the weight of that word, expand this sketch slightly. See the young mother nursing, washing, dressing, and undressing; see her cooing and gamboling with her firstborn. Then, swift as lightning, dart your eye to Italy and behold the cold cloister, where monks pass like ghosts, heads bowed, hands meekly crossed over chests dead to earthly feeling. One of these cowled specters is the man whose return, filled with love, youth, and joy—the radiant young mother awaits.

Consider the traveler in the valley of Grindelwald. To one side rise the perpendicular Alps: rock, ice, and eternal snow, towering above the clouds and piercing the sky. To the other lie the modest, everyday slopes, green as emeralds, dotted with cows and charming cottages, teeming with life. Where those lofty neighbors stand leafless, lifeless, and inhumanly sublime, the lower valleys offer a gentle contrast.

Elsewhere, the commonplaces of nature often go unnoticed; but facing the grim Alps, they soothe and gently strike the mind through their juxtaposition with such tremendous opposites. Thus, in their own way, are the two halves of this story correctly viewed: on the Italian side, rugged adventure, strong passion, blasphemy, vice, penitence, pure ice, and holy snow soaring straight toward heaven. On the Dutch side, everything remains on a humble, feminine scale, yet evergreen. Just as a path divides the ice towers of Grindelwald, aspiring to the heavens, from its little sunny hillsides, so too here exists but a single page between "The Cloister and the Hearth."

The new Pope favored the Dominican Order. From the Vatican came a summons requesting a capable friar to teach at the University of Basel. Now, Clement was the very monk suited for this task: well-versed in languages and, in his former secular life, a student of Guarini the Younger's lectures. Consequently, his planned return to England was postponed, though not abandoned. Instead, he was dispatched to Basel; however, lacking immediate need there for three months, he was instructed to preach along the way.

He departed through the northern gate with his gaze lowered, the entire figure of the man wrapped in pious contemplation. *Oh, if one could paint a mind and its history,* the observer thought, *what a living fresco was this barefooted friar!* Hopeful love, bereavement, despair, impiety, vice, suicide, remorse, religious despondency, penitence, and finally, death to the world and total resignation—all compressed into merely twelve short months. And now, the traveler was on foot again. Yet, everything had changed. There were no longer perilous adventures. Even thieves and robbers bowed before him; instead of stripping him, they forced stolen coins into his hands and begged for his prayers. Thus, this journey offered few picturesque incidents.

However, I have readers in mind who care little for melodrama and prefer a quiet glimpse into the workings of a human heart. To such observers, the undramatic is often the most vocal, revealing the slow progress of a mind. The first Sunday of Clement's journey bore witness to this internal shift. He prayed for the soul of Margaret. He had never done so before. Not because her eternal welfare was not dearer to him than anything on earth—it was his own humility. The terrible blasphemies that had burst forth from his lips upon hearing of her death had horrified his well-meaning contemporaries, but upon reflection, they horrified him far more.

For a long duration during his novitiate, Clement had been crushed by religious despair. He believed he must have committed the sin against the Holy Spirit, a transgression that doomed the soul forever. Gradually, aided by Anselmo, that dark cloud lifted; yet, a profound self-abasement remained. He felt his own salvation precarious and reasoned that it would be mocking Heaven for him, a soul so deeply stained, to pray for one as innocent and comparatively pure as Margaret's. Thus, he coaxed good Anselm and another kindly monk to intercede for her. They refused not, nor did they offer half-hearted aid. Generally, the good old monks—though every convent held a mix of good, bad, and indifferent souls—possessed a pure, tender affection for their younger brethren, a bond truly not of this world.

Clement, having preached on Sunday morning in a small Italian town and feeling mightily carried onward, was greatly encouraged. That day, a balmy sense of God's forgiveness and love descended upon him. From that hour, he prayed for the welfare of Margaret's soul. It became his daily habit, the single purified tie connecting his heart to the earth. For his family were to him as if they had never existed. The Church would not share its domain with the earth, nor could it heal the great love without annihilating the lesser attachments.

Throughout most of this journey, Clement felt no spring of life within him, except when standing in the pulpit. Other exceptions occurred when he relieved a fellow creature in distress. Once, he encountered a young man suffering from what appeared to be a tarantula bite—or perhaps, like many others, merely imagined it. Whether fancy or reality, the youth had slept for two days and suffered extraordinary convulsions, leaping, twisting, and battering the walls. Village musicians had only exacerbated his condition with their frantic tunes. Exhaustion and death awaited if the malady gained further hold.

Clement stopped to inquire. Summoning a psaltery, he attempted to soothe the patient with gentle melodies. If other tunes drove the man mad, Clement's seemed to crush him. The youth groaned and moaned, eventually collapsing onto the floor. Finally, the friar noticed that at intervals, the patient's lips moved silently. Applying his ear, he discovered the man was whispering a tune—a singular melody that had no existence outside that mind. Clement learned the song and played it. The patient's face brightened miraculously. He danced lightly across the room, enjoying the rhythm, until Clement's fingers ached from the continuous playing. Only then did the young man sink complacently into sleep, lulled by this strange creation of his own mind, for he was no musician and had never composed a tune before or after. This sleep saved his life. After teaching the melody to another, in case it were needed again, Clement moved forward with a heart slightly warmer.

On another occasion, he found a mob dragging a decently dressed man who struggled and shouted in a strange tongue. This individual had entered the town erect and sprightly, waving a mulberry branch overhead. The locals first stared in stupefied disbelief, then pounced, dragging him before the podestà. Clement accompanied them but quietly approached the prisoner, speaking to him in Italian. Receiving no answer, he switched to French, then German, then Dutch—to no avail. The man then tested Clement in tolerable Latin, albeit with a sharp accent.

"I am an Englishman," the man explained, "oppressed by the heat of Italy. I plucked a bough from the nearest tree to save my head. In my country, anyone is welcome to what grows on the highway. Confound the fools; I am ready to pay for it. But here, all of Italy rises in arms over a mere twig and a handful of leaves!"

The stubborn podestà intended to imprison the dogged islander, but Clement intervened. With some difficulty, he helped the prisoner understand that silkworms—and consequently, mulberry leaves—were sacred, falling under the protection of the Sovereign and serving as his source of income. Clement urged the podestà that ignorance of these mulberry laws was natural in a distant land where the very tree might be unknown. The opinionated islander tipped the scales by producing a long purse and reiterating his original stance: the matter was purely commercial.

*"Quid damni?"* he asked. *"Dic, et cito solvam"* ("What damage? Speak, and I will quickly pay"). The podestà sniffed the gold, fined the man a ducat for the Duke—roughly the value of the entire tree—and pocketed the coin. The Englishman shed his ire instantly, laughing heartily at the absurdity, yet remaining deeply grateful to Clement.

"You are too good for this hole of a country, father," he said. "Come to England! That is the only place in the world. I was an uneasy fool to leave it and wander among mulberries and their idiots. I am a Kentish squire, educated at Cambridge University. My name is Rolfe; my place is Betshanger. The man and the house are at your service. Come over and stay until Doomsday. We sit down forty to dinner every day at Betshanger; one more or one less makes no difference. You shall spend your days with me and my heirs if you will. Come now! What an Englishman says, he means." He gave Clement a hearty grip of the hand to confirm his sincerity.

"I will visit thee some day, my son," Clement replied, "but not to weary thy hospitality."

The Englishman then begged Clement to absolve him. "I know not what will become of my soul," he confessed. "I have lived like a heathen since leaving England." Clement consented gladly, and soon the islander knelt by the roadside, confessing the sins of the last month. Finding him so pious a son of the Church, Clement revealed he was indeed returning to England. He then asked if it were true the country was overrun with Lollards and Wickliffites. The other flushed slightly.

"There be black sheep in every land," he admitted. After a moment's reflection, he spoke gravely: "Holy Father, hear the truth regarding these heretics. None are better disposed toward Holy Church than we English. But we are ourselves, and by ourselves. We love our own ways, and above all, our own tongue. The Normans could conquer our bill-hooks, but not our tongues; and hard they tried it for many long years by law and proclamation. Our good foreign priests speak God to plain English folk in Latin, or in some French or Italian dialect, like the bleating of a sheep. Then comes the fox Wickliffe and his crew, reading Scripture out loud in plain English, so that all men's hearts warm to it. Who can withstand this? God forgive me, I believe the English would turn deaf ears to Saint Peter himself if he did not speak to them in the tongue their mothers taught them alongside their mother's kisses."

He added hastily, "I say not this for myself; I am Cambridge-bred, and good words come amiss to me in Latin; but for the people in general. *Clavis ad corda Anglorum est lingua materna.* ('The key to the hearts of the English is the mother tongue.')"

"My son," said Clement, "blessed be the hour I met thee; for thy words are sober and wise. But alas! How shall I learn your English tongue? I possess no book."

"I would give you my Book of Hours, father," the Englishman offered. "'Tis in English and Latin, cheek by jowl. But then, what would become of my poor soul, needing my 'Hours'

Clement sighed, a sound heavy with the weight of his own uncertainty. He began to wonder if he had chosen the wisest path when dealing with a spirit so fiercely passionate. Yet, even in his youth, he had absorbed many lessons of ecclesiastical prudence. Above all, he had been taught the virtue of pausing—in moments of difficulty—to neither act nor speak until the fog of emotion cleared enough to reveal truth. Thus, he chose silence and offered up a prayer for wisdom. His only physical action was to gently withdraw his foot.

But the penitent reacted instantly. With a piteous cry, she threw her arms around it, clutching it convulsively, weeping openly over the leather. In that moment, the raw agony of shame mingled with profound penitence, manifesting as a bright crimson flush that climbed her neck as she trembled beneath him.

"My daughter," Clement said softly, "take courage. Do not torment yourself further regarding this Gerard, who does not exist. As for me, I am Brother Clement, whom Heaven has sent to you today to offer comfort and aid in saving your soul. Since you have named me your confessor, I now claim your obedience."

"Oh, yes," she sobbed. "Leave this pilgrimage and return immediately to Rome. True penance cannot be found abroad; its value is little there. Where we dwell lie the specific temptations we must conquer, or else perish. It is easy to wash the feet of strangers, masking our true selves, but hard to remain merely meek and charitable toward those closest to us."

"I swear, I will never touch her again!" she declared.

"Nay," Clement corrected gently. "I speak not only of servants, but of dependents, kin, and friends. Let this be your penance: at the last hour of the night and the first after Matins, recall your sin and God's boundless goodness. Be humble and gentle toward the faults of those around you. The world courts the rich; seek instead the poor—not the beggar, for they are often neither honest nor truly destitute—but find those who blush to ask for help yet desperately need it. To give to them is to lend to Heaven. Furthermore, marry a good son of the Church."

"Me?" she recoiled. "I will never marry."

"You will marry within the year," Clement insisted firmly. "I entreat and command you to wed one who fears God. For you are very clay; mated poorly, you will become nothing. But united with a worthy husband, by God's grace, you may live as a pious princess and die a saint."

"You."

He then urged her to rise and begin the good work he had assigned. She rose to her knees, removed her mask, and cast an eloquent, searching look upon him before lowering her eyes in meek submission.

"I will obey you as I would an angel," she whispered. "How happy I am, yet how unhappy! Oh, my heart tells me I shall never look upon you again. I will not leave until I have dried your feet."

"It is unnecessary," Clement replied. "I have excused you from this bootless penance."

"'Tis no penance to me," she insisted. "Ah! You do not forgive me if you will not let me dry your poor feet."

"So be it then," Clement said, resigning himself to her plea. *Levius quid foemina,* he thought internally—*how easily women yield.* Yet, these fragile creatures, which gravitate toward the small just as celestial bodies turn to the great, possess flashes of angelic intelligence. After the princess had dried the friar's feet, she gazed at him through tear-filled, beautiful eyes and murmured with singular tenderness, "May I have masses said for her soul?" she added timidly.

A faint blush colored the monk's cheek, and moisture gathered in his cold blue eyes. It was a sudden reaction from one he had just rated so lowly.

"It is a gracious thought," he admitted. "Do as you wish; often such acts return to the doer like blessed dew. Remember, I am your confessor, not hers; it is your soul I must strive to save, or woe betide my own. My daughter, my dear daughter, I see good and evil angels battling for your soul this very day, right now. Fight on your own side. Do you remember everything I commanded?"

"Remember!" the princess affirmed. "Sweet saint, every syllable of yours is engraved in my heart."

"But one word more, then. Pray much to Christ, and little to His saints."

"I will."

"And that is the finest counsel I can offer you. Now, we part on this note. You go to the place that suits you best—your father's house—and I return to my holy mother's work."

"Adieu," the princess faltered.

"Adieu, thou whom I have loved too well, hated too ill, known and revered too late; forgiving angel, adieu—for ever."

The monk caught her words, though they broke forth only in a sigh. "For ever?" he cried aloud, seized by sudden ardor. "Christians live 'forever,' and love 'forever,' but they never part 'forever.' They separate, as the earth and sun do, only to meet more brightly in a little while. You and I part here for this earthly life. But what is our life? It is but one line in the grand story of the Church, whose son and daughter we are; one handful in the sands of time, one drop in the ocean of 'Forever.' Adieu—for the brief moment called 'a life!' We part in trouble, but we shall meet in peace; we part as creatures of clay, but we shall meet as immortal spirits; we part in a world of sin and sorrow, but we shall meet where all is purity and divine love, where no ill passions exist, but only Christ and His saints arrayed in white. There, in the turning of an hourglass, in the breaking of a bubble, in the passing of a cloud, she, thou, and I shall meet again. We shall sit at the feet of angels, archangels, apostles, and saints, beaming with joy unspeakable in the light of the shadow of God upon His throne, FOREVER—AND EVER—AND EVER."

Thus they parted. The monk stood erect, his eyes turned heavenward and glowing with the sacred fire of zeal; the princess withdrew slowly, turning more than once to cast a lingering glance of awe and tender regret upon that inspired figure. She returned home subdued and purified. Clement, in due course, reached Basel and took up his duties, teaching in the university and preaching throughout the town and its neighborhood. He led a life that could be summed in two words: deep study and mortification. My readers have already glimpsed his soul. In Basel, he advanced in both holy zeal and knowledge, and the brethren of his order began to see in him a true descendant of the saints and martyrs.

When little Gerard was barely three months old, a messenger arrived, breathless and hot from Tergou, bearing news for Catherine. "Go back immediately," she commanded him sharply. "Tell them I cannot come, and I will not. They have already taken Kate." With that dispatch sent, she turned her attention to her own plight. "There, child," she said, addressing Margaret, "I must depart. Everyone here is in a state of confusion; this is the third time they have asked. Tomorrow, my man will come himself to drag me home by the ear, flea and all."

She recounted her trials with infants, instructing Margaret meticulously on how to handle every potential emergency, and pressed money into her hands. Margaret declined with gratitude, yet Catherine insisted, her tone shifting to anger. Margaret offered reasons so logically sound that Catherine dismissed them with cold contempt, deeming them lacking in feminine spirit. "Come, out with your heart," Catherine urged, "as we part today—perhaps forever seeing one another's face again."

"Oh, Mother, do not speak thus!" Margaret pleaded.

"Alas, girl, I have witnessed this sorrow countless times," Catherine replied, her eyes clouding. "'Twill haunt me at every farewell. When I was your age, I harbored no such fears; we believed ourselves immortal then. Now, get it over with."

"Well, then, Mother... I would rather not have mentioned it—but your Cornelis must say to me, 'So you have come to share our table, eh, Mistress?' Those were his exact words. I told him I would be deeply sorry."

"Beshrew his sharp tongue! What does it signify?"

"He will surely find out eventually, but regardless, I will accept no begrudging bounty from any household. Unless I see my child starving—and Heaven alone knows what I might then be forced to do—"

"Nay, Mother, give me but your love. I value that above all silver. They do not withhold that from me, by everything I hold dear. Not a single stiver will I take from your house."

"You are a foolish lass," Catherine chided gently. "Were it me, I would take it simply to spite him."

"No, you would not. You and I are apples from the same tree."

Catherine relented with grace. When the moment of separation finally arrived, embraces and tears erupted from both women. Once Catherine was gone, the child wailed incessantly.
