All attempts to soothe him failed until Margaret suspected a pin pricking his spine. Searching between his clothes and skin, she discovered a gold angel causing him discomfort.

"There, now, Gerard," she whispered to the babe. "I thought Granny yielded too suddenly." Taking the coin, she wrapped it in linen and placed it at the bottom of her chest, telling the infant to observe that she, too, could be as resolute as his grandmother.

Later, Catherine confided in Eli about Margaret's proud defiance. Eli agreed that Margaret was right and Catherine wrong, tossing his head in disagreement. Catherine merely pondered the exchange.

Margaret, however, faced her own quiet anxieties. She still had two men to feed and could no longer labor as intensely as before. Keeping the house, caring for the child, and cooking for everyone consumed her days. Yet, she had saved a small sum, often telling her son that his father would return before their funds ran dry. Clinging to these bright hopes and the sanctuary of her boy, she endured several happy months.

But time marched on. No Gerard returned. Stranger still, no news reached them. Her mind, usually so practical, grew unsettled, drifting into sad reveries and silent sighs. As her heart troubled itself, her savings melted away. One day, she found the cupboard empty. Looking at the faces of her dependents, her bosom swelled with pity. Could she let Grandfather and poor old Martin go hungry? She retrieved Catherine's hidden angel. As she unwrapped the linen, a tear of gentle mortification fell upon the precious metal.

She sent Martin out to exchange it. While he was away, a Frenchman arrived with a dealer in illuminated manuscripts, who had previously offered her a pittance. This man claimed he was employed by his sovereign to collect masterpieces for a Book of Hours. Margaret showed him her finest works, including the shading of flowers, raspberries, and creeping vines created by her late husband, Margaret Van Eyck. The stranger was enchanted.

"Nay, mock not my desperate need, good stranger," she protested. "Three mouths await food in this house, and none to provide it but me."

A curious arithmetic indeed, leaving out number one.

"I shall not outbid you, fair mistress," he replied warmly. "My Princess charged me strictly to seek the best craftsmen, but I forbid hard bargains. Make them content with me, and I with them."

In the next instant, Margaret knelt beside the cradle, kissing little Gerard and showering him with four gold pieces, recounting the event in broken Dutch. "And oh, what a generous Princess! Was she not? We shall pray for her, won't we, my lambkin, when we are older?"

Martin entered, furious. "They will not change it! I fear they believe I stole it."

"I am indebted to you," Margaret hastened to say, snatching the coins from him, wrapping them once more, and returning them to their hiding place.

Before these unexpected funds vanished, she resumed her ironing and starching. Midway through, Martin fell ill and died after nine days. Nearly all her remaining money was spent on his decent burial. He was gone, leaving an empty chair by the fireside—a seat he had preferred to the sun since the Busy Body left. Margaret forbade anyone else to sit there. Yet, whenever her gaze lingered too long on the vacancy, it inevitably cost her a tear.

Now, with no one to carry her laundry home, she considered doing it herself, but that required leaving little Gerard in a neighbor's care. She dared not trust such a treasure to mortal hands for hours. Instead, she sought inquiries for a boy to transport her baskets on Saturdays and Mondays.

A plump, fresh-complexioned youth named Luke Peterson appeared. Though he looked fifteen, he was actually eighteen. Blushing and fidgeting with his cap, he asked if a man could not serve her better than a boy. Before she could answer, she thought, *This boy will suffice.* But taking his cue, she replied, "Nay; perhaps a man seeks more than I can afford."

"Not I," Luke declared warmly. "Mistress Margaret, I am your neighbor and work well at coopering. I can carry your basket before or after my daily work, gladly. You owe me nothing. We are neighbors, you know."

"Why, Master Luke, I recognize your face, though I cannot recall a word passing between us."

"Oh, yes, you did, Mistress Margaret. Have you forgotten? One day you struggled carrying your baby and a full pitcher of water. I said, 'Let me carry the baby.' You replied, 'Keep the bairn, I'll give you the pitcher.' I carried the pitcher home, and you took it from me at this door, saying, 'I am greatly obliged to you, young man,' in such a sweet voice—not like the harsh speech of people in this street."

"I remember now, Master Luke; and I think it was the least I could say."

"Well, Mistress Margaret, if you wish to say as much every time I carry your basket, I care not how often or how far."

"Nay, nay," Margaret murmured, flushing slightly. "I would not burden your kindness. You are young, Master Luke, and so good. How about giving you supper on Saturday night when you bring the linen home, and your morning meal on Monday? Would that balance us?"

"As you please; only do not claim I sought two meals for such a trifling favor."

With Luke Peterson's timely aid and the strength Heaven bestowed upon this poor woman to counterbalance her sorrows, the household ran smoothly for a while. Yet, her heart grew increasingly tormented by Gerard's long, now utterly mysterious silence. Suspense, that relentless tormentor, began to gnaw at her soul with hot pincers until she cried out often and desperately, "Oh, that I could know the worst!"

One day, hearing a heavy step ascend the stairs, she started and trembled. *That is no familiar step. Bad tidings?* The door opened, revealing an unexpected visitor: Eli, looking grave yet kind.

Margaret stared at him in silence, her agitation mounting. "Girl," he said softly, "the Skipper has returned."

"One word," Margaret gasped. "Is he alive?"

"Surely I hope so. No one has seen him dead."

"Then they must have seen him alive."

"No, girl. Neither dead nor alive has he been seen in Rome for many months. My daughter Kate believes he has gone to another city. She bid me tell you her suspicion."

"Ay, likely enough," Margaret muttered gloomily. "Likely enough. My poor babe!"

The old man, speaking in a faint voice, asked for a morsel to eat; he had come fasting. Filled with pity despite her agitated mind, Margaret cooked a meal for him, trembling and scarcely aware of her actions. Before departing, he laid his hand upon her head. "Whether he lives or dies, I regard you as my daughter. Can I do nothing for you today? Think on it."

"Yes, old man. Pray for him

Shortly thereafter, as the small household at Tergou gathered around the dinner table, Luke Peterson burst through the door, his clothes heavy with road dust.

"Good people," he gasped, "Mistress Catherine is required instantly in Rotterdam."

"My name is Catherine, young sir," the woman corrected him firmly. "Call me Kate, or rather, Margaret."

"Ay, dame," Luke replied breathlessly. "She spoke to me thus: 'Good Luke, hasten to Tergou, seek out Eli the hosier, and beg his wife Catherine to come to me forthwith, for God's love.' I made no delay until daylight broke."

"Holy saints!" exclaimed the hostess, her composure fracturing. "He has returned home? No, surely she would have said so. What on earth could possibly bring her here?" She launched into a rapid succession of wild guesses.

"Perhaps the young man can enlighten us," Kate ventured timidly.

"I can certainly explain," Luke declared. "Her baby is dying, and she was utterly consumed by worry over it."

Margaret sprang to her feet. "What is the matter with him?"

"Nay, I know not the specifics," Luke admitted. "But he has been wasting away, growing weaker and weaker for some time past."

A fleeting, furtive exchange of satisfaction passed between Cornelis and Sybrandt. Fortunately, Catherine did not notice; her gaze was fixed intently upon her husband.

"Now, Eli," she cried, her voice rising in fury, "if you utter a single word against this, we shall quarrel, after all these years of marriage!"

"Who gainsays thee, foolish woman?" Eli retorted calmly. "Quarrel with your own shadow while I go fetch Peter's mule for you."

"Bless thee, my good man! Bless thee! Have you never yet failed me in a crisis?" she exclaimed, her anger melting into gratitude. "Eat your dinner whoever can, while I prepare to depart."

She ushered Luke into the cart, where she interrogated him relentlessly—alternating between severe questioning and seductive coaxing—until she had extracted every scrap of information from his mind.

Upon arriving, Margaret met them at the threshold, pale and trembling. She flung her arms around Catherine's neck, looking up with desperate pleading.

"Come," Catherine said softly after examining her friend's face, "he lives, thank God."

She glanced first at the failing child, then at the hollow-eyed mother, switching her gaze between them. "Fortunate that you summoned me," she noted. "The child is poisoned."

"Poisoned! By whom?" Margaret asked, horror-struck.

"By you. You have been fretting."

"Nay, indeed, Mother. How can I avoid fretting?"

"Do not tell me," Catherine snapped. "A nursing mother has no business to fret. She must turn her mind entirely away from her grief toward the comfort resting in her lap. Do you not know that the child wastes away if the mother vexes herself? This is the result of your reading and writing. Such idle pursuits befit a man; they keep all practical wisdom far from a woman. The child must be weaned."

"Oh, you cruel woman!" Margaret cried vehemently. "I am sorry I called for you. Would you rob me of the only solace I possess in this world? While I nurse my Gerard, I forget that I am the most miserable creature under the sun."

"That you do not," Catherine shot back. "Or he would not be as he is."

"Mother!" Margaret implored.

"'Tis hard," Catherine relented finally. "But consider: would it not be harder to look down and see his lovely little face gazing up at you from within a coffin?"

"Oh, Jesus!"

"And how could you face your other sorrows with your heart perpetually full and your lap empty?"

"Oh, Mother, I consent to anything. Only save my boy."

"That is a good girl," Catherine assured her. "Trust me. I see things more clearly than you do."

Yet, there remained one final consent to secure—that of the infant himself—and he proved more stubborn than his mother.

"There," Margaret tried to feign regret at his behavior, "he loves me too dearly."

Catherine, however, was a match for both. Having noticed a robust young woman seated outside her own door, holding an infant nearby, Catherine approached her. She explained the situation and asked if she might nurse the starving child temporarily until arrangements could be made to wean him. The stranger agreed with a gentle smile, placed her own child in the cradle, and entered Margaret's home.

She curtsied gracefully, and Catherine handed the suffering infant to her. The stranger examined the baby, filled with pity, cooed over him, and immediately began to nurse him as though he were her own.

Margaret, paralyzed by shock at such assurance, cast a sorrowful glance at Catherine and burst into tears. The visitor looked up.

"What is the matter? Wife, you told me the mother was unwilling."

"She is not," Catherine interjected sharply. "She is merely a fool. Pay her no heed. And you, Margaret, I am ashamed of you."

"You are a cruel, hard-hearted woman," Margaret sobbed.

"Those who undertake to guide the weak must necessarily be stern," Catherine replied bluntly. "And forgive me; you are not my flesh and blood, whereas your boy is."

After allowing this harsh truth to settle, she added gently, "Come now. She is sacrificing her own to save yours, and you can think of nothing better than to burst into tears in the woman's face. Out, fie, for shame!"

"Nay, wife," the nurse responded kindly. "Thank heaven, I have enough for myself and hers to spare. And pray, do not blame her! Perhaps the trials of life have soured her own milk."

"And her heart as well," Catherine remarked mercilessly.

Margaret stared her full in the face, then lowered her eyes.

"I know I ought to be very grateful to you," Margaret whispered to the nurse, then turned her head away, leaning over the chair to avoid witnessing the unbearable sight of another woman nursing her Gerard, who showed no distinction between this new mother and the one he had rejected.

The nurse replied, "You are very welcome, my poor woman. And so are you, Mistress Catherine, who is my townsfolk, though you seem unaware."

"What, are you from Tergou? All the better. Yet I cannot recall your face."

"Oh, you do not know me. My husband and I are very humble folk compared to you. But true Eli and his wife are known and respected throughout the town. So, I am at your call, Dame; at yours, Wife; and at yours, my pretty poppet, night or day."

"There is a woman of the right old sort," Catherine murmured as the door closed behind her.

"I HATE her," Margaret whispered with terrible fervor. "I HATE her. I HATE her."

Catherine merely laughed at this outburst. "That is correct," she said. "Better to speak it openly and hold it in thought than to scheme silently. It is very natural, after all. Come, here is your bundle of comfort. Take it and hate it, if you can," she added, placing the child in Margaret's lap.

"No, no," Margaret protested, turning her head halfway away. She could not bear to turn the other side. "He is not my child now; he is hers. I do not know why she left him here, for my part. It was very kind of her not to take him to her own house, cradle and all... Oh! Oh! Oh! Oh! Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"Well," Catherine said soothingly, "one comfort remains: he is not dead. This gives me hope. Some other woman has taken him from me; like father, like son..."

Catherine felt compassion for her friend and allowed her to weep undisturbed. From that point forward, whenever she needed assistance from Joan, she would take Gerard outdoors for fresh air. Margaret never objected, nor did she express any disbelief; yet upon returning indoors, she was invariably in tears.

This tacit arrangement lasted only briefly. Margaret grew entirely eager to wean little Gerard. Once accomplished, the child regained his health and vigor. Soon, however, a new trial awaited him: the onset of teething. Here, Catherine's experience proved invaluable. Amidst her grief regarding the missing father, Margaret found moments of pure bliss watching her son's tiny teeth emerge.

"Teeth, Mother? I call them not teeth, but pearls of pearls," she would exclaim. Each pearl that peeked out and sparkled on his red gums seemed to her the greatest marvel nature had ever wrought. Her companion shared this illusion. If we had told them that standing corn was equally admirable, Margaret would have transformed into a reproachful gazelle, and Catherine would have thrown us out of the room; so, the arrival of each tooth was heralded with a shriek of triumph by whichever of the two happened to discover it first.

Catherine chatted with Joan and learned that she was the wife of Jorian Ketel of Tergou, who had once served Ghysbrecht Van Swieten but had fallen out of favor, returning to Rotterdam, his birthplace. His friends had secured him the position of sexton for the parish, and combined with carpentry, he managed quite well.

In return, Catherine told Joan whose

"A lie is not like a blow with a curtal axe," someone muttered—perhaps it was Sybrandt. "True enough; we can foresee, to some extent, the fallout of a physical strike. But a lie knows no bounds. Its nature is to spread far beyond human reckoning. In the ordinary course of events, a single falsehood has often cost a life or devastated several families. And so, in this tale, what catastrophic consequences flowed from that one heartless fabrication!"

Yet, ironically, the architects of the deceit reaped little reward. The brothers, who concocted the story merely to reduce the number of claimants to their father's estate, watched as the gentle Gerard stepped into the void they created, occupying the central place in their mother's heart. Worse still, one day Eli openly declared that, since Gerard was lost—and presumed dead—he had already arranged via will for both little Gerard and Margaret, the widow of his poor son. At this revelation, a silent warning passed between the outcasts. Cornelis felt the words die on his lips: *Gerard is most likely alive.* But catching his mother's gaze, he stifled them in time.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the other co-conspirator in that web of untruth, was now a dying man. He sensed the end drawing near, the moment when all his worldly wealth would slip from his grasp while his crimes clung eternally to his soul. Too wise to fully delude himself, he had never escaped the periodic storms of remorse. When he delivered Gerard's letter to Margaret, he had compromised. *"I cannot surrender land and money,"* his giant Avarice had commanded. *"I will cause her no unnecessary pain,"* his dwarf Conscience had replied. Thus, after tampering with the seal and discovering there was not a single word regarding the deed, he brought it to her personally, claiming credit for the gesture—a moral offset, calculated on a scale common where the self-accuser serves as judge.

The birth of Margaret's child struck him with fresh horror, casting his treachery in a cruel new light. If his forged letter were to take effect, he would be condemning the honor of a woman who was the daughter of one friend, the granddaughter of another, and from whom he was unjustly withholding her inheritance. These gnawing thoughts, arising during the twilight of life when bodily strength fades and memories of old friendships resurface, filled him with gloomy terrors. Yet, he dared not confess. The local physician was a man of integrity who would have forced him to purge his guilt, but for Ghysbrecht, Avarice was an ingrained habit, while penitence remained but a fleeting sentiment.

It was on such a day, returning from the town hall to his home, that he discovered a woman waiting in the vestibule, cradling a child. Veiled deeply, he assumed she harbored shame and addressed her with condescending authority. Instantly, she lowered her veil and met his gaze squarely. It was Margaret Brandt. Her sudden arrival and intense demeanor startled him, betraying a confusion he could not hide.

"Where is my Gerard?" she cried, her chest heaving. "Is he alive?"

"For all I know," Ghysbrecht stammered. "I pray he is, for your sake. Pray, enter this room. The servants—"

"Not a step," Margaret interrupted, seizing his shoulder with the fierce energy of a distressed woman. "You hold the secret that shatters my heart. Why does my Gerard not come? Why has he sent not a single line in these many months? Answer me, or the whole town shall know, along with your servants. My misery is too profound to be treated as a jest."

In vain, he insisted he knew nothing of Gerard. She countered that those who had escorted her to him had spun a different narrative.

"You do know why he neither comes nor writes," she stated firmly.

At this, Ghysbrecht paled further, yet summoning a facade of dignity, he retorted, "Would you believe those two knaves against a man of worship?"

"Which two knaves?" she pressed sharply.

He faltered. "Did you not say...? There I am, a poor, broken old man, my memory failing. You come here and confuse me so that I speak nonsense."

"Yes, sir, your memory is shaken, or surely you would not be my enemy. My father saved you from the plague when no other would approach; he was always your friend. My grandfather Floris aided you in your early poverty and loved you, man and boy. Three generations of us you have known; here stands the fourth. This is the grandson of your old friend Peter and the great-grandson of your old friend Floris. Look down upon his innocent face and recall theirs!"

"Woman, you torture me," Ghysbrecht sighed, collapsing onto a bench.

Seeing her advantage, Margaret knelt before him and placed the boy on his lap. "This fatherless babe belongs to poor Margaret Brandt, who has never wronged you, and comes from a lineage that loved you. Nay, look at his face. It will move you more than any words I could speak. Saints of heaven, what could a destitute girl and her infant have done to erase the memory of your own youth, when you were as guiltless as he is now, gazing up at you and imploring you to return his father?" Holding the child aloft with her arms beneath him, she smiled beneath the old man's weary eyes.

Ghysbrecht cast a wild look of anguish upon the child, then at the kneeling mother, before springing up and shrieking, "Away! Ye pair of adders!"

Stung, his soul granted his aged limbs a momentary surge of vigor. He paced rapidly, wringing his hands and clutching his white hair. "Forget those days? I forget everything else. Oh, woman, woman! Whether sleeping or waking, I see only the faces of the dead, hear only their voices, and soon I shall join them. There, there, what is done is done. I am in hell. I am in hell."

Unnatural force led to total collapse. He staggered, and without Margaret's support, would have fallen. With one freed arm, she steadied him as best she could, crying out for aid. Two servants rushed forward and carried him away in a state bordering on faintness. The last sight Margaret had of him was his ancient, furrowed face, pale and helpless, with white hair hanging over the servant's elbow.

"Heaven forgive me," she whispered. "I fear I have killed the poor old man."

Thus, this attempt to pierce the tormenting mystery left it as obscure, if not darker, than before. As she reflected on every word spoken, her suspicion hardened: Ghysbrecht certainly knew something about Gerard. "And who were the two knaves he claimed had performed a good deed and told me? Oh, my Gerard, my poor abandoned baby, you and I are wading in deep waters."

The journey to Tergou drained funds she could scarcely spare, and a customer fled owing her money. Once more, she was forced to reveal Catherine's angel. Strangely, however, as she descended the stairs carrying it, she found loose silver on the table accompanied by a written note: *For Gerard, his wife.*

She collapsed with a cry of astonishment upon reading the message, which quickly swelled into a cry of joy. "He is alive. He sends this through some friendly hand." She kissed the paper repeatedly and tucked it safely into her bodice.

Time passed, bringing no direct news of Gerard. Yet, roughly every two months, a small sum of silver appeared in the household. Sometimes it lay on the table; once it was thrown through the bedroom window in a purse; another time, it was found at the bottom of Luke's basket. The giver—or his agent—was never caught. Catherine disclaimed involvement; Margaret Van Eyck swore she played no part; even Eli denied knowledge. Whenever the money arrived, Margaret would tell little Gerard, "Oh, my poor deserted child, you and I are wading in deep waters."

She allocated at least half of this modest, yet vital, supply to dress little Gerard above his station in life. "If it truly comes from Gerard, he shall see his boy presentable." Soon, the mothers in the street began to sneer, particularly those with children dressed in rags.

Months rolled on, and a deadly sickness of the heart replaced the sharper pains of suspense. She returned to her initial belief: *Gerard must be dead. She should never see her boy's father again, nor secure her marriage prospects.* This final grief, previously softened by Eli and Catherine's acceptance of her engagement, now surged forth with renewed intensity. Others viewed her story with growing skepticism. Often, she lamented over her son's illegitimacy. "Is it not enough for us to be bereaved? Must we be dishonored as well? Oh, that we had never been born."

A shift occurred within Peter Brandt. His mind, clouded for nearly two years, seemed to clear; he experienced intervals of lucidity. During these times, he and Margaret spoke of Gerard until his clarity faded again. One day, after an absence of several hours, Margaret found him conversing with Catherine in a manner unseen since his paralytic stroke.

"Eh, girl, why must you be out?" she asked.

"But indeed, I have told him all; and we have been weeping together over your troubles."

Margaret stood silent, looking joyfully from

She who had wept for poor old Martin was ill-equipped to bear such a blow with the stoic resignation often expected of those mourning the elderly. Catherine attempted in vain to soothe her with trite consolations—telling her it was a blessed release for the departed, quoting his own words that "the tree was ripe." Her most grievous failure occurred when she pressed the point that there were now but two mouths to feed, suggesting they should count themselves fortunate to have one less burden.

"Such cares are all the joy I possess," sobbed Margaret. "They fill my desolate heart, leaving it both void and wasted. Oh, empty chair! My bosom aches merely to behold you. Poor old man, how could I love him by halves, I who used to sit and gaze upon him, thinking, 'But for me, thou wouldst die of hunger'? He, once so wise and learned, has become as helpless as my own sweet babe, yet I loved him as if he were my child rather than my father. Oh, empty chair! Oh, empty heart! Well-a-day! Well-a-day!"

Her pious tears refused to be stilled. At length, Catherine fell silent, hanging her head in shame. Later, she confessed, speaking from her head rather than her heart, "Thou art right to ignore me. Were I in thy place, I should mourn the old man exactly as thou dost." When Margaret embraced her, offering a rare moment of genuine sympathy, it brought her some small comfort; the platitudes had achieved nothing.

Moved by compassion, Catherine's heart yearned for the young woman, and she spoke plainly: "My poor girl, you were not born to live alone. I must regard you as my own daughter. Do not squander your youth upon my son Gerard. Either he is dead, or he is a traitor. It cuts my heart to utter it, but who can deny what is evident before their eyes? Your father is gone, and I cannot remain beside you forever. Here stands an honest lad who has loved you faithfully for many days. I would take him and Comfort together. Heaven sends us such creatures both to torment and to comfort us; indeed, we are nothing in this world without them."

Seeing Margaret look utterly perplexed, Catherine pressed on, "Surely you are not so blind as not to perceive it?"

"What? Who?"

"Who else but Luke Peterson?"

"What, our Luke? The boy who carries my basket?"

"Nay, he is past nineteen now, a fine, healthy lad. I have made inquiries for you, and everyone speaks highly of him—a capable worker who never touches a drop. That is quite remarkable for a Rotterdam lad, for they are mostly half-man, half-sponge."

Margaret smiled for the first time in many days. "Luke loves dried puddings dearly," she admitted. "I make them to his liking; 'tis for them he comes courting here." Suddenly, she turned crimson. "But if I thought he came after your son's wife—that is, the one who ought to be—I would soon put him to the door."

"Nay, nay; for Heaven's sake, let me not stir up mischief. Poor lad! Why, girl, fancy cannot be bridled. Bless you, I wormed it out of him nearly a year ago."

"Oh, Mother, and you allowed it?"

"Well, I thought of you. I reasoned to myself, 'If he is foolish enough to be her slave for nothing, all the better for her. A lone woman is lost without a man about her to fetch and carry her little matters.' But now my mind has changed, and I believe the best use you can put him to is to marry him."

"So then, his own mother is against him, ready to wed me to the first comer? Ah, Gerard, thou hast but me. I will not believe thee dead until I see thy tomb, nor false until I see thee holding another lover's hand. Foolish boy, I shall never be civil to him again."

Afflicted by the busybody's misguided protection, Luke Peterson received a cold reception in the household where he had previously found gentleness and kindness. Soon, finding himself barely spoken to, and eventually addressed sharply and irritably, the naturally soft fellow began to whimper. He approached Margaret directly, asking if he had done something to offend her.

"Nothing. I am to blame. I am cursed. If you will take my counsel, keep out of my way for a while," she replied.

"It is all because of me, Luke," said the meddler. "You, Mistress Catherine... why, what have I done for you to set her against me?"

"Nay, I meant all for the best. I told her I saw you looking toward her through a wedding ring, but she would not hear of it."

"There was no need to tell her that, wife; she knows I have been courting her this whole year."

"Not I," said Margaret. "Or I should never have opened the street door to you."

"Why, I come here every Saturday night. And that is how the lads in Rotterdam court. If we sup with a lass on Saturdays, that is the wooing."

"Oh, that is Rotterdam, is it? Then next time you come, let it be Thursday or Friday. For my part, I thought you came after my puddings, boy."

"I like your puddings well enough. You make them better than mother does. But I like you still better than the puddings," Luke said tenderly.

"Then you have seen the last of them. How dare you speak thus to another man's wife, especially when he is far away?"

She concluded gently, yet with firm resolve: "You need not trouble yourself to come here any more, Luke. I can carry my basket myself."

"Oh, very well," said Luke. After sitting silently and stupefied for a moment, he rose and said sadly to Catherine, "Dame, I daresay I have got the sack;" and walked out.

However, the following Saturday, Catherine found him seated on the doorstep, blubbering uncontrollably. He explained that he had grown accustomed to coming there, and every other place seemed strangely alien. She went inside and informed Margaret, who sighed and remarked, "Poor Luke; he might have succeeded if he knew his place and treated her like a married wife."

When this sentiment was conveyed to Luke, he hesitated.

"Pshaw!" scoffed Catherine. "Promises are mere pie-crusts. Promise her the whole world sooner than sit outside like a fool. If you simply humored her in everything, and then, should poor Gerard fail to return and claim her, you would surely have her—in time. A lone woman is ever to be worn down, thou foolish boy."

Brother Clement had labored in Basel for over a year, teaching and preaching, when one day Jerome appeared before him. Dust clung to his robes, but his eyes burned with triumph. "Glory be to God, Brother Clement!" he declared. "Now you may journey to England with me."

"I am ready, Brother Jerome," Clement replied warmly. "And having anticipated your return for many months, I have spent my intervals between lessons and prayer studying the English tongue with close attention."

"Good thinking," Jerome acknowledged. He explained that he had delayed their departure only until he secured special faculties from the Pope: authority to collect funds for the Church in England and permission to hear confessions within all secular monasteries. "Now, gird up your loins," Jerome urged, his voice hardening. "Let us go forth and strike a decisive blow for the Church, and against the Franciscans."

Thus, the two friars set out, preaching their way down the Rhine toward England. In the larger cities, they shared the pulpit; in the smaller towns, they often split apart, taking opposite banks of the river to meet again at a designated hour. Both possessed the gift of the oratory, yet their styles diverged sharply. Jerome's speech was noble and imposing, though occasionally constrained in its theological scope and prone to a certain monotony in delivery. Clement, conversely, offered a torrent of variety, often slipping effortlessly into the vernacular. His tone flowed tenderly, curling around the listener's reason and heart. Yet, whenever his thoughts ascended, Clement soared far above Jerome, while Jerome remained grounded. In those moments of fervor, Clement became everything history records of inspired prophets and pagan orators alike: *vehement as a storm, excited as a rushing torrent, inflamed as lightning*—he thundered, flashed, and swept away all obstacles with the swift waves of his eloquence.

I could quote passages to prove this, but five reasons stand in my way: the difficulty of translation, the brevity of our time, the fact that I have already written elsewhere, the existence of a superior imitator, and the simple truth that while similarity is a virtue in peas, it is a vice in literature. However, to avoid evading the subject entirely, I shall note that Clement secretly prepared for every town he visited, researching local events and identifying the specific sins plaguing each community. Jerome, however, the unbending scholar, scorned such detours, refusing to tailor his message to the particular vices of any people.

At one great city, several leagues from the Rhine, they climbed the same steps to preach in turn. Jerome addressed the vanity of dress, a favorite topic of his. He spoke with biting satire and eloquence, and the crowd listened with complacent satisfaction, for vanity was not a common fault among them. Clement, meanwhile, attacked drunkenness—a besetting sin in these regions, shielded by popular prejudice and even practiced by the clergy themselves. He struck mercilessly with scripture and worldly evidence. He called it a crime, the breeding ground of theft and murder. He recounted a recent parricide committed by a man overcome by liquor, and described how a gang of drunkards had roasted one of their own companions alive in a neighboring village.

"You claim your late prince died of apoplexy," Clement shouted, his voice ringing clear. "But you know well he died of drink! Last month, one of your aldermen perished miserably, drowned in a puddle while utterly intoxicated! You strip your children's backs bare so you may gorge yourselves on the poison that turns you into the worst of beasts—silly as calves, yet fierce as boars. This drives your families into want and your souls into hell. I tell you, your town, yes, your entire nation, would sink to the bottom of humanity if your women drank as you do. How long will they remain temperate, defying nature to resist the example of their husbands and fathers? Vice never stands still. Amend yourselves, or watch them descend to your level. Already in Bohemia, women drink alongside the men. What does a drunken woman look like? Do you wish to see your wives and mothers reduced to such a state?"

A roar of horror erupted from the audience. Medieval listeners had not yet learned to sit silently during a stirring sermon.

"Aha! That strikes home!" the friar cried. "Do you suppose it shocks the All-Pure God less to see a man, His noblest creation, turned into a drunken beast, than it shocks you, creatures of sin and unreason, to see a woman degraded into something no better than yourselves?"

He concluded with two vivid tableaux: the chaotic ruin of a drunkard's household versus the peace of a sober man's home. So true and dramatic were his descriptions that the women began to exclaim, "Oh!" and "Ah!" and whispered, "By my faith, that is true!" The discourse caused absolute uproar. Hearers clustered in knots; the men grew indignant. Consequently, the women sided openly with them against the preacher. A husband had a right to a drop; he needed it to sustain the labor of his family. They refused to trade their sturdy men for milksops. "Double-faced hypocrites!" they muttered.

That very evening, a group of men intercepted Brother Clement near the market square. They filled his wallet with coins, plucked roses from their own heads to offer him, kissed his habit, and blessed him for undertaking to cure their "sots."

Jerome observed this scene with a frown. "Drunkenness is not heresy, Clement," he remarked coolly. "It is hardly fitting that a whole sermon should be devoted to it."

As they traveled onward, Jerome discovered to his astonishment that Clement's sermons sank deeper into the hearts of his hearers than his own. They made people listen, think, weep, and sometimes even reform. "He possesses the art of descending to their level," Jerome mused, watching the crowds react. "Yet, he can soar high enough at times."

On the whole, the sight puzzled Jerome, who harbored a secret sense of superiority over his tenderer brother. After traveling roughly two hundred miles, the situation began to displease him as much as it confused him. Yet, he strove to suppress this feeling as petty and unworthy. "Souls differ like locks," he reasoned aloud. "Preachers must differ like keys, or the Church will remain closed to God's passing. And certainly, this novice holds the key to these northern souls, being himself a northern man."

Thus, they continued their slow descent down the Rhine, sometimes drifting a few miles downstream, but mostly walking the banks, preaching, teaching, and hearing confessions in the towns and villages. Finally, they reached Düsseldorf.

There lay the small quay where Gerard and Denys had once boarded their boat upstream. The friars stepped onto the stone. The streets stretched out before them, and there stood "The Silver Lion." Nothing had changed in the city, save for him who walked through it barefoot, his heart calm and cold, his hands folded across his breast, his eyes bowed meekly to the ground—a true son of Dominic and the Holy Church.

"Eli," Catherine commanded, her voice cutting through the quiet of the room. "Answer me one question like a man, and I shall ask no more today. What is wormwood?"

Eli looked momentarily helpless under the sudden demand upon his wits, but he quickly rallied. "It is a herb that tastes most bitterly," he replied.

"That is a fair answer, my man, but not the one I seek," she countered sharply.

"Then tell it yourself."

"And shall I? Wormwood is—two people in the house doing nothing but waiting for your shoes and mine," Eli groaned, the weight of his own idleness pressing down on him.

The barb struck home. "I believe waiting for their best friend's coffin, combined with having nothing to do, is enough to make them far worse than nature intended," Catherine declared. "Why not set them up elsewhere, giving them a chance?"

"I am willing," Eli admitted, though fear clouded his resolve. "But I dread they will drink and gamble their very shelves away before they earn a penny."

"Nay," Catherine retorted, her eyes flashing. "Do you take me for a simpleton? Of course, I intend to watch them closely at the start, driving them with a loose rein, as the saying goes."

"But where did you think of going? Not here; we must divide our own custom."

"Not likely," she dismissed the idea firmly. "I say Rotterdam against the world. There I could start them."

Oh, self-deception! The true motive behind this bold plan was merely to draw herself nearer to little Gerard. Following many heated discussions and earnest promises of reform from Cornelis and Sybrandt on these specific terms, Catherine traveled to Rotterdam to hunt for a shop, taking Kate with her for a change of air.

They soon located a suitable space in a respectable street, though it was sadly out of order. Yet, the dilapidated state worked in their favor; they secured it at a bargain price. Instantly, they set to work, scrubbing the floors until they shone, fitting proper shelves for the business, and rendering the dwelling habitable once more.

Meanwhile, Luke Peterson was constantly asking Margaret what he could do to assist her. Her usual response came in a tone heavy with sorrow. "Nothing, Luke, nothing."

"What, you who are so clever? Can you think of nothing for me to do for you?" he pleaded.

"Nothing, Luke, nothing," she repeated, her heart breaking at his persistence.

Finally, however, she softened her reply. "If you could make something to help my sweet sister Kate about."

The slave of love consented joyfully. Soon, Luke had crafted a small cart, cushioned it with care, and even yoked himself into it. At eventide, he drew Kate out of the town and along the pleasant boulevard, while Margaret and Catherine walked beside them. To an outside observer, it appeared a happier party than reality warranted.

Kate, for one, enjoyed the moment keenly. Little Gerard was placed in her lap, and she doted on the child with such intensity that he seemed a cherub carried by a little angel, or a rosebud resting in the cup of a lily.

Yet, the vulgarity of the crowd followed them. They jeered, asking Luke how a thistle tasted and mocking whether his mistress could not afford a horse with four legs instead of a man. Luke paid no heed to their taunts, but Kate suffered deeply for him.

"Thou hast made the cart for me, good Luke," she said gently. "'Twas much. I did ill to let thee draw me too; we can afford to pay some poor soul for that service. I love my rides, and carrying little Gerard, but I would rather ride no more than thou be mocked for it."

"Much do I care for their tongues!" Luke shouted back, his pride flaring. "If I did care, I'd knock their heads together. I shall draw you until my mistress says 'give over.'"

"Luke, if you obey Kate, you will oblige me," Margaret interjected softly.

"Then I will obey Kate," Luke declared.

An honorable exception to the prevailing mockery was Jorian Ketel's wife, Joan. She watched the scene with a knowing, pitying gaze. "That is strength well laid out, to draw the weak," she remarked. "And her prayers will be your guerdon; she is not long for this world; she smileth in pain."

These were the words of Joan, spoken with prophetic clarity. Single-minded Luke, however, offered a different defense. "I do not want the poor lass's prayers," he insisted. "I do it to please my mistress, Margaret."

After that, Luke often pressed Margaret again to give him something substantial to do, without success. But one day, seemingly tired of his constant importuning, she turned on him. With a look and an accent she knew he would struggle to interpret correctly, she spoke three devastating words:

"Find me my boy's father."

"Mistress, they all say he is dead."

"Not so," Margaret replied, her voice trembling slightly. "They feed me still with hopes."

"Ay, to your face, but behind your back they all say he is dead."

At this revelation, Margaret's tears began to flow freely. Luke whimpered for company; he possessed the sturdy frame of a man but the tender, sensitive heart of a girl.

"Prithee, weep not so, sweet mistress," he pleaded. "I would bring him back to life if I could, rather than see thee weep so sore."

Margaret paused, composing herself. "I suppose I am weeping because they are so double-tongued with me," she admitted. Recovering her composure, she placed her hand firmly on his shoulder and spoke solemnly. "Luke, he is not dead. Dying men are known to have strange sights. Listen to me, Luke! My poor father, when he was dying—and I, a simple fool, was so happy thinking he would recover completely—sat in that very chair where you stand now. Mother was here, and I was yonder making a sleeve. Suddenly, he said to us, 'I see him!' I see him! Just so. Not like a failing man at all, but all on fire. 'Sore disfigured,' he said, 'on a great river, coming this way.'"

"Oh, Luke," she cried, her desperation rising, "if you were a woman, and had the feeling for me you claim to possess, you would pity me and find him for me. Think of it! The father of my child!"

"Alack, I would if I knew how," Luke sighed. "But how can I?"

"Nay, of course you cannot. I am mad to think it. But oh, if anyone really cared for me, they would; that is all I know."

Luke reflected in silence for a long while. "The old folk all say dying men can see more than living wights," he murmured. "Let me think... for my mind cannot gallop like yours. On a great river? Well, the Maas is a great river." He pondered deeply. "Coming this way? Then if it were the Maas, he would have been here by this time. So 'tis not the Maas. The Rhine is a great river, greater than the Maas, and very long. I think it will be the Rhine."

"And so do I, Luke," Margaret interjected eagerly. "For Denys bade him come down the Rhine. But even if it is, he may turn off before he reaches his birthplace. He does not pine for me as I for him; that is clear. Luke, do you not think he has deserted me?"

She waited for him to contradict her, but he answered gravely, "It looks very much like it. What a fool he must be!"

"What do we know?" Margaret objected imploringly.

"Let me think again," Luke said, shaking his head. "I cannot gallop."

The result of this meditation was decisive. He knew of a station about sixty miles up the Rhine where all the public boats docked. He would go there and try to intercept the truant. Though he did not even know Gerard by sight, his plan was formed: as each boat arrived, he would mingle with the passengers and ask if a man named Gerard was aboard.

"And, Mistress," Luke added seriously, "if you were to give me a letter to him? For, being strangers, maybe he won't believe a word I say."

"Good, kind, thoughtful Luke," Margaret said, blushing. "I will. (How I have undervalued thee!) But give me until supper-time to write it."

At supper, she handed him a long letter, tied with silk in the fashion of the day and sealed over the knot. Luke weighed it in his hand, a shadow of discontent crossing his features. He addressed her very gravely, "Say your father was not dreaming, and say I have the luck to fall in with this man. And say that if he turns out to be a better sort of fellow than I think him, and comes home to you then and there... what becomes of me?"

Margaret flushed right to her brow. "Oh, Luke, Heaven will reward thee! I shall fall on my knees and bless thee; and I shall love thee all my days, sweet Luke, as a mother loves her son. I am so old by thee; trouble ages the heart. Thou shalt not go—it is not fair of me. Love makes us selfish."

"Humph!" Luke grunted.

"And if," he resumed, maintaining his serious tone, "that scapegrace reads thy letter and hears me tell him how thou pinest for him, yet, being a traitor or a mere idiot, refuses to turn to thee... what shall become of me then? Must I die a bachelor, and thou fare lonely to thy grave, neither maid, wife, nor widow?"

Margaret panted with fear and emotion at this terrible display of common sense and the plain question that followed. Finally, she faltered, "If—which our Lady be merciful to me and forbid—Oh!"

"Well, Mistress?"

"If he should read my letter and hear thy words—and, sweet Luke, be just enough to tell him what a lovely babe he hath, fatherless, fatherless. Oh, Luke, can he be so cruel?"

"I trow not but if?"

"Then he will give thee up my marriage lines, and I shall be an honest woman, and a wretched one, and my boy will not be a bastard. Of course, then we could both go into any honest man's house that would be troubled with us. And even for thy goodness this day, I will—I will—never be so ungrateful as to go past thy door to another man's."

"Ay, but will you come in at mine? Answer me that!"

"Oh, ask me not! Some day, perhaps, when my wounds stop bleeding. Alas, I'll try. If I don't fling myself and my child into the Maas. Do not go, Luke! Do not think of going! 'Tis all madness from first to last."

But Luke was as slow to forego an idea as to form one. His reply showed how quickly love was forging a man out of him.

"Well," he said, "madness is something, anyway. And I am tired of doing nothing for thee. I am no great talker. Tomorrow, at peep of day, I start. But hold, I have no money. My mother takes care of all mine, and I never see it again."

Then Margaret took out Catherine's gold angel, which had escaped detection so often, and gave it to Luke. He set out on his mad errand. It did not, however, seem so mad to him as it did to us. In a superstitious age, Luke acted on the dying man's dream, vision, or illusion much as we would act on respectable information.

Catherine, however, was downright angry when she heard of it. "To send the poor lad on such a wild-goose chase!" she exclaimed. "But you are like many more girls; mark my words: by the time you have worn that Luke fairly out and made him as sick of you as a dog, you will turn as fond on him as a cow on a calf, and 'Too late' will be the cry."

Meanwhile, the two friars reached Holland from the south just twelve hours after Luke had started up the Rhine. Thus, whether it was a wild-goose chase or not, the parties were nearing each other, and rapidly too. For Jerome, unable to preach in Low Dutch, now pushed on toward the coast, anxious to reach England as soon as possible. Having the stream with them, the friars would, in point of fact, have missed Luke entirely by passing him in full stream below his intended station, but for the incident I am about to relate.

About twenty miles above the station Luke was making for, Clement landed to preach in a large village. Towards the end of his sermon, he noticed a grey nun weeping. He spoke to her kindly and asked what her grief was.

"Nay," she said, "'tis not for myself these tears flow; 'tis for my lost friend. Thy words reminded me of what she was, and what she is, poor wretch."

"But you are a Dominican, and I am a Franciscan nun," Clement noted.

"It matters little, my sister, if we are both Christians, and if I can aid thee in aught."

The nun looked into his face and said, "These are strange words, but methinks they are good; and thy lips are oh, most eloquent. I will tell thee our grief."

She revealed that a young nun, the darling of the convent and her bosom friend, had been lured away from her vows. After various degradations of sin, she was actually living in a small inn as a chambermaid, in reality serving as a decoy, known to be selling her favors to wealthier customers.

"Anywhere else we might, by kindly violence, force her away from perdition," the nun explained. "But this innkeeper is the servant of the fierce baron on the height there, and holds his ear still. He would burn our convent to the ground were we to take her by force."

"Moreover, souls will not be saved by brute force," Clement stated.

While they talked

Waiting for an earnest letter rarely leaves the mind unchanged. When Margaret finally received hers, she poured out her energy and her faith—whether justified by her dying father's vision or merely his delusion. Once the letter was read and Luke departed, she recoiled in horror at her own credulity. Her conscience pricked sharply regarding Luke, and shortly after, Catherine arrived to scold her. For paying the price of false hopes and elevated spirits, Margaret was forced to sink into a depth of despondency even darker than before.

She was discovered in this state by a steadfast friend she had recently made, Joan Ketel. This good woman entered radiating with an idea. "Margaret, I know the cure for your illness!" Joan exclaimed. "There is a hermit in Gouda, a wondrous holy man. In his mood, he can foretell what is coming."

"I have heard of him," Margaret replied hopelessly.

With some difficulty, Joan persuaded her to walk as far as Gouda to consult the hermit. They carried a basket containing butter and eggs and made their way to his cave. How had these two become such close companions? Six weeks prior, Jorian had fallen violently ill with a bowel disease. It began with raging pain; when that subsided, leaving him weak, a terrible symptom followed: neither liquid nor solid food could remain in his stomach for a single minute.

The doctor declared, "If this continues for many hours, he must die. Therefore, boil a chicken with a golden angel floating in the water, and let him eat that!" Alas, the gilt-chicken broth shared the fate of the simpler dishes that preceded it. Next, the healer soaked the thumb of Saint Sergius in beef broth, yielding the same result. Desperate, Joan ran weeping to Margaret to borrow linen for a shroud.

"Let me see him," Margaret commanded.

She entered the room, felt his pulse, and gasped, "Ah! You have not struck at the root. Open the window! You are stifling him. Change all his linens immediately."

"Alack, woman, why?" the medieval wife protested. "Why soil more linen for a dying man?"

"Do as I bid you," Margaret said dully, turning away.

Joan, compelled by force of habit, obeyed. Margaret returned with her apron full of a flowering herb. She prepared a decoction and brought it to the bedside. Before administering it, she took a spoonful herself, smacking her lips hypocritically.

"That is fair," Jorian managed with a feeble attempt at humor. "First it tastes sweet, now bitter."

Once he had swallowed the mixture, she engaged him in conversation. This "bitter-sweet" tonic became his constant companion. Observing its effect, she built upon it like cards stacked high: she mixed a drop of Schiedam gin into the third spoonful and a bit of beaten egg yolk into the seventh. With the patience characteristic of her sex, she coaxed his body back from Death's grasp. Finally, Nature, having been patted rather than kicked under the bed, restored Jorian Ketel to his feet. Yet, the doctress made them both swear eternal silence on her guilty deed. "They would imprison me, separating me from my child," she whispered.

The simple remedy that saved Jorian was known as sweet feverfew, which Margaret had gathered from his own garden. Her keen eyesight had spotted it growing through his window.

Thus, Margaret and Joan arrived at the hermit's cave and placed their offering of butter and eggs on the small stone platform. Margaret pressed her mouth to the aperture designed for speaking and prayed aloud: "Holy hermit, we bring thee the finest butter and eggs. I, a poor deserted girl, technically a wife though no longer one, and mother of the sweetest babe, come to beg you tell me: Is he quick or dead? True to his vows or false?"

A faint, rasping voice drifted from within the cave: "Trouble me not with earthly matters. Send me a holy friar; I am dying."

"Alas!" cried Margaret. "Is it truly so, poor soul? Then let us enter to help you."

"Saints forbid! That is a woman's voice. Send me a holy friar."

They turned and walked back as they had come. Joan could not suppress her indignation. "Are women imps of darkness, then, that we cannot approach a dying bed?"

But Margaret was too deeply dejected to respond. Joan offered rough consolation, which was ignored until she spoke again: "And Jorian will speak out soon enough. He is just on the boil. He is very grateful to you, believe it."

"Seeing is believing," Margaret replied with quiet bitterness.

"Not that he thinks you might have saved him with something more extraordinary than that," Joan continued. "'A man of my size cured with feverfew?' he says. 'Why, if there is such a sorry herb...' He was even thinking of pulling up all his feverfew. I told him remedies aren't better because they are rare. You and the feverfew cured him when the grand medicines failed completely. So I said, 'Go down on your four bones to the feverfew.' But indeed, at bottom, he is grateful. You are his only thought and his only talk. However, he sees Gerard's people coming around, good friends... and he said only last night—"

"Well?"

"He made me vow not to tell you."

"Prithee, tell me."

"Well, he said: 'If I reveal what little I know, it won't bring him back, and it will set everyone by the ears. I wish I had more sense,' he muttered. 'I am sorely perplexed. But least said is soonest mended.' That is his favorite phrase; he returns to it from a mile away."

Margaret shook her head. "Yes, we are wading in deep waters, my poor babe and I."

It was Saturday night, and there was no sign of Luke.

"Poor Luke!" Margaret murmured. "It was very kind of him to go on such an errand."

"He is one out of a hundred," Catherine responded warmly. "Mother, do you think he would be kind to little Gerard?"

"I am sure he would. So please be kinder to him when he returns! Will you promise?"

"Yes."

***

Brother Clement, guided by the nuns, navigated a bend in the river, striding vigorously toward a station miles closer to the coast than where Luke was lying in wait for Gerard Eliassoen. The following morning, he started early and arrived in Rotterdam by noon. He headed straight for the port, eager not to keep Jerome waiting.

He observed several monks of his order on the quay and approached them, but Jerome was nowhere to be seen. He asked one if Jerome had arrived.

"Surely, brother," the monk replied.

"Prithee, where is he?"

"Where? Why, there!" the monk pointed to a ship setting sail. Clement noticed that all the other monks were gazing seaward.

"What? Gone without me! Oh, Jerome! Jerome!" he cried in a voice of anguish.

Several friars turned to stare.

"You must be Brother Clement," one said eventually. On hearing this, they kissed him and greeted him with warm brotherly affection, handing him a letter Jerome had entrusted to them. It was a hasty scrawl. The writer informed him coldly that a ship was about to depart for England and that he was loath to waste time. He noted that Clement might follow if he wished, but advised him to stay behind and preach to his own countrymen.

"Give glory to God, brother. You possess a wonderful power over Dutch hearts, but you are no match for those haughty islanders; you are too tender."

"Know that on my journey, I met someone who asked for you under the name you bore in the world. Be on your guard! Do not let the world catch you again in any silken net. Remember: Solitude, Fasting, and Prayer are the sword, spear, and shield of the soul. Farewell."

Clement was deeply shocked and mortified by this contemptuous abandonment and the icy tone of the message. He promised the good monks he would sleep at the convent and preach wherever the prior appointed, for Jerome had once raised him to the skies as a preacher. Now, stung to the quick, he withdrew abruptly, needing solitude.

He questioned himself: Was there some incurable fault in him, repulsive to such a true son of Dominic? Or was Jerome himself devoid of the Christian Love that St. Paul held above faith itself? Shipwrecked together, saved on the same fragment of wreckage—his pupil, his penitent, his son in the Church—and now, for four hundred miles, his fellow traveler in Christ, only to be shaken off like dirt at the first opportunity with harsh, cold disdain.

"Why are worldly hearts no colder nor less untrustworthy than this," he lamented. "The only one who ever truly loved me lies in a grave nearby. Fly me, fly to England, man born without a heart. I shall go and pray over a grave at Sevenbergen."

Three hours later, he passed Peter's cottage. A troop of noisy children played near the door, and the house had been repaired, a new outhouse added. He turned his head hastily away, unwilling to disturb a picture cherished in his memory, and proceeded to the churchyard.

He searched among the tombstones for Margaret's but could not find it. Unable

The following day, a Sunday brimming with activity, saw Catherine's household in the Hoog Straet buzzing with purpose. The shop was finally prepared, and Cornelis and Sybrandt were set to open their doors the morrow; their names were already painted boldly above the entrance, accompanied by their distinctive sign: a white lamb suckling a gilded sheep. Eli had arrived, bringing fresh stock from his own stores to ensure they began with a solid foundation. The parents' hearts burned with pride at their enterprise, and the couple strolled together through the garden, united in their resolve. They confessed a weariness for their former existence, agreeing that earning wealth held a far greater charm than squandering it. With vows spoken as firm as cooling wax, they pledged to remain steadfast in commerce.

Catherine's sharp, ever-watchful ear caught this declaration drifting through an open window. She relayed the news to Eli: the evening supper would feature Margaret and her son, serving as a sort of inauguration feast. Here, seasoned wisdom regarding trade would flow from the elders, paired with generous libations to toast the successful transition of these converts from agriculture—a path often yielding wild oats—to the lucrative fields of commerce.

Margaret had joined her mother-in-law, eager to assist and perhaps shake off the deep lethargy that had lately weighed upon her spirit; indeed, both women's cheeks flushed with the heat of exertion and anticipation. Soon, Joan entered carrying a crisp salad harvested from Jorian's garden. "He cut it specifically for you, Margaret," Joan remarked warmly. "You are his constant topic; I fear I shall grow jealous. I did tell him you were hosting a feast today. But, alas, lass, what a sermon was delivered in the new church! Did you hear preaching? I never witnessed it until this very day."

"Would that I had been present," Margaret sighed, her voice tinged with longing. "For I feel utterly parched, lacking the dew of heaven."

"Why, he preaches again this afternoon," Joan noted. "Though perhaps you are needed here?"

"Not she," Catherine interjected firmly. "Go forth if you are inclined."

"Indeed," Margaret replied, hesitating. "Yet, methinks I would dampen the spirits less if I could attend, warmed by the inspiration of a fine sermon."

"Then you must move swiftly," Joan advised. "Observe how the congregation drifts that way. By my soul, there goes the holy friar!" The energetic woman bounded from the room, crossed the street, and intercepted the friar before returning promptly. "There, I was determined to catch him close at hand."

"What did he say to you?"

"He declared, 'My daughter, I shall visit him ere sunset, God willing,'" Joan recounted, her tone softening. "His voice was sweeter than honey. Yet, oh, my ladies, look at those thin cheeks on a young man, and those large eyes—so much like yours, Margaret."

"I have a strong desire to hear him," Margaret admitted. "But my cap is not quite clean, and everyone will be wearing their pristine white head coverings."

"There, take my handkerchief from the basket," Catherine instructed gently. "You cannot keep the child; I require him for my poor Kate. She is having one of her difficult days."

Margaret ascended the stairs, leaving the toddler behind. She found Kate lying in bed. "How dost thou, sweetheart? Nay, I need not inquire. Thou art in grievous pain, yet thou smilest so brightly."

She placed something in the girl's lap. "Here is one thou lovest."

"Two, by my count," Kate answered with an ethereal smile. Suddenly, a spasm seized her, a convulsion that might have caused onlookers to roar like bulls.

"In your lap?" Margaret asked, responding to the girl's gesture.

"Nay, he is too heavy, and thou art in such agony," Kate murmured.

"I love him too dearly to feel his weight," the patient replied softly.

Seizing the moment, Margaret attended to her own preparations. "I am bound for the church," she announced, "to listen to a magnificent preacher."

Kate sighed deeply. "A short while ago, I too was eager to depart. Such is my state for the past month; up and down, up and down, like the waves of the Zuyder Zee. I would rather stay beside thee; but say the word!"

"Nay," Kate insisted, her voice trembling slightly. "Pray, go. Bring me back every single word. Alas, that I cannot go myself." Tears welled in the sufferer's eyes.

This plea resolved Margaret. She kissed Kate tenderly, glanced beneath her eyelashes at the sleeping boy, and let out a small, resigned sigh. "I believe I dare not," she whispered. "I could never kiss him lightly; nor could my father bear to wake a child by day or night. If it is thy wish to rouse him, speak to Aunt Kate the two new words thou hast learned."

With a final, loving glance over her shoulder, she exited, closing the door without a sound.

"Joan, lend me a hand to peel these vegetables," Catherine called out.

"That I shall, mistress," Joan responded immediately.

Cooking resumed with silent, vigorous efficiency. "Now, Joan, remember this rule: those who aid me in cooking and serving the meat shall also share in eating it."

"There are harsher laws in Holland than that," Joan quipped cheerfully. "Thy will is my delight, mistress. Besides, my Luke has missed his supper due to bad luck; he is digging today, though fortune smiles upon him now."

Margaret descended the stairs. "Eh, woman, that is a grim trade," Catherine observed, snapping her fingers toward Rotterdam in admiration. "Look at her! She has merely washed her face and arranged her hair, and now who resembles her? Truly, Rotterdam is the place for you!"

"Give us a kiss, hussy!" Catherine commanded playfully. "Mind now, Eli will not wait for the Duke to finish his sermon. Do not linger once the service concludes."

Joan and Catherine followed Margaret to the doorway, standing there and watching her walk steadily down the street. Amongst the daily routines of homely housewives, a departure holds half the significance of an event itself.

"There, Joan," Catherine mused as they watched her go. "It feels almost as though I had just sent my own daughter to church and stood watching her leave, which I have done countless times. Joan, lass, she will not hear a word against our Gerard. And since he lives, he has treated her cruelly—that is why my heart aches for the poor wretch. I am older and wiser than she. I shall wed her to that simple Luke, and thus, an end to it. What is one grandchild more?"

Margaret stepped into the vast, unfinished cathedral of Saint Laurence just as the sermon began. It was a monumental structure, far from completion; such edifices took years, not seasons, to rise. While the side aisles were roofed, the central nave and the chancel remained open to the sky. The stone pillars and arches stood nearly finished, some freshly whitewashed, yet only a single window in the entire building was glazed. The rest were gaping, jagged wounds in the masonry.

But today, these raw imperfections rendered the church breathtakingly beautiful. It was a glorious summer afternoon, and sunlight poured through the irregular openings in dazzling, fractured beams. These rays danced across the rough surfaces, casting rivers of golden glory into the cool, dark shadows of the side aisles while illuminating the white pillars with a blinding radiance. The great central aisle was checkered with shifting patterns of light and shade—the shadows appearing deceptively cool and soothing, while the lights shimmered like patches of amber diamonds animated by divine fire. Above, the azure sky vaulted the lofty space, feeling impossibly close. The white bonnets of the congregation formed a sea of purity against the vivid blue dome. Though the main aisle was packed to capacity, the silence was absolute. The preacher's words and his mellow, earnest voice held the multitude spellbound.

Margaret stood transfixed by the sheer beauty and the profound devotion of the scene—a "great calm" that settled over her soul. Seeking shelter, she slipped behind a pillar in the north aisle. Even without hearing every syllable, a sweet, languorous peace washed over her at the sight of the place and the melody of the voice. It felt as though balm trickled over the waves within her heart, smoothing them until they stilled. Leaning against the stone, her eyes half-closed, everything seemed soft and dreamlike. Being there felt right.

Soon, she noticed a woman vacate a prime spot directly under a sunbeam, seeking relief from the heat. Margaret moved quickly and silently around the pillar, securing the empty seat. Now positioned beside a pillar in the south aisle, less than fifty feet from the pulpit, she was practically at the preacher's side, slightly behind him, able to catch every word clearly.

Her concentration broke, however, when she spotted the shadow of a man's head and shoulders bobbing up and down with comical rhythm nearby. She struggled to suppress a smile, realizing too late that it wasn't a joke at all. It was the sexton, diligently digging. Looking through the open window toward the source of the shadow, she confirmed it was Jorian Ketel working among the foundations.

As she watched Jorian, a specific tone of the preacher's voice struck her ear with such distinct force that it seemed to physically vibrate her very bones. Her hand flew instinctively to her bosom, startled by the sudden intensity of the sensation. Turning sharply, she sought the source, but the preacher's back was turned to her; all she could see was the crown of his tonsured head. She sighed, frustrated that the visual confirmation was absent despite the overwhelming auditory impact. Still, she leaned forward, eyes cast downward, waiting for that particular inflection to return. It did not come immediately, but the voice continued to grow upon her, rising and falling as the speaker warmed to his theme. It awakened faint echoes of a thousand happy memories, filling her with a melancholy pleasure she refused to dispel by looking away.

Suddenly, in the midst of an eloquent passage, the preacher halted. Margaret almost sighed aloud; the soothing music had ceased abruptly. Was the sermon over? She glanced around, but the congregation remained seated, unmoving. Then, she sensed eyes upon her. Many faces seemed to turn toward her.

She spun around sharply, expecting someone standing behind her, but the space was empty. Nearby, startled countenances fixed themselves on the preacher. Following their gaze, Margaret froze. In the pulpit, a face stared back at her with the blank horror of a corpse. The friar's eyes, naturally large and set wide by the hollows of his cheeks, were dilated to a supernatural size, glaring directly at her from a bloodless visage.

Margaret cringed, turning fearfully in circles, convinced some terrible specter stood behind her. There was nothing. She was merely part of the listening crowd, an outsider figure in the chaos. Just then, the church erupted into commotion. People surged upward, craning necks forward; hundreds of agitated faces swung between the friar and Margaret. The rapid turning of caps created a deafening rustle. Shrieks from nervous women and buzzing murmurs from men filled the air. Terrified by the weight of so many leveled stares, Margaret shrank back behind her pillar, stealing one frantic, hurried glance at the preacher before burying herself in the crowd.

That fleeting glimpse froze her blood. On the stricken face of the friar, she had seen an expression that made her shudder from head to toe. She turned pale and collapsed onto a pile of wood chips left by the workmen, covering her face with her hands.

When the sermon resumed, she heard the sounds but grasped none of the meaning. Her mind spun in a vortex of terror, fixated on a single image: the look stamped upon that prodigy-stricken face. It was *recognition*. The memory of that gaze sent fresh tremors coursing through her body.

Finally, the sermon concluded with a strain of feverish, exalted eloquence that drove the crowd to forget the preacher's bizarre pause and ghastly stare. Margaret hurriedly merged with the throng and exited the church. As the others dispersed toward their homes, she paused at the threshold and turned back into the churchyard, walking straight to Peter's grave. Despite her poverty, she had commissioned a slab and a headstone for him. Sitting upon the cold stone, she pressed a kiss to it, then pulled her apron over her head to hide her hair from any passersby.

"Father," she whispered to the silent earth, "you have often heard me say I am wading in deep waters, but now I believe God alone knows the depth. I will follow that friar around the world, but I shall keep him at arm's length. He must tell me why he looked toward me like a dead man waking from the grave—and not a soul stood behind me. Oh, Father, you often praised me here; please speak a word for me there. For truly, I am wading in deep waters."

From her position on the tomb, which offered a clear view of the church door, she waited, face hidden, to intercept the holy preacher.

The cool church, checkered with shafts of sunlight and crowned with a canopy of heavenly purple, soothed and charmed Father Clement as it had done Margaret long ago. More than mere comfort, however, it carried his mind directly to the Creator of all such pure delights. As his gaze swept across the great aisle, he saw it crammed with his country folk—a thousand snowy caps filigreed with gold. Though many hundreds of leagues separated him from home, he had seen nothing comparable to this multitude save perhaps a winter landscape blanketed in snow.

In the morning, he had thundered with judgment; yet this sweet afternoon seemed entirely out of tune with threats. His heart yearned over the crowd below him. He realized he must tell them of God's love; alas, poor souls, they heard almost as little of it from the pulpit in those days as the heathen did. So, he spoke the glad tidings of salvation. The people hung upon his gentle, earnest tongue. He was not one of those preachers who gyrate in the pulpit like a weathercock atop a steeple; rather, he moved the hearts of others far more than his own body allowed him to move. Yet, he did not entirely neglect those who stood in need.

Warm with this theme, determined that none of the multitude should miss the joyful news of Christ's love, he turned toward the south aisle. There, in a brilliant stream of sunshine pouring through the window, stood the radiant face of Margaret Brandt. He gazed at it without immediate emotion; it simply numbed him, soul and body alike. Soon, however, the words died in his throat, and he trembled as he stared.

There, with her auburn hair bathed in the beams and glittering like the halo of a saint, her face glowing doubly—once with its own inherent beauty and twice with the light that framed it—stood his dead love. She leaned lightly against a white column, listening with tender, downcast lashes. He had seen her listen to him so a hundred times before. There was no change in her. This was the blooming Margaret he had left, merely a shade riper and more lovely.

He started back with monstrous eyes and bloodless cheeks. The congregation faded from his sight. He heard, as in a dream, a rustling and rising throughout the church, yet he could not tear his prodigy-stricken eyes away from that face—all life, bloom, beauty, and that wondrous auburn hair glistening gloriously in the sun. He gazed, convinced she must vanish like a mirage. She remained.

Suddenly, she looked at him, fully. Her own violet eyes! At this, he was beside himself, his lips parting to shriek her name. But she turned her head swiftly, and moments later, she vanished. Not without one final glance, however—one rapid as lightning that met his own, leaving her cowering and quivering with her mind in a whirl, and him panting, gripping the pulpit convulsively. That glance, though lacking explicit recognition, was the startled, inquiring, nameless look that precedes it.

Making a mighty effort, he muttered something unintelligible, then feebly resumed his discourse, stammering and babbling for a while. Gradually, forcing himself to view her presence as a vision from another world since she was now gone, he rose into a state of unnatural excitement. He concluded his sermon in a style of eloquence that electrified the simple minds present, bordering on rhapsody.

When the service ended, he sat down on the pulpit stool, terribly shaken. Presently, an idea very characteristic of the age took possession of him. He had sought her grave at Sevenbergen in vain. Now, she had been permitted to appear to him, showing that she was buried here—probably hard by that very pillar where her spirit had manifested. Once adopted, this idea settled on his mind with the certainty of fact. He felt he needed only to speak to the sexton—who, to his great disgust, he had seen working during the sermon—to learn the exact spot where she lay.

The church was now quite empty. Climbing down from the pulpit, Father Clement stepped through an aperture in the south wall onto the grass and approached the sexton. He recognized him instantly, though Jorian never suspected the poor lad whose life he had saved was hidden beneath the guise of this holy friar. The loss of his shapely beard had wonderfully altered the outline of Jorian's face. Combined with his tonsure, his short hair sprinkled with premature grey, and cheeks thinned and paled by fasts and vigils, the transformation was complete.

"My son," said Friar Clement softly, "if you keep any memory of those whom you lay in the earth, pray tell me: is any Christian buried inside the church, near one of the pillars?"

"Nay, father," replied Jorian. "Here in the churchyard lie buried all that are buried within. Why?"

"No matter. Pray tell me then where lieth Margaret Brandt."

"Margaret Brandt?" Jorian stared stupidly at the speaker. "She died about three years ago and was buried here."

"Oh, that is another matter," said Jorian. "That was before my time; the vicar could tell you, likely, if so be she was a gentlewoman, or at least rich enough to pay him his fee."

"Alas, my son, she was poor—and paid a heavy penalty for it—but born of decent folk. Her father, Peter, was a learned physician; she came hither from Sevenbergen... to die."

When Clement uttered these words, his head sank upon his breast, and he seemed to possess neither the power nor the wish to question Jorian further. One doubts even if he knew where he stood; he was utterly lost in the past.

Jorian put down his spade, standing upright in the grave with his arms akimbo, saying sulkily, "Are you making a fool of me, holy sir, or has some wag been making a fool of you!" Having thus relieved his mind, he proceeded to dig again with a vigor that showed his somewhat irritable temper was ruffled.

Clement gazed at him with a puzzled but gently reproachful eye, for the tone was rude and the words unintelligible. Good-natured though crusty, Jorian had not thrown up three spadefuls before he became ashamed of himself.

"Why, what a base churl am I to speak thus to thee, holy father? And thou standest there looking at me like a lamb." Aha! I have it; 'tis Peter Brandt's grave you would fain see, not Margaret's. He does lie here, hard by the west door. There; I'll show you."

And he laid down his spade, putting on his doublet and jerkin to accompany the friar. He did not know there was anyone sitting on Peter's tomb. Still less did he know she was watching for this holy friar. Neither Pietro Vanucci nor Andrea recognized him without his beard. Indeed, the fact remains that a beard which has never known a razor grows in a very picturesque and characteristic form, becoming a defining feature of the face; so that its removal may, in some cases, serve as an effectual disguise.

As Jorian donned his doublet and jerkin, preparing to depart for Peter's tomb, his tongue remained ceaselessly active. "They used to call him a magician out toward Sevenbergen," he began. "And they say he bestowed a final touch of his craft upon them before leaving. He claimed to see Margaret's son descending the Rhine in splendid attire, laden with wealth, yet his face marred by a foreign blade, lacking neither entirely his arms nor his legs. But, dear heart, nothing came of it.
