Margaret still languishes for her boy; and Peter lies as silent as his neighbors—though she has placed a stone slab over him to keep him exactly where he rests, as you shall soon witness."

He pressed both hands against the grave's edge, poised to rise, when the friar laid a trembling hand upon his shoulder, whispering strangely, "How long since Peter Brandt passed?"

"About two months. Why ask?"

"And his daughter buried him, you say?"

"Nay, I performed the burial rites, though she paid the fee and commissioned the stone."

"Then—but he had but one daughter, Margaret?"

"No more, at least, that he acknowledged."

"Then you believe Margaret is... is alive?"

"Believe? Why, I should be dead otherwise. Puzzle that out for yourself."

"Alas, how can I? You love her!"

"Not more than reason demands, being a married man and father of four sturdy lads like myself. No, the truth is, she saved my life scarcely six weeks past. Had she been dead, she could not have sustained me. Bless your heart, I could barely keep food in my stomach; physicians could do naught for me. My Joan declared, 'It is time to purchase a shroud for thee.' 'I dare say so,' I replied, 'yet try borrowing one first.' Enter my lady, this Margaret—who, by your account, died three years ago—opens the windows, shifts me from my bed, and cures me in the blink of an eye. With what? Prickles the shoe; with the vilest herb, plucked from my own garden, sweet feverfew. An herb, quotha, 'tis a weed; leastways, it was a weed until it healed me. Now, whenever I pass my hunch, I lower my cap and cry, 'Fly service to thee!' Why, Father, why do you grow so pale, then so red, then so white? What troubles you? What is the matter?"

"The surprise—the joy—the wonder—the fear," gasped Clement.

"Why does it concern thee? Are you of kin to Margaret Brandt?"

"Nay; but I knew one who loved her dearly, so deeply her death nearly destroyed him, body and soul. Yet you claim she lives. And I begin to believe you."

Jorian stared, and after a prolonged silence, spoke very gravely, "Father, you have asked many questions, and I have answered them truthfully. Now, for our Lady's sake, answer me but two. Did you truly know one who loved this poor maiden? Where?"

Clement stood on the brink of revelation but recalled Jerome's letter and recoiled from using the name he had once borne in the world. "I knew him in Italy," he said.

"If you knew him, you can tell me his name," Jorian urged cautiously.

"His name was Gerard Eliassoen."

"Oh, but this is strange. Wait, what led you to say Margaret Brandt was dead?"

"I was with Gerard when a letter arrived from Margaret Van Eyck. It informed him that the one he loved was dead and buried. Let me sit down, for my strength fails me. Foul play! Foul play!"

"Father," Jorian interjected, "I thank Heaven for sending you to me. Yes, sit; you look like a specter. You fast too much to remain strong. My mind misgives me; I seem to hold the key to this riddle. If I do, there are two knaves in this town whose heads I would gladly crush as easily as this mold." He clenched his teeth and hoisted his long spade high, bringing it down furiously upon the earth several times.

"Foul play? You have never spoken a truer word in your life. If you know where Gerard is now, lose no time in showing him the trap they have set. Mine is but a dull head, but sometimes the slow hound discerns the scent. Go on. I believe you and I have seized two ends of the same stick, and a most foul one at that."

After some typical preliminary chatter common among men of his station, Jorian came directly to the crux of the matter. He had been hired by the burgomaster of Tergou to repair the floor of an upper chamber. As he finished and gathered his tools, curiosity sparked by loud voices below. He lay prone on the floor and overheard the burgomaster discussing a letter Cornelis and Sybrandt intended to deliver to replace one that Hans Memling was carrying to Gerard. "'Their will was good, but their stomach was small,'" the old man had remarked. "'To give them courage, I showed them a drawer full of silver, and if they complete the task, they may take as much as fits in their grasp.'"

"Well, Father," Jorian continued, "I paid little heed then, save for the bargain itself, which kept me awake most of the night. Consider this: next morning, at dawn, who should I see emerging from their homes but Cornelis and Sybrandt, each sporting a black eye? 'Oho,' I muttered, 'what mark has Hans left upon you? Well, I hope that is all you gained for your pains.' Did they not rush straight to the burgomaster's house? I hid myself."

At this revelation, the monk's nostrils flared, and his restless eyes betrayed intense suspense.

"Well, Father," Jorian proceeded, "the burgomaster summoned them to that very room. He held a letter in his hand. Though I am no scholar, I possess as many eyes as the Pope, and I saw the drawer open. Those two rogues plunged a hand in each, drawing it out brimming. Saints in glory, how they struggled to fit more and more of that stuff! Sybrandt had smeared his hand in something sticky—I think glue—and managed to carry one or two pieces away adhering to his palm. Ha, ha, ha! 'Tis a sin to laugh. See, fortune favored the wrong side as usual; they completed the trick, yet how they did it remains unknown until Doomsday. Off they went, leaving their immortal treasures in that drawer. They received a handful of silver; the Devil won that bargain. There, Father, that clears my mind. Often I longed to confide in someone, but dared not speak to the women, lest Margaret lose her sole friend in the world. Those two black-hearted villains are the favorites. 'Tis always so. Have not the elders recently secured a grand new shop for them right here in this town, on Hoog Straet? There you may see their sign: a gilt sheep and a lambkin. A pair of wolves suckling their dam would be closer to the mark. There the entire family feasts today; oh, 'tis a fine world. Not a word, holy father? You sit like stone, offering not even a curse upon them, the stony-hearted wretches. Was it not enough that the poor lad stood alone in a strange land? Must his own flesh and blood go and bury the one blessing his enemies left him? And consider her pining, pining all these years, seated at the window gazing down the street for Gerard! So constant, so tender, so true: my wife insists no woman ever loved a man truer than she loves the boy those villains tore from her side. Not a single day passes without her weeping salt tears for him. And when I reflect that, but for those two greedy, lying knaves, that winsome lad—whose life I saved—might be by her side today, the happiest man in Holland; and that sweet girl, who saved my life, might be resting her cheek upon her sweetheart's shoulder, the happiest woman in Holland instead of the saddest; oh, I thirst for their blood! The nasty, sneaking, lying, cheating, cowardly, heartless, bowels-less—how now?"

The monk leaped up wildly, his face livid with fury and despair, rushing headlong from the spot with both hands clenched aloft. Such was the terrifying, inarticulate eruption of rage that Jorian's petty anger instantly evaporated at the sight. He stood transfixed, dismayed, watching the human tempest he had unleashed.

While lost in this reverie, he felt his arm grasped by a small, trembling hand. It was Margaret Brandt. He started; her presence there seemed utterly inexplicable. She had waited long upon Peter's tombstone, but the friar never appeared. She entered the church to search for him but found him absent. Soon, ascending the south aisle, the gigantic shadow of a friar swept rapidly across the floor and vanished behind a pillar, seeming to pass right through her. She nearly screamed, but remembering Jorian's shadow had emerged so abruptly from the churchyard, she attempted to exit via the nearest path. She succeeded, albeit with difficulty, by which time Clement was just disappearing down the street. Yet, so expressive can the body be as well as the face, she perceived he was greatly agitated.

Jorian and Margaret exchanged glances, their eyes

Supper was being served. Eli's family gathered around the long table, save for one: Margaret was absent. To Catherine's astonishment, Eli declared he would wait for her return. "But I told her you wouldn't wait for the Duke," someone remarked.

"She is no longer the Duke," Eli replied firmly. "She is a poor, good girl who has waited not mere minutes, but years, for a graceless son of mine. Set the food down anyway; once she arrives, we shall eat without further delay."

The aroma of the smoking dishes was irresistible, and Eli relented. "She will come if we begin," he insisted. "They always do." He motioned for Mistress Joan to take her seat. "You are not here as a servant, I believe, but as a guest." Just then, he heard a hurried pace approaching the threshold. "Come, sit down!" he called out. "And fall to!"

The tablecloths were drawn back, and the knives were raised. Instead of the anticipated Margaret, however, a Dominican friar burst into the room, his face drained of all color by sheer fury. In a single bound, he crossed the space between the door and the table, throwing his tall frame across the narrow surface. With both hands hovering menacingly above the shrinking heads of Cornelis and Sybrandt, like eagles poised over trembling prey, he unleashed a torrent of invective, cursing them by name, soul, and body—here and in the life to come.

That era was eloquent in its curses, and this particular diatribe was so dense, so precise, so utterly blighting and terrible that I dare not transcribe every syllable.

"Cursed be the lips," he shrieked, "that spoke the lie declaring Margaret dead! May they rot before the grave and kiss white-hot iron in hell thereafter! Doubly cursed be the hands that altered those letters, may they be severed by the hangman's blade and forced to handle hellfire forever! Thrice accursed be the cruel hearts that conceived that damnable falsehood to tear true lovers apart! May they sicken and wither on earth, joyless, loveless, and hopeless; may they turn to dust before their time and burn in eternal flames!"

He cursed the meat before their mouths and every atom of their existence, from the top of their heads to the soles of their feet. Turning then to the cowering, shuddering pair who had nearly hidden beneath the table, he tore a letter from his bosom and hurled it onto the floor before his father.

"Read this, you hard-hearted old man who imprisoned your own son! Read it and behold the monsters you have spawned! The memory of my wrongs and hers shall haunt you all eternally! I shall meet you again at Judgment Day; on this earth, you shall never see me once more."

With the speed of lightning, he vanished as he had arrived, leaving behind a tableau of horror: Eli and his sons stood stiff, cold, and white as statues around the untouched, steaming meal.

This was the scene that greeted Margaret and Jorian. Pale figures of men and women seemed petrified around the uneaten food, much as Eastern poets might imagine. Margaret scanned the room, her breath catching in her throat. "Oh, joy! All are here! No blood has been spilled!" she gasped. "Oh, you cruel, cruel men! I thank God he has not slain you."

At the sight of her, Catherine emitted an eloquent scream before turning her head away in distress. But Eli, having just glanced at the forged letter and beginning to grasp the full weight of the deception, saw the other victim enter at that very moment, her sorrows mirrored in her sweet, pale face. A surge of righteous rage lifted him from his chair.

"Stand clear!" he roared. "Let me reach these traitors! I will hang myself for them!"

In an instant, he drew his short sword and lunged at them.

"Flee!" screamed Margaret. "Flee!"

They scrambled howling under the table and crawled out the opposite side. Before they could reach the door, the furious old man circled around and blocked their path. Catherine merely screamed and wrung her hands; such notable figures are often useless in moments of crisis. Blood surely would have flowed, but Margaret and Jorian seized the elderly man's arms, holding him fast with all their strength until they escaped the house. Only then did they release him, and he went forth, raging vainly after them into the street.

They were a furlong away, fleeing like hares, when he hacked down the board bearing their names, dragged it inside, and flung it into the fireplace.

Catherine sat rocking herself, her apron pulled over her head. Joan had rushed to her husband. Margaret embraced Catherine tightly; though pale and panting, she strove to comfort her. Yet, it proved impossible.

"Oh, my poor children!" Catherine wailed. "Oh, miserable mother! It is a mercy Kate was ill upstairs. There, I live to thank God for that!" She broke into fresh sobs. "It would have killed her. He had better have remained in Italy than returned home to curse his own flesh and blood and drive us all mad."

"Hold your tongue, woman!" Eli snapped angrily. "You remain on the side of the wrongdoer. You are poorly served; your weakness made these rogues what they are. I intended to correct them in their youth; for sore ills, sharp remedies are needed. But you sided with their faults, undermined me, and thwarted wise severity. And you, Margaret, cease comforting her when you ought to comfort yourself. What is her injury compared to yours? Besides, she never possessed a grain of justice within her, nor will she ever. Come to me now, for I am your father henceforth."

It was a command. Margaret kissed Catherine goodbye and tottered toward him. He placed her on a chair beside him, and she rested her feeble head against his honest chest, though not a tear fell—it was sorrow too profound for tears.

"Poor lamb," he murmured.

After a pause, true Eli spoke in a broken voice to Jorian and Joan. "Come, good folk, we are in some trouble, as you see. But that is no reason to starve. For our Lady's sake, eat; do not add to my grief by earning the reputation of a miser. What the devil!" he added, attempting a sudden, ghostly display of stout-heartedness, "the more knaves I manage to shake off, the greater my need for true men and women to help clear the dish and cheer my eyes with honest faces where gaps otherwise exist. Eat, I implore you."

Catherine, sobbing, encouraged his request. Poor, simple, antique, and hospitable souls! Jorian, whose appetite, particularly since his illness, was keen, was ready to accept this generous invitation. But Joan whispered something in his ear, and he immediately recoiled.

"Nay, I will touch no meat that the Holy Church has cursed."

"In truth, I forgot," Eli apologized humbly. "My son, who was raised at my table, has cursed my provisions. That seems strange indeed. Well, whatever God wills, man must submit."

The supper was thrown out into the yard. Heavy sadness settled over Eli's house that night.

Meanwhile, where was Clement? He lay stretched full-length upon the floor of the convent church, his lips pressed against the lowest step of the altar. He existed in an indescribable state of terror, misery, penitence, and self-abasement, through which struggled faint glimmers of joy that Margaret was alive.

Night fell, finding him there weeping and praying. Morning would likely have found him in the same posture; but suddenly, he recalled that, absorbed in his own grievances and Margaret's plight, he had committed another sin besides his intemperate rage. He had neglected a dying man.

He rose instantly, groaning under the weight of his accumulated wickedness, determined to make amends for his omission. The weather had shifted; it was raining heavily. As he cleared the town, he heard wolves bay—they were close at hand. Yet Clement was himself again, or nearly so. He cared little for danger or discomfort, driven solely by the shameful neglect of his religious duty. He moved stoutly forward through the rain and darkness.

As he walked, he frequently beat his breast and cried out, "Mea culpa! Mea culpa!"

It is difficult for language to capture the turmoil that swept through Clement's sensitive mind, his tender conscience, and his loving heart within mere hours. Faced with such a sudden and overwhelming crisis, the depth of his internal struggle defies description. Imagine a man in his position; try to feel the weight of it. If I were to dedicate a whole book to this single moment, I would eventually arrive here. For now, let us simply observe his next two decisive actions, which reveal the state of his spirit once the initial storm of anguish had begun to subside.

After spending the night with the dying hermit, offering and receiving sacred comfort, Clement did not head toward Rotterdam. Instead, he traveled to Tergou. His destination was the Burgomaster, his mortal foe. Armed with a parchment—a document whose own history reads like a romance—he intended to force the official to surrender what rightfully belonged to Margaret.

Exhausted and covered in dust, Clement paused at a public fountain. There, he broke his fast on coarse black bread and drank cool water. Mid-meal, however, a female servant burst forward, breathless, begging him to come immediately to shrive her dying master. Without hesitation, he tucked the bread back into his wallet and followed her silently.

She led him to the Stadthouse. At the threshold, seeing her enter, Clement recoiled slightly, a shiver running through him. But he quickly composed himself and stepped inside, ascending the stairs until they reached the bedroom. There, propped high against pillows, lay his deadly enemy, Ghysbrecht, appearing already lifeless.

Clement stood in the doorway for a moment, his eyes tracing the path of the tower, the forest, and the stolen letter. Then, in a low voice, he spoke: "Peace be with you." His voice trembled slightly as the words left his lips.

The sick man greeted him with desperate eagerness, given his frailty. "Thank heaven you have come in time to absolve me of my sins, Father!" Ghysbrecht cried out. "Pray for my soul, you and your brothers!"

"My son," Clement replied calmly, "absolution comes only after confession. In this act, there must be no holding back, if you value the salvation of your soul. Consider carefully where you have most grievously offended God and the Church, while I offer a prayer for wisdom to guide you."

Clement knelt and prayed. Rising, he addressed Ghysbrecht again. "Now, my son, confess your sins."

"Oh, Father," the dying man sighed, "there are many and great ones."

"Then let your penitence be equally great, my son," Clement urged. "Only thus will you find God's mercy vast."

Ghysbrecht clasped his hands and began to confess, displaying every sign of remorse. He admitted to eating meat during Lent, frequently missing Mass on Sundays and feast days, and trifling with various religious duties, listing them with scrupulous detail.

When finished, the friar said softly, "That is well, my son. Those are faults. Now, turn to your crimes. You would have done better to start with those."

"Why, Father?" Ghysbrecht asked, confused. "What crimes stand to my charge if these are not enough?"

"Am I confessing to you, or are you confessing to me?" Clement asked, his tone sharpening.

"Forgive me, Father! Surely I am speaking to you. But I do not understand what you mean by 'crimes.'"

"The Seven Deadly Sins—do you believe you are free of them?"

"Heaven forbid I should be guilty of such things," Ghysbrecht protested. "I do not even know them by name."

"Many commit them all without knowing their titles," Clement reminded him sternly. "Begin with the sin that leads to lying, theft, and murder."

"I am clear of that one, anyway," Ghysbrecht insisted. "What do you call it?"

"Avarice, my son."

"Avarice?" Ghysbrecht blinked, looking puzzled and innocent. "As for that, I have been a thrifty man all my days. Yet I have kept a good table and have not entirely forgotten the poor. But alas, I am a great sinner! Perhaps the next one will catch me. What is the next?"

"We have not finished with this one," Clement warned quietly. "Remember, the Church is not to be trifled with."

"But Father, am I in a condition to trifle with Her now? Avarice? Avarice?" He sounded bewildered.

"Hast thou ever robbed the fatherless?" the friar inquired sharply.

"Me? Robbed the fatherless?" Ghysbrecht gasped. "Not that I mind."

"Again, my son, I am forced to tell you that you are playing with fire. Wretched man! One more evasion, and I depart. Then demons will instantly surround your bed and drag you down to the bottomless pit."

"Oh, do not leave me! Do not leave me!" the terrified old man shrieked. "The Church knows everything. I must have robbed the fatherless. I will confess. With whom shall I begin? My memory for names is failing me."

His defense was clever, but it failed utterly in this instance.

"Hast thou forgotten Floris Brandt?" Clement asked, his face like stone.

The sick man threw himself upright in bed, seized by paralyzing terror. "How did you know that?" he stammered.

"The Church knows many things," Clement replied coldly. "And by many paths unknown to you. Miserable impenitent soul, you summoned her to your side hoping to deceive her. You thought, 'I will not confess to the parish priest, but to some friar who does not know my misdeeds. Thus I will cheat the Church on my deathbed and die as I have lived.' But God, kinder to you than you are to yourself, sent someone you could not fool. He tested you; He was patient, warning you not to mock Holy Church. But all is in vain. You cannot confess because you are impenitent as a rock. Die, then, as you have lived. It seems I see the demons crowding around the bed for their prey. They wait only for me to leave. And I go."

He turned his back, but Ghysbrecht, driven to the brink of madness, grabbed the friar's robe. "Oh, holy man, mercy! Stay! I will confess everything! I robbed my friend Floris! Alas, may it end there, for he lost little by me. But I withheld the land from Peter, his son, and from Margaret, Peter's daughter. I always meant to return it, but I could not, I could not!"

"Avarice, my son! Avarice!" Clement repeated, though his voice softened. "Happy for you, it is not too late."

"No! I will leave it to her in my will!" Ghysbrecht pleaded. "She will not have to wait long—not more than a month or two at most."

"For a month's possession of which you would damn your soul forever, you fool!" Clement retorted.

The sick man groaned, begging the friar to be reasonable. But Clement, firm yet gentle, persisted with infinite patience, slowly loosening the dying man's grip on another person's property.

There were moments when Clement's patience was nearly exhausted, tempting him to reach into his bosom and produce the very deed he had carried for this exact purpose. However, recalling the violent outburst of the previous day, he guarded himself against anger. Furthermore, he overcame his deep-seated personal revulsion for the man—a hatred so intense it made his own flesh crawl while he struggled with this miser for his soul. Finally, without mentioning the deed, he succeeded in winning the dying man's promise to make full and immediate restitution.

How exactly the restitution was executed will be recounted elsewhere, along with the strange effects it produced upon Ghysbrecht, and the terms under which Ghysbrecht and Clement finally parted ways. I promised to describe two acts of the latter that indicate his state of mind. This is the first. The second requires only two words.

As soon as Clement was absolutely certain that Margaret had regained her inheritance and was once again a wealthy woman... he vanished.

It was the day following that dreadful scene. The small dwelling on the Hoog Straet felt less like a home and more like a tomb, heavy with silence and grief. None bore the weight of this desolation more heavily than Catherine, a woman naturally buoyant and industrious, now rendered listless and dejected. After dinner, her eyes swollen and red from weeping, she resolved to visit the convent. Her sole purpose was to attempt to soften Gerard's heart, laying the very first stone toward a reconciliation.

For some time, she struggled to convince the porter of her identity and intent. Finally, she learned that Gerard had departed late the previous evening and was not expected until the morrow. Sighing deeply under the burden of this delay, she returned to Margaret. She found her sitting motionless, a figure from whom life seemed to have drained every drop of flavor. Margaret held her young son so tightly in her arms, clutching him as if he were the only possession remaining in a world that might soon strip her of everything else, fearing perhaps that someone would take him too.

Catherine implored her to accompany her back to the Hoog Straet. "What for?" Margaret sighed, her voice hollow. "You must admit to yourself that she is the cause of all this ruin."

"Nay, nay," Catherine protested gently. "We are not so ill-hearted. Besides, Eli is so fond of you; perhaps your presence will soften him."

"Oh, if you truly believe I can do any good, I will come," Margaret replied, exhaling a weary breath.

They arrived to find Eli and a carpenter installing a new signboard, replacing the names of Cornelis and Sybrandt. What name had been chosen to fill the void? Margaret Brandt's. Despite her enduring affection for Margaret, the sight cut through poor Catherine's heart like a blade.

"The bane of one is the meat of another," she muttered bitterly.

"Can he make me squander money unjustly?" Margaret retorted, her tone icy.

"You are a good soul," Catherine said softly.

"Ay, so best, seeing that he is the strongest," Margaret answered coldly.

The next day, Giles dropped by. Catherine recounted the entire tragedy in favor of the "black sheep," inviting his pity for them—cursed by their brother and cast adrift by their father. Yet, Giles's prejudices ran in the opposite direction. He listened patiently, only to dismiss the tale with blunt contempt. "Those knaves have gotten off far too cheap!" he declared. "They deserve to be hanged at Margaret's door, and nothing less." Dismissing them with scorn, he crowed with delight at the prospect of reclaiming his favorite nephew.

"I'll show him," Giles vowed, "what it means to have a brother at court with a heart loyal to a friend and a mind sharp enough to guide the way."

"Bless thee, Giles," Margaret murmured softly.

"Thou wast ever his staunch friend, dear Giles," little Kate added, though her voice trembled. "But alas, I know not what thou canst do for him now."

Once Giles had departed, the house fell into a profound sadness and silence. Just then, a well-dressed stranger opened the door quietly and asked if Margaret Brandt was within.

"D'ye hear, lass? You are wanted," Catherine announced briskly. The spirit of gossip was indestructible within her.

"Well, mother," Margaret said listlessly, rising. "And here I am."

A shuffling sound echoed from the doorway, accompanied by the appearance of a colorless, frail old man being assisted into the room. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten.

At the sight of him, Catherine shrieked, throwing her apron over her head in horror. Margaret shuddered violently, turning her face swiftly away, refusing to meet his gaze. From the stranger's lips came a feeble voice. "Good people, a dying man has come to ask your forgiveness."

"Come to look upon your work, you mean," Catherine snapped, removing her apron and bursting into fresh sobs.

"There, there, she is fainting! Look to her, Eli, quickly!"

"Nay," Margaret whispered, her voice weak. "The sight of him gave me a turn; that is all. Pray, let him speak his piece and depart. For he is the murderer of me and mine."

"Alas," Ghysbrecht gasped, "I am too feeble to stand and speak. No one bids me sit."

Eli, who had followed the intruder inside, intervened, his voice half-sullen, half-apologetic. "Well, Burgomaster, 'tis not our custom to leave a visitor standing while we sit. But man, man, you have wrought us too much ill." The honest fellow's voice began to shake with suppressed anger, fighting hard to contain the rage that burned within him, knowing it was his own home that suffered such intrusion.

Then, Ghysbrecht found an unexpected advocate in the youngest member of the family. It was little Kate.

"Father, Mother," she said, "my duty to you compels me, but this is not well. Death squares all accounts. Do you not see death written plainly upon his face? I shall not live long, good friends; and his time is shorter still."

Moved by her plea, Eli hurried to set a chair for their dying enemy with his own hands. Ghysbrecht's attendants helped him into it.

"Go fetch the boxes," he commanded.

Two boxes were brought in, and the servants withdrew, leaving their master alone amidst the family he had so cruelly injured. Every eye turned toward him, save Margaret's. With trembling fingers, Ghysbrecht unlocked the boxes. From one, he produced the title deeds to a property in Tergou.

"This land and these houses belonged to Floris Brandt, and they belong to thee of right, his granddaughter," he stated, his voice breaking. "These I usurped for a debt long since paid in full with interest. I now restore them to their rightful owner with penitent tears. In this other box lie three hundred and forty golden angels—the rent and fines I collected from that land exceeding Floris Brandt's original debt to me. I kept them, counting the days, intending to be just eventually; but Avarice withheld me, tempting me against my better judgment. I was not born dishonest; yet, you see."

"Well, to be sure!" Catherine exclaimed. "And you, the Burgomaster! You have whipped many thieves in your day. However," she amended after a moment's thought, "'tis better late than never." Turning to Margaret, she cried, "What, Margaret, are you deaf? The good man has brought back your own heritage. You are a rich woman! Alack, what a mountain of gold!"

"Let him keep the land and the gold," Margaret said, her head still averted. "Give me back my Gerard, whom he stole from me with his treason."

"Alas!" Ghysbrecht lamented. "Would I could! What can I have done? It cost me a fierce struggle. I rose from my last bed to deliver this myself, lest some mischance prevent her from receiving her rights."

"Old man," Margaret said, her voice barely audible, "since thou, whose idol is wealth, hast done this, may God and the saints, as I hope, forgive thee. As for me, I am neither saint nor angel, but only a poor woman whose heart thou hast broken. Speak to him, Kate, for I am like the dead."

Kate paused for a moment, then her soft, silvery voice fell upon the air like a soothing melody. "My poor sister bears a sorrow that riches cannot heal. Give her time, Ghysbrecht. 'Tis not in nature for her to forgive thee all immediately. Her boy is fatherless; she is neither maiden, wife, nor widow; and the blow fell but two days hence that laid her heart a-bleeding."

A single, heavy sob escaped Margaret as her response.

"Therefore, give her time!" Kate continued. "And ere thou diest, she will forgive thee all, yea, even to please me, who mayhap shall not be long behind thee, Ghysbrecht. Meanwhile, we, whose wounds are sore but not so deep as hers, do pardon thee, a penitent and a dying man. And I, for one, will pray for thee from this hour. Go in peace!"

Her little oracle had spoken; it was sufficient. Even Eli invited him to break a loaf of bread and drink a cup of wine to strengthen him for his final journey. But Ghysbrecht declined. "What I have done serves as a cordial to me," he said. "Man sees but a little way ahead, neighbor. This land, which I clung to so desperately, was a bed of nettles to me all along. 'Tis gone, and I feel happier and livelier for the loss of it."

He summoned his men, who lifted him into the litter. As he departed, Catherine gazed upon the recovered treasure with a gleam in her eye. She had never seen so much wealth assembled at once and was nearly angry with Margaret for "sitting there like an image." She dilated endlessly on the advantages of money, teasing Margaret until she finally prevailed upon her to inspect the hoard.

"Better let her be, mother," Kate interjected gently. "How can she relish gold with a heart in her bosom likelier to lead?"

But Catherine

Scarcely had the dust settled on the events of the previous days when news arrived that Margaret Van Eyck was dead and buried. By a will executed a year prior, she had bequeathed all her possessions—after settling funeral costs and granting specific gifts to Reicht Heynes—to her beloved daughter, Margaret Brandt. Her final instruction was clear: she requested that Reicht remain in her service as long as the daughter remained unmarried.

Through this inheritance, Margaret acquired a fully furnished house, alongside a collection of pictures and sketches that, in modern times, would constitute a vast fortune. Among these treasures was one painting she cherished above all others, valuing it more highly than an entire gallery. It depicted "A Betrothal." The solemn gravity of the occasion was etched into the grave expression of the groom, while the bride wore a look of demure complacency. Though largely painted by Margaret Van Eyck herself, the remainder of the masterpiece was the work of Jan. Its accessories were finished with exquisite precision, remaining a marvel of skill to this day.

Margaret Brandt sent word to Reicht to remain in the house until she felt strong enough to step inside, asking him to guard the painting carefully as it rested in a small cupboard—the diptych.

Soon after, Luke Peterson returned home and learned that Gerard had become a monk. He nearly went mad with joy. Rushing to Margaret, he declared, "Take heed, mistress. If he cannot marry you, I can."

"You?" Margaret asked, surprised. "Why, I have seen him."

"But he is a friar!" Luke insisted.

"He was my husband, and the father of my boy, long before he took vows," Margaret retorted firmly. "I have seen him; I have seen him."

Luke was thoroughly confused. "Tell you what," he suggested, "I have a cousin who is a lawyer. I will go ask him whether you are married or single."

"Nay," Margaret snapped. "I shall ask my own heart, not a lawyer. So that is your regard for me? To turn me into the subject of town gossip? Oh, fie!"

"That has already been done without a word from me," Luke muttered.

"But not by someone seeking my respect. And if you proceed, never come near me again."

"Ay," sighed Luke, "you are like a dove to everyone else, but a hard-hearted tyrant to me."

"'Tis your own fault, dear Luke, for wooing me," Margaret explained gently. "That prevents me from being as kind to you as I wish, Luke, my bonny lad, listen. I am rich now; I can make my friends happy, though perhaps not myself. Look around the street, look around the parish. There are many women fairer than I, twice told, and not ruined by weeping. Look higher; choose wisely. Speak to the maiden herself, and I will speak to her mother; they shall not refuse you; take my word for it."

"I see what you mean," Luke said, turning crimson. "But if I cannot have your favor, I want none of your money. I was your servant when you were as poor as I, and poorer. No; if you would rather be a friar's mistress than an honest man's wife, you are not the woman I took you for. Let us part without malice. Seek your comfort on that road where no woman has ever found it, and for me, I will live and die a bachelor. Good evening, mistress."

"Farewell, dear Luke," Margaret said sadly. "And may God forgive you for speaking thus to me."

For several days, Margaret dreaded, almost as much as she desired, the inevitable interview with Gerard. She reasoned to herself, "I wonder he stays away for a while; for so should I." Yet, he knew he was a father, and the longing to see their child would surely overcome all barriers. "And," the poor girl murmured, "if that meeting does not kill me, I feel I shall be better afterward than I am now."

However, as day succeeded day and Gerard remained unheard of, a freezing suspicion began to creep into her mind. Was his absence intentional? Had he gone to some cold-blooded fellow monks who forbade him from seeing her again? The convent had already proven itself as merciless to true lovers as the grave itself. At this thought, the very life seemed to drain from her.

Now, for the first time, deep indignation mingled with her grief and apprehension. "Could he have ever truly loved me?" she wondered. "To flee from me and his own son without a word! Why, this poor Luke thinks more of me than he does."

Just as her mind churned in this turmoil, Giles burst in, shouting, "I've hit the mark! Our Gerard is the Vicar of Gouda!"

A brief sketch of the dwarf's rise at court prepares the reader for his account of this achievement. Months prior, his intelligence had begun to bud. He himself dated the change to a certain eighth of June, when, swinging by one hand along with a week's laundry on a tightrope in the drying yard, something cracked inside his head. Lo! Intellectual powers were unleashed. At court, his shrewdness, blunt speech, gigantic voice, and diminutive stature made him a power; without the last attribute, they might have led him to the gallows. Both the young Duchess of Burgundy and Marie, the heiress apparent, doted on him, as great ladies have always favored dwarfs. The court poet melted butter by the six-foot rule and poured enough down his back to stew Goliath. Even he amplified, versified, and softened certain rough sentences dictated by Giles.

The resulting centipede-like verbosity was sent to Eli by letter, titled: *"The high and puissant Princess Marie of Bourgogne her lytel jantilman hys complaynt of y' Coort, and praise of a rusticall lyfe, versificated, and empapyred by me the lytel jantilman's right lovynge and obsequious servitor, etc."*

But Giles reached his zenith through a happy mixture of mind and muscle. The day before a grand tournament, he challenged the Duke's giant to a trial of strength. This provoked a grimace of amusement and raised expectations.

Giles had a lofty pole erected. At the appointed hour, he ascended it like a squirrel, using the strength of his arm to form a right angle with his body, holding the pose. Then, sliding down so swiftly that the High and Puissant Princess squealed and hid her face in her hands, he escaped before anyone could witness the demise of her pocket-Hercules.

The giant managed only about ten feet, then looked ruefully up and down before descending, bathed in sweat to explain the discrepancy. "It was not the dwarf's greater strength, but his smaller body," he argued.

The spectators received this excuse with loud derision. Here was the fact: the dwarf was great at climbing poles; the giant only great at making excuses. In short, Giles had gauged their intellects—with his own body, no doubt.

"Come," he said, "if you go to that, I'll wrestle you, my lad, if you will let me blindfold your eyes."

Smarting under defeat and hoping to recover honor this way, the giant readily agreed.

"Madam," Giles announced, "do you see yonder blind Samson? At a signal from me, he shall make me a low obeisance and remove his cap."

"How can that be, being blinded?" inquired a maid of honor.

"I'll wager on Giles for one," the princess declared.

"That is my affair."

Once several wagers were placed, Giles struck the giant squarely in the breadbasket. The giant doubled over (the obeisance), and his bonnet fell off.

The company yelled with delight at this delicate stroke of wit, and Giles took to his heels. The giant followed as soon as he could catch his breath and tear off his bandage. But it was too late; Giles had prepared a small door in the wall, wide enough for him but not for a giant, colored so artfully it resembled a section of the wall. He tore it open and went headlong through, leaving no trace but a poem written in large letters on the reverse side of his trick door:

After this, Giles became a force. Speaking for himself, he recounted the following to Margaret, who struggled to believe the good news and doubted that Holy Church affairs were administered by dwarfs:

"When the princess summoned me to her bedroom, as was her custom, to keep her spirits up, I did not arrive merry or full of country ditties, as is my wont, but dull as lead.

"'Why, what ails thee?' she asked. 'Are you sick?'

'"At heart,' I replied.

'"Alas, he is in love,' she said. Whereupon five brazen hussies, whom they called maids of honor, giggled loudly.

'"Not so mad as that,' I said, 'considering what I see at court regarding women folk.'

'"There, ladies,' the princess interjected, 'let him be. He is a liberal manikin and still gives more than he takes in saucy words.'

'"In all seriousness,' she asked, 'what is the matter?'

"I told her I was meditating, explaining that what perplexed me was that commoners sometimes kept their word, but princes never.

Yes, the hermit of Gouda was indeed the vicar of Gouda, though he remained unaware of his own identity due to the absolute nature of his seclusion. My readers know that the moment the frenzy of his passion subsided, he was seized with profound remorse for having yielded to it. Yet, perhaps only those who have ascended to the highest peaks of spiritual aspiration and then suddenly tumbled down can truly comprehend the terror that gripped him. He felt as though self-confidence had betrayed him to the very brink of a precipice.

"Ah, good Jerome!" he cried aloud. "How much better you knew me than I knew myself! How bitter, yet how wholesome, was your admonition!"

Accustomed to searching his own heart, he immediately discerned that the true source of his fury lay in Margaret. "I love her then better than God," he confessed despairingly. "Better than the Church! From such a love, what can possibly spring for me, or for her?" He shuddered violently at the thought. "Let the strong battle temptation; 'tis for the weak to flee. And who is weaker than I have proven myself? What is my penitence, my religion? Merely a pack of cards built degree by degree into a fair-seeming structure; and lo! one breath of earthly love, and it lies in the dust. I must begin again, and on a surer foundation."

He resolved to leave Holland immediately and spend years of his life in some distant convent before returning. By that time, the temptations of earthly passion would be doubly baffled; older and a better monk, he would be more master of his affections. Furthermore, seeing himself abandoned, Margaret would likely marry another and love someone else. The very anguish this final thought caused him demonstrated that the self-searcher and self-denier was indeed walking the path of religious duty. However, in leaving her for his immortal good—and hers—he was not to neglect her temporal welfare. Indeed, the sweet thought that he could provide for her comfort and enrich her in this world's goods, which she was not bound to despise, sustained him through the bitter struggle of turning his back on her without offering a single kind word or glance.

"Oh, what will she think of me?" he groaned. "Shall I not seem to her, of all creatures, the most heartless, inhuman? But so best! Ay, better she should hate me, miserable that I am. Heaven is merciful, and gives my broken heart this comfort: I can make that villain restore her dowry, and she shall never lose another true lover because of poverty. Another? Ah me! Ah me! God and the saints to my aid!"

How he fared on this errand has been previously related. But first, as you may recall, he went at night to hear the confession of the hermit of Gouda. He found him dying and never left until he had closed his eyes and buried him beneath the floor of the small oratory attached to his cell. It was the peaceful end of a stormy life. The hermit had been a soldier, and even then he wore a steel corselet next his skin, declaring that he was now Christ's soldier as he had once been Satan's. When Clement had heard his confession and prayed beside him, the dying man, in his turn, sought counsel from one who was passing away in such a pious frame. The hermit advised him to become his successor in this peaceful retreat. "His had been a hard fight against the world, the flesh, and the devil, and he had never thoroughly baffled them until he retired into the citadel of Solitude."

These words, coupled with the hermit's pious and peaceful death—which swiftly followed and seemed to set a seal of immortal truth upon them—made a deep impression upon Clement. In his case, there was no prejudice to combat; the solitary recluse was still profoundly revered in the Church, whether immured as an anchorite in a cave or cell belonging to a monastery, or hidden in the more savage but laxer seclusion of an independent hermitage. Clement knew more about the hermits of the Church than most divines of his time. He had read extensively on the subject at the monastery near Tergou, devouring their lives with wonder and delight in the manuscripts of the Vatican, and conversing earnestly about them with mendicant friars of various nations. Before the invention of printing, these friars were the great circulators of local annals and biographies that accumulated in convents across every land.

Moreover, his teacher, Jerome, had spent three years as an anchorite on the heights of Camaldoli, where the Thebaid had been revived for more than four centuries. Jerome, usually cold and curt on most religious themes, burned with enthusiasm regarding this one. He had pored over the annals of St. John Baptist's Abbey, round which the hermit's caves were scattered, telling Clement the names of many nobles and famous warriors who ended their days there as hermits, and of many bishops and archbishops who passed from the see to the hermitage, or vice versa. Among the former was the Archbishop of Ravenna; among the latter, Pope Victor the Ninth. He recounted, with grim delight, their multifarious austerities and how each hermit set himself to find where he was weakest, attacking himself without mercy or remission until, even there, he became strongest. He described how seven times in twenty-four hours, in thunder, rain, or snow, by daylight, twilight, moonlight, or torchlight, the solitaries flocked from distant points over rugged, precipitous ways to worship in the convent church—at Matins, Prime, Tierce, Sext, None, Vespers, and Compline.

Under eager questioning, Clement also learned of the persons of famous anchorites he had sung the Psalter and prayed with there—the only intercourse their vows permitted, save with special permission. There was Moncada, Duke of Moncada and Cardova, and Hidalgo of Spain, who retired there in the flower of his youth from the pomps, vanities, and pleasures of the world; Father John Baptist of Novara, who had led armies to battle but was now a private soldier of Christ; Cornelius, Samuel, and Sylvanus. This last, when the great Duchess de' Medici obtained the Pope's leave—hitherto refused—to visit Camaldoli, went down and met her at the first wooden cross. Surrounded as she was with courtiers and flatterers, he remonstrated with her, persuaded her, and warned her not to profane that holy mountain, where no woman had placed her foot for so many centuries. She, awed by the place and the man, retreated with all her captains, soldiers, courtiers, and pages from that one hoary hermit.

At Basel, Clement found fresh materials, particularly concerning German and English anchorites. He had even prepared a *Catena Eremitarum* spanning from the year of our Lord 250, when Paul of Thebes commenced his ninety years of solitude, down to the year 1470. He called them *Angelorum amici et animalium*, i.e., Friends of Angels and Animals. Thus, although in those days he never intended to be a recluse, the road was paved, so to speak. When the dying hermit of Gouda blessed the citadel of Solitude, where he had fought the good fight and won it, and invited Clement to take up the breastplate of faith that now fell from his shrunken body, Clement said within himself: "Heaven itself led my foot hither to this end."

It struck him, too, as no small coincidence that his patron, St. Bavon, was a hermit—an austere one, a cuirassier of the solitary cell. As soon as he was reconciled with Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, he went eagerly to his abode, praying that Heaven might not have already occupied it during these three days. The fear was not vain; these famous dens never lacked a human tenant for long. He found the rude stone door ajar; fearing he was too late, he opened it and went softly inside. No; the cell was vacant. There lay the hermit's great ivory crucifix, his pens, ink, seeds, and, as a *memento mori*, a skull; his cilice of hair, and another of bristles; his well-worn sheepskin pelisse and hood; his hammer, chisel, and psaltery, and so forth. Men and women had passed that way, but none had ventured to intrude, far less to steal. Faith and simplicity had guarded that keyless door more securely than the houses of the laity were defended by their gates like a modern gaol, complete with iron bars at every window, and the gentry by moats, bastions, *chevaux de frise*, and portcullises.

As soon as Clement was fairly in the cell, there was a loud flap and a flutter, and down came a great brown owl from a corner, whirling out of the window and driving the cold air onto Clement's face. He started and shuddered. Was this seeming owl something diabolical, trying to deter him from his soul's good? On second thought, might it not be some good spirit the hermit had employed to keep the cell for him, perhaps the hermit himself? Finally, he concluded it was merely an owl and decided to try and make friends with it. He knelt down and inaugurated his new life with prayer.

Clement had not only an earthly passion to quell, the power of which made him tremble for his eternal

One day, while lying there sighing and groaning—prayerless, tuneless, and utterly hopeless—a sudden thought pierced his mind. He realized what he had done for the poor and the wayfarer, he would now do for himself. He resolved to fill his den of despair with the Name of God, the sacred words of Scripture, and the pious consolations of the Church.

Then, like Christian standing at the feet of Apollyon, he suddenly reached out his hand. He grasped not his sword, for he possessed none, but the humble weapon of peaceful labor: his chisel. With a fervor as though his very soul depended upon the movement of his arm, he began to work.

It is said that Michelangelo, in the generation following, carved statues unlike the timid sculptors of his day who modeled works in clay before handing them to mechanics. Instead, Michelangelo would seize the block, conceive the image instantly, and with mallet and steel cause the marble chips to fly wildly around him until the mass sprouted into form. So too did Clement draw no preliminary lines to guide his hand. Turning to his memory for the gracious words, he dashed at his work, eagerly carving them into the soft stone between moments of labor and combat.

He begged his visitors for candle ends and rancid oil. "Anything is good enough for me," he declared, "if it will but burn." Thus, at night, the cave glowed afar off like a blacksmith's forge, visible through the window and the gaps in the rough stone door. Rustics beholding the light crossed themselves, suspecting devilries, while within, holy talismans appeared one after another upon the walls. Sparks and chips flew day and night, night and day, as the soldier of Solitude and the Church wielded his bloodless weapon, sighing and groaning, between working and fighting.

*Kyrie Eleison.* *Christe Eleison.* *(Ton Satanan suntripson upo tous pothas ymwn)* — Beat down Satan under our feet.
*Sursum Corda.* — Lift up your hearts.
*Deus Refugium nostrum et virtus.* — O God, our refuge and strength.
*Agnus Dei, qui tollis peccata mundi, miserere mihi.* — O Lamb of God, who taketh away the sins of the world, have mercy on me.
*Sancta Trinitas unus Deus, miserere nobis.* — O Holy Trinity, one God, have mercy on us.
*Ab infestationibus Daemonum, a ventura ira, a damnatione perpetua. Libera nos Domine.* — From the assaults of demons, from the wrath to come, from everlasting damnation, deliver us, O Lord.
*Quem quaerimus adjutorem nisi te Domine, qui pro peccatis nostris juste irascaris?* — Of whom may we seek succour but of Thee, O Lord, who for our sins art justly displeased?
*Sancte Deus, Sancte fortis, Sancte et misericors Salvator, amarae morti ne tradas nos.* — O Holy God, O Holy Strong, O Holy and Merciful Savior, deliver us not into bitter death.

And beneath the great crucifix, fastened to the wall, he carved these words from Augustine:
*"O anima Christiana, respice vulnera patientis, sanguinem morientis, pretium redemptionis. Haec quanta sint cogitate, et in statera mentis vestrae appendite, ut totus vobis figatur in corde, qui pro vobis totus fixus est in cruce. Nam si passio Christi ad memoriam revocetur, nihil est tam durum quod non aequo animo toleretur."*

Which may be thus rendered:
*"O Christian soul, look on the wounds of the suffering One, the blood of the dying One, the price paid for our redemption! Think how great these things are, and weigh them in the balance of thy mind, that He may be wholly nailed to thy heart, who for thee was all nailed unto the cross. For if thou dost recall the sufferings of Christ, there is naught on earth too hard to endure with composure."*

Soothed a little, a very little, by the sweet and pious words he raised all around him, yet weighed down by watching and working night and day, Clement one morning sank prostrate with fatigue, and a deep sleep overpowered him for many hours.

Awakening quietly, he heard a tiny chirp. Opening his eyes, behold! Upon his breviary, resting on a low stool near his feet, perched a robin redbreast. With a single pull, the bird ruffled all its feathers, smoothed them instantly, and cocked its bill this way and that with a vast display of cunning purely imaginary. Clement held his breath, half closing his eyes lest he frighten the airy guest.

Down came the robin onto the floor. There, it enacted its pantomime of astuteness; then *pim, pim, pim*, with three stiff little hops, like a ball of worsted on vertical wires, it landed squarely on the hermit's bare foot. On this eminence, it swelled and contracted again, with the ebb and flow of its feathers. But Clement lost interest, for he quite closed his eyes, scarcely drawing breath in fear of frightening and losing his visitor. He was content merely to feel the minute claw upon his foot. He could barely feel it, aided only by knowing it was there.

Presently, a little flutter of two small wings, and the feathered busybody returned to the breviary. Then Clement determined to try feeding this pretty little fidget without scaring it away. It proved very difficult. He had a piece of bread within reach, but how to get at it? He spent five minutes creeping his hand toward the bread, realizing he must not move his arm once there. Slyly, he got a crumb between finger and thumb and shot it forward, as boys shoot marbles, keeping his hand perfectly still.

Cock-robin saw the crumb fall nearby and showed sagacity, yet remained motionless. When another followed, and then another, the bird popped down, caught up one of the crumbs, but not fully understanding this mystery, fled with it to a higher eminence—for greater security—to wit, the hermit's knee. Thus the game proceeded until a much larger fragment than usual rolled along. Here was a prize! Cock-robin pounced upon it, bore it aloft, and fled so swiftly into the world that the cave resounded with the buffeted air.

"Now, bless thee, sweet bird!" sighed the stricken solitary. "Thy wings are music, and thou, a feathered ray, hast come to lighten my darkened soul."

From that moment onward, during his orisons, and then returning to his tools with a little renewed courage, this became his day's work:
*"Veni, Creator Spiritus, Mentes tuorum visita, Imple superna gratia, Quae tu creasti pectora. Accende lumen sensibus, Mentes tuorum visita, Infirma nostri corporis, Virtute firmans perpeti."*

Thus the days rolled on. The weather grew colder, but Clement's heart grew warmer, and despondency rolled away. By and by, somehow or another, it was gone entirely. He had outlived it. It had come like a cloud, and departed like one. Presently, all was reversed; his cell seemed illuminated with joy. His work pleased him; his prayers were full of unction; his psalms were songs of praise.

Hosts of little birds followed their crimson leader, flying from the snow. A parish full of Cains made friends one after another with Abels—fast friends indeed. And on one keen frosty night, as he sang the praises of God to his tuneful psaltery, causing his hollow cave to ring forth with holy psalmody—as if the cave itself were Tubal's surrounding shell or David's harp—he heard a clear whine, not unmelodious. It grew louder and less in tune. Peeping through the chinks of his rude door, he saw a great red wolf moaning melodiously, its nose held high in the air.

Clement was rejoiced. "My sins are going," he cried, "and the creatures of God are owning me, one after another." In a burst of enthusiasm, he struck up the laud: *"Praise Him, all ye creatures of His! Let everything that hath breath praise the Lord."* All the time he sang, the wolf bayed at intervals.

Above all, he seemed now drawing nearer to that celestial intercourse which was the sign and bliss of the true hermit. He had dreams of saints and angels so vivid they were more like visions. He saw bright figures clad in woven snow. They bent upon him eyes lovelier than those of the antelope he had seen at Rome, fanning him with broad wings hued like the rainbow. Their gentle voices bade him speed upon his course.

He had not long enjoyed this felicity when his dreams began to take another, strange complexion. He wandered with Fra Colonna over the relics of antique nations. The friar was lame, leaning on a staff, which he waved over mighty ruins. Were they Egyptian, Greek, or Roman, straightway the temples and palaces, whose wrecks they were, rose again like an exhalation, thronged with the famous dead. Songsters that might eclipse both Apollo and

Her demeanor invited pity rather than dread, to anyone whose eyes were not clouded by prejudice. Her chest heaved like a trapped bird's, the crimson and pallor of her cheeks shifting rapidly, while her hand clutched the rough stone wall—a reflex of timid creatures. She trembled violently. The gaze she cast upon the emaciated, glaring hermit was a marvel: a mixture of love, pious awe, pity, and tender memory. Those purple eyes fixed on the suffering recluse were a phenomenon in nature.

"Aha!" he cried. "Thou art come at last in flesh and blood; come to me as thou camest to holy Anthony. But I am wary of thee. I thought thy wiles were not exhausted. I am armed."

With this, he snatched up his small crucifix, holding it out at her, astonished, while clutching a candle in his other hand. Both objects shook violently. "Exorcizo te."

"Ah, no!" she cried piteously, holding up two pretty, deprecating palms. "Alas! work me no ill! It is Margaret."

"Liar!" shouted the hermit. "Margaret was fair, but not so supernaturally fair as thou. Thou didst shrink at that sacred name, thou subtle hypocrite. *In Nomine Dei* exorcizo vos."

"Oh, Jesus!" gasped Margaret, in extremity of terror. "Curse me not! I will go home. I thought I might come. Do not Latinize me for the sake of manhood! Oh, Gerard, is this how we meet, after all, after all?"

She cowered almost to her knees, sobbing with superstitious fear and wounded affection. Though steeped in *Satanophobia*, he might perhaps have still doubted whether this distressed creature, all woman and nature, was merely art and fiend. But her spontaneous appeal to that sacred name dissolved his delusion; he saw with his eyes and heard with his ears. He uttered a cry of self-reproach and tried to raise her, but fasting and the overwhelming emotion of a long solitude broken so abruptly rendered him unable.

"What," he gasped, shaking over her, "is it thou? And have I met thee with harsh words? Alas!"

They were both choked with emotion, unable to speak for a while.

"I heed it not much," said Margaret bravely, struggling with her tears. "You took me for another—for a devil! Oh! oh! oh! oh! oh!"

"Forget me, sweet soul!" As soon as he could speak more than a word at a time, he added, "I have been much beset by the Evil One since I came here."

Margaret looked around with a shudder. "Likely enough. Then, oh, take my hand and let me lead thee from this foul place."

He gazed at her in astonishment. "Desert my cell? Go back into the world again? Is that why thou hast come to me?" he said sadly and reproachfully.

"Yes, Gerard, I have come to take thee to thy pretty vicarage. Art vicar of Gouda, thanks to Heaven and thy good brother Giles; and mother and I have made it so neat for thee, Gerard. 'Tis well enough in winter, I promise thee. But wait until the hawthorn blooms, and anon thy walls will be clad in their kirtle of brave roses and sweet woodbine. Have we forgotten thee, and the foolish things thou lovest? And, dear Gerard, thy mother is waiting; 'tis late for her to be out of bed. Prithee, prithee, come! And the moment we are out of this foul hole, I'll show thee a treasure thou hast gained, and knowest naught of, or surely hadst never fled from us so. Alas, what have I ignorantly said to be regarded thus?"

For he had drawn himself up into a heap, staring at her with a strange gaze blending fear and suspicion.

"Unhappy girl," he said solemnly, yet deeply agitated, "would you have me risk my soul and yours for a miserable vicarage and the flowers that grow upon it? But this is not thy doing: the bowelless fiend sends thee, poor simple girl, to me with this bait. But oh, cunning fiend, I will unmask thee even to this thine instrument, and she shall see thee and abhor thee as I do. Margaret, my lost love, why am I here? Because I love thee."

"Oh! No, Gerard, you do not love me, or you would not have hidden from me; there was no need."

"Let there be no deceit between us two, who have loved so truly; and after this night, we shall meet no more on earth."

"Now God forbid!" she exclaimed. "I love thee, and thou hast not forgotten me, or thou hadst married ere this, and wouldst not be the one to find me, buried here from sight of man. I am a priest, a monk: what but folly or sin can come of us living neighbors, feeding a passion innocent once, but now (so Heaven wills it) impious and unholy? No, though my heart breaks, I must be firm. 'Tis I that am the man, 'tis I that am the priest. We must meet no more, till I am schooled by solitude, and thou art wedded to another."

"I consent to my doom but not to thine. I would ten times liefer die; yet I will marry, ay, wed misery itself, sooner than let thee lie in this foul, dismal place, with yon sweet manse awaiting for thee."

Clement groaned. With each word she spoke, two things stood clearer and clearer: his duty, and the agony it must cost.

"My beloved," he said, with a strange mixture of tenderness and dogged resolution, "I bless thee for granting me one more sight of thy sweet face. May God forgive thee, and bless thee, for destroying in a minute the holy peace it hath taken six months of solitude to build. No matter. A year of penance will, *Dei gratia*, restore me to my calm. My poor Margaret, I seem cruel: yet I am kind; 'tis best we part; ay, this moment."

"Part, Gerard? Never! We have seen what comes of parting. Part? Why, you have not heard half my story; no, nor the tithe. 'Tis not for thy mere comfort I take thee to the Gouda manse. Hear me!"

"I may not. Thy very voice is a temptation, with its music, memory's delight."

"But I say you shall hear me, Gerard, for forth this place I go not unheard."

"Then must we part by other means," said Clement sadly.

"Alack! what other means? Wouldst thou put me to thine own door, being the stronger?"

"Nay, Margaret, well thou knowest I would suffer many deaths rather than use force upon thee; thy sweet body is dearer to me than my own; but a million times dearer to me are our immortal souls, both thine and mine. I have withstood this direst temptation of all long enough. Now I must fly it: Farewell! Farewell!"

He moved toward the door, having actually opened it and stepped halfway out, when she darted after him and caught hold of his arm.

"Nay, then another must speak for me. I thought to reward thee for yielding to me; but unkind that thou art, I find I need his help. Turn then this way one moment."

"Nay, nay."

"But I say ay! And then turn thy back on us if thou canst."

She somewhat relaxed her grasp, thinking he would never deny her so small a favor. But at this, he saw his opportunity and seized it.

"Flee, Clement, flee!" he almost shrieked. His religious enthusiasm giving him for a moment his old strength, he burst wildly away from her. After a few steps, he bounded over the little stream and ran alongside it, but finding he was not followed, he stopped and looked back.

She lay on her face, hands spread out. Yes, without meaning it, he had thrown her down and injured her. When he saw that, he groaned and turned back a step; but suddenly, by another impulse, he flung himself into the icy water instead.

"There, kill my body!" he cried, "but save my soul!"

While he stood there, up to his throat in liquid ice, Margaret uttered one long, piteous moan and rose to her knees. He saw her as plainly as in midday—her pale face, her glistening eyes—and then, in the still night, he heard these words:

"Oh, God! Thou that knowest all, Thou seest how I am used. Forgive me then! For I will not live another day."

With this, she suddenly sprang to her feet and flew like some wild creature, wounded to death, close by his miserable hiding-place, shrieking:

What manifold anguish may burst from a human heart in a single syllable. There were wounded love, wounded pride, despair, and coming madness all in that piteous cry. Clement heard, and it froze his heart with terror and remorse, worse than the icy water chilled the marrow of his bones. He felt he had driven her from him forever, and in the midst of his dismal triumph—the greatest

The hermit's gaze darted frantically between the child in his arms and the woman standing before him, his eyes wide with a mixture of shock and agitated disbelief. Only moments prior, he had overpowered a pale, terrified girl; now, kneeling at his side, stood an imperious beauty. Her cheeks flushed with vitality, her eyes sparkled with triumph, and her lips parted in a sweet, victorious smile. Yet, beneath this radiance lay a new expression he had never witnessed on her face before—a profound, glowing maternal pride. He stared, transfixed, shifting his gaze from the sleeping infant to the mother, his heart pounding with bewildered awe.

"Us?" he finally gasped, his voice cracking. His wonder-struck eyes continued to wander between them, unable to reconcile the reality before him.

Margaret, equally stunned by the magnitude of the moment, cried out, "What! Does not a father recognize his own child? And a man of God, too? Fie, Gerard, to pretend! Nay, you are too wise, too good not to know—look at you both! 'Tis your own flesh and blood held to your heart!"

Clement trembled violently. "What words are these?" he stammered, clutching the sleeping figure. "Is this... is this my angel?"

"Whose else? Since he is mine."

Turning toward the slumbering child, Clement wore an expression beyond description, his entire frame shaking as his eyes seemed to drink in the tiny life. Margaret followed his gaze. "He bears no resemblance to me," she declared proudly. "Yet, at times, he is your very image in miniature. See this golden hair; yours was exactly this color at his age. Ask your mother. And notice the mole on his little finger. Now look at your own! There! Your mother once told me you bore that mark before him. Oh, Gerard, it was this child who found you for me! By that small mark on your finger, I recognized you as his father when I watched from above your window and saw you feeding the birds."

Seizing the child's hand, Margaret kissed it fervently, pressing half of it to her lips. "Ah! Bless thee! Thou didst find thy poor daddy for her, and now thou hast made us friends again after our little quarrel—the first and the last. Thou wast very cruel to me but now, my poor Gerard, but I forgive thee; for I love thy child."

"Ahh! Ahh! Ahh!" sobbed Clement, choking on his emotions. Lowered by prolonged fasting and unnerved by solitude, the once-strong man succumbed to hysteria, teetering on the brink of fainting.

Margaret felt alarm, yet her experience tempered her fear with deep pity. "Nay, do not distress yourself so," she murmured soothingly, placing a gentle hand upon his brow. "Be brave! So, so. Dear heart, thou art not the first man to journey abroad and return richer by a lovely little companion than he departed. As a man of God, take courage. Say that Heaven sends thee this child to comfort thee for what thou hast lost in me—and that is not so grievous, my lamb. For surely the better part of love shall never cool toward thee, though it may in thine, and ought, being a priest and parson of Gouda."

"I? Priest of Gouda? Never!" Clement whispered faintly. "I am a friar of St. Dominic. Yet, speak on, sweet music. Tell me all that has befallen thee before we part again."

Some might have protested against parting at all, raising the eternal question of separation. But women like Margaret do not repeat their mistakes; it is exceedingly difficult to defeat them twice when their hearts are set on a thing. She assented, turning her back on the Gouda manse as a place to be avoided, and began her tale. She dwelt especially on the kindness shown to her by his parents. As she recounted her troubles, Clement's hand instinctively sought hers. Often she felt him wince and tremble with righteous anger, and often he pressed her hand, sharing her grief in every vein.

"Oh, pitiable tale of a true heart battling alone against such bitter odds," he said. "It all seems small now that I see thee here again, nursing my boy. We have received a warning, Gerard. True friends like you and I are rare, and it is madness to part before death divides us."

"And that is true," Clement admitted, caught off guard.

Then she urged him to recount his sufferings for her sake. He initially begged pardon, asking her to excuse him, but she quietly revoked his request through gentle questioning until he yielded. Many a sigh she heaved for him, hiding her face in her hands with terror at the perils he had endured, though they were now past. To console him, she knelt, sliding her arms under the little boy to lift him gently.

"Kiss him softly," she whispered. "Again, kiss thy fill if thou canst; he is sound. 'Tis all I can do to comfort thee until thou art out of this foul den and in thy sweet manse yonder."

Clement shook his head.

"Well," she said, "let that pass. Know that I have been sorely affronted for the loss of my lines."

"Who dared affront thee?"

"No matter. Those who will do it again if thou hast lost them, which the saints forbid."

"I lose them? Nay, there they lie, close to thy hand."

"Where? Where, oh, where?"

Clement hung his head. "Look in the Vulgate."

"Heaven forgive me," she breathed, realizing her mistake. "I thought thou wert dead, a saint in heaven."

She looked, and on the blank leaves of the poor soul's Bible, she found her marriage lines.

"Thank God!" she cried, tears springing to her eyes. "Oh, bless thee, Gerard, bless thee! What is here, Gerard?"

On the other leaves were pinned every scrap of paper she had ever sent him: their two names written together in jest, the lock of her hair she had given him, half a silver coin broken with him, and even a straw she had used to suck her soup the first day he saw her. When Margaret beheld these proofs of love and signs of a gentle heart bereft, her exultation at recovering her lines was overwhelmed by surging tenderness. She almost staggered, her hand flying to her bosom, leaning her brow against the stone cell wall. She wept so silently that he did not perceive it; indeed, she refused to let him see, knowing that to befriend him now required her to be the stronger, for emotion weakens resolve.

"Gerard," she said, "I know you are wise and good. You must have a reason for what you are doing, however unreasonable it may seem. Let us talk like old friends. Why are you buried alive?"

"Margaret, to escape temptation. My impious rage against those two sprang from the heart; that heart I must deaden, and, *Dei gratia*, I shall. Shall I, a servant of Christ and the Church, court temptation? Shall I pray daily to be led into it, and walk into it with open eyes?"

"That is good sense anyway," Margaret remarked with feigned candor. "'Tis unanswerable," Clement sighed.

"We shall see. Tell me, have you escaped temptation here? I ask because, when I am alone, my thoughts grow wilder and more foolish than in company. Nay, speak truth; come!"

"I must confess I have been more severely tempted here by evil imaginings than in the world."

"There now."

"Ay, but so were Anthony and Jerome, Macarius and Hilarion, Benedict, Bernard, and all the saints. 'Twill wear off."

"How do you know?"

"I feel sure it will."

"Guessing against knowledge. Here, men are sillier than we women. Wise in their own conceits, they refuse to see; their stomachs are too proud to be taught by their eyes. If a woman went into a hole in a bank to escape temptation and found it there, she would simply lift her skirt and step out, unaware of her wisdom until she watched a man in similar plight."

"Nay, I grant that humility and a teachable spirit are the paths to wisdom; but when all is said, here I wrestle only with imagination. At Gouda, she whom I loved as no priest or monk must love any but angels, she will tempt a weak soul, unwilling yet not loath to be tempted."

"Ay, that is another matter; I should tempt thee then? To what, in God's name?"

"Who knows? The flesh is weak."

"Speak for yourself, my lad. Why, you are thinking of some other Margaret, not Margaret a Peter. Was ever my mind turned to folly and frailty? Stay, is it because you were my husband once, as these lines prove? Do you think the road to folly is beaten for you more than for another? Oh! How shallow are the wise, and how little able are you to read me, who can read you so well from top to toe. Come, learn thy ABC. Were a stranger to proffer me unchaste love, I should shrink a bit, no doubt, and feel sore, but I should defend myself without making a scene; for men, I know, are so, the best of them sometimes. But if

Margaret returned to Rotterdam long before Gerard awoke, leaving her young son behind. She dispatched the loyal, sturdy Reicht immediately to Gouda, entrusting him with a vicar's grey frock and a large felt hat, along with detailed instructions on how to care for his new charge.

Then she visited Jorian Ketel. To herself, she reasoned, "He is the closest confidant I have ever known; therefore, he is the man for me." Together, they orchestrated two acts of mortal cunning—though, in my view, not malicious, despite her belief that they were. If pressed to name these unnamed deeds, the answer remains silent, for she, being merely a woman, guarded them until her deathbed, whereas I, a man—"Verbum non amplius addam."

She deliberately avoided the Gouda parsonage.

Matters often overlooked during heated arguments can later rankle deeply. Upon reviewing their history, Margaret was offended that Gerard had assumed she could ever forget the priest who had once been her lover. "For what did he take me?" she asked herself. This realization bred a profound shyness she would not otherwise have felt, given her inherent innocence. Pride joined forces with modesty, whispering, "Do not return to the Gouda parsonage."

She left little Gerard there to complete the spiritual conquest her maternal heart attributed to him—not to her own eloquence or wisdom—but to secure his father forever within the fold of humanity.

Yet, this generous act of policy cost her dearly. She had never been parted from her boy for an hour, and she felt strangely desolate in his absence. By the second day, the separation became unbearable. What did the poor soul do but creep to the Gouda parsonage at dusk, lurking around the grounds like a thief until she spotted Reicht Heynes alone in the kitchen? She tapped softly at the window and pleaded, "Reicht, for pity's sake, bring him out to me unseen." At that moment, the person occupying Margaret's thoughts lost her name and sank into a mere pronoun.

Reicht soon found an excuse to retrieve little Gerard. A scene of mutual rapture ensued, followed by shared tears when mother and child were forced apart again.

An arrangement was made: Reicht would take the boy halfway to Rotterdam daily at a fixed hour, meeting Margaret on the journey. During these encounters, after the initial joyous reunion and playful gamboling between mother and child, the boy would occasionally amuse himself alone at their feet. Seizing this opportunity, the two women engaged in serious conversation regarding Luke Peterson.

"Reicht," Margaret began, "I practically promised him I would marry Luke Peterson. 'Say the word,' I told him, 'and I will wed him.'"

"Poor Luke!" Reicht sighed.

"Prithee, why 'poor Luke'?"

"To be tossed back and forth between yes and no."

"Why, Reicht, have you never been so foolish as to cast an affectionate glance upon the boy, thereby taking his part?"

"Me?" Reicht replied, tossing his head dismissively.

"Oh, forgive me. Well, then, you can do me a favor."

"Hush! Whisper! That little darling is listening to every word, with eyes wide as saucers."

Upon hearing this, both women instinctively ducked their heads under a single cap. Two women plotting against one boy? Oh, you great, cowardly serpents!

However, after approximately five days of these clandestine meetings, Margaret began to feel the injustice of the situation, growing irritated and unhappy.

She was weeping over it when a cart arrived at her door. Inside sat the Vicar of Gouda, dressed in the grey frock and large felt hat she had sent him. He appeared as clean as a new penny, his beard freshly shaved, his hands white as snow, with a faint color returning to his pale face.

She rushed upstairs immediately, washing away the traces of her tears, and changed into a cap that was theoretically cleaner, having just been removed from the drawer. Descending to greet him, he seized both her hands and kissed them, a tear falling upon them. She turned her head away to conceal the one starting in her own eyes.

"My sweet Margaret," he cried, "why this distance? Why hold yourself aloof from your own good deed? We have waited for you every day, and no Margaret appears."

"You said things."

"What! When I was a hermit and a donkey?"

"Ay! No matter, you said things. And you had no reason."

"Forget everything I said there. Who listens to the ravings of a madman? For I now see that in a few months I should have become a gibbering idiot; yet no mortal could have persuaded me away but you. Oh, what an expenditure of wit and goodness was yours! But it is not here that I can thank and bless you as I ought.
