No, it is in the home you have given me, among the sheep whose shepherd you have made me. Already I love them dearly; there it is I must thank 'the truest friend a man ever had.' So now I say to you, as once you said to me, come to the Gouda manse."

"Hmph! We shall see about that."

"Why, Margaret, do you think I ever kept the dear child so long unless I was certain you would return to him day after day? Oh, he curls round my very heartstrings, but what claim do I have to him compared to yours? Confess now, you have harbored hard thoughts of me for this."

"Nay, nay, not I. Ah! You are yourself again; you have always been thoughtful of others. I have half a mind to go to the Gouda manse simply because you said so."

"Come then, with half your mind; 'tis worth the whole of other folk's."

"Well, I suppose I will; but there is no such urgent hurry," she said coolly (though she was literally burning to go). "First, tell me how you agree with your people."

"Why, already my poor have taken root in my heart."

"I thought as much."

"And there are such good creatures among them; simple, rough, and superstitious, yet wonderfully good."

"Oh, I leave you alone for finding a grain of good amidst a bushel of ill."

"Hush! Hush! And Margaret, two of them had been enemies for four years, each coming to the manse hoping to gain advantage over me on the other's blind side. But give the glory to God, I gained their bright side, made them friends, and caused them to laugh at their own folly."

"But are you truly their vicar? Answer me that."

"Certainly; have I not gone to the bishop, taken the oath, rung the church bell, touched the altar, the missal, and the holy chalice before the church-wardens? And they have handed me the parish seal; see, here it is. Nay, 'tis a real vicar inviting a true friend to the Gouda manse."

"Then my mind is at ease. Tell me oceans more."

"Well, sweet one, nearest to me of all my parish is a poor cripple that my guardian angel and hers (whose name you know, even by this turning of your head away) have placed beneath my roof. Sybrandt and I are brothers now, something we never were before. 'Twould gladden you, yet sadden you, to hear how we kissed and forgave one another. He is full of your praises and wholly in a pious state; he says he is happier since his affliction than ever he was in the days of his strength. Oh! Out of my house, he shall never go anywhere but to heaven."

"Tell me something that happened to you yourself, poor soul! All this is good, but yet no news to me. Do I not know you of old?"

"Well, let me see. At first, I was much dazzled by the sunlight and could not go abroad (an owl!), but that has passed. And good Reicht Heynes—hmmph!"

"What of her?"

"This to your ear only, for she is a diamond. Her voice cuts through me like a knife, and all voices seem loud save yours, which is so mellow and sweet. Stay, now I will fit you with tidings; I spoke yesterday with an old man who pretends to be ill-tempered, sweating to appear so to others, but oh, so threadbare, and possessing the best good heart beneath."

"Why, 'tis a parish of angels," Margaret remarked ironically.

"Then why do you keep aloof from it?" Gerard retorted. "Well, he was telling me there is no parish in Holland where the Devil holds such sway as at Gouda. Among his examples, he said, 'We had a hermit, the holiest in Holland; but being Gouda, the Devil came for him this week and took him, bag and baggage; not a scrap of him left but a goodish piece of his skin, just like a hedgehog's, and a piece of old iron polished up.'"

Margaret smiled.

"Ay, but," continued Gerard, "the strange thing is, the cave has truly collapsed. Had I been so perverse as to resist you, it would surely have buried me dead there where I had buried myself alive. Therefore, in this, I see the finger of Providence, condemning my former path and approving my current way of life. What sayest thou?"

"Nay, can I

A priest finds his truest vocation not in the pulpit, but in the chamber of death. Gerard performed the final rites for the departed with the same mechanical precision he would have applied to his humblest parishioner. Once the duties were done, however, he sat stunned, paralyzed by the sudden, terrible blow, unable yet to comprehend the depth of his loss. In a fleeting surge of religious fervor, he knelt to thank Heaven for her Christian passing. Immediately after, every thought turned to removing her from the gaze of strangers and laying her to rest in his own churchyard.

That very evening, a covered cart drawn by a single horse set off toward Gouda. Inside lay a coffin and a brokenhearted man, his arms and chin resting heavily upon it. The mourner's brief reserve of strength had been spent on the necessary preparations; now he lay crushed, clutching the cold iron latch that secured her remains. The driver, hired for the journey, walked beside the horse's head in silence, offering not even a word of comfort. When the horse needed rest, the driver whispered respectfully to the man, acknowledging the mute agony before him.

When they halted for the night, both the driver and the innkeeper made a kindly attempt to urge the mourner to leave the cart and seek rest and food. Gerard repelled them fiercely. When they pressed further, he snarled at them like a loyal dog guarding its master, refusing to let go of the cold metal all through the long night. Finally, they draped a heavy cloak over him and left him undisturbed.

At noon the following day, the sorrowful cart arrived at the rectory. A dozen parishioners had gathered, drawn by some trivial concern, only to find the parson missing. Upon seeing the scene—their healer so utterly stricken—they erupted in a loud wail of grief. This sound tore through Gerard's lethargy of despair. He looked around, recognizing the familiar faces, and tried to speak. "Oh, my children! My children!" he cried, but choked back the words against the tide of his anguish.

Yet, despite all protests, he buried her himself the next day. He read the burial service with a voice that trembled only occasionally, though many tears fell upon the fresh earth. When the service concluded, he remained standing amidst the graves like a statue, while the people departed out of deep respect. He stood frozen in a trance until the sexton—a man of simple wit—began filling the grave without warning. At the first sound of earth falling upon her, Gerard let out a piercing scream. The sexton paused immediately. Gerard staggered, pressing a hand to his breast. The sexton rushed to support him and summoned aid.

Jorian Ketel, who had lingered nearby mourning his benefactress, ran into the churchyard. Together, the two men helped Gerard back to the manse. "Ah, Jorian! Good Jorian!" he gasped, gripping the other's arm. "Something has snapped within me; I felt it, and I heard it break. Here, Jorian, right here..." He pressed his hand firmly against his chest.

Two weeks later, a pale, bowed figure made its way into the Dominican convent situated in the suburbs of Gouda. Seeking audience with Brother Ambrose—who governed the house as deputy following the recent death of the prior, whose successor had yet to arrive—the visitor was none other than Gerard. He came to end his life as he had begun it. Though he had entered initially as a novice upon probation, the truth was that he was a failing man, acutely aware of his decline. He sought only to pass away in peace, surrounded by the kindness of Ambrose, his dear friend, and the other monks whose doors and hearts had always remained open to him.

His own home had become unbearable; it was too saturated with painful memories of her. Ambrose, recognizing both the worth of his soul and the depth of his grief, harbored a gentle hope that he might be cured and restored to his parish duties. To this end, he assigned Gerard a comfortable cell directly above the gateway and strictly forbade him from fasting or engaging in any acts of austerity. Yet, before these remedies could take hold, the newly appointed prior arrived. He proved to be a stern disciplinarian, a "very Tartar" indeed. Initially consumed with rectifying abuses and tightening the monastic rules, his demeanor shifted abruptly when he learned that the Vicar of Gouda had also entered the order as a novice.

"'Tis well," the new prior declared sharply. "Let him first resign his vicarage, then remain; otherwise, depart. I will have no fat parsons in my house."

Soon thereafter, the prior summoned Gerard. The younger monk obeyed and presented himself before the superior. The moment their eyes met, both men recoiled in startled recognition.

"Clement!" cried the prior.

"Jerome!" replied the novice.

Jerome remained as stern and gloomy as ever in his general disposition, yet he had grown considerably softer toward Gerard. Throughout his stay in England, he had missed his brother far more than he realized until now, frequently wondering where he had gone. The news he received in Gouda elevated his opinion of the younger monk; particularly the report that Gerard had stood firm against the Pope and the Minorites regarding "the infernal heresy of the Immaculate Conception," as Jerome termed it. However, when a young novice recounted the cause of Gerard's illness with tears streaming down his face, Jerome's contempt flared anew. "Dying for a woman?" he muttered. Determined to prevent such a scandal, Jerome visited Clement twice daily in his cell, deploying all his former influence and eloquence to urge him to abandon this unspiritual despondency. He argued that the Church could not afford to lose its piety and eloquence at such a critical juncture.

Gerard listened, acknowledged the logic, admired Jerome's strength, and confessed his own frailty, though he visibly continued to fade into the realm of the dead. One day, Jerome revealed that he had heard Gerard's full story and admitted hearing it with pride. "But now," Jerome declared, "you ruin everything. This is the triumph of earthly passion. It would have been better to yield and repent than to resist while she lived and finally succumb now, destroying both body and soul."

"Dear Jerome," Clement replied, his sweetness stripping the rebuke of its edge, "I believe you misunderstand me. There is no passion here, only a profound affection for which I need not blush, nor shall I blush for it in heaven. Consider, Jerome: does the poor dog who dies of grief beside his master's grave commit sin through passion? Neither do I. Passion would have saved my life but cost me my soul. She was my guardian angel; she upheld me in my duties and charity. Her face encouraged me in the pulpit; her lips soothed me under ingratitude. She became woven into every good thing in my life, and after leaning on her so long, I could not walk alone. And, dear Jerome, believe me, I am no rebel against Heaven. It is God's will to release me. When they threw the earth upon her poor coffin, something snapped within my chest—a break that cannot be mended. From that moment, Jerome, no food tasted of anything. Even with my eyes bandaged, I could not distinguish bread from flesh by taste."

"Holy saints!" Jerome exclaimed.

"And from that same hour," Clement continued, "my deep depression vanished, and I began to smile again. Why? I read it thus: He who holds the keys of life and death gave me that minute the great summons; a cord of life snapped within me. He is merciful. I would have lived unhappily, but He said, 'No; enough is done, enough is suffered. Poor, feeble, loving servant, your faults are forgiven, your sorrows mark your end; come to your rest.' I come, Lord, I come!"

Jerome groaned. "The Church has always had her holy yet feeble servants," he lamented. "Now I would give ten years of my life to save yours. But I see it cannot be. Die in peace."

Thus, within a few days, Gerard lay dying in a state of such holy happiness that several aged saints seemed present to gather his final words. That evening, he had seen Giles, begging him not to let poor Jack starve, ensuring the trustees fulfilled their duties, kissing his parents for him, and sending Denys to his friends in Burgundy: "Poor thing, he will feel so strange here without his comrade." Following this, he had a private audience with Jerome alone. What transpired between them remains unclear, but it must have been extraordinary, for Jerome left the room with his hands clasped over his breast, his proud head bowed low, sighing heavily as he walked away.

The two monks who kept vigil with him until Matins reported that throughout the night, Gerard periodically burst forth in pious ejaculations, praises, and thanksgivings. Once, however, they noted he wandered, seemingly seeing her walking in green meadows among other spirits clad in white, beckoning him; and they, too, smiled and waved back. Both monks claimed—though perhaps fancifully—that just before dawn, three light taps echoed against the wall, one after another, very slowly. The dying man heard them and whispered, "I come, love, I come."

It is certain that Gerard uttered these words and prepared for his departure. He summoned all the monks keeping vigil. They gathered around him like gentle spirits, speaking holy words. Some prayed silently with faces touching the ground; others tenderly supported his head. When one mentioned his life of self-denial and charity, Gerard stopped him. Addressing them all, he said, "My dear brethren, take note: he who dies here so happy does not hold these new-fangled doctrines of human merit. Oh, what a miserable hour it would have been for me if I did! Nay, I hold, with the Apostles and their successors in the Church, the ancient Fathers, that we are justified not by our own wisdom, piety, or works performed in holiness of heart, but by faith."

Silence fell, and the monks exchanged significant glances. "If you please, sweep the floor," the dying Christian commanded, his voice suddenly clear and powerful, seeming supernaturally restored. They instantly obeyed, moved by awe and curiosity. "Make a great cross with wood ashes." They scattered the ashes on the floor in the shape of a large Cross. "Now lay me down upon it, for thus I shall die."

They gently lifted him from his bed and placed him upon the ash-cross. "Shall we spread out your arms, dear brother?" someone asked.

"Now God forbid! Am I worthy of that?" he replied. He lay silent, eyes raised in ecstasy. Soon, he spoke half to them, half to himself, "Oh," he murmured with subdued but intense rapture, "I feel it buoyant. It lifts me, floating in the sky, whereas my merits had sunk me like lead."

Daybreak revealed his face cast upward in silent rapture, his hands joined, resembling Margaret's. Just about the hour she died, he spoke his last word in this world. "Jesu!" And with that word, he fell asleep.

They laid him out for burial. Beneath his linen, they discovered a horsehair shirt. "Ah!" cried the young monks, "Behold a saint!" Underneath the haircloth, they found a long, thick tress of auburn hair. They recoiled, horrified, and a babble of voices erupted, some condemning, some excusing. Amidst the commotion, Jerome entered. Hearing the dispute, he turned to an ardent young monk named Basil, who was shouting scandal most loudly.

"Basil," Jerome said, "Is she alive or dead that owns this hair?"

"How can I know, Father?"

"Then, for all you know, it may be the relic of a saint?"

"Certainly it may," Basil replied skeptically.

"You have then broken our Rule, which says, 'Do not make evil constructions on any act done by a brother which can be construed innocently.' Who are you to judge such a man? Go to your cell and do not leave for a week as penance."

He then removed the lock of hair. When the coffin was to be sealed, Jerome cleared the cell and placed the tress upon the dead man's bosom. "There, Clement," he said to the lifeless face. He set himself a penance for the act and nailed the coffin shut himself.

The following day, Gerard was buried in the Gouda churchyard. The monks followed him in procession from the convent. Jerome, clearly executing the deceased's wishes, read the service. The grave was deep, containing a leaden coffin at the bottom. Poor Gerard's body, light as a feather due to his wasting, was lowered and placed beside it. After the service, Jerome addressed the crowd of parishioners who had come for a final look at their beloved friend. As he spoke of the departed's virtues, loud wailing and weeping burst forth, and tears fell upon the coffin like rain.

The monks returned home. Jerome gathered them in the refectory and spoke thus: "We have today laid a saint in the earth. The convent will keep his trentals, but we shall feast, not fast; for our good brother is freed from the burden of the flesh; his labors are over, and he has entered into his joyful rest. I alone shall fast and do penance; for to my shame I confess, I was unjust to him and knew not his worth until it was too late. And you, young monks, do not be curious to inquire whether the lock he bore on his bosom was a token of pure affection or the relic of a saint; but remember the heart he wore beneath. Most of all, fix your eyes upon his life and conversation, and follow them if you may: for he was a holy man."

So, after life's fitful fever, these true lovers found peace. The grave, kinder to them than the Church, united them forever. Now, a man of another age and nation, touched by their fate, has labored to build their tombstone and rescue them from long and undeserved oblivion. He asks for your sympathy, but not your pity. No, put this story to a wholesome use. Fiction often presents false views of life and death. Here, constrained by history, it offers true ones

In obedience to a custom I detest, yet lack the courage to defy, I pause here to gather the scattered fragments of humanity I have introduced along the way. Let it be understood that these minor figures hold little personal claim upon me; they serve merely to convince you that Gerard traversed the distance from Holland to Rome among real people, rather than walking through a parade of lifeless dolls.

Eli and Catherine lived to a remarkable age. They survived so long that both Gerard and Margaret eventually faded into dim memories. Giles too was long-lived; he traveled to the court of Bavaria and remained alive there at ninety, though he had somehow shrunk into nothing more than leather and bone, his voice reduced to a hollow trumpet tone. Cornelis, unchallenged by rivals and long forgiven by his mother—who clung to him with increasing intensity as her children dispersed—waited endlessly for his parents' deaths. But Catherine's sharp prediction proved true: before she and her partner wore themselves out, this worthy man rusted away. At sixty-five, lying dying of old age in his mother's embrace—a hale woman of eighty-six—he regained consciousness in his final moments. Seeing her beside him, he spoke of transforming the shop and its premises once they were his own. "Yes, my darling," the poor old woman soothed gently. Within another minute, he was clay, and that clay was followed to the grave by every footstep he had ever waited for.

Denys, shattered by his comrade's death, returned to Burgundy. There, a modest pension granted by the court sustained him until, unexpectedly, he inherited a substantial sum from a distant relative. For many years, he was known in his homeland as a crusty old soldier, capable of recounting tales of war when he pleased, yet always a bitter critic of women. Jerome, disgusted by the laxity of the north, retired to Italy. Through high connections, he became a mitred abbot at the age of seventy. He imposed strict discipline upon his monks, whom he simultaneously revered and loathed. He ruled with an iron rod for ten years. Then, one night, he died alone, for he had never learned the path to a single human heart. The Vulgate lay upon his pillow, the crucifix in his hand, and upon his lips rested something resembling a smile—not seen during his entire life. Thus, I believe, in that terrible hour, he was not entirely alone. *Requiescat in pace.* The Master He serves has many servants, each with many minds; occasionally, a faithful one proves to be a surly one, just as it often happens within our mortal dwellings.

The golden-haired lad, Gerard Gerardson, does not belong to fiction but to history. She has recorded his birth in terms far different from mine. Above the tailor's house in the Brede Kirk Street, she has inscribed: *"Haec est parva domus natus qua magnus Erasmus,"* and she has penned half a dozen biographies of him. Yet, there remains much for her still to accomplish. She understands the great Erasmus no better than any other dwarf comprehends a giant, or a partisan judges a magistrate. As the first scholar and divine of his era, he was also the heaven-born dramatist of his century. Many of the finest scenes in this new volume originate from his medieval pen, illuminating the pages where they appear. For the words of such a towering genius are not born to die. Once their immediate impact upon mankind is fulfilled, they may seem to lie dormant; yet, with every fresh shower of intelligence that Time pours upon their students, they demonstrate their immortal lineage. They revive, rising from the dust of great libraries; they bud, flower, bear fruit, and sow seeds, passing from generation to generation, from age to age.

End of Project Gutenberg's *The Cloister and the Hearth*, by Charles Reade.
